Chapter 1: Crimson

Pain!

What unbearable pain!

My head is killing me!

A dazzling dreamscape filled with murmurs shattered in an instant. Deep in sleep, Zhou Mingrui felt a violent throbbing in his skull, as though someone were striking him over and over with a club. No—it felt more like a sharp object had pierced both temples and was twisting inside them.

Ouch...

Still half-conscious, Zhou Mingrui tried to turn over, raise his head, and sit up. His limbs refused to move, as though his body no longer belonged to him.

Looks like I'm still asleep. This is still a dream...

Maybe the next thing I'll dream is that I've woken up, only to discover I'm still asleep...

It was not the first time Zhou Mingrui had experienced something like this. He struggled to focus, hoping to break free of the darkness and confusion that held him captive.

But whatever willpower he could summon was as insubstantial as mist. His thoughts slipped beyond his control, and every attempt to concentrate dissolved into a stream of stray ideas.

Why would I suddenly get such a terrible headache in the middle of the night?

And why does it hurt so much?

Could it be a brain hemorrhage?

Shit. Don't tell me I'm going to die young.

I need to wake up. Now!

Huh? It doesn't hurt quite as much as before. So why does it still feel as though someone is sawing through my brain with a dull knife?

There's no way I'm getting back to sleep. How am I supposed to go to work tomorrow?

Why am I worrying about work at a time like this? With a headache this bad, of course I'm taking the day off. Let my manager complain all he wants!

Actually, when I put it that way, this might not be so bad. Heh. I could finally have some time to myself.

Wave after wave of pain washed through Zhou Mingrui. Slowly, he gathered enough strength to move his back and force open his eyes, finally wrenching himself free of the nightmare.

His blurred vision gradually cleared beneath a faint crimson glow. Directly in front of him stood a sturdy wooden desk. An open notebook lay at its center, its pages coarse and yellowed. Strange words had been written across the page in heavy black ink, stark enough to seize the eye.

About eight books were stacked neatly to the notebook's left. On the wall beyond them ran several grayish-white pipes connected to a lamp.

The lamp was fashioned in an old Western style and was roughly half the size of an adult's head. Clear glass formed its inner layer, while a black metal grille enclosed the outside.

Below it and slightly to one side, a bottle of black ink sat shrouded in pale red light. A blurred angel had been embossed on its surface.

In front of the ink bottle, to the notebook's right, lay a dark, round-bodied pen. Its nib caught the light. Beside its cap rested a brass revolver.

A gun? A revolver?

Zhou Mingrui stared in astonishment. Everything before him was unfamiliar. This looked nothing like his room.

As shock gave way to confusion, he noticed that the desk, notebook, ink bottle, and revolver were all draped in a veil of crimson light streaming through the window.

He raised his head almost without thinking, his gaze inching upward.

A crimson moon hung high against the night, glowing silently upon a curtain of black velvet.

What...?

An inexplicable terror seized Zhou Mingrui, and he shot to his feet. Before he could fully straighten, another pulse of agony tore through his skull. The strength vanished from his legs, and he dropped heavily back onto the sturdy wooden chair.

Thud!

He barely noticed the impact. Bracing himself against the desk, he rose again and spun around, frantically taking in his surroundings.

The room was small, with a brown door set into either side. A low wooden bed stood against the wall opposite him.

Between the bed and the door on the left was a cabinet. Both cabinet doors hung open, with five drawers beneath them.

Beside the cabinet, another grayish-white pipe ran along the wall at shoulder height. It connected to a peculiar mechanical device whose gears and bearings lay exposed.

Something resembling a coal stove occupied the corner to his right, near the desk. Soup pots, iron pans, and other kitchen utensils surrounded it.

Opposite the right-hand door stood a dressing mirror cracked in two places. It rested in a plain wooden frame decorated with simple patterns.

As his gaze swept across it, Zhou Mingrui caught sight of his reflection—of the person he had become.

Black hair. Brown eyes. A linen shirt. A slender build, ordinary features, and a face with rather pronounced contours...

Zhou Mingrui sucked in a breath. A host of bewildering possibilities rushed through his mind, none of them comforting.

The old-fashioned European revolver and the crimson moon—so different from the moon he knew—could mean only one thing.

H-have I transmigrated?

His mouth fell slightly open.

He had grown up reading web novels and had often imagined himself in exactly this situation. Yet now that it had happened, he found it almost impossible to accept.

So this is what it means to be Lord Ye, claiming to love dragons until a real one appears...

Within moments, Zhou Mingrui was already cursing his former self even as he tried to make the best of his predicament.

If the pain in his head had not kept his thoughts tense but unmistakably clear, he would have been certain this was a dream.

Calm down. Calm down. Calm down...

He took several deep breaths and forced his panic under control.

As his mind and body gradually settled, memories began to surface, flooding into his consciousness.

Klein Moretti, a citizen of Tingen City in Awwa County, part of the Loen Kingdom on the Northern Continent. A recent graduate of the History Department at Khoy University...

His father had been a sergeant in the Imperial Army and had died in a colonial conflict on the Southern Continent. The bereavement allowance had enabled Klein to attend a private language school, laying the foundation for his eventual admission to university...

His mother, a devout follower of the Evernight Goddess, had died the same year Klein passed Khoy University's entrance examinations...

He had an elder brother and a younger sister. The three of them shared a two-bedroom apartment...

The family was far from wealthy. In truth, they could barely make ends meet. At present, they depended entirely on the wages of Klein's elder brother, a clerk at an import-export company...

As a history graduate, Klein could read ancient Feysac, considered the root of every language on the Northern Continent. He also knew Hermes, a language frequently found in ancient mausoleums and in texts concerning sacrifices and prayer rituals...

Hermes?

Something stirred in Zhou Mingrui's mind. Rubbing his throbbing temples, he turned toward the open notebook on the desk. The writing on its yellowed page changed before his eyes: first strange, then merely foreign, and at last familiar and legible.

It was written in Hermes.

In dark ink, the page read:

“Everyone will die, including me.”

Zhou Mingrui drew a sharp breath. A nameless horror came over him, and he instinctively leaned away, putting as much distance as he could between himself and the notebook—and the sentence written inside it.

Weak as he was, he nearly collapsed. He flung out both hands and caught the edge of the desk just in time. The air around him seemed to ripple, faint whispers echoing through it. They reminded him of the ghost stories elders had told him as a child.

He shook his head, telling himself it was an illusion. Once he had regained his balance, he tore his gaze from the notebook and stood there panting.

This time, his eyes came to rest on the gleaming brass revolver. A question surfaced at once.

Given Klein's family circumstances, how could they possibly afford a revolver? And where would they even have obtained one?

Zhou Mingrui frowned.

As he considered the question, he noticed a red handprint beside the notebook. It was darker and thicker than the crimson veil cast by the moonlight.

It was blood.

A bloody handprint?

Zhou Mingrui turned over the right hand with which he had been gripping the desk. His palm and fingers were smeared with blood.

Meanwhile, the throbbing in his head continued. It had weakened slightly, but it had never stopped.

Did I split my head open?

He turned and walked toward the cracked dressing mirror.

After a few steps, the reflection of a black-haired, brown-eyed young man of average build came clearly into view. He had the distinct air of a scholar.

Is that me now? Klein Moretti?

Zhou Mingrui stood frozen for a moment. The room was poorly lit, and he could not make out every detail. He moved closer until he was only a step away from the glass.

Guided by the veil-like crimson moonlight, he tilted his head and examined his temple.

The mirror revealed a grotesque wound. Its edges were blackened as though burned, and blood stained the surrounding skin. Within the gash, grayish-white brain matter slowly writhed.

Translator's note: “Lord Ye loved dragons” is a Chinese idiom about professing enthusiasm for something while fearing the real thing. Lord Ye decorated his home with images of dragons, but when a real dragon came to visit him, he fled in terror.

Chapter 2: Situation

Tap! Tap! Tap!

Zhou Mingrui staggered backward in horror. The figure in the dressing mirror looked less like him than a desiccated corpse.

How could anyone still be alive with a wound like that?

He turned his head and checked the other side in disbelief. Despite the distance and poor light, he could still make out the deep wound and the dark-red blood surrounding it.

“This...”

Zhou Mingrui drew a long breath and struggled to compose himself.

He pressed a hand to the left side of his chest. His heart raced beneath his palm, each beat brimming with vitality.

Next, he touched his exposed skin. It felt slightly cold, but there was living warmth beneath it.

He crouched to make sure his knees could bend, then rose again, a little calmer than before.

“What's going on?” he muttered with a frown. He needed to examine the wound more carefully.

After taking two steps toward the mirror, however, he stopped. The crimson moon cast too little light for a proper inspection.

A fragment of memory surfaced. Zhou Mingrui turned toward the grayish-white pipes and the metal-grilled lamp mounted on the wall beside the desk.

It was an ordinary gas lamp of the period, prized for its steady flame and excellent illumination.

Given the Moretti family's finances, even a kerosene lamp would have been a luxury, let alone a gas lamp. Candles were far more appropriate to their means. Four years earlier, however, when Klein had been studying late into the night for Khoy University's entrance examinations, his elder brother Benson had insisted that the family's future depended on giving him suitable conditions in which to study—even if they had to go into debt.

Benson was literate and had already spent several years in the workforce. He was no reckless fool. Instead of borrowing money outright, he persuaded their landlord that installing gas pipes would improve the apartment and make it easier to rent in the future. The landlord agreed to pay for the basic modifications. Benson then used his position at an import-export company to purchase a new gas lamp at nearly wholesale price. In the end, his savings covered the expense.

As the memory faded, Zhou Mingrui approached the desk, opened the valve on the pipe, and turned the lamp's switch.

The mechanism sputtered, and friction produced a spark. But the expected light did not appear.

He tried several more times. Each attempt brought only another sputter, while the lamp remained stubbornly dark.

“Hmm...”

Zhou Mingrui withdrew his hand and pressed his left temple, searching Klein's fragmented memories for an explanation.

A few seconds later, he turned and walked toward the mechanical device embedded in the opposite wall, connected to the same grayish-white pipes.

It was a gas meter.

At the sight of its exposed gears and bearings, Zhou Mingrui reached into his trouser pocket and took out a coin.

It was dark yellow with a bronze sheen. The front bore the portrait of a crowned man; the reverse showed the number “1” over a sheaf of wheat.

Zhou Mingrui recognized it as the smallest unit of currency in the Loen Kingdom: a copper penny. Its purchasing power was roughly equivalent to three or four yuan in his former world. Other copper denominations included the five-pence coin, the halfpenny, and the quarter-penny. Even with those options, everyday prices did not always divide neatly enough, so people sometimes had to buy several items simply to use a single coin.

He turned the penny over a few times. It was a design minted only after King George III had ascended the throne. Then he slid it into the meter's narrow vertical slot.

Clink! Clang!

The coin dropped to the bottom, and the gears immediately began to turn, producing a brief but pleasing mechanical rhythm.

Zhou Mingrui watched the meter for several seconds before returning to the sturdy wooden desk. He reached out and turned the lamp's switch again.

The mechanism sputtered, followed by a sharp click.

A tongue of flame sprang to life and quickly swelled. Warm light filled the lamp, passed through the clear glass, and spread across the room.

The darkness retreated, and the crimson glow withdrew beyond the window. For reasons he could not explain, Zhou Mingrui felt some of his unease lift. He hurried back to the dressing mirror.

This time, he examined his temple carefully, overlooking nothing.

After several passes, he realized that no fresh blood was flowing from the grotesque wound. It looked as though it had already received expert treatment. Grayish-white brain matter still shifted faintly inside it, but flesh was visibly knitting together around the edges. At that rate, the injury might close within thirty or forty minutes—two or three hours at most—leaving behind only a faint scar.

“A benefit of transmigration?” Zhou Mingrui murmured, one corner of his mouth twitching upward.

Then he released a long breath. Whatever else had happened, he was alive.

Once he had steadied himself, he opened a drawer and removed a small piece of soap. He took an old, frayed towel hanging beside the cabinet, opened the door, and headed for the communal bathroom shared by the tenants on the second floor.

I need to wash the blood off my head, or I'll keep looking like a murder scene. Frightening myself is one thing, but if Melissa sees me like this when she wakes up tomorrow morning, there'll be no end of trouble.

The corridor outside was pitch-black. Crimson moonlight filtered through the window at its far end, barely outlining the objects around him. In the darkness, the window resembled a pair of monstrous eyes silently watching the living deep into the night.

Suppressing a shiver, Zhou Mingrui softened his footsteps and made his way to the bathroom.

More moonlight entered there, enough for him to see clearly. He stopped before a washbasin and turned the tap.

At the sound of rushing water, he suddenly remembered his landlord, Mr. Franky.

Water was included in the rent, so the short, thin gentleman—forever dressed in a top hat, waistcoat, and black suit—made a habit of patrolling the bathroom and listening for running taps.

If the water flowed too freely, Mr. Franky would cast aside every trace of gentlemanly restraint. He would bang his walking stick against the bathroom door and shout, “Damn thief!” or “Waste is a disgrace!” He might follow it with, “I'll remember you!” “Do that again and you can leave with your filthy luggage!” or, inevitably, “Mark my words, this is the best-value apartment in all of Tingen! You will never find a kinder landlord!”

Putting the thought aside, Zhou Mingrui dampened the towel and repeatedly wiped the blood from his face.

He checked his reflection in the bathroom's battered mirror. Once he had confirmed that only a hideous wound and a deathly pale complexion remained, he relaxed. Then he removed his linen shirt and scrubbed away the bloodstains with soap.

As he worked, his brow suddenly furrowed.

The wound was horrific, and there had been far too much blood. His body could not be the only place where traces remained. His room must still bear evidence of whatever had happened.

A few minutes later, after washing the shirt, Zhou Mingrui hurried back to the apartment with the damp towel. He first wiped the bloody handprint from the desk, then searched the room by gaslight for anything else he might have missed.

He quickly found a substantial spray of blood on the floor beneath the desk. A brass-colored bullet lay near the wall to the left.

“A revolver fired against the temple?”

Putting the clues together, Zhou Mingrui formed a rough idea of how Klein had died.

He did not immediately test the theory. Instead, he painstakingly wiped away the blood and cleaned the scene. Only then did he pick up the bullet and return to the desk. He swung out the revolver's cylinder and emptied its contents.

Five live rounds and one spent casing fell out, all gleaming brass.

“Just as I thought...”

Zhou Mingrui studied the empty casing, nodded, and loaded the cartridges back into the cylinder.

His gaze drifted left to the sentence in the notebook: “Everyone will die, including me.” More questions crowded into his mind.

Where had the gun come from?

Had Klein truly killed himself, or had someone staged his suicide?

What sort of trouble could a history graduate from a poor family possibly have become entangled in?

And why had such a violent death left so little blood behind? Had Zhou Mingrui arrived at exactly the right moment, bringing some miraculous healing ability with him?

After thinking for a while, he changed into a clean linen shirt and sat down. There were more important matters to consider.

For now, Klein's experiences were secondary. The real questions were why Zhou Mingrui had transmigrated—and whether he could return home.

His parents and relatives. His closest friends. The boundless world of the internet, and every kind of delicious food...

He had far too many reasons to go back.

Click. Click. Click...

Without realizing it, Zhou Mingrui repeatedly swung the revolver's cylinder open and snapped it shut.

Nothing about the past few days was particularly different from usual. I've had some bad luck, but why would that make me transmigrate out of nowhere?

Bad luck...

Wait. I performed that luck-enhancement ritual before dinner today!

The thought flashed through his mind, illuminating memories that had been hidden behind a fog of confusion.

As a respectable armchair politician, historian, economist, biologist, and folklorist, Zhou Mingrui had always considered himself someone who knew a little about everything. His best friend preferred to say that he knew almost nothing about anything.

One of his many casual interests had been Chinese divination.

During a visit to his hometown the previous year, he had found a thread-bound book in an old bookstore titled Quintessential Divination and Arcane Arts of the Qin and Han Dynasty. It had looked interesting—and useful for showing off online—so he bought it. Unfortunately, his enthusiasm had been brief. The vertical script made it miserable to read, and after skimming the opening pages, he tossed it into a corner.

Then came a month of relentless bad luck. He lost his phone, was cheated by clients who disappeared afterward, and made mistakes at work. That was when he remembered the luck-enhancement ritual described at the beginning of the old book. Better yet, it required neither training nor elaborate preparation.

He needed only four portions of whatever staple food was common in his region. These were to be placed in the four corners of his room, either on the floor or atop furniture such as tables and cabinets. Standing in the center, he then had to walk four steps counterclockwise to trace a square.

On the first step, he was to chant sincerely, “The Immortal Lord of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

On the second: “The Sky Lord of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

On the third: “The Exalted Thearch of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

And on the fourth: “The Celestial Worthy of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

After completing all four steps, he had to remain where he was, close his eyes, and wait five minutes. Only then would the ritual be complete.

Since it cost nothing, Zhou Mingrui had dug out the book and followed its instructions before dinner. At the time, however... nothing happened.

Who could have imagined that he would transmigrate in the middle of the night?

Transmigration!

“There's a good chance the ritual caused this,” Zhou Mingrui said. He stopped fidgeting with the revolver and sat bolt upright. “I should try it again here tomorrow. If the ritual brought me here, it might be able to send me back!”

Whatever happened, he had to try.

It was a desperate gamble—but it was the only chance he had.

Chapter 3: Melissa

Once he had settled on a plan, Zhou Mingrui felt as though he had found something solid to cling to. His fear and unease retreated to a distant corner of his mind.

Only then could he examine Klein's fragmented memories in earnest.

He rose out of habit and closed the valve on the gas pipe. The wall lamp gradually dimmed until its flame went out. Then he sat again, one hand pressed to the side of his head while the other absently toyed with the revolver's brass cylinder. In the darkness, stained crimson by the moon, he sifted through the memories like an attentive spectator watching a film.

Perhaps because a bullet had passed through Klein's head, the memories resembled shattered glass. They were discontinuous and riddled with obvious gaps. There was nothing to explain where the ornate revolver had come from, whether Klein had killed himself or been murdered, or what the sentence “Everyone will die, including me” was supposed to mean. Nor could Zhou Mingrui recall whether Klein had encountered anything unusual during the two days before his death.

The damage was not limited to those particular events. Even ordinary knowledge had pieces missing. In his present condition, Zhou Mingrui doubted Klein could graduate if sent back to university, despite having left campus only days earlier without ever slacking in his studies.

In two days, he was supposed to interview for a position in Tingen University's History Department...

Graduates in the Loen Kingdom did not traditionally remain at their own universities. Klein's mentor had therefore written him letters of recommendation for both Tingen University and Backlund University...

Through the window, Zhou Mingrui watched the red moon sink slowly toward the western horizon. As it disappeared, a faint golden light emerged in the east.

Sounds of movement came from elsewhere in the apartment. Before long, footsteps approached his door.

“Melissa's awake,” Zhou Mingrui said with a smile. “Punctual as ever.”

Klein's memories made Melissa feel like his own younger sister.

But I don't have a younger sister, he immediately reminded himself.

Unlike Benson and Klein, Melissa had not received her basic education through Sunday classes run by the Church of Evernight. By the time she reached school age, the Loen Kingdom had enacted the Basic Education Act and established a Primary and Secondary Education Committee with dedicated funding, greatly increasing the kingdom's investment in education.

In less than three years, numerous church schools had been incorporated into the new system, and many public primary schools had opened. They adhered strictly to religious neutrality, preventing disputes among the churches of the Lord of Storms, the Evernight Goddess, and the God of Steam and Machinery from entering the classroom.

Sunday school cost only one copper penny a week, while public primary school charged three pence. The latter seemed expensive by comparison, but Sunday school met only once a week, whereas public school provided six full days of lessons. In practice, the fee was almost nothing.

Melissa had always been different from most girls. Since childhood, she had been fascinated by gears, springs, bearings, and similar mechanisms. Her ambition was to become a steam mechanic.

Benson's own lack of education had taught him its value. He supported Melissa's dream just as firmly as he had supported Klein's university studies. Tingen Technical School counted only as secondary education, after all; she would not need the costly years of language school and university that Klein had required.

The previous July, fifteen-year-old Melissa had passed the entrance examination and earned a place in the Steam and Machinery Department at Tingen Technical School. Her tuition had risen to nine pence a week.

At the same time, conditions on the Southern Continent had hurt Benson's company. Profits and transactions fell sharply, and more than a third of the employees were dismissed. To keep his position and support the family, Benson took on increasingly difficult assignments. He worked longer hours and often traveled to places with harsh conditions. One such assignment had kept him away for the past several days.

Klein had considered helping to ease his brother's burden. But as a commoner educated at an undistinguished language school, he had entered university painfully aware of how far he lagged behind his wealthier classmates. Noble and affluent children, for example, learned ancient Feysac—the supposed root of every Northern Continent language—from an early age. Klein had not encountered it until university.

There had been many disadvantages of that kind. He studied late into the night and rose early each morning, giving nearly everything he had simply to catch up. In the end, he graduated with average marks.

Memories of his elder brother and younger sister continued to occupy Zhou Mingrui until the doorknob began to turn. He jolted back to the present—and remembered the revolver in his hand.

It was a restricted weapon!

It would frighten her!

And what about the wound on his head?

With Melissa about to enter, Zhou Mingrui pressed a hand to his temple. He yanked open a desk drawer, tossed the revolver inside, and slammed it shut.

“What happened?” Melissa asked, glancing over at the noise.

She was still in the first bloom of youth. Poor nutrition had left her face thin and slightly pale, but her skin retained the soft luster of a young girl.

When her brown eyes turned toward him, Zhou Mingrui forced himself to remain composed. He grabbed the nearest object and closed the drawer as casually as he could, concealing the revolver. His other hand explored his temple and found smooth skin.

The wound had already healed.

The object he had picked up was a silver pocket watch engraved with vine leaves. He pressed its crown, and the cover sprang open.

The watch had once belonged to the siblings' father and was the most valuable possession the Imperial Army sergeant had left behind. Unfortunately, it had already been secondhand when he acquired it and had begun malfunctioning in recent years, despite repeated visits to a watchmaker. Benson liked to carry it because it made him appear more respectable, but after it embarrassed him one time too many, he finally left it at home.

Melissa, however, seemed to possess a genuine gift for machinery. Once she understood the watch's basic principles, she borrowed tools from the technical school and began tinkering with it. Recently, she had even announced that she had repaired it.

Zhou Mingrui looked through the open cover. The second hand was motionless. Without thinking, he turned the crown several times to wind it.

He heard no spring tightening, and the second hand did not move.

“Looks like it's broken again,” he said, seizing on the first conversational topic that came to mind.

Melissa gave him a blank look, walked over, and took the watch from his hand.

Without moving from the spot, she pulled up the crown and turned it a few times. The second hand immediately began to tick.

Isn't pulling out the crown usually how you set the time?

Zhou Mingrui stared at her, speechless.

At that moment, bells rang from a distant cathedral—six ethereal chimes drifting across the waking city.

Melissa tilted her head to listen. She pulled out the crown again and adjusted the hands to match the hour.

“It's fine now,” she said flatly. She pushed the crown back into place and returned the watch.

Zhou Mingrui offered an embarrassed, polite smile.

Melissa stared at her elder brother for a moment, then turned toward the cabinet. She gathered her toiletries and towel and left for the communal bathroom.

Why did that look contain both disdain and resignation?

Was that the loving concern one showed a particularly stupid brother?

Zhou Mingrui lowered his head with a quiet laugh. He snapped the watch shut, then opened it again.

As he repeated the motion, his wandering thoughts settled on a question.

Klein had fired a gun without a suppressor—assuming for now that it really was suicide. The shot should have made a tremendous noise. Yet Melissa, sleeping on the other side of a single wall, had noticed nothing.

Had she simply been sleeping too deeply? Or was Klein's death mysterious from the very beginning?

Click. The watch opened. Clack. It closed.

When Melissa returned, she found her brother still opening and shutting it without conscious thought.

That exasperated look returned to her eyes. In a clear, pleasant voice, she said, “Klein, take out the rest of the bread. Remember to buy fresh bread today, along with some meat and peas. Your interview is coming up. I'll make mutton stew with peas.”

As she spoke, she pulled the stove from the corner, added charcoal, and set a pot of water to boil.

Before it was ready, she opened the cabinet's bottom drawer and removed a treasured tin of low-grade tea. She dropped perhaps ten leaves into the pot and pretended it was the real thing.

Melissa poured two large cups. She and Zhou Mingrui shared two pieces of rye bread with their tea.

At least it isn't mixed with sawdust or too much bran, but it still tastes awful...

Weak and ravenous, Zhou Mingrui swallowed the bread with the help of the tea while complaining inwardly.

Melissa finished within a few minutes. She adjusted the black hair that fell to her waistcoat and said, “Remember the fresh bread. We only need eight pounds. It's hot, so it spoils quickly. And buy the mutton and peas. Don't forget.”

So she really was worried about her slow-witted brother. She had even repeated the instructions for emphasis.

Zhou Mingrui smiled and nodded. “All right.”

Comparing Klein's instincts with his own memories, he estimated that a Loen pound was close to half a kilogram.

Melissa said nothing more. She rose and tidied the room. After packing the last of the bread for lunch, she put on the worn veil bonnet their mother had left behind and picked up the homemade bag that held her books and stationery.

It was not Sunday, so she had a full day of classes.

The walk from their apartment to Tingen Technical School took nearly fifty minutes. Public horse carriages charged one penny per kilometer, up to four pence within the city and six in the outskirts. To save money, Melissa always left early and walked.

She had just opened the main door when she stopped and half-turned.

“Klein, don't buy too much mutton or too many peas. Benson might come back on Sunday. Oh, and remember—we only need eight pounds of bread.”

“All right. I remember,” Zhou Mingrui replied helplessly.

At the same time, he repeated the word Sunday several times in his mind.

Here on the Northern Continent, a year was also divided into twelve months and contained either 365 or 366 days. A week likewise consisted of seven days.

The arrangement of the months came from astronomical observation, making Zhou Mingrui wonder whether this might be a parallel world. The seven-day week, however, had religious origins. The Northern Continent recognized seven orthodox deities: the Eternal Blazing Sun, the Lord of Storms, the God of Knowledge and Wisdom, the Evernight Goddess, the Earth Mother, the God of Combat, and the God of Steam and Machinery.

Zhou Mingrui watched his sister close the door behind her and sighed.

Soon his thoughts returned to the luck-enhancement ritual.

I'm sorry. I truly want to go home...

Chapter 4: Divination

As Zhou Mingrui returned to his chair, the distant cathedral bells rang again. They chimed seven times before he finally rose and went to the cabinet for his clothes.

A black waistcoat and matching suit, close-fitting trousers, a half top hat, and his vaguely scholarly air made him look like an actor in a Victorian drama.

He stared at himself, then shook his head with a rueful smile.

“I'm not going to an interview. I'm only buying ingredients for the luck-enhancement ritual...”

Klein had worried so much about his approaching interviews that dressing formally had become instinctive. Whenever his attention wandered, he automatically reached for the only respectable outfit he owned.

With a sigh, Zhou Mingrui removed the suit and waistcoat and put on a yellow-brown coat instead. He exchanged the top hat for a rounded felt hat of the same color.

Once dressed, he walked to the bed and lifted a square cushion. His hand found an inconspicuous opening underneath and searched until his fingers slipped into a hidden layer.

He withdrew a small roll of faded, dark-green banknotes. There were eight in all.

This was everything Benson had managed to save, including the family's living expenses for the next three days. Two were five-soli notes, while the rest were worth one soli each.

The soli occupied the middle tier of the Loen Kingdom's currency system and took its name from an ancient silver coin. One soli equaled twelve copper pence, and notes came in denominations of one and five soli.

Above it stood the gold pound. These, too, were paper notes, but their value was guaranteed by and directly tied to gold. One pound equaled twenty soli, with notes worth one, five, or ten pounds.

Zhou Mingrui unfolded one of the bills and caught its faint, distinctive scent of ink.

The smell of money.

Whether because of Klein's memories or his own persistent desire for wealth, he fell in love with the notes at once.

Look at that beautiful design. Even stern, old-fashioned George III and his ridiculous mustache look charming...

And that watermark when you hold it up to the sun. That intricate anti-counterfeiting pattern—nothing like the gaudy nonsense on cheap imitations!

He admired the money for nearly a minute before selecting two one-soli notes. The rest he rolled up and returned to the cushion's hidden compartment.

After smoothing the fabric over the opening, he folded the two notes neatly and placed them in the left pocket of his yellow-brown coat, separate from the few copper coins in his trousers.

He slipped a key into his right pocket, picked up a dark-brown paper bag, and strode toward the door.

His brisk steps gradually slowed, then stopped altogether.

Standing at the door, Zhou Mingrui realized he was frowning.

Klein's death was riddled with unanswered questions. If he simply walked outside, might some further “accident” be waiting for him?

After a moment's consideration, he returned to the desk, opened the drawer, and removed the gleaming brass revolver.

It was the only means of defending himself he could think of—and the only one with enough stopping power to matter.

He had never fired a gun, but merely producing a revolver would be enough to intimidate most people.

Zhou Mingrui ran his fingers over the cold metal, then tucked the weapon into the same pocket as the banknotes. His hand closed around the money while his fingers rested against the grip, hiding the gun completely.

The extra protection reassured him. Then, as someone who knew a little about everything, he thought of a new problem.

What if it goes off by accident?

The concern quickly became impossible to ignore. He drew the weapon, swung out the cylinder, and positioned the empty chamber left by Klein's “suicide” beneath the hammer. Then he snapped the cylinder shut.

Now, even if the trigger was accidentally pulled, the hammer would fall on an empty chamber.

He returned the revolver to his pocket and kept his left hand there. With his right, he pressed down his hat, opened the door, and stepped outside.

The corridor remained dim even in daylight, receiving only a sliver of sun through the window at its far end. Zhou Mingrui descended the stairs, left the apartment building, and emerged into warmth and dazzling light.

It was nearly July, the height of summer. But Tingen lay in the northern reaches of the Loen Kingdom and had a distinctive climate. Even the hottest days of the year would have remained below thirty degrees Celsius on Earth, and the mornings were cooler still.

The streets, however, were slick with filthy water and scattered with refuse. According to Klein's memories, such conditions were common in poorer districts despite the presence of sewers. There were simply too many people crowded together, all struggling to survive.

“Come try our delicious roasted fish!”

“Hot oyster soup! One bowl in the morning will keep you strong all day!”

“Fresh fish from the harbor! Only five pence each!”

“Muffins and eel soup—the perfect breakfast!”

“Conch! Fresh conch!”

“Vegetables straight from the farms outside the city! Fresh and cheap!”

Vendors selling produce, fruit, and hot food called from both sides of the street, competing for the attention of hurrying pedestrians. Some people stopped to compare goods and haggle over prices. Others waved the hawkers away impatiently; they had yet to find work for the day and could afford nothing.

Zhou Mingrui breathed air laden with appetizing spices and foul street odors. Hunching slightly, he kept a tight grip on the revolver and notes with his left hand while his right held his hat in place. Then he plunged into the crowd.

Thieves were inevitable wherever so many people gathered. This street was filled with laborers taking temporary work after losing steady employment, as well as hungry children forced by adults to steal on their behalf.

He continued until the crowd thinned to a more manageable flow. Straightening, he looked farther down the street.

A wandering accordionist was playing for coins, the melody tender one moment and exuberant the next.

Several ragged, sallow children stood nearby. Malnutrition had hollowed their faces, but they listened to the music and danced in steps of their own invention. Their faces shone with delight, as though each of them were a prince or an angel.

A woman passed with a lifeless expression, her skirt dirty and her complexion dull. Her gaze remained sluggish until it fell upon the children. For an instant, a faint light entered her eyes, as if she had glimpsed the girl she had been thirty years before.

Zhou Mingrui passed her, turned onto another street, and stopped outside Smyrin Bakery.

The owner, Wendy Smyrin, was a woman of more than seventy with completely gray-white hair and an unfailingly kind smile. As far back as Klein could remember, she had stood behind this same counter selling bread and pastries.

Her Tingen biscuits and lemon cakes are wonderful...

Zhou Mingrui swallowed and smiled. “Mrs. Smyrin, eight pounds of rye bread, please.”

“Oh, dear Klein. Has Benson still not returned?” Wendy asked warmly.

“He'll be back in a few days,” Zhou Mingrui replied vaguely.

Wendy began gathering the loaves. “What a hardworking young man. He'll make some woman a fine husband.”

Her lips curved into a playful smile.

“Things will be better now. You've graduated—a history graduate from our own Khoy University! Soon you'll be earning money. You shouldn't have to remain in that apartment much longer. At the very least, you deserve a home with a private bathroom.”

“Mrs. Smyrin, you seem especially young and energetic today,” Zhou Mingrui replied with an awkward smile.

If Klein passed his interview and became a lecturer at Tingen University, the family would indeed rise immediately into a different social class.

In his memories, Klein had imagined renting a small house in the suburbs. It would have five or six rooms and two bathrooms, with a broad balcony upstairs. The ground floor would contain two bedrooms, a dining room, a sitting room, a kitchen, and another bathroom, with storage beneath the house.

It was not an impossible dream. Even a probationary lecturer at Tingen University earned two gold pounds a week. After probation, the salary rose to three pounds and ten soli. Benson, despite his years of experience, earned only one pound and ten soli a week. An ordinary factory worker made less than a pound, or only slightly more. Meanwhile, a house in the suburbs could be rented for between nineteen soli and one pound eighteen.

“The difference between earning three or four thousand yuan a month and earning fourteen or fifteen thousand...” Zhou Mingrui murmured.

But that future depended on passing an interview at either Tingen University or Backlund University.

There were few alternatives. Without connections, he had no hope of receiving the recommendation needed for a civil-service position. History graduates had especially limited prospects, since aristocrats, banks, and industrial magnates had little need for private historical advisers.

Considering how incomplete Klein's knowledge had become, Zhou Mingrui felt both uncomfortable and guilty beneath Mrs. Smyrin's expectant gaze.

“Nonsense. I have always been this young,” Wendy replied cheerfully.

She packed the sixteen half-pound loaves into the brown paper bag he had brought, then held out her hand. “Nine pence.”

“Nine?” Zhou Mingrui asked automatically. “Wasn't it eleven pence two days ago?”

Two months earlier, the same amount had cost fifteen.

“You can thank those people who protested in the streets until the Grain Act was repealed,” Wendy said with a shrug.

Zhou Mingrui nodded as though he understood. Klein's memories of the matter were incomplete. He recalled only that the Grain Act protected domestic agricultural prices by halting grain imports from southern countries such as Feynapotter, Masin, and Lenburg whenever prices fell below a certain level.

Why would ordinary people protest against that?

Rather than expose his ignorance, Zhou Mingrui carefully removed one of the notes, taking care not to pull out the revolver with it, and handed the money to Mrs. Smyrin.

She returned three copper pence in change. He dropped them into his trouser pocket, collected the bag of bread, and headed for the Lettuce and Meat Market across the street. Melissa had demanded mutton stew with peas, and he intended to deliver.

At the intersection of Iron Cross Street and Daffodil Street lay a municipal square. Several tents had been erected there, and clowns in brightly colored, absurd costumes were handing out leaflets.

“A circus performance tomorrow night?” Zhou Mingrui read from one of the flyers held by a passerby.

Melissa would certainly enjoy that. But how much were the tickets?

He approached the tents.

Just as he was about to question a clown whose face was painted red and yellow, a hoarse woman's voice came from beside him.

“Would you care to have your fortune told?”

Zhou Mingrui turned. A woman in a pointed hat and long black dress stood before a small tent. Red and yellow paint covered her face, while her eyes were a deep gray-blue.

“No, thank you.” He shook his head. Divination was not in his budget.

The woman laughed. “My tarot readings are very accurate.”

“Tarot...”

Zhou Mingrui froze.

The word sounded almost identical to the name of the tarot cards on Earth—the illustrated deck used for divination, with each image representing a different omen.

Wait...

He suddenly remembered the origins of tarot in this world.

It was neither a legacy of ancient times nor a revelation from any of the seven orthodox deities. Tarot had been invented more than 170 years ago by Roselle Gustav, then Consul of the Intis Republic.

Roselle had invented the steam engine, improved sailing ships, overthrown the Intis Kingdom, and won recognition from the God of Craftsmanship. He then became the first Consul of the new republic.

Later, he invaded neighboring states and forced Lenburg and several other countries under Intis protection. Even the Loen Kingdom, Feynapotter, the Feysac Empire, and the other great powers of the Northern Continent had been made to yield before the republic. Roselle eventually transformed the republic into an empire and proclaimed himself “Emperor Caesar.”

During his reign, the Church of Craftsmanship received its first public divine revelation of the Fifth Epoch. From that point onward, the God of Craftsmanship became known as the God of Steam and Machinery.

Roselle had also invented tarot divination and established the modern system of playing cards, including their standard designs and rules. Many of the games were disturbingly familiar to Zhou Mingrui: Upgrade, Fight the Landlord, Texas Hold'em, Quint...

The fleets Roselle commissioned had discovered a route through the violent, storm-tossed seas to the Southern Continent, ushering in the colonial age.

Yet in his later years, Roselle was betrayed. In 1198 of the Fifth Epoch, an alliance comprising the Church of the Eternal Blazing Sun, the former Intis royal family—the Sauron dynasty—and several aristocratic houses assassinated him in White Maple Palace.

Recalling all this common knowledge, Zhou Mingrui nearly slapped a hand to his forehead.

Could Roselle have been another transmigrator?

The thought made him curious to see what this world's tarot cards looked like. He nodded to the woman in the pointed hat.

“If the price is reasonable, then... all right. I'll try it.”

The woman immediately laughed. “You're my first customer today, sir. This reading is free.”

Chapter 5: Ritual

Free? Nothing costs more than something free.

Zhou Mingrui repeated the warning silently and resolved not to purchase any additional services. Whatever the woman offered, he would refuse.

If you're really that talented, try divining that I transmigrated here.

With that thought, he followed the woman with the red-and-yellow-painted face, ducking to enter the low tent.

The interior was almost completely dark, lit only by a few narrow shafts of sunlight. In their faint glow, he could just make out a table covered in cards.

The darkness did not inconvenience the woman in the pointed hat. Her long black dress seemed to glide across water as she circled the table and sat opposite him. Then she lit a candle.

Its dim yellow flame flickered, casting the tent alternately into light and shadow and lending the cramped space an immediate air of mystery.

Zhou Mingrui sat quietly and studied the cards scattered across the table. He recognized The Magician, The Emperor, The Hanged Man, Temperance, and several others.

Could Roselle really have been a predecessor? I wonder if he came from the same country as me...

Before he could finish examining the exposed cards, the woman gathered them, squared them into a deck, and pushed it toward him.

“Shuffle and cut the cards,” the circus fortune-teller said in a subdued voice.

“Me?” Zhou Mingrui asked automatically. “I shuffle them?”

The red and yellow paint creased as she smiled. “Of course. Only you can reveal your own destiny. I merely interpret it.”

Zhou Mingrui regarded her warily. “This doesn't cost extra, does it?”

As an experienced armchair folklorist, he had seen far too many tricks of this kind.

The fortune-teller stared at him for a moment before answering in a muffled voice, “It's free.”

Relieved, Zhou Mingrui pushed the revolver deeper into his pocket. He calmly picked up the deck, shuffled it with practiced hands, and cut it.

“Done.” He placed the cards in the center of the table.

The fortune-teller took the deck in both hands and studied it solemnly. After a moment, she said, “I'm sorry. I forgot to ask—what do you wish to know?”

Zhou Mingrui had studied tarot while pursuing his first love. Without hesitation, he replied, “Past, present, and future.”

It was a standard three-card spread, with the cards representing those three stages in sequence.

The woman nodded, then smiled. “In that case, please shuffle them again. You can only draw the cards you truly seek after fixing your question in your mind.”

Were you playing with me just now? Do you have to be this petty just because I asked whether it was free?

Zhou Mingrui's cheek twitched. He drew a breath, accepted the deck, and shuffled and cut it once more.

“There won't be another problem, will there?” He returned it to the table.

“No.”

The fortune-teller drew the top card and placed it to his left.

“This represents your past,” she said, her voice dropping lower.

She set a second card directly in front of him. “This represents your present.”

Then she drew a third and placed it to his right.

“And this represents your future.”

When the arrangement was complete, she raised her head and fixed her deep gray-blue eyes on him. “Which would you like to see first?”

Zhou Mingrui considered the question. “The present.”

The fortune-teller nodded slowly and turned over the card before him.

It depicted a brightly dressed traveler in elaborate headgear, carrying a stick over one shoulder. A bundle hung from its end, and a small dog followed at his heels. The card bore the number zero.

“The Fool,” the woman read softly, her gray-blue eyes never leaving Zhou Mingrui.

The Fool? Card zero of the Major Arcana. A beginning? A new journey filled with limitless possibilities?

Zhou Mingrui was not even an amateur tarot enthusiast. He could only construct a rough interpretation from what little he remembered.

Before the fortune-teller could speak again, the tent flap was abruptly pulled aside. Sunlight poured in, so bright that Zhou Mingrui, whose back faced the entrance, narrowed his eyes instinctively.

“Why are you pretending to be me again?” an angry woman demanded. “I'm the one responsible for the readings! Get back to your post. You're an animal trainer, remember?”

An animal trainer?

Zhou Mingrui's eyes adjusted to the glare. At the entrance stood another woman in a pointed hat and black dress, her face likewise painted red and yellow. She looked much like the first, except that she was taller and more slender.

The woman seated across from him rose at once.

“Don't make such a fuss. I simply enjoy it,” she complained. “And I have to say, sometimes my readings are extremely accurate. I mean it...”

Gathering her skirt, she slipped around the table and hurried out of the tent.

The genuine fortune-teller smiled at Zhou Mingrui. “Would you like me to interpret the cards for you, sir?”

His lips twitched. “Would that be free?”

“No.”

“Then never mind.”

Zhou Mingrui withdrew his hands into his pockets, gripping both his money and the revolver, then ducked out of the tent.

Damn it. I had an animal trainer telling my fortune?

Then again, is an animal trainer who doesn't dream of becoming a fortune-teller even qualified to be a clown?

He soon put the absurd encounter behind him. At the Lettuce and Meat Market, he spent seven pence on a pound of mediocre mutton, then bought tender broad beans, cabbage, onions, potatoes, and several other ingredients. Including the bread, the morning's purchases totaled twenty-five pence—two soli and one penny.

“Money disappears far too quickly. Poor Benson...”

Not only had Zhou Mingrui spent both notes he brought, he had needed to add a penny from his trouser pocket.

He sighed, dismissed the thought, and hurried home.

Now that he had the staple food, he could perform the luck-enhancement ritual.

Even after the other second-floor tenants had gradually left, however, Zhou Mingrui did not begin immediately. First, he translated “The Immortal Lord of Heaven and Earth for Blessings” and the other incantations into ancient Feysac and Loen. If the original Chinese failed, he intended to try again the next day in the languages of this world.

Different worlds might follow different rules. When in Rome, do as the Romans do.

He considered translating the incantation into Hermes, the language traditionally used for ritual prayers, but Klein's limited vocabulary made the task impossible.

Once everything was ready, Zhou Mingrui took out four loaves of rye bread. He placed the first in the corner where the coal stove usually stood, the second beneath the dressing mirror, the third atop the cabinet where two walls met, and the fourth among the miscellaneous objects to the right of the desk.

He drew a deep breath and moved to the center of the room. After spending several minutes calming himself, he solemnly stepped forward and began tracing a square counterclockwise.

With the first step, he whispered, “The Immortal Lord of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

On the second, he chanted sincerely, “The Sky Lord of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

With the third, he breathed, “The Exalted Thearch of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

On the fourth, he exhaled and concentrated. “The Celestial Worthy of Heaven and Earth for Blessings.”

Returning to his starting point, Zhou Mingrui closed his eyes and waited. Anticipation and unease mingled within him, along with hope—and fear.

Could he return home?

Would the ritual have any effect?

What if something unexpected happened?

The darkness behind his eyelids was tinged with hopeful crimson. His thoughts whirled beyond his control.

Then the air around him seemed to stop. It grew dense, strange, and secretive.

Whispers sounded beside his ears—low and indistinct, now real, now illusory; sometimes piercing, sometimes seductive, sometimes feverish with madness.

Zhou Mingrui understood none of the murmured words, yet he felt compelled to listen, desperate to distinguish their meaning.

Pain erupted in his head as though someone had driven a steel spike into his skull.

His thoughts dissolved into a riot of psychedelic color. It felt as if his head might burst.

Knowing something had gone terribly wrong, he tried to open his eyes. He could not perform even that simple act.

His entire body drew tighter and tighter, as though it might shatter at any moment. Amid the agony, a single mocking thought floated through his mind:

If you don't court death, you won't die...

He could endure no more. Just as his consciousness approached its breaking point, the whispers receded and the world fell utterly silent.

His emotions lurched wildly—and his body seemed to follow.

He tried once more to open his eyes. This time, it was effortless.

An endless gray fog filled his vision, hazy and indistinct.

“What is this?”

Zhou Mingrui looked around in shock, then lowered his gaze and discovered that he was floating at the edge of an immeasurable sea of fog.

The mist flowed like water. Countless crimson “stars” dotted its expanse, some enormous and others tiny. A few seemed buried in its unfathomable depths, while others drifted near the surface.

The scene looked almost like a hologram. Half bewildered and half curious, Zhou Mingrui extended his right hand toward a crimson star floating close by, hoping it might offer a way out.

The instant his fingers touched it, ripples spread outward from his body. The star erupted in crimson radiance, blazing like a flame within a dream.

Startled, Zhou Mingrui jerked back his hand and accidentally brushed another star.

It, too, burst into brilliant light.

His mind went blank, and his spirit seemed to scatter.

——

In Backlund, capital of the Loen Kingdom, Audrey Hall sat before the dressing table in a luxurious villa in the royal district.

An antique bronze mirror rested before her, its surface cracked.

“Mirror, mirror, awaken...

“In the name of the Hall family, I command you to awaken!”

She tried one incantation after another, but the mirror showed no response.

After more than ten minutes, she finally gave up and pouted.

“Father really was lying to me,” she muttered softly. “He always says this mirror was a treasure belonging to the Black Emperor of the Solomon Empire, that it's an extraordinary item...”

Her voice trailed off.

The bronze mirror suddenly shone with crimson light, which surged outward and swallowed her completely.

——

On the Sonia Sea, an ancient-looking three-masted sailing ship battled through a storm.

Alger Wilson stood on deck. His body rose and fell with the vessel, yet he maintained his balance with ease.

He wore a robe embroidered with lightning patterns and held an oddly shaped glass bottle. Bubbles surged inside it one moment; the next, frost condensed into snow or miniature gusts of wind swept through its interior.

“I still need the Ghost Shark's blood...” Alger murmured.

At that instant, crimson light flared between the bottle and his palm. In the blink of an eye, it engulfed everything around him.

——

Within the gray fog, Audrey Hall regained her sight. Horrified and bewildered, she tried to understand her surroundings. Then she noticed the hazy figure of a man opposite her doing the same.

A moment later, both of them discovered a third, mysterious figure standing nearby, shrouded in gray mist.

That mysterious figure was Zhou Mingrui, and he was every bit as bewildered as they were.

“Sir, where are we?”

Audrey and Alger hesitated, falling briefly silent. Then both spoke at once.

“What are you planning to do?”

Chapter 6: Beyonder

Not only did the two strangers speak Loen, they shared the same tense, somber air.

Where am I? What am I planning to do? I'd like to know that too...

Zhou Mingrui calmed himself while silently repeating their questions.

What struck him most was not what they had said, but the bewilderment, vigilance, panic, and awe behind their words.

These two people had been inexplicably dragged into a world of gray fog. Zhou Mingrui, who had apparently caused it, was already shocked and utterly confused. How much more terrifying must it be for those who had been pulled here against their will?

To them, this experience had likely surpassed the limits of imagination.

Two possible approaches occurred to him. He could pretend to be another victim, concealing his role and earning their trust while he watched events unfold. Or he could preserve the mysterious identity they had already assigned him, influence what happened next, and extract valuable information from them.

He had no time for careful deliberation. Seizing the thought as it came, he chose the second option.

He would use their assumptions to secure the greatest possible advantage.

After several seconds of silence, Zhou Mingrui gave a soft chuckle. In a calm, understated voice, as though responding to guests who had politely called upon him, he said, “An experiment.”

An experiment? Audrey Hall stared at the mysterious figure veiled in gray-white fog. The situation seemed absurd, fascinating, terrifying, and incomprehensible all at once.

Only moments ago, she had been sitting at the dressing table in her bedroom. She had merely turned around—and somehow arrived in this endless fog.

It was inconceivable.

Audrey drew a breath and arranged her features into a flawlessly courteous smile.

“Sir, has the experiment concluded? Would you permit us to return?” she asked, unable to keep all the unease from her voice.

Alger Wilson had considered probing the mysterious figure with a similar question, but experience made him more cautious. He suppressed the impulse and remained a silent observer.

Zhou Mingrui studied the young woman. Through the hazy mist, he could discern only the outline of a tall girl with smooth blond hair; her features remained indistinct.

Rather than answer immediately, he turned toward the man. The second stranger was of average height and build, with disheveled dark-blue hair.

A realization came to him. If he became stronger—or gained a deeper understanding of this fog-shrouded realm—he might eventually be able to see through the mist and distinguish their faces.

Here, they are the visitors. I am the host.

That change in perspective made him notice details he had previously overlooked.

Both the melodious-voiced young woman and the reserved, mature man appeared partly incorporeal. Faint crimson light stained their forms, making them resemble projections cast by two of the red stars beyond the fog.

The projections depended on a connection between those stars and Zhou Mingrui—an intangible link that only he could sense, yet one he felt certain he could grasp.

If I sever the connection, their projections should vanish and send them home...

Zhou Mingrui gave a slight nod and looked back at the blond woman with a chuckle.

“Of course. If you formally request it, you may return this very moment.”

Hearing no malice in his voice, Audrey breathed a sigh of relief. A gentleman capable of such miracles would surely honor his word.

Once reassured, however, she found herself in no hurry to leave. Her bright green eyes darted from side to side, sparkling with extraordinary interest.

Anxiety, anticipation, and temptation mingled in her voice. “This is such a marvelous experience... I've always hoped something like this might happen. I mean, I enjoy mysteries and supernatural miracles. No, what I mean is—Sir, how can I become a Beyonder?”

The more she spoke, the more excited she became, until she began stumbling over her words. The dream planted by the thrilling tales of her elders suddenly seemed capable of becoming reality.

In only a few sentences, she had forgotten nearly all her earlier fear.

Excellent question. I'd like to know the answer myself...

Zhou Mingrui considered how to respond without damaging his unfathomable image.

At the same time, standing here felt distinctly inappropriate. Should he not be seated at the head of a long table in some vast palace, occupying a mysterious high-backed chair carved with ancient designs as he silently observed his visitors?

The instant the thought arose, the gray fog churned, startling both Audrey and Alger.

Towering stone pillars materialized around them. Far overhead appeared an immense dome that enclosed the space.

The structure was magnificent, lofty, and grand—a legendary palace built for giants.

Beneath the dome, where gray mist gathered, a long bronze table emerged. Ten high-backed chairs stood along each side in an asymmetrical arrangement, with one more at either end. Faint crimson light shimmered across every chair back, tracing strange constellations unlike any in the real world.

Audrey and Alger found themselves sitting opposite each other, each beside the seat of honor.

The young woman looked around and whispered, “How fascinating...”

It certainly is...

Maintaining an unruffled expression, Zhou Mingrui extended his right hand and lightly brushed the edge of the bronze table.

Alger surveyed their surroundings. After several seconds of silence, he answered Audrey's question in Zhou Mingrui's place.

“Are you from Loen?

“If you wish to become a Beyonder, join the Church of the Evernight Goddess, the Lord of Storms, or the God of Steam and Machinery.

“Most people live their entire lives without ever meeting a Beyonder. Because of this, many ordinary people—and even some clergymen in the great Churches—doubt that Beyonders exist at all. But I can assure you that they still serve in courts, tribunals, and enforcement agencies. They continue to fight the dangers growing in the darkness. Their numbers are simply far smaller than they were before and during the early Iron Age.”

Zhou Mingrui listened closely while doing his best to appear indifferent, as though Alger were a child telling him a familiar story.

From Klein's fragmented historical knowledge, he knew that the Iron Age referred to the present era: the Fifth Epoch, which had begun 1,349 years earlier.

Audrey waited until Alger finished, then sighed.

“Sir, I already know all of that. I know even more—the Nighthawks, the Mandated Punishers, and the Machinery Hivemind. But I don't wish to lose my freedom.”

Alger gave a quiet, ambiguous laugh. “No one becomes a Beyonder without sacrifice. If you refuse to join a Church and accept the duties it imposes, your alternatives are the royal families or one of the few noble houses with more than a thousand years of history. Otherwise, you can trust to luck and seek out a secret organization—most likely an evil one.”

Audrey instinctively puffed out her cheeks, then glanced around in alarm. Once she was sure neither the mysterious host nor Alger had noticed, she asked, “Is there no other way?”

Alger fell silent. Nearly half a minute later, he turned toward the mysterious figure who had been watching them without comment.

When Zhou Mingrui still showed no intention of speaking, Alger looked back at Audrey.

“I possess two Sequence 9 potion formulas,” he said deliberately.

Sequence 9? Zhou Mingrui repeated inwardly.

“Truly? Which two?” Audrey clearly understood what the term meant.

Alger leaned back slightly and replied without haste.

“As you know, humans can become true Beyonders only through potions. The potion names originate from the Blasphemy Slate. After repeated translation through Jotun, Elvish, ancient and modern Hermes, and ancient Feysac, those names changed to suit the language of each period. The name itself is not what matters. What matters is whether it expresses the potion's core characteristics.

“One formula I possess is for the Sequence 9 potion called Sailor. It grants extraordinary balance. Even aboard a ship in a violent storm, you could move as freely as if you were on land. You would also gain tremendous strength and illusory scales beneath your skin. You could swim like a fish, become extremely difficult to catch, and move underwater with the agility of a marine creature. Without any equipment, you could remain submerged for at least ten minutes.”

“That sounds wonderful... Is it the Lord of Storms' Keeper of the Seas?”

“That was its former name,” Alger replied. He continued without pausing. “The second Sequence 9 potion is called Spectator, though I do not know its ancient name. It gives you an exceptionally keen mind and acute powers of observation.

“You understand what a spectator is from attending plays and operas. Like a member of the audience, a Spectator watches the actors upon the stage of society. Through their emotions, behavior, and habitual expressions, a Spectator glimpses the thoughts they try to conceal.”

Alger's voice grew emphatic.

“Remember this: whether you stand in a lavish ballroom or on a crowded street, a Spectator must always remain a spectator.”

Audrey's eyes shone. After a long pause, she asked, “Why? No, never mind—that's another question. I... I think I've fallen in love with the idea of being a Spectator. How can I obtain the formula? What would you accept in exchange?”

Alger seemed to have anticipated the question.

“At least one hundred milliliters of Ghost Shark blood.”

Audrey nodded eagerly, then frowned with concern. “If I can obtain it—and I only mean if—how would I deliver it to you? And how can you guarantee that I'll receive an authentic formula in exchange?”

“I'll give you an address,” Alger replied calmly. “Once I receive the Ghost Shark blood, I will mail you the formula—or tell it to you directly here.

“As for guarantees, I believe both of us can feel secure with this mysterious gentleman as our witness.”

He turned his gaze toward Zhou Mingrui, who sat upright in the seat of honor.

“Sir, your ability to bring us here demonstrates power beyond anything we can imagine. Neither of us would dare violate an agreement made beneath your gaze.”

“Exactly!” Audrey agreed, her eyes sparkling.

To her, a mysterious gentleman capable of such miracles was the ultimate authority. Neither she nor the man opposite her would dare deceive him.

Audrey turned partly in her chair and looked earnestly at Zhou Mingrui.

“Sir, please witness our exchange.”

Only then did she realize how impolite they had been. In their excitement, neither had asked the most basic question.

“Sir, how should we address you?” she added hurriedly.

Alger inclined his head and repeated in a solemn voice, “Sir, how should we address you?”

Zhou Mingrui paused. His fingers tapped lightly against the bronze tabletop as the memory of his tarot reading flashed through his mind.

He leaned back, withdrew his right hand, and interlaced his fingers beneath his chin. Then he smiled faintly at the two figures before him.

“You may call me...”

He let the silence linger for a moment before finishing in a calm, amiable voice.

“The Fool.”

Translator's note: Jotun is the language of giants.

Chapter 7: Code Names

“You may call me The Fool.”

The simple declaration echoed through the grand hall and faded into the fog. Yet it continued to reverberate in Audrey's and Alger's hearts, sending ripple after ripple through their thoughts.

Neither had expected such a title, but both felt it suited him perfectly. It captured everything mysterious, powerful, and uncanny about the figure before them.

After several seconds of silence, Audrey rose, lifted her skirt slightly, and curtsied to Zhou Mingrui.

“Honorable Mr. Fool, may I take the liberty of asking you to witness our exchange?”

“It is no trouble.” Zhou Mingrui's thoughts raced, but his reply was measured and appropriate to his new status.

“The honor is ours, Mr. Fool.” Alger also stood. He placed his right hand over his chest and bowed slightly.

Zhou Mingrui lowered his hand and smiled. “Please, continue.”

Alger nodded, sat down, and turned to Audrey.

“If you obtain the Ghost Shark blood, have someone deliver it to the Warrior & Sea Bar on Pelican Street in Pritz Harbor's White Rose Borough. Tell the owner, Williams, that it is for the Captain.

“Once I confirm its arrival, would you prefer to give me an address where I can mail the potion formula, or should I recite it to you here?”

Audrey considered the options, then smiled. “I'll choose the safer method. Tell me here, although it will put my memory to the test.”

Since Mr. Fool had agreed to witness the exchange, there would evidently be another gathering.

Her eyes brightened at the realization. She turned eagerly toward Zhou Mingrui.

“Mr. Fool, would you consider conducting a few more experiments like this?”

Alger listened calmly, but the suggestion tempted him as well.

“Mr. Fool,” he added, “do you not find gatherings such as this interesting? Your power may exceed our imagination, but there must be fields even you have not studied or do not specialize in. The young lady opposite me is clearly of elevated status. I have my own experience, knowledge, contacts, and resources. Perhaps one day we could handle some minor matter that would be inconvenient for you to address personally.”

From Alger's perspective, the mysterious Mr. Fool had drawn him into this place without warning and without giving him any means to resist. Participation in future gatherings might not truly be optional.

In that case, it was better to gain as much as possible from the experience and offset his passive, disadvantaged position.

The three people at the table came from different backgrounds and possessed different resources, channels of information, and knowledge of the mystical world. Even limited cooperation among them might produce extraordinary and unpredictable results.

The exchange they had just negotiated was one example. If Alger wished to have someone killed, for instance, he could ask a member of the gathering who appeared to have no connection with him. Any investigation would be led hopelessly astray.

A young lady of elevated status... Were my manners and accent truly that obvious?

Audrey stared at him, momentarily speechless. Then she recovered and nodded without hesitation.

“Mr. Fool, I think it's an excellent proposal. If these gatherings become regular, you may entrust us with matters that would be inconvenient for you to handle—provided, of course, that they are within our abilities.”

Zhou Mingrui had been weighing the advantages and risks from the moment she made the suggestion.

Regular gatherings would allow him to learn more about Beyonders and the secrets of this world, perhaps helping him find a way home. The Spectator formula, for example, was likely to appear at the next meeting. Other information he acquired could also prove invaluable in his present life.

But every additional gathering increased the chance that he would expose himself.

There really is no such thing as a free lunch, no matter the world...

Zhou Mingrui extended his right hand and lightly tapped a finger against the bronze table.

He controlled when the participants were summoned and dismissed. That meant the risk of exposure remained, to some extent, under his control. The benefits clearly outweighed the dangers.

He stopped tapping and smiled beneath the expectant, anxious gazes of his guests.

“I value fair and equal exchange.

“Any assistance you provide will be rewarded.

“Every Monday at three in the afternoon, do your best to be alone. Once I have conducted a few more experiments and clarified certain matters, you may be able to request leave in advance, so that you need not fear being summoned at an inconvenient moment.”

It was an acceptance of their proposal.

Audrey was only seventeen and had been sheltered throughout her life. Unable to restrain her delight, she clenched a fist and gave a small, excited pump before her chest.

Before Alger could speak, she said brightly, “Then should we give ourselves code names? We can't use our true names when we speak here.”

Mr. Fool may already know who I am, but the man across from me could be dangerous. I mustn't let him discover my identity.

“A sensible idea,” Zhou Mingrui replied with casual ease.

Audrey's thoughts immediately began to race, and she voiced them as they came.

“Your title, Mr. Fool, comes from the tarot. Since this is to be a permanent, secret gathering, our names should follow the same pattern. Yes, I'll choose a tarot card too.”

Her voice grew increasingly cheerful.

“I've decided. I shall be Justice!”

Justice was one of the twenty-two Major Arcana.

“And what about you, sir?” Audrey asked the man opposite her with a playful smile.

Alger frowned briefly, then relaxed.

“The Hanged Man.”

Another card of the Major Arcana.

“Then we are the founding members of the Tarot Club!” Audrey exclaimed happily. Only afterward did she look uneasily toward Zhou Mingrui, concealed within the fog. “Would that be acceptable, Mr. Fool?”

Zhou Mingrui shook his head in amusement.

“You may decide such trivial matters yourselves.”

“Thank you!” Audrey's delight was unmistakable.

She turned back to Alger. “Mr. Hanged Man, could you repeat the address? I'm afraid my memory might fail me.”

“Certainly.”

Alger approved of her caution and repeated the address.

After silently reciting it three times, Audrey asked with renewed excitement, “I heard that Emperor Roselle invented tarot cards as a game. But don't they actually possess the power to divine the future?”

“No,” Alger replied. “Most divination originates within the diviner. Every person possesses a spiritual aspect, which allows them to resonate with the spirit world and connect to information about themselves on a higher level. Ordinary people cannot perceive this, much less interpret the signs they receive. Divination tools allow that information to take a form they can understand. Dreams and their interpretation are a simple example.

“Tarot cards are such a tool. Their rich symbolism and structured relationships allow us to interpret those signs more easily and accurately.”

He glanced at Zhou Mingrui. When the mysterious host offered no response, Alger continued without concern.

Zhou Mingrui appeared indifferent, but he listened to every word. As he did, his previously empty mind began to feel heavy, and a dull ache pulsed inside his skull.

“I understand.” Audrey nodded, then quickly added, “But that isn't what I meant. I don't doubt the tarot. I heard Emperor Roselle created another deck first—a secret and mysterious set of twenty-two cards, each symbolizing an unknown power. Only later did he use them as the basis for the twenty-two Major Arcana in the modern tarot deck. Is that true?”

She looked toward Zhou Mingrui, clearly hoping the enigmatic Mr. Fool would answer.

He merely smiled and turned his gaze to The Hanged Man, as though testing him.

Alger instinctively straightened.

“It is true. According to legend, Emperor Roselle saw the Blasphemy Slate. That secret deck contains the profound mysteries of the twenty-two paths of the divine.”

“The twenty-two paths of the divine...” Audrey repeated longingly.

At that moment, the pain in Zhou Mingrui's head intensified. He felt his invisible connections to the crimson stars and the gray-white fog begin to weaken.

“That will be all for today's gathering,” he announced in a deep voice, making the decision at once.

“By your will.” Alger bowed his head respectfully.

“By your will,” Audrey echoed, imitating The Hanged Man.

She still had countless questions and was plainly reluctant to leave so soon.

Zhou Mingrui began severing their connections.

“Let us look forward to our next gathering,” he said with a smile.

The stars flared once more, and crimson light receded like water. As Audrey and Alger heard Mr. Fool's final words, their bodies blurred and faded.

A second later, both projections shattered, leaving the gray fog silent once more.

Zhou Mingrui felt his body grow rapidly heavier. The world around him became indistinct. Darkness filled his vision, only to be replaced by brilliant sunlight.

He was standing in the center of his apartment.

“It felt like a dream... What was that world above the fog? Who—or what—caused everything that just happened?”

Completely bewildered, Zhou Mingrui walked toward the desk on legs that felt weighted with lead.

He picked up the pocket watch he had left outside the ritual and checked the time.

“Time passed at the same rate.”

After setting the watch down, he could no longer endure the splitting pain in his head. He collapsed into the chair, lowered his face, and massaged his temples with his left thumb and middle finger.

After a long while, he sighed and spoke in Mandarin.

“It seems I won't be going home anytime soon...”

Only the ignorant could be fearless. After witnessing such an extraordinary event and learning of Beyonders and the hidden world surrounding them, Zhou Mingrui no longer dared repeat the luck-enhancement ritual in ancient Feysac or Loen.

Who knew what it might cause? The result could be stranger, more horrifying—even a living nightmare.

At the very least, I shouldn't attempt it again until I understand mysticism far better.

Fortunately, the gathering might provide exactly the knowledge he needed.

After another stretch of silence, he muttered with dismay, disappointment, pain, and grief, “From this moment onward, I am Klein.”

Klein forced himself to focus on practical questions and future plans, using them to drive away the dark emotions gathering inside him.

Perhaps he could indirectly learn the Spectator formula...

That gathering truly was remarkable. People living hundreds or thousands of kilometers apart could meet face-to-face, exchange information, and provide one another with what they needed. Come to think of it, that sounds strangely familiar...
