Klein froze for several seconds, then burst out laughing. Still pressing a hand to his temple, he joked beneath his breath:

“Wasn't that just a social network?”

Chapter 8: A New Era

Whoosh!

The wind howled through the downpour. Mountainous waves lifted and dropped the three-masted sailing ship, tossing it about like a toy in the hands of a giant.

The crimson light faded from Alger Wilson's eyes. He found himself still standing on deck, with nothing around him apparently changed.

Almost at once, the oddly shaped glass bottle in his palm shattered. The frost within it melted into the rain, and within seconds, no trace of the extraordinary artifact remained.

A hexagonal snowflake, clear as crystal, appeared on Alger's palm. It rapidly faded, seeming to sink into his flesh before vanishing entirely.

Alger gave an almost imperceptible nod, as though considering something. He remained motionless and silent for a full five minutes.

Then he turned toward the cabins. Just as he reached the entrance, another man emerged, dressed in a robe similarly embroidered with lightning.

The soft-haired blond man stopped and looked at Alger. Pressing his right fist to his chest, he said, “May the Storm be with you.”

Alger returned the gesture and the blessing, his rugged, sharply defined face betraying no emotion.

Afterward, he entered the cabin and proceeded down the corridor toward the captain's quarters.

Strangely, he encountered no sailors along the way. The ship was as silent as a graveyard.

Inside the captain's cabin, a soft brown carpet covered the floor. A bookshelf occupied one wall and a wine rack the other. Yellowed books and dark-red bottles looked uncanny in the flickering candlelight.

An ink bottle, a quill, a black metal telescope, and a brass sextant rested on the desk beside the candle.

Behind the desk sat a pale, middle-aged man wearing a captain's hat emblazoned with a skull. As Alger approached, the man declared fiercely, “I will never submit!”

“I believe you,” Alger replied with the calm indifference of someone discussing the weather.

“You...”

The man faltered, clearly wrong-footed by the response.

In that instant, Alger leaned forward and exploded into motion, crossing the cabin until only the desk separated them.

Whap!

His shoulder tightened as his right hand shot out and closed around the man's throat.

Illusory fish scales appeared across the back of Alger's hand. His grip contracted with terrifying force, denying the captive any chance to resist.

Crack!

The man's eyes bulged as Alger lifted him from the floor.

His legs kicked violently, then gradually fell still. His pupils spread, staring at nothing. A foul smell rose as the front of his trousers darkened.

Still holding him aloft, Alger lowered his stance and strode toward the wall.

Bang!

He used the corpse as a battering ram. His monstrously powerful arm drove it through the wooden planks, breaking open a hole through which rain and the briny scent of the sea poured into the cabin.

Alger hurled the body outside, into waves as tall as hills.

The wind continued to howl in the darkness as the overwhelming power of nature swallowed everything.

He took out a white handkerchief, carefully wiped his right hand, and tossed the cloth into the sea as well.

Then he stepped back and waited.

Less than ten seconds later, the blond man rushed into the room.

“What happened?”

“The Captain escaped,” Alger answered, panting and affecting irritation. “I didn't realize he had retained some of his Beyonder powers.”

“Damn it!” the blond man cursed.

He hurried to the opening and peered into the distance, but could see nothing beyond rain and churning waves.

“Forget him. He was only additional spoils,” the man said, waving dismissively. “We'll still be rewarded for recovering this ghost ship from the Tudor Era.”

Even a Keeper of the Seas would not recklessly dive into the ocean in weather like this.

“If the storm continues, the Captain won't survive for long.” Alger nodded in agreement.

Before their eyes, the broken wooden wall began repairing itself.

Alger watched it for a moment, then unconsciously turned his gaze toward the rudder and sails.

He knew perfectly well what lay beyond the cabin walls.

The chief mate, second mate, crew, and sailors were all absent. Not a single living person remained aboard.

Yet in that emptiness, the rudder turned and the sails shifted eerily by themselves.

Alger again pictured The Fool, concealed within the gray-white fog, and sighed.

He looked back at the colossal waves. Reverence and anticipation entered his distant voice.

“A new era has begun...”

——

In Empress Borough, Backlund, capital of the Loen Kingdom, Audrey Hall pinched her own cheek, still unable to believe what she had just experienced.

The antique bronze mirror on her dressing table had shattered into fragments.

She lowered her eyes. Crimson light swirled across the back of her hand like a tattoo in the shape of a star. It gradually dimmed and sank beneath her skin.

Only then was Audrey certain she had not been dreaming.

Her eyes sparkled, and an irrepressible smile spread across her face. She rose, gathered the hem of her dress, and began to dance.

It was the Ancient Elven Dance, currently the height of fashion among the nobility. Audrey moved with bright-eyed delight and effortless grace.

Knock! Knock!

Someone rapped at the bedroom door.

“Who is it?” Audrey stopped at once. She smoothed her dress and composed herself.

“My lady, may I enter? You should begin preparing for the ceremony,” her maid called from the corridor.

Audrey glanced into another mirror on the dressing table and quickly erased her grin, leaving behind only the faintest pleasant smile.

Once she was satisfied with her appearance, she answered gently, “Come in.”

The handle turned, and her maid Annie entered.

“Oh, it's broken...” Her eyes went immediately to the shattered bronze mirror.

Audrey blinked. “Ah... yes. Susie was here a moment ago. You know how much she enjoys causing trouble.”

Susie was an imperfectly bred golden retriever given to Earl Hall when he purchased a foxhound. Audrey adored her nevertheless.

“You should train her properly,” Annie said.

She gathered the shards deftly and with great care, ensuring none could injure her mistress.

When the mess was cleared away, Annie smiled. “Which dress would you like today?”

Audrey considered the question. “The one Mrs. Guinea designed for my seventeenth birthday.”

“You cannot wear the same gown twice to formal ceremonies. People would gossip and question the Hall family's finances,” Annie replied, shaking her head.

“But I truly like it,” Audrey protested mildly.

“Then wear it at home, or to a less formal engagement.” Annie's firm tone made it clear the matter was not open to negotiation.

Audrey drew an almost imperceptible breath while preserving her sweet smile. “In that case, the gown Mr. Sades sent two days ago—the one with the ruffled sleeves.”

“Your taste is always impeccable.” Annie stepped back and called through the doorway, “The sixth dressing room! Ah, never mind. I'll fetch it myself.”

The maids set to work. The gown, jewelry, shoes, hat, cosmetics, and hairstyle all demanded individual attention.

As the preparations neared completion, Earl Hall appeared at the door in a dark-brown waistcoat and matching hat. He wore a handsome mustache, and his blue eyes shone with pleasure. Loosening muscles, an expanding waist, and deepening wrinkles were gradually erasing the beauty of his youth.

He knocked twice against the open door.

“The brightest jewel in Backlund, it is time to leave.”

“Father, please stop calling me that.” Supported by her maids, Audrey rose.

“Very well. My beautiful little princess, it is time to leave.” Earl Hall bent his left arm, inviting her to take it.

Audrey shook her head. “That arm belongs to Mother—to Countess Hall.”

“Then this one.” He offered his right arm with a smile. “This belongs to my greatest pride.”

——

Oak Island, Pritz Harbor Imperial Naval Base.

When Audrey descended from the carriage on her father's arm, the behemoth before her stopped her in her tracks.

In the nearby military harbor floated an enormous vessel that gleamed with reflected metal. It carried no sails, only an observation platform, two towering smokestacks, and massive gun turrets at bow and stern.

The ship was so majestic, so impossibly large, that the sailing vessels around it resembled infant dwarfs clustering about a giant.

“Holy Lord of Storms...”

“My lord...”

“An ironclad!”

Excited voices rose from the crowd. Audrey, too, stared in amazement at this unprecedented feat of human creation—a wonder unlike anything ever to sail the seas.

It took some time for the aristocrats, ministers, and Members of Parliament to compose themselves. Then a black speck appeared in the sky. It rapidly expanded until it covered nearly a third of their view, and the atmosphere below grew solemn.

A gigantic flying machine hovered overhead, its silhouette graceful and streamlined. Cotton gasbags filled the deep-blue body, held in place by a framework of light, durable alloy. Beneath the structure were openings fitted with machine guns, projectile launchers, and cannon muzzles. The thunderous hum of its steam engines mingled with the beat of its tail propellers in an awe-inspiring mechanical symphony.

The royal family had arrived aboard their airship, projecting an authority both lofty and absolute.

On either side of the gondola shone the emblem of House Augustus: two downward-pointing swords, each with a ruby crown at its hilt. Known as the Sword of Judgment, the symbol had been passed down from the previous epoch.

Audrey was not yet eighteen and had therefore not attended her debut—the ceremony presided over by the Queen that formally introduced a young noblewoman to Backlund society as an adult. She could not stand near the airship and remained quietly toward the rear.

That suited her perfectly. She was relieved not to have to entertain the princes.

The man-made miracle descended gently. Handsome young guards in red ceremonial coats and white trousers emerged first. Medals decorated their uniforms. Carrying rifles, they formed two orderly lines to await King George III, the Queen, and the princes and princesses.

Audrey had met important people often enough that their arrival held little interest for her. Instead, her attention settled on the two black-armored riders flanking the king like statues.

In an age of iron, steam, and cannon fire, someone still wore full plate armor.

The cold gleam of metal and the dull-black helmets conveyed solemn, crushing authority.

Could they be high-ranking Disciplinary Paladins?

Audrey recalled fragments of conversation overheard from adults. Curiosity stirred within her, but she dared not approach.

With the arrival of the royal family, the ceremony began. The current Prime Minister, Lord Aguesid Negan, stepped forward.

He was a member of the Conservative Party and only the second commoner in the kingdom's history to become prime minister. For his achievements, he had been granted a lordship.

Audrey, of course, knew more. The Conservative Party's chief patron was the present Duke of Negan, Pallas Negan—Aguesid's own brother.

Aguesid was a slender man in his fifties, nearly bald, with penetrating eyes. He surveyed the assembly before beginning.

“Ladies and gentlemen, before you stands an ironclad warship that will make history. It measures 101 meters in length and 21 across the beam. Its armor belt is 457 millimeters thick, and it displaces 10,060 tons. It carries four 305-millimeter main guns, six rapid-fire cannons, twelve six-pounders, eighteen six-barreled machine guns, and four torpedo launchers. It can reach a speed of sixteen knots!

“It will be a true sovereign of the seas. It will conquer the ocean!”

The crowd stirred with excitement. The specifications alone conjured terrifying images; the actual vessel towering before them was more overwhelming still.

Aguesid smiled and spoke a few further words. Then he saluted the king.

“Your Majesty, we ask that you bestow a name upon her.”

“As she will set sail from Pritz Harbor, she shall be called the Pritz,” George III declared, undisguised delight on his face.

“The Pritz!”

“The Pritz!”

The name passed from the Minister of the Navy and the Admiral of the Imperial Fleet to the officers and sailors aboard the warship. Together, they roared:

“The Pritz!”

Amid gun salutes and celebration, George III ordered the Pritz to begin her sea trials.

Hooooonk!

Dense smoke poured from the chimneys. Beneath the deep blast of the ship's horn came the muffled thunder of machinery.

The behemoth steamed out of the harbor. Then, to the astonishment of everyone watching, the two forward main guns fired upon an uninhabited island in its path.

Boom! Boom!

The ground trembled. Dust erupted into the sky, and the shock waves raised new swells across the water.

Satisfied, Aguesid turned back to the crowd.

“From this day onward, doom descends upon the seven pirates who style themselves Admirals and the four who call themselves Kings. Let them tremble!

“Their age is over. The ironclad will rule the seas, whether our enemies command Beyonder powers, ghost ships, or cursed vessels.”

As though on cue, Aguesid's chief secretary asked, “Could the pirates not build ironclads of their own?”

Several nobles and Members of Parliament nodded. The possibility did not seem entirely unreasonable.

Aguesid smiled at once and slowly shook his head.

“Impossible. Utterly impossible! To build our ironclad required three great coal-and-steel conglomerates; more than twenty steelworks; sixty scientists and senior engineers from the Backlund Cannon Academy and the Pritz Nautical Academy; two royal shipyards; nearly one hundred factories producing component parts; an Admiralty; a shipbuilding committee; a Cabinet; a resolute and farsighted king; and a great nation capable of producing twelve million tons of steel each year!

“The pirates can never match that.”

He paused, raised both arms, and cried with fierce excitement:

“Ladies and gentlemen, the age of cannon and warship has dawned!”

Chapter 9: The Notebook

After resting for half an hour, Zhou Mingrui—who now regarded himself as Klein—finally recovered. During that time, he discovered four black dots on the back of his hand, arranged in a small square.

They quickly faded from view, but Klein knew they remained hidden within his body, waiting to be awakened.

“Four dots forming a square... Do they correspond to the four portions of staple food placed in the room's corners? Does this mean I can perform the ritual from now on without preparing the food?”

That might have sounded convenient, but the appearance of the marks filled him with unease. Nothing was more frightening than a force one could neither understand nor control.

An obscure Chinese divination ritual from Earth had produced a tangible effect in this world. He had transmigrated mysteriously in his sleep. During the ritual, unintelligible whispers had nearly driven him mad, after which he had entered a strange, dreamlike realm above the gray fog whose purpose he could not begin to guess.

Despite the June heat, Klein shivered.

“The oldest and strongest emotion of mankind is fear, and the oldest and strongest kind of fear is fear of the unknown.”

He had never understood that saying more keenly.

Two contrary urges warred within him. One was an unprecedented, irresistible desire to enter the mysterious world, learn its secrets, and explore the unknown. The other screamed at him to run—to pretend none of this had ever happened.

Brilliant sunlight streamed through the window and fell across the desk like scattered grains of gold. Klein stared at it, feeling as though he had touched warmth and hope.

Some of the tension left him. In its place came crushing exhaustion.

His eyelids grew heavy as lead and kept sinking shut. A sleepless night combined with the ordeal above the gray fog had taken its toll.

Klein shook his head and pushed himself upright with the desk. He stumbled toward the bunk bed, forgetting entirely about the loaves of rye bread in the four corners. The moment he lay down, he fell asleep.

Growl! Growl!

Hunger pangs dragged Klein awake. When he opened his eyes, he felt refreshed.

“Still a slight headache.”

He rubbed his temples and sat up, ravenous enough to eat a horse.

Straightening his shirt, he returned to the desk and picked up the silver pocket watch engraved with vine leaves.

Click!

The cover sprang open, revealing the steadily ticking second hand.

Half past twelve. I slept for three hours...

Swallowing hard, Klein returned the watch to the pocket of his linen shirt.

On the Northern Continent, a day contained twenty-four hours, an hour sixty minutes, and a minute sixty seconds. Whether a second here lasted precisely as long as one on Earth, Klein had no way of knowing.

At the moment, he could not spare any thought for mysticism, rituals, or the world above the fog. His mind contained only one word:

Food!

He could think after eating. Only then would his brain work properly.

Klein collected the four loaves of rye bread, brushed off the tiny specks of dust, and selected one for lunch without hesitation.

He had only five pence left, and in his hometown there was a tradition of eating ritual offerings afterward. Nothing about the bread appeared to have changed. Waste would be unforgivable.

The original Klein's habits and memories doubtless contributed to that conclusion.

Using expensive gas merely to heat water would be extravagant, so Klein brought out the stove, added coal, and set a pot to boil. As he waited, he paced around the room.

Without water, that dense rye bread would choke anyone.

Living on meat only at dinner is going to be miserable... No, even tonight is a special occasion. If not for my interview, Melissa would permit meat only twice a week.

Hungry and with nothing else to occupy him, Klein looked around. His eyes turned almost predatory when they landed on the pound of mutton in the cabinet.

No. I have to wait and share it with Melissa.

He shook his head, rejecting the temptation to cook half of it immediately.

Having lived alone in a large city and frequently eaten away from home, Zhou Mingrui had acquired at least basic cooking skills. His food was rarely delicious, but it was edible.

Klein turned his back on the mutton so it could no longer “seduce” him. Then he remembered the peas and potatoes he had bought that morning.

Potatoes!

An idea came to him at once. He returned to the cabinet and selected two from the small pile.

After washing them in the communal bathroom, he dropped them into the pot to boil with the water. A little later, he found a container of coarse yellow salt in the cabinet and sprinkled some into the pot.

He waited patiently for several minutes. Then he lifted the pot and poured the hot, salted water into several cups and a bowl before fishing out the potatoes with a fork and setting them on the desk.

“Hot!”

He blew on the first potato as he peeled it a little at a time. The comforting aroma of cooked potato spread through the room.

His mouth watered uncontrollably. Unable to endure the wait, Klein bit into it while it was still only half peeled.

Wonderful. Soft, floury, and faintly sweet.

Overcome by emotion and hunger, he devoured both potatoes, eating even part of the skins.

Then he lifted the bowl and drank the “soup.” The pinch of salt made the hot water surprisingly satisfying and thirst-quenching.

I loved potatoes prepared this way when I was a child...

Now comfortably full, Klein tore off pieces of rye bread, dipped them into the broth to soften them, and ate.

Perhaps the ritual had consumed more energy than he realized. By the time he stopped, he had eaten two entire loaves—a full pound of bread.

At last, strength returned to his body. He savored the simple pleasure of being alive as he finished the broth and tidied the room. Then he sat for a moment in the lustrous sunlight, content.

Eventually, he returned to the desk and began to plan.

I can't run from this. I need to find a way into the mystical world and become a Beyonder, like Justice and The Hanged Man described.

I have to overcome my fear of the unknown.

For now, my only opportunity is the next gathering. Perhaps I can learn the Spectator formula—or obtain other knowledge about mysticism.

Monday is still four days away. Before then, I need to solve Klein's problem. Why did he kill himself? What happened to him?

Unable to return home and wash his hands of the entire affair, Klein picked up the notebook from the desk. He hoped it might contain clues capable of restoring his missing memories.

The original Klein had clearly been a diligent note-taker and had also kept a diary.

A large stack of completed notebooks filled the cabinet supporting the right side of the desk.

The current volume began on May 10. Its early pages dealt with university, Klein's mentor, and various academic subjects.

“May 12. Mr. Azik mentioned that the common language of the Balam Empire on the Southern Continent also developed from ancient Feysac, itself a branch of Jotun. Why? Does that mean every intelligent creature once spoke the same language? No, there must be an error. According to The Revelation of Evernight and The Book of Storms, giants were not the only rulers of the primordial world. There were also elves, mutants, and dragons. In any case, those are only myths and fantasies.”

“May 16. Senior Associate Professor Cohen and Mr. Azik debated whether the Age of Steam was inevitable. Mr. Azik argued that it was a historical accident: without Emperor Roselle, the Northern Continent might still rely on swords, as the Southern Continent does. My mentor said Mr. Azik placed too much importance on a single individual. In his view, progress would eventually have produced an Emperor Robert even without an Emperor Roselle. The Age of Steam might have arrived later, but it would still have come. I found the argument rather pointless. I prefer discovering new things and uncovering the hidden past. Perhaps I am better suited to archaeology than history.”

“May 29. Welch came to me and said he had acquired a notebook from the Fourth Epoch. Oh, Goddess—a notebook from the Fourth Epoch! He did not want to involve students from the Archaeology Department, so he asked Naya and me to help decipher it. How could I refuse? Of course, I can only begin after defending my thesis. I cannot afford any distractions before then.”

That entry immediately seized Klein's attention. Compared with the academic notes and disagreements over historical theory, a mysterious notebook from the Fourth Epoch seemed far more likely to be connected to Klein's death.

The Fourth Epoch preceded the present Iron Age. Its history was fragmentary and shrouded in mystery. So few tombs, ruined cities, and contemporary records had been discovered that archaeologists and historians were forced to rely on vague accounts preserved by the seven great Churches—accounts shaped by religious doctrine—to construct even a rough picture of the period.

They knew of the Solomon Empire, the Tudor Dynasty, and the Trunsoest Empire, but little more.

Determined to recover lost history, the original Klein had shown less interest in the first three epochs, whose accounts resembled legend, than in the Fourth Epoch, also known as the Age of the Gods.

So Klein was anxious about his career and concentrated on his interviews. In the end, none of it mattered...

Universities were still rare. Most students came from noble or wealthy families. Nevertheless, a commoner who gained admission could, unless he isolated himself completely, form valuable connections through study groups and social gatherings despite the prejudice of established circles.

The generous Welch McGovern was one such connection. The son of a banker from Constant City in Midseashire, he had frequently asked Naya and Klein for assistance after working with them on the same academic team.

Klein continued reading.

“June 18. I have graduated. Farewell, Khoy University!”

“June 19. I have seen the notebook. By comparing sentence structures and word roots, I determined that the language is a modified form of ancient Feysac. More precisely, Feysac changed gradually throughout the notebook's thousand-year history.”

“June 20. We deciphered the first page. The author belonged to a family named Antigonus.”

“June 21. The author mentioned the Black Emperor. That title is anachronistic for the period in which Professor Cohen believes the notebook was written. Is the professor mistaken? Or was ‘Black Emperor’ a title shared by every ruler of the Solomon Empire?”

“June 22. The Antigonus family apparently occupied a very high position within the Solomon Empire. The author described a secret exchange with someone named Tudor. Tudor? Could this person be connected to the Tudor Dynasty?”

“June 23. I am trying not to think about the notebook or visit Welch. I need to prepare for my interviews. They are too important!”

“June 24. Naya says they have discovered something new. I think I should go and see it.”

“June 25. According to the newly deciphered passage, the author accepted a mission to visit the Nation of the Evernight, located at the summit of the highest peak in the Hornacis mountain range. Oh, Goddess! How could a nation exist atop a mountain more than six thousand meters high? How would its people survive?”

“June 26. Are these strange things real?”

There the diary ended. Zhou Mingrui had transmigrated during the early hours of June 28.

Which means there was an entry on June 27. It was the sentence I saw when I first arrived...

Everyone will die, including me.

Goosebumps rose across Klein's skin as he turned to that page.

To solve the mystery of the original Klein's death, he would need to visit Welch and examine the ancient notebook. But a lifetime of novels, films, and television dramas warned him that if the notebook truly was responsible, going near it would be extremely dangerous. Countless fools had entered haunted castles despite every warning, and none had met pleasant ends.

Still, he had to go. Running away would not solve the problem. It would only allow the danger to grow until it overflowed and drowned him.

Perhaps I should call the police. But what would I say—that I committed suicide?

Knock!

Knock, knock!

A series of rapid, forceful blows struck the door.

Klein sat bolt upright and listened.

Knock!

Knock, knock!

The pounding echoed through the empty corridor.

Chapter 10: The Usual Pattern

“Who is it?”

Klein had been contemplating the former owner's mysterious suicide and the unknown danger he might face when the sudden knocking interrupted him. Instinctively, he opened the drawer, took out the revolver, and called through the door in a guarded voice.

There were two seconds of silence. Then a slightly shrill voice with an Awwa accent replied, “It's me—Mountbatten. Bitsch Mountbatten.”

After a brief pause, the man added, “Police.”

Bitsch Mountbatten...

The name immediately summoned an image from Klein's memories.

Mountbatten was the officer responsible for the street where the apartment stood. He was a rude, violent man who preferred solving problems with his hands. Then again, perhaps only someone like him could deter the district's drunks, thieves, occasional thieves, scoundrels, and hooligans.

His peculiar voice was one of his most recognizable traits.

“All right. I'll be there in a moment!” Klein called.

He started to return the revolver to the drawer. Then he remembered that he had no idea why the police had come. If they searched the room, the weapon would be discovered at once.

Moving quickly but carefully, he went to the stove, whose fire had already gone out, and placed the revolver inside. He lifted the coal basket, shook several pieces over the gun, and set the kettle back on top to conceal everything.

Once satisfied, he straightened his clothes, hurried to the door, and opened it.

“Sorry. I was taking a nap.”

Four policemen stood in the corridor, wearing black-and-white checked uniforms and peaked caps. Brown-bearded Bitsch Mountbatten cleared his throat.

“These three inspectors have some questions for you.”

Inspectors?

Klein automatically examined the insignia on the strangers' shoulders. Two wore three silver hexagons, while the third wore two. All three clearly outranked Mountbatten, whose epaulets displayed only three chevrons.

Despite being a history graduate, Klein knew almost nothing about police ranks. He remembered only that Mountbatten frequently boasted of being a senior sergeant.

So these three are inspectors?

Conversations with Benson, Welch, and his classmates had at least taught him the proper social response. He stepped aside and gestured into the room.

“Please, come in. How may I help you?”

The apparent leader was a middle-aged man with piercing eyes that seemed capable of reading a person's thoughts and exposing every secret. Fine wrinkles framed those eyes, and light-brown hair showed beneath the edge of his cap.

He surveyed the room before asking in a deep voice, “Do you know Welch McGovern?”

“What happened to him?” Klein blurted, his body tightening.

“I am the one asking the questions.” The middle-aged inspector's gaze hardened.

The other inspector with three silver hexagons smiled reassuringly at Klein.

“Don't be nervous. This is a routine inquiry.”

He appeared to be in his thirties and had a straight nose and gray eyes. Those eyes possessed an indescribable depth, like a lake hidden within an ancient, unvisited forest.

Klein took a breath and arranged his answer.

“If you mean Welch McGovern, a Khoy University graduate from Constant City, then yes. We were classmates and studied under the same mentor, Senior Associate Professor Quentin Cohen.”

In the Loen Kingdom, professor was both an academic title and an official post—something like a combination of professor and department chair on Earth. A university department could have only one. An associate professor seeking promotion therefore had to wait for the current professor to retire or somehow displace that superior.

To keep talented scholars from leaving, the kingdom's Higher Education Commission had eventually added senior associate professor to the existing hierarchy of lecturer, associate professor, and professor. The title recognized scholars with exceptional achievements or long service who had not obtained a professorial chair.

Klein met the lead inspector's gaze and considered his next words.

“To be honest, Welch and I were fairly close. Recently, Naya and I met him often to interpret and discuss a Fourth Epoch notebook he owned. Inspectors, has something happened to him?”

Instead of answering, the middle-aged inspector glanced toward his gray-eyed colleague.

The ordinary-looking man in the peaked cap replied gently, “I'm sorry. Mr. Welch is dead.”

“What?”

Despite his suspicions, Klein could not suppress the cry of shock.

Welch died, just like the original Klein?

This is worse than I thought.

“What about Naya?” he demanded.

“Ms. Naya is also dead,” the gray-eyed inspector said with disturbing calm. “Both were found in Mr. Welch's home.”

“Murdered?” Klein already had a darker suspicion.

Perhaps it was suicide...

The inspector shook his head.

“The scene indicates that they took their own lives. Mr. Welch repeatedly struck his head against a wall until it was covered in blood. Ms. Naya drowned herself in a washbasin—the ordinary kind used for washing one's face.”

“That's impossible...”

Every hair on Klein's body stood on end as his imagination supplied the scene.

A young woman kneeling before a chair, her face submerged in a basin of water. Soft brown hair stirring in the air while the rest of her remained perfectly still.

Welch sprawled on the floor, staring blankly at the ceiling, his forehead reduced to a bloody ruin. On the wall, streaks of blood marking every impact...

The gray-eyed inspector continued, “We agree that it appears impossible. However, the autopsies and the evidence at the scene rule out drugs and external force. Neither Mr. Welch nor Ms. Naya showed any sign of resistance.”

Before Klein could respond, the man stepped farther into the room and asked with deliberate casualness, “When did you last see either of them?”

As he spoke, his eyes signaled to the colleague wearing two silver hexagons.

The younger inspector appeared close to Klein's age. He was handsome, with dark sideburns and green eyes, and possessed the romantic air of a poet.

Klein searched his memories before answering carefully.

“June twenty-sixth. We were reading a newly deciphered section of the notebook. Afterward, I came home to prepare for my interview on June thirtieth—with the History Department at Tingen University.”

Tingen was famous as a city of universities. It contained both Tingen University and Khoy University, along with technical institutes and colleges of law and commerce. In education, it ranked behind only the capital, Backlund.

As Klein finished speaking, he noticed the young inspector approach his desk and pick up the notebook that served as a diary.

Damn it. I forgot to hide that!

“Wait!” Klein cried.

The young man looked back with a smile but continued turning the pages.

“A necessary procedure,” the gray-eyed inspector explained.

Bitsch Mountbatten and the stern, middle-aged inspector merely watched. Neither helped with the search nor attempted to stop it.

Where is your search warrant?

Klein nearly asked aloud. Then he remembered that the Loen Kingdom's legal system might not even include search warrants. At least, he had no memory of them. The modern police force had existed for only fifteen or sixteen years.

When the original Klein was a child, its members were still called Public Security Officers.

Unable to interfere, Klein watched the young inspector examine the diary while the gray-eyed man remained silent.

“What is this peculiar symbol?” The young officer had reached the final page. Then he asked, “And what does this mean? ‘Everyone will die, including me’...”

Isn't it obvious that everyone except the gods will die?

Klein prepared an evasive answer. Then he remembered his earlier desire to establish contact with the police in case the danger surrounding Welch reached him. Until now, he had lacked both an excuse and an opportunity.

He made his decision in less than a second. Pressing a hand to his forehead, he answered in a pained voice:

“I don't know. I truly don't... When I woke this morning, something felt wrong, as though I had forgotten part of my life. My recent memories are especially unclear. I don't even remember writing that sentence.”

Sometimes honesty was the best solution, provided one used it skillfully. A person needed to know which truths could be told, which had to remain concealed, and in what order to reveal them.

As a seasoned keyboard warrior, Klein was well practiced in selective argument.

“Ridiculous! Do you take us for fools?” Bitsch Mountbatten interrupted angrily.

The lie was so poor that it insulted the intelligence of every officer present.

Pretending to be insane would have been more convincing than claiming amnesia.

“I'm telling the truth.” Klein looked openly at Mountbatten and the middle-aged inspector.

He could hardly have been more truthful.

“Perhaps you are,” the gray-eyed inspector said slowly.

What? He actually believes me?

Klein was more surprised than anyone.

The inspector smiled. “A specialist will arrive in two days. Believe me, she should be able to help you recover your missing memories.”

A specialist? Someone who can restore memories? A psychologist?

Klein frowned.

Wait. What if she uncovers my memories of Earth?

He suddenly wanted to slap himself.

The young inspector set down the diary and searched the desk and the rest of the room. Fortunately, he concentrated on the books and never lifted the kettle.

“Very well, Mr. Klein. Thank you for your cooperation,” the gray-eyed man said. “We advise you not to leave Tingen during the next few days. If you must travel, notify Inspector Mountbatten first. Otherwise, you will be treated as a fugitive.”

That's it? They're finished for today? No further interrogation? No trip to the station to have a confession beaten out of me?

Klein was baffled. Nevertheless, he also wished to uncover the truth behind Welch's death, so he nodded.

“That won't be a problem.”

The officers filed out. The young inspector left last. At the doorway, he suddenly patted Klein on the shoulder.

“This is good. You're very fortunate.”

“What do you mean?” Klein asked, bewildered.

The green-eyed officer with the air of a poet smiled.

“In cases like this, the usual pattern is for everyone involved to die. We are both pleased and fortunate to find you still alive.”

He stepped into the corridor and politely closed the door behind him.

The usual pattern is for everyone to die?

They're pleased I'm alive? Fortunate that I'm alive?

On that June afternoon, a chill settled into Klein's bones.

Chapter 11: Real Cooking

The usual pattern is for everyone involved to die? They're pleased I'm alive? Fortunate I'm alive?

A shiver ran through Klein. He rushed toward the door, intending to catch the policemen and demand protection.

His hand had almost reached the handle when he stopped.

If that officer believed the situation was so dangerous, why hadn't they offered to protect an important witness—or perhaps their most valuable lead?

Wasn't that far too careless?

Had they only been testing him? Or was this bait?

Possibility after possibility crowded Klein's mind. He began to suspect that the police had not truly left at all. Perhaps they were secretly observing him, waiting to see how he reacted.

The thought calmed him. He opened the door slowly and deliberately called down the stairwell in a trembling voice:

“You'll protect me, won't you?”

Tap, tap, tap...

The officers did not answer, nor did the rhythm of leather soles against wooden stairs change.

“I know you will!” Klein called again, forcing conviction into his voice. He did his best impression of an ordinary man terrified by the danger surrounding him.

The footsteps grew fainter and finally disappeared below.

Klein snorted softly. “That reaction was much too artificial. Their acting needs work.”

He made no attempt to follow. Instead, he returned to the room and closed the door.

For the next several hours, Klein gave a thorough performance of what people in China's Foodie Empire might have called unease, nervousness, agitation, distraction, and occasional muttering. He did not relax merely because no one appeared to be watching.

An actor must cultivate his craft, he joked inwardly.

By the time the sun sank westward and the clouds along the horizon turned reddish orange, tenants were returning to the apartment one after another. Klein finally allowed himself to focus on something else.

“Melissa will be home soon...”

He went to the stove, removed the kettle, brushed aside the coal, and retrieved the revolver.

Without hesitation, he crossed to the bunk bed. More than ten wooden slats supported the boards beneath the mattress. He wedged the revolver's cylinder securely between one slat and the board behind it.

Then he straightened and waited, his nerves tight, half expecting the police to smash open the door and charge inside with weapons drawn.

If this were merely an age of steam and machinery, he could be confident that no one had seen him. But extraordinary powers existed here. He had experienced them himself.

Several minutes passed without any disturbance. The only sound from the corridor was a conversation between two tenants on their way to the Heart of the Wild Bar on Iron Cross Street.

“Phew.” Klein exhaled, finally reassured.

Now he needed only to wait for Melissa and prepare the mutton stew with fresh peas.

The thought alone filled his imagination with rich gravy. He recalled how Melissa usually cooked the dish.

First, she boiled water and browned the meat. Then she added onion, salt, a little pepper, and more water. After simmering it for a while, she added peas and potatoes, covered the pot, and cooked everything for another forty or fifty minutes.

“Simple and crude,” Klein said, shaking his head. “It relies entirely on the meat's natural flavor.”

But ordinary families had little choice. They could not afford a cupboard full of seasonings or elaborate cooking methods. Their food needed to be simple, practical, and economical. For people who ate meat only once or twice a week, anything unburned and unspoiled was a treat.

Zhou Mingrui had never been a gifted cook and had usually ordered takeout. Still, cooking three or four times a week over many years had made him competent. At the very least, he would not waste the pound of mutton.

“If I wait for Melissa, dinner won't be ready until after seven-thirty. She'll be starving by then. It's time she learned what real cooking tastes like.”

Having found an excellent excuse, Klein relit the stove, fetched water from the communal bathroom, and washed the meat. Then he brought out the cutting board and knife and chopped the mutton into small pieces.

If Melissa asked about his sudden culinary ability, he would credit the late Welch McGovern. Welch had employed a chef skilled in Midseashire cuisine and had often devised dishes of his own for friends to sample.

The dead can't contradict me.

Then again, this is a world of Beyonders. Perhaps the dead can speak after all...

A flicker of guilt passed through him.

Klein dismissed the thought and transferred the meat to a soup bowl. From the spice box, he took a spoonful of coarse salt, half of it already yellowing. He carefully shook several black peppercorns from a precious little bottle, then mixed the seasonings through the meat and left it to marinate.

He placed a saucepan over the stove. While it heated, he found the carrots left from the previous day and chopped them together with the onions he had bought that morning.

When the preparations were complete, he removed a small tin from the cabinet. Very little lard remained inside.

Klein scooped out one spoonful and melted it in the hot pan. Then he added the carrots and onions and stirred them until their fragrance filled the room.

Next came the mutton. He tipped in the entire bowl and browned the pieces carefully.

At this stage he ought to have added cooking wine, or at least red wine. The Moretti family could afford neither luxury; even beer was limited to one glass a week. Klein had to make do and poured in boiled water instead.

After the meat had simmered for about twenty minutes, he removed the lid, added the fresh peas and chopped potatoes, then poured in another cup of hot water and two spoonfuls of salt.

He replaced the lid, lowered the flame, and exhaled with satisfaction as he waited for his sister.

Minute by minute, the aroma grew stronger. Savory meat mingled with earthy potatoes and the clean sweetness of onion.

The scents blended into something irresistible. Klein repeatedly swallowed as he checked the time on his pocket watch.

More than forty minutes later, unhurried but rhythmic footsteps approached. A key entered the lock, the handle turned, and the door opened.

Before Melissa had even stepped inside, he heard her murmur in confusion, “That smells wonderful...”

Still holding her schoolbag, she entered and stared toward the stove.

“You made this?”

Her hand stopped halfway through removing her veil bonnet. She looked at Klein in astonishment.

Melissa sniffed again. The aroma softened her expression, yet disbelief remained.

“You made this?” she repeated.

“Are you worried I'll waste the mutton?” Klein countered with a smile. Before she could answer, he continued, “Don't worry. I specifically asked Welch to teach me this dish. You know he employed an excellent cook.”

“This is your first time?” Melissa's brows drew together, but the fragrance soon smoothed them again.

“Apparently I have talent.” Klein laughed. “It's nearly ready. Put down your books and hat, wash your hands, and prepare to taste it. I'm quite confident.”

Hearing her brother issue such orderly instructions and seeing the gentle composure of his smile, Melissa remained frozen in the doorway, momentarily unable to respond.

“Would you prefer the mutton overcooked?” Klein teased.

“Oh! Right, of course!”

Melissa came back to herself and hurried into the room, bag in one hand and bonnet in the other.

When Klein lifted the saucepan's lid, a cloud of steam billowed up before his face. He placed two slices of rye bread beside the stew, letting them absorb its heat and fragrance until they softened.

By the time Melissa had put away her things, washed her hands and face, and returned, a platter of mutton stewed with peas, potatoes, carrots, and onions stood on the table. Each plate held a slice of rye bread lightly soaked in gravy.

“Go on. Try it.” Klein pointed to the wooden fork and spoon beside her plate.

Still looking slightly dazed, Melissa obeyed. She speared a piece of potato, placed it in her mouth, and bit down cautiously.

The soft, floury potato released the rich flavor of the gravy. Her mouth watered, and she swallowed the piece in only a few bites.

“Try the mutton,” Klein urged with a tilt of his chin.

He had sampled it already and judged it barely adequate. But Melissa had little experience of fine food and ate meat only occasionally. For her, adequate would be more than enough.

Anticipation filled her eyes as she carefully picked up a piece of mutton.

It was tender enough to nearly dissolve on her tongue. Its savory juices flooded her mouth.

The sensation was unlike anything Melissa had tasted. Before she knew it, she had eaten several pieces.

“K-Klein, this was supposed to be for you...” she stammered, blushing.

“I sampled plenty while cooking. That's the cook's privilege.” Klein smiled reassuringly and picked up his own utensils.

He ate casually, sometimes taking a piece of meat, sometimes a spoonful of peas. At other moments, he set down the utensils, tore off some rye bread, and dipped it in gravy.

Seeing him behave normally, Melissa relaxed and surrendered herself once more to the meal.

“It's truly delicious. It doesn't taste as though you've never made it before.” She looked at the empty platter and praised him wholeheartedly. Even the last drop of gravy was gone.

“It can't compare with Welch's chef. Once I'm earning money, I'll take you and Benson to a restaurant for something much better.” Klein found himself genuinely looking forward to it.

“Your interview... Burp!”

Melissa's sentence ended in an involuntary sound of contentment.

She clapped a hand over her mouth, mortified.

It was the stew's fault. It had simply been too delicious.

Suppressing his laughter, Klein decided not to tease her. He pointed at the empty dishes.

“Those are your responsibility.”

“All right!”

Melissa sprang to her feet, gathered the basin and dishes, and hurried out.

When she returned, she opened the cabinet and inspected the spices and provisions as usual.

“Did you use these?” She turned toward Klein with the black pepper bottle in one hand and the lard tin in the other.

Klein shrugged and smiled. “Only a little. Fine food has its price.”

Melissa's eyes flickered. Her expression changed several times before she finally said, “I'll cook from now on.

“You need to prepare for your interview—and think seriously about finding work.”

Chapter 12: Here Again

Melissa, must you rub salt into the wound?

Klein complained inwardly as a dull ache pulsed through his head.

The amount of knowledge he had lost was not enormous, but neither was it insignificant. His interview was in two days. How could he possibly relearn everything in time?

Worse, he was entangled in a bizarre supernatural incident. How was he supposed to concentrate on revising?

He gave his sister a perfunctory answer and put on a convincing display of study. Melissa moved a chair beside him and, by the light of the gas lamp, began working on her own assignments.

The evening passed peacefully. At almost eleven, the siblings wished each other good night and went to bed.

Knock!

Knock, knock!

Heavy pounding dragged Klein from his dreams.

Through the window, he saw the first pale trace of dawn. Still dazed, he rolled over and sat up.

“Who is it?”

Look at the time. Why didn't Melissa wake me?

“Dunn Smith,” a deep male voice answered from beyond the door.

Dunn Smith? I don't know anyone by that name...

Klein climbed out of bed, shook his head clear, and walked to the entrance.

When he opened the door, he found the gray-eyed police inspector from the previous day waiting outside.

Alarm tightened Klein's voice. “Is something wrong?”

Dunn's expression remained stern. “We located a carriage driver. He testified that you visited Mr. Welch on the twenty-seventh—the day Mr. Welch and Ms. Naya died. Mr. Welch also paid your fare.”

Klein froze, but felt none of the fear or guilt one might expect from a man whose lie had been exposed.

He had not been lying. The evidence surprised him as much as it did anyone.

The original Klein truly had visited Welch on June twenty-seventh. That night, after returning home, he had killed himself in the same inexplicable manner as Welch and Naya.

Klein forced a smile. “That isn't sufficient evidence. It doesn't directly connect me to their deaths. To be honest, I'm as curious about this as you are. I want to know what happened to my two friends. But... I truly can't remember. Almost everything I did on the twenty-seventh is missing. You may find that difficult to believe, but I only inferred that I'd gone to Welch's house by reading my diary.”

“You have remarkable composure,” Dunn said with a slight nod. His face showed neither anger nor amusement.

“Then you should also hear the sincerity in my voice.” Klein looked directly into his eyes.

I'm telling the truth. Part of it, at least.

Dunn did not respond at once. He swept his gaze across the room before speaking slowly.

“Mr. Welch is missing a revolver. I suspect I would find it here. Wouldn't I, Mr. Klein?”

So that's where the gun came from.

The final piece fell into place, and Klein made his decision instantly.

He raised his hands halfway, stepped back to clear the doorway, and indicated the bunk bed with his chin.

“Behind the bed board.”

He did not specify the lower bunk. No sensible person would hide something behind the upper board, where any visitor could notice it at a glance.

Dunn did not enter. One corner of his mouth twitched.

“Anything else you'd like to tell me?”

“Yes,” Klein replied without hesitation.

“When I woke in the middle of the night yesterday, I was lying across my desk with the revolver beside me. A bullet was in the corner of the room. It appeared that I had tried to kill myself. Perhaps because I had never fired a gun, or because I lost my nerve at the final moment... whatever happened, the shot failed to produce the intended result. My head is still attached, and I'm still alive.

“Since then, parts of my memory have been missing, including everything I saw and did at Welch's house on the twenty-seventh. I'm not lying. I genuinely don't remember.”

To clear himself as a suspect—and free himself from the strange danger closing around him—Klein revealed almost everything that had happened. He omitted only his transmigration and the gathering above the gray fog.

He chose every word carefully, ensuring that each statement could bear more than one interpretation. He did not say that the bullet had entered his brain; only that it had failed to achieve its intended result and that his head remained in place.

To another listener, those might have sounded like the same thing. In truth, they were worlds apart.

Dunn listened without interruption.

“That agrees with my hypothesis,” he said at last. “It also matches the underlying pattern of similar incidents. I cannot explain how you survived.”

“I'm glad you believe me. I don't know how I survived either.” Klein released a quiet breath of relief.

“However...” Dunn let the word hang between them. “My belief is irrelevant. You are presently the principal suspect. An expert must confirm that your memories are truly missing—or that you otherwise had no part in the deaths of Mr. Welch and Ms. Naya.”

He cleared his throat, his expression growing solemn.

“Mr. Klein, I require your cooperation. You will accompany me to the police station for further investigation. If no problem is discovered, the process should take two or three days.”

“The expert is already here?” Klein asked blankly.

Hadn't they said she would need two more days?

“She arrived earlier than expected.” Dunn stepped aside and gestured toward the corridor.

“Allow me to leave a note.”

Benson was still away, and Melissa had already gone to school. Klein needed to explain that he had become involved in the investigation of Welch's death, or they would be sick with worry.

Dunn nodded, plainly unconcerned. “Very well.”

Klein returned to the desk and searched for paper while considering what lay ahead.

In truth, he had no desire to meet this expert. He possessed a far greater secret than the police suspected.

In a world dominated by seven great Churches—and where Emperor Roselle, himself possibly a transmigrator, had eventually been assassinated—admitting to transmigration sounded like an excellent way to be dragged before a tribunal.

But Klein had neither weapons nor fighting skill nor supernatural abilities. He was no match for a trained inspector, much less the several officers he suspected were waiting out of sight in the corridor.

If they draw their guns, I'm finished.

“I'll have to take things one step at a time.”

He left the note, collected his keys, and followed Dunn out.

Four policemen in black-and-white checked uniforms waited in the dark corridor, two on either side. Every one of them was alert.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

Klein walked beside Dunn as they descended the wooden staircase, which creaked beneath their feet.

Outside stood a four-wheeled carriage bearing the police emblem—two crossed swords beneath a crown. Around it, the street bustled as noisily as ever.

“After you.” Dunn indicated the carriage.

Klein was about to climb aboard when an oyster seller suddenly seized a customer and accused him of theft.

The two began wrestling. Nearby horses shied, and chaos spread through the crowd.

An opportunity!

Klein had no time for further thought. He bent low and dashed into the confusion.

Shoving past some people and dodging others, he fled desperately toward the far end of the street.

To avoid the expert, he would have to reach the docks outside the city, take a boat down the Tussock River, and escape to Backlund. The capital's immense population would make it easy to disappear.

Alternatively, he could board a steam train east to Enmat Harbor, then travel by sea to Pritz and onward to Backlund.

Before long, Klein reached Iron Cross Street, where several carriages waited for passengers.

“The docks outside the city.” He raised a hand and jumped into one.

His plan was simple. He would lead the police to believe he was traveling to the docks, then leap from the carriage once he had put enough distance between them.

“Right away.” The driver flicked the reins.

Clop! Clop! Clop!

The carriage rolled away from Iron Cross Street.

Just as Klein prepared to jump, it turned onto another road—one that did not lead out of the city.

“Where are you taking me?” he demanded, momentarily stunned.

“To Welch's house...” the driver answered in a flat voice.

What?

Klein stared, speechless. The driver turned around, revealing cold gray eyes.

Dunn Smith.

“You!”

Panic surged through Klein. The world blurred and spun, and he abruptly sat upright.

Sat upright?

He looked around in confusion. The crimson moon hung beyond the window, draping the room in its red veil.

Klein touched his forehead. His skin was cold and wet with sweat. So was his back.

“A nightmare...” He breathed deeply. “Everything is fine. Everything is fine...”

Yet the dream disturbed him. Throughout it, he had remained unusually lucid, capable of calm thought and deliberate decisions.

Once his heartbeat settled, Klein checked the pocket watch. It was only two in the morning.

He quietly climbed out of bed, intending to wash his face in the communal bathroom—and relieve his painfully full bladder.

He opened the door and stepped into the dark corridor. Guided by faint moonlight, he walked softly toward the bathroom.

Then he noticed a figure outside the window at the corridor's far end.

The silhouette wore a black trench coat—shorter than an overcoat, longer than an ordinary jacket.

Half concealed by darkness, it stood bathed in crimson moonlight.

Slowly, the figure turned.

Its eyes were deep, gray, and cold.

Dunn Smith.

Chapter 13: Nighthawk

Thump!

Klein stumbled backward. For a moment, he could not tell whether he was awake or still dreaming.

The figure removed his black top hat and bowed slightly.

“Allow me to introduce myself again. Dunn Smith, Nighthawk.”

Nighthawk? One of the Church of the Evernight Goddess's Beyonder teams—the name Justice and The Hanged Man mentioned?

The pieces came together, and Klein exclaimed, “You can control dreams? You created the dream I just had?”

Dunn replaced his hat, concealing his slightly receding hairline. His deep gray eyes remained fixed on Klein.

“No. I merely entered your dream and provided the necessary guidance.”

His voice was low and soothing. It carried through the dim corridor without disturbing the other tenants' sleep.

“In dreams, suppressed emotions and darker thoughts are amplified. Everything becomes chaotic, absurd, and irrational—but it remains rooted in reality. To someone with my experience, the underlying truth is perfectly clear. I place greater trust in the person you are while dreaming than in the person you present while awake.”

This is bad. What ordinary person can control his dreams? If I had dreamed of Earth, wouldn't Dunn have discovered everything?

The thought petrified Klein.

Yet something felt wrong. Throughout the dream he had remained unusually lucid and rational, fully aware of what he should and should not reveal.

Put simply, it had not felt like a dream.

Did Dunn see only what I allowed him to see?

Klein's thoughts raced as a possible explanation emerged.

Was this a benefit of transmigration—some special quality of his body or soul? Or had the luck-enhancement ritual changed him?

He gathered his thoughts. “Then, Mr. Smith, do you believe I've truly lost part of my memory?”

Dunn did not answer directly. Instead, he studied Klein with unsettling intensity.

“You aren't particularly surprised by any of this.”

He continued, “I've met people who refused to believe in Beyonder powers. They preferred to think they had never really awakened.”

Klein acknowledged the point with a small nod. “Perhaps I've always hoped some extraordinary power might exist—something capable of helping me.”

“An interesting response...” Dunn nodded without expression. “Perhaps luck was not the only reason you survived.

“I can now confirm that the incident damaged your memory, particularly the memories associated with it.”

Klein released a quiet breath. “Then may I go home?”

Dunn slipped one hand into his pocket and walked slowly toward him. The surrounding darkness seemed to grow tranquil and gentle.

“No. You must still accompany me to meet the expert,” he said with a courteous smile.

“Why?” Klein blurted. “Don't you trust what you found in my dream?”

You must be joking. If this expert uses hypnosis or reads minds, my greatest secret will be exposed.

The consequences would be unimaginable.

“I'm ordinarily a modest man, but I have complete confidence in matters involving dreams,” Dunn replied calmly. “Still, there is no harm in confirming something this important. Her specialty differs from mine. She may even help you recover some of what you lost.”

His voice deepened.

“After all, you are connected to the disappearance of the Antigonus family's notebook.”

“What?” Klein froze.

Dunn stopped before him and locked those gray eyes on his.

“The Fourth Epoch notebook was nowhere at the scene. Welch is dead. Naya is dead. You are our only remaining lead.”

“Very well.” After a moment's silence, Klein exhaled.

The notebook is missing? This is even stranger than I thought.

How did I fail to wonder what happened to it?

Dunn nodded, passed him, and said, “Lock your door and come with me to Welch's residence. The expert is waiting there.”

Klein drew a silent breath. His heart pounded with unease.

He wanted to refuse. He even considered running. But after what had happened in the dream, Dunn would certainly be watching him closely. Against a Beyonder, an ordinary man had little chance of escaping by force.

He probably carries a revolver—and unlike me, he knows how to use it.

Thoughts flashed through Klein's mind. In the end, he accepted reality.

“All right.”

I can only take things as they come. Perhaps the strange protection I had in the dream will work again.

“Then let's go,” Dunn said indifferently.

Klein turned to follow. After two steps, he stopped.

“Mr. Smith, I... need to use the bathroom first.”

That was why he had entered the corridor in the first place.

Dunn made no attempt to stop him. He merely gave Klein a penetrating look.

“Of course. But believe me, Klein—I am far more powerful in the darkness than you can imagine.”

In the darkness...

Klein repeated the phrase silently.

He made no foolish attempt to escape. Instead, he relieved himself honestly, then washed his face with cold water until he had completely calmed down.

After changing his clothes and locking the apartment, he followed Dunn downstairs with quiet steps.

In the stillness, Dunn suddenly asked, “At the end of your dream, why did you run? What were you afraid of?”

Klein produced an answer at once.

“I don't remember what I did at Welch's house, or whether I had any direct involvement in his and Naya's deaths. I was afraid you might prove I had killed them. If that happened, I would rather gamble on escape and try to begin a new life on the Southern Continent.”

“I would have done the same,” Dunn said.

He pushed open the building's front door, and the cool midnight breeze entered, dispersing the heat trapped inside.

Dunn showed no concern that Klein might run as they approached the waiting carriage. It was exactly the vehicle from the dream: a four-wheeled carriage drawn by a single horse, with a driver at the front and the police emblem—two crossed swords beneath a crown—carved into its side.

Klein climbed in after Dunn. Thick carpet covered the floor, and a soothing fragrance filled the interior.

Once seated, he searched for a topic that might reveal useful information.

“Mr. Smith, suppose the expert confirms that I've truly lost part of my memory, and there is no other evidence identifying me as either perpetrator or victim. Would the matter be over?”

“In theory.” Dunn smiled, and the expression looked strangely out of place on him. “We would search for the notebook by other means. If it still exists, it can be found.

“Before releasing you, however, we would confirm that you carry neither a curse nor the aura of an evil spirit. We would also ensure that no associated psychological problems remain, so that you can return to a peaceful, healthy life.”

Klein immediately caught the qualification.

“In theory?”

“Yes. Only in theory.” Dunn met his eyes. “In our line of work, events are often distorted, unconventional, and inexplicable. Whether they continue or truly end is not always something we can predict or control.”

“Can you give me an example?” A genuine chill passed through Klein.

The carriage sped along an almost empty street. Dunn took out a pipe and held it beneath his nose without lighting it.

“Sometimes, just when we believe everything has ended and life has returned to normal, the horror resurfaces in a far more chilling form.

“Several years ago, we investigated an evil cult. Its leaders used human sacrifice to appease an evil god, persuading followers to take their own lives. When one man was selected, his will to survive overcame fanaticism, distorted doctrine, and hallucinogenic drugs. He escaped in secret and reported the cult to the police.

“The case was transferred to us. It was a simple operation. None of the cultists were Beyonders, and the supposed god had been invented by their leader for money and personal gratification. There was no divinity involved—only human depravity.

“Two of our members, with police support, destroyed the cult and arrested everyone involved. We examined the informant and confirmed that no demonic aura lingered on him. He was not cursed. He displayed no mental illness, personality disorder, or other abnormality. Nothing.

“In the years that followed, he advanced in his career, married a good woman, and had a son and a daughter. His dark past seemed impossibly distant. All the fear and bloodshed appeared to have vanished.”

Dunn gave a quiet, humorless laugh.

“Then, in March of this year, despite his comfortable finances, loving wife, and wonderful children... he strangled himself in his office.”

Crimson moonlight entered through the carriage window and washed across Dunn's face.

In that moment, his faintly self-mocking smile filled Klein with indescribable horror.

“Strangled himself...” Klein drew a silent breath, as though glimpsing his own future.

Even if I escaped once, perhaps the reprieve is only temporary.

Is there any way to end this completely?

Could I become a Beyonder and fight it?

Silence returned to the carriage as thoughts surged through Klein's mind.

They traveled at speed for what felt like a long time. Just as Klein resolved to ask Dunn whether any true solution existed, the carriage slowed to a halt.

“Mr. Smith, we've arrived at Welch's residence,” the driver called.

“Let's get out.” Dunn straightened his knee-length black trench coat.

Then he added, “I should introduce the expert before you meet. Her public identity is Awwa County's most celebrated spirit medium.”

Klein suppressed his other questions. “And her real identity?”

Dunn turned halfway toward the door, then looked back over his shoulder. His gray eyes were deep and unreadable.

“A genuine Spirit Medium.”

Chapter 14: The Spirit Medium

A genuine spirit medium...

Klein repeated Dunn's description silently. Without another word, he followed the Nighthawk out of the carriage.

Welch's Tingen residence was a detached house with its own garden. The road beyond the wrought-metal gates was broad enough for four carriages abreast, with streetlamps positioned every fifty meters along either side.

They differed from the lamps Klein remembered on Earth. Each was a gas lamp no taller than an adult man, allowing the lamplighters to reach it easily. Black metal pressed close around the glass in a checkered grille, producing patterns like the cut paper of an antique lantern. Cold metal and warm light intertwined; darkness and illumination occupied the same object.

The last rays of sunset covered the path as Klein and Dunn passed through the half-open gate.

A paved drive, wide enough for two carriages, led directly to a two-story house. A garden lay to the left and a lawn to the right. The faint fragrance of flowers mingled with the cool scent of fresh grass, an ordinarily soothing combination.

The instant Klein entered, however, he shivered and looked around.

Something in the garden was watching him. More eyes observed from the lawn, the roof, behind the swing, and every dark corner.

He could see no one, yet the grounds felt as crowded as a busy street.

The contradiction tightened every muscle in his body. A chill crawled up his spine.

“Something is wrong,” he blurted to Dunn.

The Nighthawk's expression did not change. Walking calmly beside him, he replied, “Ignore them.”

If Dunn said so, Klein could only endure the sensation of being followed, examined, and watched by beings he could not perceive. Step by step, they approached the house.

If this continues much longer, I'll go mad...

As Dunn raised a hand to knock, Klein quickly looked back. Flowers swayed in the evening breeze, but not a person was visible.

“Come in, gentlemen,” an ethereal female voice called from within.

Dunn turned the handle and entered.

“Daly, did you find anything?” he asked the woman seated on the sofa.

The chandelier in the living room remained dark. Two leather couches surrounded a marble coffee table.

A single candle burned upon the table, but its flame glowed cobalt blue. The strange, eerie light covered the partly open living room, dining room, and kitchen.

At the center of one sofa sat a woman in a hooded black robe, wearing blue eye shadow and rouge. A silver bracelet circled her exposed wrist, with a white crystal pendant hanging from it.

At first glance, Klein felt she looked exactly like a spirit medium ought to look.

Had she deliberately shaped herself to fit the stereotype?

Daly possessed an uncanny beauty. Her emerald eyes flicked briefly over Klein before settling on Dunn.

“The original spirits have all vanished, including Welch's and Naya's. These little rascals know nothing useful.”

Spirits? She's a Spirit Medium...

Were all those invisible things watching me spirits? Were there truly that many?

Klein removed his hat, held it against his chest, and bowed slightly. “Good evening, madam.”

Dunn sighed. “That complicates matters.

“Daly, this is Klein Moretti. See whether you can learn anything from him.”

Daly immediately returned her attention to Klein. She pointed toward an armchair.

“Please sit.”

“Thank you.”

He nodded, crossed the room, and obeyed. His heart raced beyond his control.

Whether I survive this—whether I leave safely or have every secret exposed—depends on what happens next.

Worst of all, he possessed no method of resisting. He could only trust in the mysterious difference he had displayed inside Dunn's dream.

I hate feeling this helpless.

Dunn sat on the two-person sofa opposite him. Daly reached into a pouch at her waist and removed two glass bottles, each about the size of a thumb.

Her emerald eyes smiled at Klein.

“I'll need a little assistance. You aren't an enemy, so I cannot treat you roughly. That might cause discomfort or pain and could even leave serious aftereffects. Instead, I'll use several fragrances. They'll make everything gentle and smooth, helping you relax little by little until you can surrender yourself completely to the sensation.”

That sounds very wrong...

Klein stared at her, appalled.

From the opposite sofa, Dunn laughed. “Don't be alarmed. We aren't like those fellows in the Church of Storms. Our women are permitted to tease men.

“You should understand that much. Your mother was a devout follower of the Goddess, and you and your brother attended the Church's Sunday school.”

“I understand. I simply didn't expect her to be so... so...” Klein gestured helplessly, unable to find a suitable term.

He nearly used an internet expression from Earth that implied someone was exceptionally experienced in sexual matters.

The corners of Dunn's mouth lifted. “Don't worry. Daly rarely behaves this way. She only wants you to relax. She prefers corpses to men.”

“You make me sound depraved,” Daly interjected with a smile.

She uncorked one bottle and dripped several drops into the brilliant blue flame.

“Night vanilla, slumber flower, and chamomile, distilled and blended into an aromatic essence. I call it Amantha—the Hermes word for tranquility. The scent is delightful.”

As she spoke, the candle flickered. The essence evaporated, filling the room with perfume.

An enchanting fragrance entered Klein's nose. The tension drained from him at once, replaced by the peace of gazing into a silent night.

Daly lifted the second bottle.

“This is called the Eye of the Spirit. The bark and leaves of drago and poplar trees are dried in sunlight for seven days, decocted three times, then steeped in Lanti wine. Naturally, several incantations must be spoken during the process...”

She dripped amber liquid into the cobalt flame.

An airy scent of aromatic wine spread through the room. The candle began to dance wildly. Daly's blue eye shadow and rouge gleamed strangely, and her image doubled in Klein's vision.

“It is extremely useful in mediumship—and quite intoxicating as a fragrance...”

As Daly continued speaking, her voice seemed to come from every direction.

Klein looked around in confusion. The room swayed and blurred, as though layer after layer of fog had descended over it. His body became insubstantial. He floated upward, weightless and unbalanced.

Colors bled together like paint on a canvas. Red became redder, blue deeper, and black darker, each more vivid than reality and yet hazy as a dream. Indistinct murmurs rose around him, as though hundreds or thousands of invisible people were arguing at once.

This resembles the luck-enhancement ritual—but without the madness that felt ready to split my head apart.

Klein observed his surroundings while thinking clearly.

Then his vision fixed on a pair of crystalline emerald eyes. Daly sat upon the blurred outline of the sofa in her black robe. Strangely, her gaze focused not on Klein's face but on a point above his head.

She smiled, her voice gentle.

“Allow me to introduce myself properly. I am Daly, a Spirit Medium.”

I can still think rationally, just as I could during the ritual and the gathering...

Deliberately making himself appear dazed, Klein answered, “Hello...”

“The human mind is vast,” Daly continued. “Countless secrets lie hidden within it. Consider the ocean: we know only its surface, yet beneath it stretches a far greater, unseen world. Beyond the islands lies the whole sea, and above the sea an endless sky that symbolizes the spirit world...

“You are the spirit within your body. You know not only the islands above the water, but what lies beneath them and throughout the ocean...

“Everything that exists leaves a trace. Memories visible upon the islands may be erased, but corresponding impressions remain beneath the sea and within the ocean itself...”

Her voice flowed on, seductive and compelling. The wind and shadows around Klein adopted corresponding shapes. His spirit seemed to unfold as an ocean, exposed and waiting to be searched.

Klein watched patiently. Occasionally, he allowed the surface of that ocean to “churn.” Then he answered in a drifting voice:

“No... I don't remember... I've forgotten...”

He expressed precisely the right degree of anguish.

Daly tried several more times to guide him, but Klein's clear mind remained beyond her influence.

“Very well. We will end here. You may leave.”

“Leave...”

“Leave...”

The ethereal word echoed as Daly vanished. The shadows stilled, and the fragrance of herbs and aromatic wine became distinct once more.

Colors returned to normal. The haze withdrew. Klein's body shuddered, and he felt solid ground beneath him again.

He opened eyes he did not remember closing. The blue candle still burned before him. Dunn remained comfortably seated on the opposite sofa, and Daly occupied the same place in her hooded robe.

Dunn frowned at her. “Why did you use a theory belonging to those lunatics in the Psychology Alchemists?”

Daly put away the bottles. “Because I find it convincing. At the very least, it agrees with some of the things I've encountered.”

Before Dunn could respond, she shrugged.

“This troublesome fellow left no trace at all.”

Relief flooded Klein.

Pretending confusion, he asked, “Oh, is it over? What happened? It felt as though I had only fallen asleep...”

That means I passed, doesn't it?

Thank goodness the luck-enhancement ritual gave me a rehearsal.

“Think of it that way,” Dunn said. Then he turned to Daly. “Did you examine Welch's and Naya's bodies?”

“Corpses reveal far more than you imagine. Unfortunately, Welch and Naya did take their own lives. Whatever drove them to it is frightening. It left no trace whatsoever.”

Daly stood and pointed toward the candle. “I need to rest.”

The cobalt flame went out, and blurry crimson moonlight immediately flooded the house.

“Congratulations. You may go home,” Dunn said, leading Klein toward the door. “But you must promise not to tell your family what happened here.”

Surprised, Klein asked, “Don't you need to check me for curses or traces left by evil spirits?”

“Daly didn't mention any. Therefore, no,” Dunn replied simply.

Klein relaxed. Then he remembered his earlier fears and hurriedly asked, “How can I know that the danger won't return?”

“Don't worry.” Dunn's mouth twitched. “According to statistics from similar incidents, eighty percent of survivors experience no terrifying aftereffects. That figure is based on the cases I know of—roughly speaking, more or less.”

“Which leaves one in five...” Klein had no desire to gamble on those odds.

“Then consider joining us as a civilian employee. If warning signs appear, we'll discover them quickly,” Dunn said casually as they approached the carriage. “Or you could simply become a Beyonder. We aren't nursemaids. We can't watch you all day—not even while you're with a woman.”

“Could I?” Klein immediately seized on the statement.

He expected little. Surely no one could join the Nighthawks so easily and receive Beyonder powers.

Those were Beyonder powers.

Dunn stopped and looked back at him.

“It isn't impossible. It depends.”

What?

The abrupt qualification stunned Klein. Standing beside the carriage, he stared at Dunn.

“Truly?”

Are you joking? Can becoming a Beyonder really be that simple?

Dunn laughed softly, his gray eyes hidden in the carriage's shadow.

“You don't believe me. But joining the Nighthawks costs a great deal. Your freedom, for one.

“Even setting that aside, there are other difficulties. First, you are neither clergy nor a devoted believer. You would not be permitted to choose freely, nor necessarily receive the safest option.”

He gripped the handle and climbed into the carriage.

“Second, among all the cases handled each year by us, the Mandated Punishers, the Machinery Hivemind, and the other official Beyonder organizations, one quarter are caused by Beyonders losing control.”

One quarter? Beyonders losing control?

Klein stared in shock.

Dunn turned partly toward him. His gray eyes were deep, and every trace of humor had vanished.

“And a large proportion of that quarter are our own teammates.”

Chapter 15: The Invitation

At Dunn's words, turbulent emotions surged through Klein.

“Why?” he asked instinctively.

Did Beyonder powers carry such terrible hidden dangers that even the Church's own operatives—the people responsible for confronting supernatural incidents—frequently became threats themselves?

Dunn climbed into the carriage and settled into his seat, his expression and tone unchanged.

“You don't need to know that, nor could you properly understand it unless you became one of us.”

Klein stared at him for a moment before sitting down.

Half skeptical and half joking, he asked, “If I can't understand the risks without joining, how am I supposed to decide whether to join?”

And if he refused to join, he would never learn the answer. It was a perfect deadlock.

Dunn again took out his pipe and held it beneath his nose, breathing in its scent.
