“You misunderstand. Our civilian employees are also considered members of the team.”

Klein reorganized the explanation in his mind.

“So if I join as a civilian employee, I'll be permitted to learn the relevant secrets—the dangers Beyonders face and the hidden risks of becoming one. Then I can decide later whether I wish to pursue those powers myself?”

Dunn smiled. “Essentially. With one correction: wishing to become a Beyonder does not mean you will be allowed to do so. Every Church controls that decision very strictly.”

It would be stranger if they didn't.

Klein leaned forward, his tone and gestures growing more emphatic. “What about civilian employees? Surely you're equally strict in selecting them.”

“In your case, there should be no difficulty.” Dunn's eyelids were half closed, his face relaxed as he inhaled the pipe's fragrance without lighting it.

“Why?” Klein asked, baffled once more.

At the same time, he joked inwardly:

Could my uniqueness as a transmigrator shine as brightly as a firefly in the night, impossible to overlook?

Dunn opened his eyes. Their gray depths remained as tranquil as ever.

“First, you survived an incident of this kind without our assistance. That suggests you possess qualities most people lack. Luck, for example. Fortunate people are generally welcome.”

When Klein's expression went blank, Dunn smiled faintly.

“Consider that a joke. The second reason is that you graduated from Khoy University's History Department, and we urgently need someone with your training.

“Leumi, a believer in the Lord of Storms, holds opinions about women that I find repulsive. But his observations on society, culture, economics, and politics are often incisive. He once said that talented people are the foundation of continued growth and competitive advantage. On that point, I agree with him completely.”

Noticing Klein's slight frown, Dunn explained in a casual tone:

“We frequently encounter documents and artifacts from the Fourth Epoch or earlier. Cults and heretical groups often attempt to obtain power from such objects. Sometimes the objects themselves cause bizarre and horrifying events.

“Aside from Beyonders in certain specialized paths, most of us were never accomplished scholars—or left our school years far behind.”

Dunn pointed toward his own head, one corner of his mouth lifting in self-mockery.

“Dry academic material puts us to sleep. Not even a Sleepless can resist it. In the past, we consulted historians and archaeologists, but doing so risked exposing confidential information. It could also draw innocent professors and associate professors into dangerous incidents. That makes it difficult to refuse an opportunity to add a trained historian directly to our team.”

Klein nodded, accepting the explanation. His thoughts remained scattered, however, and another question occurred to him.

“Then why not train one yourselves?”

“That brings us to the third reason—the final and most important one,” Dunn said. “You've already experienced a supernatural incident. Inviting you therefore does not violate our confidentiality rules.

“If I approached an uninvolved person and the recruitment failed, I would be held responsible for exposing our existence. Most Nighthawks, including civilian staff, are recruited from within the Church.”

Klein listened silently, then asked with genuine curiosity, “Why is secrecy so important? Wouldn't informing the public make people more cautious and reduce the chance of similar mistakes? The greatest fear is fear of the unknown. We could make the unknown familiar.”

Dunn shook his head. “Human stupidity exceeds your imagination. Public knowledge would inspire imitation, producing more chaos and even worse disasters.”

Understanding dawned on Klein.

“The only lesson humanity learns from history is that humanity learns nothing from history—and forever repeats the same tragedies.”

“That famous observation of Emperor Roselle's is indeed deeply philosophical,” Dunn agreed.

Emperor Roselle said that too? That predecessor left nothing for those who came after him to show off with...

Klein found himself unable to continue the conversation.

Dunn turned toward the carriage window. Dim yellow streetlamps intertwined outside, displaying the splendor of civilization against the night.

“A similar saying circulates within the official organizations of the great Churches. It may be the principal reason ordinary people are forbidden from learning the truth.”

“What saying?” Klein asked, delighted at the prospect of another secret.

Dunn looked back at him. His facial muscles shifted so slightly that the movement was almost invisible.

“Faith and fear bring trouble. Greater faith and greater fear bring greater trouble, until everything is destroyed.”

He sighed.

“Other than praying for the gods' blessing and aid, humanity has no way to resolve its greatest problems.”

Faith and fear bring trouble. Greater faith and greater fear bring greater trouble...

Klein repeated the words silently but could not fully grasp them.

His uncertainty gave rise to a new fear of the unknown. It resembled the shadows cast by the streetlamps outside, where darkness seemed to conceal cold eyes and gaping mouths.

The horse maintained a swift, steady pace. As the carriage wheels carried them toward Iron Cross Street, Dunn abruptly broke the silence with a formal question.

“Would you like to join us as a civilian employee?”

Countless considerations arose in Klein's mind, leaving him unable to decide.

“May I have some time to consider it?”

The decision had too many consequences to be made rashly.

“Of course. Give me your answer before Sunday,” Dunn said with a nod. “And remember: everything you've learned is confidential. You must not discuss Welch's case with anyone, including your brother and sister. Violating that rule would endanger them and might bring you before a special tribunal.”

“Understood,” Klein replied solemnly.

Silence settled over the carriage once again.

When they reached Iron Cross Street and his apartment drew near, another question occurred to Klein. He hesitated for several seconds.

“Mr. Smith, what salary and benefits does a civilian employee receive?”

It was a very serious question.

Dunn looked briefly surprised, then smiled.

“You needn't worry about that. Our funding comes jointly from the Church and the police department. A newly registered civilian employee earns two pounds and ten soli a week, plus ten soli in compensation for danger and confidentiality. That makes three pounds in total—hardly worse than the salary of a fully appointed university lecturer.

“Your pay then rises with experience and contributions.

“Civilian contracts ordinarily last five years. After that, you may resign if you no longer wish to remain, though you must sign a lifelong confidentiality agreement. You may not leave Tingen without permission. If you later move to another city, you must first register with the local Nighthawk team.

“There are no regular weekends. We work in shifts, with three civilian employees on duty at all times. If you wish to vacation in the south or at Desi Bay, you'll need to coordinate with your colleagues.”

As Dunn finished, the carriage stopped beside the apartment building where Klein lived with his siblings.

“I understand.” Klein turned and climbed down.

Standing beside the carriage, he added, “Mr. Smith, where can I find you once I've decided?”

Dunn gave a low chuckle.

“Go to the Hound Pub on Besik Street and speak to the owner, Wright. Tell him you wish to hire a small mercenary team for a job.”

“What?” Klein stared in confusion.

“Our base is also confidential. Until you agree to join us, I cannot give you its location directly.” Dunn smiled. “Very well, Mr. Klein Moretti. I wish you another pleasant dream tonight.”

Klein removed his hat in farewell and watched the carriage gather speed as it departed.

He took out his pocket watch.

Click.

It was only a little after four in the morning. A cool, gentle breeze moved through the street, illuminated by the dim yellow glow of the gas lamps.

Klein drew a deep breath and absorbed the silence around him.

The district that was busiest and loudest by day now seemed lifeless. Yet even this quiet felt utterly different from the unseen observation and spirit medium's ritual at Welch's residence.

Only then did Klein notice that the back of his linen shirt was drenched in sweat, cold and clammy against his skin.

Chapter 16: Rat-Baiting with Dogs

Phew. I finally made it through the spirit medium's examination.

Klein released a long breath. Turning slowly, he savored the cool breeze and the stillness of the night as he approached the apartment building.

He took out his keys, selected the correct one, and turned it gently. The door creaked open, revealing darkness faintly tinged with crimson.

As he climbed the empty staircase through the cool air, a peculiar but exhilarating feeling came over him. It was as though the night had granted him several hours unavailable to everyone else. His steps unconsciously quickened.

Still caught in that mood, he unlocked the apartment. Before he crossed the threshold, he saw a figure sitting silently at his desk.

Reddish-black hair, bright brown eyes, and a delicate, pretty face—it could only be Melissa Moretti.

“Klein, where did you go?” Her knitted brows relaxed slightly.

Before he could answer, she continued, “I woke up to use the bathroom and discovered you were gone.”

Her expression made it clear she intended to understand the entire matter, from its cause and consequences to every step in between.

Drawing upon years of experience lying to his parents, Klein thought quickly. Then he offered a rueful smile and answered with practiced calm.

“I couldn't fall asleep after waking, so rather than waste the time, I went out for a run. Look how much I sweated.”

He removed his coat, turned partly around, and pointed to his back.

Melissa rose and gave it a cursory glance. After several moments of thought, she said, “Honestly, Klein, you don't need to put so much pressure on yourself. I'm certain you'll pass the Tingen University interview. And even if you don't—if, I mean—you can still find another good position.”

I haven't thought about the interview at all...

Klein nodded. “I understand.”

He did not mention Dunn's offer. He had yet to decide whether he would accept it.

Melissa studied him intently, then abruptly turned and hurried farther into the apartment. She returned carrying an object that vaguely resembled a tortoise, assembled from gears, rusty metal, torsion springs, ordinary springs, and other scraps.

After quickly winding the mechanism, she placed it on the desk.

Click! Click! Click!

Thump! Thump! Thump!

The mechanical creature advanced in a series of rhythmic hops, irresistibly drawing the eye.

“Whenever I'm upset, watching it move makes me feel better,” Melissa said, her eyes brightening. “I've used it often lately, and it really works. You should try it.”

Klein did not reject his sister's kindness. He walked over and watched until the device stopped.

“Simple, regular motion really can be relaxing.”

Before Melissa could respond, he pointed toward it and asked casually, “Did you build this yourself? When? How did I not know about it?”

“I used discarded material from school and pieces I found in the street. I finished it two days ago,” Melissa replied in her usual even tone, though the corners of her mouth rose a little higher.

“That's impressive,” Klein said sincerely.

As a boy, Zhou Mingrui had struggled even to assemble a four-wheeled toy car. Mechanical skill was not among his gifts.

Melissa raised her chin slightly, her eyes curving with pleasure. “It's adequate.”

“Excessive modesty isn't a virtue.” Klein smiled, then added, “It is a tortoise, isn't it?”

The warmth vanished from the room. A grave silence lingered before Melissa answered in a voice as obscure as the crimson veil outside.

“It's a puppet.”

A puppet...

Klein smiled awkwardly. “The materials are to blame. They're rather crude.”

He hurried to change the subject.

“Why did you need the bathroom in the middle of the night? Don't you normally sleep until morning?”

Melissa froze.

Several seconds passed before she opened her mouth to answer.

At that moment, a violent rumble came from somewhere between her chest and abdomen.

“I-I'm going back to sleep!”

She snatched up her tortoise-like “puppet,” hurried into the adjoining room, and slammed the door.

Last night's dinner was too rich. She ate too much, and now she can't digest it properly...

Klein shook his head with a smile and walked toward the desk. He sat without a sound and began considering Dunn Smith's invitation while the crimson moon emerged from behind dark clouds.

Joining the Nighthawks as a civilian employee had obvious disadvantages.

As a transmigrator, The Fool, and the creator of the mysterious gathering, I carry too many secrets. Working beneath the noses of the Church of Evernight's specialists in supernatural affairs would be extremely dangerous.

If I join Dunn's team, I'll inevitably seek to become a Beyonder so I can disguise the benefits I receive from the gathering.

But full membership would impose even more restrictions on my freedom. Even civilian employees must report before leaving Tingen. I would no longer be able to travel or act as I pleased, and I might lose valuable opportunities.

The Nighthawks are a disciplined organization. Once assigned a mission, I would have to obey their arrangements. Refusal wouldn't be an option.

And every Beyonder risks losing control.

After listing the drawbacks one by one, Klein considered the necessities and advantages.

Judging by the luck-enhancement ritual, I am not among the fortunate eighty percent. More bizarre events will certainly find me, and the danger will only increase.

Only by becoming a Beyonder—or at least joining the Nighthawks—can I gain the ability to resist.

I can't rely entirely on the gathering to become a Beyonder. Obtaining a formula might be manageable, but where would I find the necessary ingredients? How would I acquire and prepare them?

Even ordinary training and practical knowledge present serious obstacles.

I can't consult Justice and The Hanged Man about every minor question or trade with them for every object. That would damage The Fool's image, invite suspicion, and consume far too much of our limited meeting time.

Besides, I currently have nothing they would find valuable.

Repeatedly obtaining materials would also leave clues to my identity. An argument conducted at a safe distance could quickly become a confrontation in the real world.

Joining the Nighthawks would give me access to basic mystical knowledge and legitimate channels for obtaining what I need. I could build experience, contacts, and resources, then use them as leverage within the gathering. The knowledge gained from Justice and The Hanged Man could in turn benefit my real life, creating a cycle in which each side strengthened the other.

There was another possibility: he could seek out an organization suppressed by the Churches, such as the Psychology Alchemists Dunn had mentioned.

But he would still sacrifice freedom, while living in constant fear. More importantly, he had no idea how to find them. Even if The Hanged Man provided a lead, approaching such an organization recklessly might cost Klein his life.

A civilian position with the Nighthawks offered both a buffer and a possible exit.

The lesser recluse hides in the wilderness; the greater recluse hides in the crowd. Perhaps a Nighthawk identity would be the finest disguise of all.

If I eventually rise to a position of authority within the official organizations, who would ever suspect me of being a heretic—and the hidden leader of a secret society?

The first rays of morning appeared. Crimson moonlight faded before gold along the eastern horizon.

Watching the dawn, Klein made his decision.

Today, he would find Dunn Smith and join the Nighthawks as a civilian employee.

At that moment, Melissa emerged from her room again. She looked surprised to find her brother stretching inelegantly.

“You didn't sleep?”

“I had some things to think through.” Klein smiled, feeling lighter.

Melissa considered this. “Whenever I face a difficult problem, I list the advantages and disadvantages separately and compare them. Usually, that tells me what I should do.”

“A good habit. That's exactly what I did,” Klein replied.

Her expression relaxed. Without adding anything else, she gathered a yellowed sheet of paper and her toiletries and left for the bathroom.

After breakfast and Melissa's departure, Klein did not immediately leave. He slept for several hours, knowing that almost no tavern would be open in the morning.

At two in the afternoon, he used a small brush to remove dust and smooth the creases from his silk top hat and handkerchief. Then he dressed in his formal suit as though preparing for an interview.

Besik Street was some distance away. Afraid of missing the Nighthawks' “business hours,” he decided against walking and waited on Iron Cross Street for public transportation.

The Loen Kingdom had two kinds of public horse-drawn carriage: tracked and untracked.

An untracked carriage was drawn by two horses and seated about twenty passengers, including those on its roof. It followed a general route but had no fixed stops, so passengers could hail it anywhere as long as room remained.

Tracked carriages were operated by the Orbital Carriage Company. Rails laid along the main streets guided their wheels while horses traveled between them. The reduced resistance allowed the animals to pull larger, double-decker vehicles carrying nearly fifty passengers.

Their disadvantage was that both routes and stops were fixed, leaving many parts of the city inaccessible.

After ten minutes, the clatter of wheels against rails approached. A double-decker horsecar stopped at the Iron Cross Street station.

“Besik Street,” Klein told the driver.

“You'll need to change at Champagne Street. From there, Besik Street is about a ten-minute walk,” the driver explained.

“Champagne Street, then.” Klein nodded.

“More than four kilometers. Four pence.” A clean-faced young conductor held out his hand.

“All right.” Klein took four copper coins from his pocket and paid.

He climbed aboard and found the carriage sparsely occupied. Even on the lower level, several seats remained empty.

Only three pence left. I'll have to walk home.

Holding down his hat, he settled into a seat.

Most of the men and women around him were neatly dressed, though a few wore work clothes. Several passengers read newspapers. Almost no one spoke, and the carriage remained quiet.

Klein closed his eyes to rest, ignoring the flow of people on and off.

Stop after stop passed until he finally heard the conductor announce Champagne Street.

After disembarking, Klein asked for directions and soon found Besik Street. A tavern marked by a yellow-brown hound stood ahead.

He pushed hard against the heavy door. It gradually opened, releasing a wave of noise and oppressive heat.

Despite the early hour, the tavern was already crowded. Some patrons were day laborers waiting to be hired. Others had nothing to do and sought numbness in drink.

The room was dim. At its center stood two large iron cages, the lower third of each sunk into the floor so that no gap remained beneath it.

Men carrying wooden mugs crowded around them, laughing, arguing, and shouting curses.

Curious, Klein looked more closely. Each cage contained a dog. One was black and white and resembled a husky from Earth. The other was entirely black, with glossy fur and a healthy, ferocious appearance.

“Want to place a bet?” A short man in a brown beret approached and pointed toward the black dog. “Doug has won eight matches in a row!”

Bet?

Klein stared for a moment before understanding. “Dogfighting?”

At Khoy University, aristocratic and wealthy students had sometimes asked him—with equal parts contempt and curiosity—whether crude laborers and unemployed hooligans spent their time boxing and gambling in taverns.

Besides boxing and cards, such establishments were known for bloody spectacles like cockfighting and dogfighting.

The short man smirked. “Sir, we are civilized men. We would never participate in anything so vulgar.”

Then he lowered his voice. “Besides, those activities were outlawed last year.”

“Then what are you betting on?” Klein asked.

“Which is the better hunter.”

The moment he finished, a roar rose from the crowd.

The short man spun toward the cages and waved excitedly. “Too late for this round. Place your bet on the next!”

Klein stood on tiptoe and craned his neck.

Two muscular men approached the cages, each dragging a sack. They opened the doors and emptied the contents inside.

Gray, revolting creatures poured out.

Klein squinted before recognizing them.

Rats. Hundreds of rats.

Because the cage walls extended below floor level without gaps, the animals scattered in every direction but found no escape.

As soon as the doors closed, the dogs were released.

“Woof!”

The black dog lunged and killed a rat with a single bite.

The black-and-white dog hesitated, then began bounding excitedly among the rodents as though playing a game.

Around the cages, men raised their mugs, leaned forward, and roared:

“Bite it! Kill it!”

“Doug! Doug!”

Bloody hell. They're baiting dogs with rats.

Klein finally understood, and the corners of his mouth twitched.

They were betting on which dog would kill more rats. Perhaps people even wagered on the exact number.

No wonder someone was buying live rats on Iron Cross Street.

How very... distinctive.

Shaking his head with an incredulous laugh, Klein retreated from the cages. He edged around the drinking spectators until he reached the bar.

“First time here?” The bartender glanced at him while polishing a mug. “Rye beer is one penny. Enmat beer, two. Southville beer, four. Or would you prefer a glass of pure malt Lanti?”

“I'm looking for Mr. Wright,” Klein said directly.

The bartender whistled and called toward the side. “Old man! Someone wants you.”

“Hmm? Who...”

An indistinct voice answered, and an intoxicated old man rose from behind the bar. He rubbed his eyes and focused on Klein.

“You looking for me, lad?”

“Mr. Wright, I wish to hire a small mercenary team for a job,” Klein recited as Dunn had instructed.

“A mercenary team?” the bartender interrupted with a laugh. “Do you think you're living in an adventure novel? Those haven't existed for years.”

Wright remained silent for several seconds. “Who directed you here?”

“Dunn Smith,” Klein answered honestly.

Wright immediately chuckled. “I see. The small mercenary team does still exist, though under another form and a more modern name. You'll find it on the second floor of Number Thirty-Six, Zouteland Street.”

“Thank you.”

Klein expressed his sincere gratitude, turned, and squeezed back through the crowd.

Before he reached the exit, the tavern suddenly fell quiet. Murmurs spread among the patrons.

“Doug actually lost...”

“He lost...”

Klein smiled, shook his head, and left quickly. After asking for directions, he soon reached nearby Zouteland Street.

“Thirty... thirty-two... thirty-four... Here.”

He entered the stairwell at Number Thirty-Six.

Rounding the corner and slowly climbing to the second floor, he found a vertical sign bearing the modern name of the supposed mercenary team:

BLACKTHORN SECURITY COMPANY

Translator's note: Rat-baiting was a real blood sport practiced in Victorian Britain, not an invention of the author.

Chapter 17: Special Operations Department

BLACKTHORN SECURITY COMPANY

The sign surprised Klein, yet somehow seemed entirely appropriate.

What can one even say about this?

He shook his head with a laugh, climbed the remaining steps, and gently knocked on the half-closed door.

Knock! Knock! Knock!

The sound echoed in a slow rhythm, but no one answered. He heard only a faint sputtering noise from inside.

Knock! Knock! Knock!

Klein tried again with the same result.

He finally pushed the door wider and peered through the opening. A traditional sofa, presumably for receiving guests, occupied one side of the room, accompanied by a softly upholstered armchair and a sturdy wooden coffee table. Directly opposite the entrance stood a desk. Behind it, a brown-haired young woman sat with her head drooping.

Even if the security company is only a disguise, this is impressively unprofessional. How long has this place been without customers? Then again, they don't actually need customers...

Complaining silently, Klein approached and knocked on the desktop beside the young woman's ear.

Knock! Knock!

She jerked upright and snatched up the newspaper in front of her, using it to hide her face.

The Tingen City Honest Paper... An excellent name.

Klein silently read the masthead facing him.

“The Soaring Express, providing direct steam-train service to Constant City, begins operations today...” the woman read with elaborate concentration. “Oh, honestly, when will they build a direct line to Brindisi Bay? I never want to make that voyage by ship again. It was dreadful—truly dreadful...”

She gradually lowered the paper, revealing a broad forehead and light-brown eyes. Her ingratiating expression turned to surprise.

“Who are you?”

“Good afternoon. I'm Klein Moretti. Dunn Smith invited me.” He removed his hat, held it to his chest, and bowed slightly.

The brown-haired woman appeared to be in her early twenties. She wore a pale-green dress in the Loen style, trimmed with delicate lace at the sleeves, collar, and bodice.

“The Captain? All right, wait here. I'll fetch him.”

She sprang from her chair and hurried through a side door.

She didn't even offer me water. Their understanding of customer service is deeply concerning...

Klein smiled faintly and waited.

Two or three minutes later, the woman returned.

“Mr. Moretti, please follow me. The Captain is guarding the Chanis Gate and cannot leave his post.”

“Of course.”

Klein followed quickly, turning the unfamiliar name over in his mind.

What is a Chanis Gate?

Beyond the partition lay a short corridor with three offices on either side. Some doors were locked, while others stood open, revealing clerks working steadily at heavy mechanical typewriters.

Klein immediately recognized one person: the young officer who had searched his apartment, with black hair, green eyes, and the romantic air of a poet.

He was out of uniform. His white shirt hung untucked, giving him a distinctly unruly appearance.

Perhaps he really is a poet.

Klein nodded in greeting and received a smile in return.

At the corridor's end, the brown-haired woman opened the door to the office on the left and pointed inside.

“We still have several levels to descend.”

There was no furniture in the room, only a gray-white stone staircase leading downward.

Gas lamps lined both walls, their steady glow dispersing the darkness and lending the passage an unexpected warmth.

The woman went first, watching her feet and stepping carefully.

“I use these stairs all the time, but I'm still afraid I'll fall and tumble down like a barrel. You wouldn't believe what Leonard did. On his first day as a Sleepless—before he had learned to control his new abilities—he tried to run down the stairs and turned himself into a cartwheel. It was hilarious. Leonard is the man who greeted you just now. That happened about three years ago.

“Come to think of it, I've worked with the Nighthawks for five years. I was only seventeen when I joined...”

She continued talking while carefully watching every step. Suddenly, she slapped a hand to her forehead.

“I forgot to introduce myself. I'm Rozanne. My father was a Nighthawk. He sacrificed himself during an incident five years ago. I suppose we'll be colleagues now. Yes, colleagues—that's the proper word. We aren't teammates because neither of us is a Beyonder.”

“I hope I'll have the honor, but the decision still belongs to Mr. Smith.”

Klein examined the enclosed passage. They were clearly descending underground. Dampness seeped from the stone walls, driving away the summer heat.

“Don't worry. If you came this far, the Captain has already agreed,” Rozanne said in her sweet, rapid voice. “I've always been a little afraid of him, even though he's kind—almost fatherly. I can't explain it, but he still frightens me.”

Klein replied dryly, “Isn't being afraid of one's father perfectly normal?”

“True.” Rozanne held the wall as they rounded a bend.

The stairs ended at a stone-paved corridor. Gas lamps enclosed in metal grilles lined both walls, stretching their shadows along the floor.

Klein noticed a Dark Sacred Emblem every few meters: the symbol of the Evernight Goddess. Against a deep-black background, glittering points gathered around exactly half a crimson moon.

The emblems did not appear extraordinary, yet walking among them filled Klein with peace. Even the talkative Rozanne fell silent.

Before long, they reached an intersection.

Rozanne briefly explained the layout. “The passage on the left leads to Saint Selena Cathedral. On the right are the armory, storeroom, and archives. Straight ahead is the Chanis Gate.”

Saint Selena Cathedral? Then Zouteland Street must run directly behind Red Moonlight Street.

Klein was momentarily surprised.

Saint Selena Cathedral on Red Moonlight Street was the Tingen headquarters of the Church of the Evernight Goddess, a sacred destination for believers throughout the region. Together with the Church of the God of Steam and Machinery's Holy Numerics Cathedral in the suburbs and the Church of Storms' River and Sea Cathedral in northern Tingen, it formed the foundation of religious life for the city and its surrounding towns and villages.

Aware that his present status did not permit too many questions, Klein listened without interrupting.

They crossed the intersection and continued straight. Less than a minute later, a massive double gate of black iron appeared before them, carved with seven sacred emblems.

Heavy, cold, and imposing, it stood in the darkness like a silent giant.

“The Chanis Gate,” Rozanne said. She pointed to a room beside it. “The Captain is inside. You should go in alone.”

“Thank you.”

The indicated room stood directly before the gate. Its windows were open, revealing a brightly lit interior.

Klein drew a breath and calmed himself before knocking.

Knock! Knock! Knock!

“Come in.” Dunn's deep, amiable voice answered.

Klein gently opened the unlocked door. The room contained only a table and four chairs. Dunn Smith sat reading a newspaper at his leisure. He wore the same black coat as the previous night, with a gold watch chain crossing his chest; the lamplight emphasized his receding hairline.

“Sit down.” Dunn lowered the paper and smiled. “Have you decided? Are you certain you wish to join us?”

Klein removed his hat and bowed before taking a seat at the table.

“Yes. I'm certain.”

“Then read this deed—or contract, as people prefer to call it nowadays.”

Dunn opened a drawer and removed two copies.

The document contained few clauses, most of which Dunn had already described. It placed particular emphasis on confidentiality. Violations would be tried by the Church of Evernight's tribunal rather than an ordinary court, much as soldiers and officers were subject to court-martial.

A five-year contract. Two pounds and ten soli in weekly wages, plus ten soli for risk and confidentiality...

Klein read the entire document before answering firmly, “I have no objections.”

“Then sign.” Dunn pointed toward a dark-red fountain pen and ink.

Klein tested the pen on a scrap of paper. After drawing a breath, he signed both copies:

Klein Moretti.

Because he possessed no personal seal, he added his thumbprint.

Dunn accepted the documents, took a stamp from the drawer, and applied it at the end of each contract and across several important sections.

When he finished, he stood. With one hand he returned Klein's copy; with the other, he offered a handshake.

“Welcome. From this moment onward, you are one of us. And remember—the contract itself is confidential.”

Klein rose, accepted the document, and shook Dunn's hand.

“Then should I address you as Captain?”

“Yes.” Dunn's gray eyes appeared especially deep in the dim room.

They sat again. Klein glanced at the stamp on his contract.

NIGHTHAWK TEAM, TINGEN CITY, AWWA COUNTY, LOEN KINGDOM

He laughed. “I still can't believe you use Blackthorn Security Company as your disguise.”

“We have other identities as well.” Dunn removed another document from the drawer.

It bore official stamps from the municipal government and police department. Its heading read:

SEVENTH UNIT, SPECIAL OPERATIONS DEPARTMENT

AWWA COUNTY POLICE, LOEN KINGDOM

“The first four units are conventional police formations responsible for ordinary security—the VIP Protection Unit and Key Installations Protection Unit, for example,” Dunn explained. “Beginning with Unit Five, each city's remaining units handle supernatural incidents.

“Our unit responds to incidents involving followers of the Evernight Goddess in Tingen. When believers of different Churches are involved, jurisdiction is divided geographically. We are primarily responsible for the northern and western districts, along with the Golden Indus area.

“Unit Six, the Mandated Punishers of the Church of Storms, oversees the harbor, eastern, and southern districts. The university district and suburbs belong to Unit Five—the Tingen Machinery Hivemind of the Church of the God of Steam and Machinery.”

“I see.” Klein found the arrangement sensible.

Then he smiled. “What happens if someone sees the Blackthorn Security Company sign and genuinely tries to hire us?”

“We accept the work. Why wouldn't we?” Dunn replied with slow humor. “As long as it doesn't interfere with our duties, the payment becomes an additional bonus. Most members are happy to take such jobs. Private detectives have already monopolized trivial nuisances like finding lost cats and dogs, in any case.”

“How many people belong to the Tingen Nighthawks?” Klein asked.

“Supernatural incidents are uncommon, and Beyonders rarer still. Including me, Tingen has six full Nighthawk members. With your addition, we also have six civilian employees.”

Klein nodded. At last, he asked the question that concerned him most.

“Captain, what exactly does it mean for a Beyonder to lose control? And why does it happen?”

Chapter 18: Origins and Causes

At Klein's question, Dunn glanced through the window toward the corridor leading to the Chanis Gate. He took out his pipe, packed it with tobacco and mint leaves, and held it beneath his nose. After drawing in the fragrance, he spoke in a distant, reflective voice.

“Only here at home can I enjoy tobacco mixed with mint without concern. Klein, are you familiar with the creation myth?”

“Of course. Sunday school used The Revelation of Evernight to teach us to read. Its Book of Wisdom and Letters of the Saints both discuss creation.”

Klein searched the original owner's fragmented memories and deliberately slowed his speech.

“The Creator awoke within Chaos and shattered the darkness, bringing forth the first ray of light. He then dissolved Himself into the universe and became all that exists. His body formed the land and stars. One eye became the sun and the other the crimson moon. His blood flowed into the seas and rivers, nourishing life...”

Klein trailed off, partly because the remaining memories were blurred and partly because the account resembled the Chinese myth of Pangu to an astonishing degree.

People in different worlds apparently imagined remarkably similar myths.

Noticing his difficulty, Dunn smiled and continued.

“His lungs became the elves. His heart, the giants. His liver became the treants; His brain, the dragons; His kidneys, the feathered serpents; His hair, the phoenixes; His ears, the demonic wolves; and His mouth and teeth, the mutants. His remaining bodily fluids became sea monsters, whose purest descendants were the Naga.

“His stomach, intestines, and all the corruption within His body became devils, evil spirits, and countless nameless, malevolent beings. His spirit became the Eternal Blazing Sun, the Lord of Storms, and the God of Knowledge and Wisdom...”

“And His wisdom gave birth to humanity,” Klein finished. “Thus began the First Epoch, the Chaos Epoch.”

He found the whole account both amusing and absurd.

As an armchair folklorist, he had never encountered a creation myth organized in such extraordinary detail. Nearly every important species corresponded to a particular organ of the Creator.

It sounds almost like a children's rhyme—everyone sitting in a row and receiving a piece of fruit.

More surprisingly, the story did not belong solely to the Church of Evernight. The Churches of the Lord of Storms and the God of Steam and Machinery taught similar versions. None elevated its own deity by belittling the others.

Either the creation myth was substantially true, or the Churches had fought for a very long time before reaching some compromise prior to the Fifth Epoch.

The thought raised another question.

Klein frowned. “Something seems wrong. Why were the Eternal Blazing Sun, the Lord of Storms, and the God of Knowledge and Wisdom born directly from the Creator's spirit, while the Goddess was not?”

According to the prehistoric chapters of The Revelation of Evernight, the Evernight Goddess had not awakened until the end of the Second Epoch. Together with the Lord of Storms, the Eternal Blazing Sun, and the other gods, She then protected humanity through the Cataclysm.

That period became the Third Epoch, also called the Cataclysm Epoch.

The Earth Mother and God of Combat likewise appeared during that age. The God of Steam and Machinery—then known as the God of Craftsmanship—was born only in the Fourth Epoch.

The implication regarding divine status seemed obvious: greater antiquity meant greater legitimacy.

This question had long troubled believers of the Evernight Goddess.

Instead of answering, Dunn shifted the pipe to his other hand.

“Recite the Goddess's full title.”

Klein immediately felt as though he had stabbed himself. He ransacked the damaged memories, struggling to retrieve the words.

“The Evernight Goddess stands higher than the cosmos and is more eternal than eternity. She is the Lady of Crimson, the Mother of Concealment, the Empress of Misfortune and Horror, and the Mistress of Repose and Silence.”

Fortunately, Klein's mother had been a devout believer. While alive, she recited the title every evening before dinner. Even the fragmented memories retained most of it.

“And what does Lady of Crimson signify?” Dunn prompted.

“The red moon.”

The moment Klein answered, he understood.

Dunn smiled. “And from which part of the Creator did the crimson moon originate?”

“One of His eyes.”

The two exchanged smiles.

That origin was no less impressive than being born from one third of the Creator's spirit.

The Churches of the Earth Mother and the God of Combat presumably offered similar explanations. The God of Steam and Machinery had appeared too late for any such claim, which might explain why His Church remained comparatively weak for more than a thousand years. Only after the invention of the steam engine had it seized the opportunity to stand as an equal among the others.

Dunn gently stroked his pipe.

“Humanity was born from the Creator's wisdom. That gave us extraordinary minds but none of the other species' supernatural gifts. From the creation myth, however, our ancestors derived a simple and important conclusion: everything shares the same origin.”

“Everything shares the same origin...” Klein repeated.

“Using that principle, humans under the gods' protection gradually discovered a way to resist giants, devils, mutants, and other extraordinary creatures. They combined the corresponding parts of evil spirits, dragons, monsters, magical plants, flowers, or crystals with supplementary materials to produce potions. By drinking and assimilating those potions, humans could acquire supernatural powers. This is basic mystical knowledge.”

Dunn kept the explanation brief.

“Through painful trial and error, our ancestors discovered that directly consuming powerful or unusual potions almost always ended in tragedy. There were three common outcomes.”

“What were they?” Klein asked eagerly.

“First, the mind died and the body disintegrated, with every piece of flesh transforming into a horrific monster. Second, the power within the potion rewrote the drinker's personality, making them cold, oversensitive, irritable, cruel, or indifferent. Third...”

Dunn set down his pipe, lifted a porcelain cup, and took a sip.

“Fermo coffee from the Paz River Valley is bitter but wonderfully aromatic, with an excellent finish. Would you like some?”

“I prefer coffee from the Feynapotter Plateau, though I've only tasted it a few times at Welch's home.” Klein declined politely. “What is the third outcome?”

“Immediate insanity. The drinker becomes more demonic than a devil. That is what we call losing control.” Dunn emphasized the final words.

Before Klein could respond, he set down his cup and continued.

“After centuries of experimentation and the appearance of the Blasphemy Slate, humanity finally perfected the potion system. Stable progression paths were organized into a hierarchy called Sequences. The lower the Sequence number, the higher the potion's level.

“Each of the seven great Churches currently controls at least one complete Sequence pathway. They have also collected incomplete pathways during the past several centuries—or millennia.”

“The Blasphemy Slate?” Klein immediately noticed the term.

The Hanged Man had mentioned it during the gathering as the critical factor in the establishment and completion of the potion system.

That seemed to conflict with Dunn's account.

“Something created by evil gods,” Dunn answered vaguely. “I don't know when it appeared, precisely what it contains, or what makes it unique. If you ever uncover a clue connected to it, report to me immediately. Such information demands the highest possible response.

“I have described one way a person may lose control. Now I'll explain the other four.”

“Please.” Klein put the Blasphemy Slate aside for the moment and listened intently.

“Human beings generally possess intelligence but no supernatural power. Yet there are exceptions—a fortunate few, or perhaps unfortunate ones, born with unusually strong perception. More precisely, they possess natural spiritual perception.

“They hear voices others cannot hear and see things others cannot see. In a sense, they already possess part of a Beyonder's nature.”

As Dunn spoke, he looked into the apparently empty air beside them. Klein shuddered.

“You might call such a person half a Sequence 9 Beyonder with fixed characteristics. Sequence 9 is the lowest level in a pathway. Because the person's nature is already fixed, they can choose only the corresponding Sequence. If they consume a potion from another pathway, the consequences may range from mental illness to complete loss of control—or death.”

“I understand.” Klein nodded slowly.

“The third cause resembles the second. Once you choose a Sequence pathway, you are committed to it. There is no room for regret.

“If you later consume a potion of the corresponding level from another pathway, you may acquire a mixture of strange and distorted abilities. But you will almost certainly remain partly insane—oversensitive and irritable, cruel and bloodthirsty, or silent and depressive.

“And you have only that single chance. Afterward, whether you drink from your original pathway or the new one, the result will be loss of control. Your mind may die; your body may collapse into monsters; or you may transform directly into an evil spirit.”

Dunn lifted his coffee and took another sip.

Alarmed by the description, Klein remained silent for several seconds.

“And the fourth?”

“The fourth is extremely common.” Dunn gave a quiet chuckle. “When we consume potions and appropriate powers that naturally belong to extraordinary beings, we undergo an unnatural transformation. Remnants of the ingredients' spirituality inevitably affect us.

“The symptoms may not immediately manifest and can remain undetectable to others, but those traces persist within the mind. If a Beyonder consumes the next potion before mastering the abilities granted by the previous one and eliminating its subtle influence, the madness accumulates, greatly increasing the likelihood of losing control.”

Dunn suddenly fell silent.

After a brief pause, he sighed.

“Under Nighthawk regulations, even a member who has made exceptional contributions cannot be promoted unless at least three years have passed since their last potion, followed by a complete examination. Even with those precautions, many still lose control each year.”

How terrifying...

Klein drew a quiet breath. “What is the final cause?”

Dunn's mouth curved, but there was no smile in his eyes.

“The fifth is the most common of all. Every Beyonder possesses enhanced spiritual perception. The higher the Sequence, the stronger it becomes. They hear what ordinary people cannot hear, see what others cannot see, and encounter things no one else would ever meet.

“They live amid mysterious temptations and illusory seduction. Once overstimulated—or driven by greed and desire—they gradually travel the road toward losing control.”

Dunn looked directly at Klein. His gray pupils reflected Klein's figure.

His voice turned bleak.

“Archbishop Chanis, founder of the Nighthawks, once said:

“‘We are guardians, but we are also a group of miserable wretches constantly fighting against danger and madness.’”

Chapter 19: Sealed Artifacts

“We are guardians, but we are also a group of miserable wretches constantly fighting against danger and madness.”

Beyond the window, the passage to the Chanis Gate remained sealed and deathly cold. Bright yellow lamplight filled the room. Dunn Smith's words echoed within it, striking Klein again and again until he could think of nothing to say.

Seeing his silence, Dunn shook his head and smiled.

“Disappointed? Beyonders aren't what you imagined. We walk beside danger at all times.”

“Everything one gains has a price.” Klein recovered enough to answer, though his voice trembled slightly.

He had never imagined that the wonder and mystery of Beyonder powers concealed threats of this magnitude. Perhaps because he had only heard a description rather than witnessed someone lose control—and because a supernatural incident had already dragged him into its vortex—he soon managed to restrain his fear, unease, and apprehension.

The impulse to retreat remained, however, circling stubbornly at the edge of his thoughts.

“Not bad. A mature and rational response.” Dunn finished the last of his coffee.

“Beyonders are also less powerful than you might suppose, especially at the lower Sequences. Incidentally, why do we call Sequence 1 the highest and Sequence 9 the lowest? It runs contrary to ordinary intuition. When we say low Sequence, we mean low level but high number—the beginning of a pathway.

“In any case, a low-Sequence Beyonder cannot withstand a firearm, much less a cannon. Their abilities are simply stranger than guns and more difficult to defend against. If you ever receive the opportunity to become one, remember everything I've told you today. Do not decide impulsively.”

Klein smiled self-deprecatingly. “I don't even know whether I'll ever have the opportunity.”

In truth, he doubted he would refuse if the chance arose.

Consuming a potion of the wrong pathway or attempting a level beyond one's capacity were dangers that could largely be avoided. The most serious risks were the potion's subtle influence and the things revealed by heightened spiritual perception.

For the first, he could rely on lessons accumulated over generations. If he did not rush to advance and patiently mastered each new ability, the chance of losing control should remain comparatively low.

Besides, Klein needed to resolve the danger already surrounding him, understand the foundations of mysticism, and search for a way home. Those goals required only the first step. He had no immediate ambition to climb through the higher Sequences. If advancement proved too dangerous, he could remain at his original level and use knowledge rather than power to find a path back.

As for the second risk, refusing to become a Beyonder offered no protection. The luck-enhancement ritual had already nearly driven him insane, and he still remembered the whispers that threatened to burst his skull.

If danger was unavoidable, he would be better served by possessing the strength to resist it.

With that conclusion, the advantages clearly outweighed the risks, and his remaining desire to withdraw nearly disappeared.

Dunn raised his pipe again, a hint of amusement entering his gray eyes.

“I can't tell you when an opportunity might appear. To become a Beyonder, you must first make sufficient contributions. Perhaps tomorrow you'll interpret a crucial ancient document, or provide a valuable insight into one of our cases. Second, the decision depends on our superiors. No one can predict their arrangements.

“You now know enough about Beyonders to avoid making a reckless choice. Let me explain your civilian duties.”

He rose and walked toward the door, pointing away from the Chanis Gate.

“We employ an accountant and another person who purchases necessities, collects supplies from the Church and police department, and serves as our carriage driver. They are specialists who do not join the duty rotation and may rest on weekends.

“Our other civilian employees are Rozanne, Bredt, and Old Neil. They receive visitors, clean the rooms, prepare case files and inventory records, and guard the armory, storeroom, and archives. They strictly register everyone who enters those areas or borrows and returns an item. Each receives one day off per week, excluding Sunday, and they arrange night shifts and rest days among themselves.”

“Will my duties be the same as theirs?” Klein asked, putting aside his thoughts about Beyonder powers.

“Not yet. You're a trained specialist.” Dunn smiled. “For now, you have two tasks. First, spend each morning or afternoon walking outside. Concentrate on every street between Welch's residence and your apartment.”

“What?” Klein stared.

How could walking the streets be specialist work?

With both hands in the pockets of his black trench coat, Dunn explained:

“Now that we've confirmed your memory loss, the immediate investigation into Welch's and Naya's deaths has reached its end. The Antigonus family's notebook has also vanished completely. We suspect you took it and hid it somewhere on your way home. That would explain why we found no trace in your apartment and why you did not kill yourself until after returning there.

“Although the supernatural influence erased your conscious memory, the human mind and spirit are extraordinary. Traces may remain. Daly's mediumship could not find them, but that does not prove they no longer exist. At a familiar and important location, you might experience recognition or déjà vu.

“That is what we hope to provoke.”

“I understand.” The logic became clear to Klein.

The Nighthawks' theory about the missing diary was entirely reasonable.

He was the only participant still alive. Only he had possessed both the opportunity and motive to remove the notebook and hide it on the journey home.

“If you recover it, your contribution may be sufficient to earn an opportunity to become a Beyonder,” Dunn added encouragingly, indirectly emphasizing the notebook's importance.

“I hope so.” Klein nodded.

Dunn moved to the second subject.

“You receive one day off each week, which you may choose for the time being. When you aren't searching the route, report to the armory and read the relevant documents and canonical texts. That is work suited to a professional historian. Once you've finished them, you'll join Old Neil and the others in the regular duty rotation.”

“No problem.” Klein relaxed.

That doesn't sound difficult.

Dunn turned halfway around and indicated the outward-opening black iron doors carved with seven sacred emblems.

“That is the Chanis Gate. It is named for Archbishop Chanis, founder of the modern Nighthawk system. Every central cathedral in a major city has one beneath it.

“A full Nighthawk member guards it at all times, supported by at least two Keepers assigned by the Church and countless defensive measures. Never approach the gate without authorization. If you do, misfortune will follow.”

“That sounds terrifying,” Klein admitted.

“The space beyond is divided into several areas. It contains potion formulas for certain Sequences and a variety of mystical materials. It also temporarily holds heretics, mutants, cultists, and members of secret organizations. Eventually, those prisoners are transferred to the Holy Cathedral.”

The Holy Cathedral? The Church of Evernight's headquarters—the Cathedral of Serenity in Winter County, in the kingdom's far north?

Klein nodded thoughtfully.

“There are also classified documents and records,” Dunn continued. “Once your clearance rises, you may have the opportunity to read some of them.”

He hesitated before adding, “And in the basement beyond the Chanis Gate are the Sealed Artifacts.”

“Sealed Artifacts?” Klein repeated, recognizing a specialized term.

“Some extraordinary objects we recover are too important and powerful to circulate freely. In the wrong hands, they could cause catastrophic destruction. We therefore keep them secret and under strict guard. Even Nighthawks may use them only under special circumstances.

“Other objects are stranger still. They possess certain living characteristics. They tempt their Keepers, influence their surroundings, attempt to escape, and may cause disasters. Such items require especially severe containment.”

“Fascinating,” Klein said with heartfelt wonder.

“Nighthawk headquarters divides Sealed Artifacts into four grades,” Dunn explained.

“Grade 0 means Extremely Dangerous. These artifacts carry the highest importance and secrecy. They must not be investigated, disseminated, described, or observed. They may be contained only beneath the Holy Cathedral.

“Grade 1 means Highly Dangerous. Such artifacts may be used only in tightly limited circumstances. Access requires the rank of diocesan bishop or Nighthawk deacon. A diocesan headquarters such as Backlund may retain one or two; all others are sent to the Holy Cathedral.

“Grade 2 means Dangerous. These may be used carefully and in moderation. Access requires a bishop, Nighthawk captain, or higher authority. A city's central cathedral may store three to five; the remainder are transferred to the diocesan headquarters or Holy Cathedral.

“Grade 3 means Considerably Dangerous. They must still be handled cautiously and may be requested only for operations involving at least three people. Any full Nighthawk member has the necessary clearance.”

Dunn continued, “You'll encounter related records later. The numbering tells you the grade and identity. For example, 2-125 indicates Dangerous Sealed Artifact Number 125.”

He abruptly turned back into the room and removed a sheet of paper from the bottom drawer.

“One more thing. Three years ago, a newly appointed archbishop lost control. For reasons still unknown, he broke through multiple layers of security and disappeared with a Grade 0 Sealed Artifact. Memorize this photograph. If you encounter him, do not approach or alert him. Return and report immediately. Otherwise, your chance of dying in the line of duty is one thousand percent.”

“What?”

Klein accepted the paper. It bore no heading, only a black-and-white photograph and several lines of text.

INCE ZANGWILL. MALE. FORTY YEARS OLD. FORMER ARCHBISHOP. A GATEKEEPER WHO FAILED HIS PROMOTION, SUCCUMBED TO DEMONIC TEMPTATION, AND BECAME CORRUPTED. ESCAPED WITH SEALED ARTIFACT 0-08. DISTINGUISHING FEATURES...

The photograph showed Ince Zangwill in an entirely black clerical robe fastened by two rows of buttons and a soft cap. His hair was dark blond, and his eyes were so deep a blue that they appeared almost black. He had a high nose and tightly compressed lips, with features as smooth and severe as a classical sculpture. Most strikingly, one eye was blind.

“The description of the corrupted archbishop is extremely detailed, but the artifact receives only a number,” Klein observed.

“Because it has the highest security classification. Information about 0-08 is transmitted orally, never committed to paper. Even then, very little is revealed.” Dunn sighed. “It appears to be an ordinary quill, except that it writes without ink. That is all.”

He did not pursue the subject. Instead, he tugged the gold chain across his coat, removed an ornate matching pocket watch, and clicked it open.

After checking the time, Dunn pointed toward the corridor.

“I've told you everything necessary for today. Go to the armory and find Old Neil. Ask him to prepare the documents you need to read.

“He isn't an ordinary clerk. He was once a full Nighthawk. Age prevented his further advancement, and declining health made fieldwork unsuitable. He refused both an internal post as a Keeper and retirement at home. All he wants now is the companionship of documents and records.”

Chapter 20: Forgetful Dunn

“Understood.”

Klein bowed slightly and replaced his short top hat, though his thoughts remained fixed on Sealed Artifact 0-08.

It looks like an ordinary quill and writes without ink.

But what does it actually do? Why is it classified at the highest level—Extremely Dangerous?

Could it kill anyone whose name is written with it?

No. That would be absurdly powerful. If it worked like that, Ince Zangwill would have no reason to flee and remain in hiding.

Klein had just turned to leave when Dunn called after him.

“Wait. I forgot something.”

“What is it?” Klein looked back in confusion.

Dunn returned his watch to his pocket and smiled. “Before you leave, visit our accountant, Mrs. Orianna. Collect four weeks' salary in advance—a total of twelve pounds. For the following weeks, you'll receive half pay until the advance has been recovered.”

“That's too much. I don't need such a large advance,” Klein answered automatically.

He had no objection to receiving some money early. He could not even afford the public carriage home. But being handed twelve pounds at once made him distinctly nervous.

“It is necessary.” Dunn shook his head. “Do you truly intend to remain in your current apartment, sharing one bathroom with all those tenants? Even if you disregard your own comfort, consider the young lady.”

When Klein nodded, Dunn paused, looked over his clothing, and added meaningfully, “Besides, you need a cane—and a new suit.”

Klein froze, then flushed with embarrassment. His present suit was cheap and obviously inferior.

A proper silk top hat ordinarily cost five or six soli. A bow tie was three soli; a silver-inlaid walking stick, seven or eight; and a shirt, another three. Trousers, waistcoat, and tailcoat together could cost around seven pounds, while leather boots added nine or ten soli. Altogether, a gentleman's suit exceeded eight pounds and seven soli.

And a truly presentable gentleman also required a watch chain, pocket watch, and wallet.

The original Klein and Benson had once saved painstakingly before visiting a respectable clothing shop. After seeing the prices, they fled without even attempting to bargain. In the end, each bought an entire outfit from a discount shop near Iron Cross Street for less than two pounds.

That humiliating experience had left Klein with a very clear memory of clothing prices.

“A-all right,” he stammered.

Like his predecessor, he cared about his appearance.

Dunn removed the pocket watch again and checked it.

“Perhaps you should see Mrs. Orianna first. You'll probably spend considerable time with Old Neil, and she may leave for home soon.”

“Very well.” Painfully aware of his poverty, Klein offered no further objection.

Dunn returned to the table and tugged several hanging cords.

“I'll ask Rozanne to take you.”

The cords shifted, gears turned, and a bell rang in Blackthorn Security Company's reception room. Rozanne immediately rose and carefully made her way downstairs.

She appeared before them a short time later.

“I didn't interrupt your rest, did I?” Dunn asked with dry humor. “Take Moretti to Mrs. Orianna.”

Rozanne's lips curled almost imperceptibly before she answered with exaggerated cheer, “Of course, Captain.”

“Is that all?” Klein asked in surprise.

Surely he needed a written authorization before the accountant could issue an advance.

Dunn looked back at him. “What else?”

“Don't I need your signature to collect the money?” Klein asked as plainly as possible.

“No. Rozanne's presence is sufficient proof.” Dunn pointed toward the brown-haired woman.

Captain, your financial controls appear to be nonexistent...

Klein suppressed the sarcastic remark, turned, and followed Rozanne toward the door.

“Wait,” Dunn called again. “One more thing.”

Could we possibly cover everything at once?

Maintaining a pleasant expression, Klein turned around. “Yes?”

Dunn pressed a finger to his temple. “When you meet Old Neil, remember to collect ten demon-hunting bullets.”

“For me?” Klein asked in astonishment. “Demon-hunting bullets?”

“Welch's revolver is still in your possession. You may keep it.” Dunn slipped one hand into his coat pocket. “With demon-hunting rounds, you'll have some means of defending yourself if you encounter a supernatural threat. Or at least they'll give you courage.”

You could have omitted that last sentence.

Despite the complaint, Klein answered immediately, “All right. I won't forget.”

“That does require written authorization. One moment.”

Dunn sat, picked up the dark-red fountain pen, and scribbled a note. He signed and stamped it.

“Thank you, Captain.” Klein accepted the paper sincerely.

He backed away slowly, then turned.

“Wait.”

Dunn stopped him yet again.

Captain, you're only in your thirties. Why are you already showing signs of senility?

Klein forced another smile and looked back. “Is there something else?”

“I forgot that you have no firearms training. Demon-hunting ammunition would be useless to you for now.

“Instead, collect thirty ordinary rounds each day. While you're outside, visit the underground shooting range at Number Three, Zouteland Street. Most of the facility belongs to the police, but one lane is reserved for the Nighthawks.

“You'll also need a badge from Old Neil. Without it, they won't let you enter.”

Dunn slapped his forehead, took back the authorization, added the new instructions, and stamped it again.

“A good marksman is made by expending ammunition. Don't underestimate the importance of practice.”

He returned the amended note.

“I understand.”

Given his fear of the dangers ahead, Klein wanted to visit the range that very day.

He took two steps toward the exit, then cautiously turned halfway around.

“Captain, is there anything else?”

“No.” Dunn nodded with absolute certainty.

Klein breathed out and walked directly through the doorway, fighting the powerful urge to look back and ask, Are you sure?

At last, he escaped the Keeper's room.

“The Captain has always been like that. He constantly forgets things,” Rozanne whispered as they walked. “Even my grandmother has a better memory. Of course, he forgets only unimportant things. Yes—unimportant things.

“I'll call you Klein from now on. Mrs. Orianna is very kind and easy to talk to. Her father is a remarkably skilled watchmaker...”

Listening to the brown-haired woman chatter, Klein climbed the stairs to the upper floor. He found Mrs. Orianna in the office farthest to the right.

She was a dark-haired woman in her thirties, dressed in a lace-trimmed gown. Her hair was curled in the latest fashion, and her clear green eyes smiled with refined elegance.

After Rozanne relayed Dunn's instructions, Orianna filled out an advance-payment form.

“Sign here. Do you have a personal seal? If not, use your thumbprint.”

“Of course.” Familiar with the procedure now, Klein quickly completed the formalities.

Orianna took out a copper key and opened the room's safe. As she counted banknotes, she said with a smile, “You're fortunate—we have sufficient cash today. Captain invited you because you were involved in a supernatural incident and possess some useful specialty, didn't he?”

“Yes. Your intuition is impeccable.” Klein offered the compliment freely.

Orianna removed four notes with pale-gray backgrounds and intricate black designs. After locking the safe, she turned and smiled.

“Because I had a similar experience.”

“Truly?” Klein displayed the proper amount of surprise.

“Do you remember the serial killer who terrified Tingen sixteen years ago?” Orianna handed him the four notes.

“The Bloody Butcher? He murdered five young women and removed the hearts and stomachs from some of them. My mother often used the story to frighten Melissa when we were children.”

Klein accepted the money. Two notes were worth five pounds each, and two were worth one. All had gray backgrounds printed with black ink, with elaborate anti-counterfeiting designs in the corners.

The larger five-pound notes bore the portrait of Henry Augustus I, fifth king of Loen and direct ancestor of George III. A white hairband sat above his round face. His eyes were narrow, and his expression unnaturally solemn. Nevertheless, Klein felt an immediate and profound affection for him.

A five-pound note.

Nearly four weeks of Benson's wages.

The one-pound notes depicted George III's father, William Augustus VI. The former king, known as the Might-wielder, had a thick mustache and resolute gaze. During his reign, he freed Loen from the old order's constraints and restored the kingdom to the pinnacle of power.

Such excellent kings...

The faint scent of ink left Klein refreshed and delighted.

“Yes,” Orianna said, lingering fear entering her voice despite the passing of sixteen years. “If the Nighthawks had not arrived in time, I would have been his sixth victim.”

“I heard the murderer—the Butcher—was a Beyonder?” Klein folded the banknotes carefully, placed them in his inner pocket, and patted the spot several times.

“He was.” Orianna nodded firmly. “He had killed many others before coming to Tingen. The Nighthawks caught him because he was preparing a ritual for a devil.”

“That explains the different organs. I'm sorry, Mrs. Orianna. I shouldn't have made you revisit such memories.”

She smiled. “I'm no longer afraid. At the time, I was studying accounting at a commercial college. After the incident, I joined the team and have remained ever since.

“Now I won't keep you. Old Neil is waiting.”

“Goodbye, Mrs. Orianna.”

Klein removed his hat and bowed. Before descending the stairs, he could not resist patting his inner pocket again to ensure the twelve pounds remained safely inside.

At the underground intersection, he turned right. Before long, he reached a half-open iron door.

Knock! Knock! Knock!

An elderly voice answered from within. “Come in.”

Klein pushed the metal door open and found a cramped room containing only a desk and two chairs.

Another iron door, firmly locked, stood in the inner wall. Behind the desk sat a white-haired old man in a classical black robe, reading several yellowed pages beneath a gas lamp.

He raised his head.

“You must be Klein Moretti. Rozanne came by earlier and said you were very polite.”

“Miss Rozanne is exceptionally friendly. Good afternoon, Mr. Neil.” Klein removed his hat respectfully.

“Sit down.” Old Neil pointed toward a silver tin decorated with elaborate floral patterns. “Would you care for hand-ground coffee?”

Deep wrinkles surrounded his eyes and mouth. His dark-red pupils were slightly clouded.

Klein glanced at the clear water in the man's porcelain cup. “You don't appear to be drinking coffee yourself.”

“An old habit. I never drink it after three in the afternoon,” Neil explained with a laugh.

“Why not?” Klein asked casually.

The old man's smile disappeared. He looked directly into Klein's eyes.

“Because it interferes with my sleep and allows me to hear the whispers of unknown beings.”

Klein found himself unable to answer. He changed the subject.

“Mr. Neil, which documents and books should I begin with?”

As he spoke, he produced Dunn's authorization.

“Anything historical, complicated, or incomplete. I've always tried to study such matters, but my understanding remains rudimentary. Diaries, contemporary books, epitaphs, and similar records are simply too troublesome.” Neil sighed. “These pages, for example, require detailed historical research before their contents can be understood.”

“Why?” Klein asked.

Neil pointed to the yellowed sheets before him.

“They come from Emperor Roselle Gustav's lost diary. To preserve secrecy, he recorded it in a set of strange symbols he invented.”

Emperor Roselle—the possible transmigrator?

Klein immediately focused all his attention.

“Many people believe Roselle never truly died but became a hidden god. Cults devoted to him conduct rituals in hopes of receiving power. We occasionally investigate such incidents and recover original pages or copies of the diary,” Neil explained, shaking his head.

“No one has ever deciphered the symbols. The Holy Cathedral therefore permits local teams to retain copies for research, in the hope that someone will eventually produce a useful discovery.”

A self-satisfied smile appeared on his face.

“I have already deciphered several symbols and confirmed that they represent numbers. More importantly, I determined that the text is a diary. My plan is to compare entries with historical events from the corresponding dates—especially events involving the Emperor—and gradually deduce additional symbols.

“The reasoning of a genius, wouldn't you agree?” The white-haired man looked eagerly at Klein.

Klein nodded with complete sincerity. “Absolutely.”

“You should examine them as well. Tomorrow, you can begin assisting me with the diary.” Old Neil pushed the yellowed sheets across the desk.

Klein turned them around and glanced down.

He froze.

The symbols had been copied so badly that many were distorted, but there was no possibility of mistake.

They were the characters he knew better than any others.

Chinese.

And not merely Chinese—fucking Simplified Chinese.

Chapter 21: An Old Friend in Another World

For one impossible instant, Klein thought he had returned to Earth.

Then the elegant gas lamp enclosed in a brass grille and the silver-inlaid tin in which Old Neil kept his hand-ground coffee reminded him where he truly was.

Emperor Roselle really was a fellow countryman? He used Simplified Chinese—a script that doesn't exist in this world—to record his secrets?

Overcome by the indescribable sensation of encountering an old friend in a foreign land, Klein hurried through the three pages.

“November 18. A fascinating development. An experiment prompted by idle curiosity—and an accidental mistake—allowed me to discover the pitiful fellow lost in the deep darkness amid the storms. He can approach the reality of this world only during each month's full moon, yet remains unable to transmit his cries. Fortunately for him, he encountered me, the protagonist of this era.”

“Reading the paragraph above has left me strangely melancholy. Even my Chinese now sounds like a translation. Four decades passed in the blink of an eye. My memories of home feel increasingly like a dream.”

“January 1, 1184. Lady Florena looked magnificent at the grand New Year's ball.”

“January 2. Every one of my diplomats is an idiot!”

“January 3. I chose too hastily. Looking back, I should have selected Apprentice, Seer, or Marauder. Unfortunately, there is no second chance.”

“January 4. Why are my children so stupid? I've repeated this countless times: don't be deceived by those charlatans! The key to a potion is not controlling it, but digesting it. The answer isn't merely using its powers, but acting. A potion's name is not symbolic alone. It creates a concrete image and provides the key to digestion.”

“September 9. An alliance has formed against me: Feysac from the north, Loen from the east, and Feynapotter from the south. My enemies have finally united, but I am not afraid. Facts will teach them that several generations of superior weapons and knowledge cannot be overcome by numbers or low-Sequence Beyonders. Nor am I without powerful subordinates. As for those of higher Sequence—have they forgotten who I am?”

“September 23. I have lost contact with the ship searching for the Forsaken Land of the Gods. I should consider inventing wireless telegraphy, though I hope the storms will not interfere with it.”

“September 24. Miss Ithaca is more enchanting than Lady Florena. Perhaps I am simply nostalgic for my youth.”

Simplified Chinese characters required more space than ordinary Loen script, so each page held relatively little text. Furthermore, the reverse side had been left blank for preservation and research.

Even so, the diary sent Klein's emotions into turmoil. Roselle's remarks about the essence of potions in particular seemed to illuminate a possible solution. Klein felt as though he had stumbled upon a priceless secret.

Perhaps this will become a beacon on my future path as a Beyonder.

The three pages clearly came from different periods. Roselle appeared to record the year only in the first entry of each new year, making it impossible to determine when the September and November passages had been written.

Who was the pitiful man he discovered?

What precisely did digesting and acting mean?

Where was the Forsaken Land of the Gods?

Questions crowded Klein's mind. He wanted nothing more than to gather every surviving page of Roselle's diary and read it from beginning to end.

“Klein?” Old Neil asked from across the desk.

Klein jolted back to awareness and hurriedly concealed his reaction with a laugh.

“I thought I might be the special one. I was trying to decipher the symbols.”

“You are young indeed.” Old Neil nodded with amusement. “I once believed I was special too.”

Klein checked the pages again to ensure he had missed nothing before returning them.

“Are these the only ones we have?” he asked without thinking.

I need to see more of Roselle's diary.

“Did you expect a library full?” Old Neil scoffed, deepening the wrinkles on his face as he stroked the pages.

“Only a handful of incidents each year involve Beyonders or mysticism. The main reason is the gradual disappearance of extraordinary species from the Northern Continent. Fewer creatures mean fewer potion ingredients, and therefore fewer Beyonders.

“Over the past several centuries, dragons, giants, and elves have become little more than entries in books. Even the sea folk are rarely seen in coastal waters.”

The explanation reminded Klein of a joke.

“Perhaps it's time to establish a Dragon and Giant Protection Association.”

Old Neil stared at him blankly. It took several seconds for the meaning to register. Then he slapped the desk and laughed with thoroughly ungentlemanly delight.

“Klein, you truly are amusing. Humor is a fine Loen tradition. But your proposal is too narrow. Why protect only dragons and giants? We should call it the Fantastic Beast Protection Association.”

“No, no. What about all the unfortunate magical plants?” Klein shook his head.

They looked at each other and declared together:

“The Fantastic Organism Protection Association!”

Both laughed, and much of the awkwardness between them disappeared.

“There are too few interesting young men these days...” Old Neil smiled. “Now, where was I? Ah, yes. Supernatural cases are uncommon, and lunatics who worship Emperor Roselle form only a small fraction of those. Obtaining three pages is already fortunate. The larger cathedrals and diocesan headquarters may possess more.”

After muttering to himself, he picked up Dunn's authorization from the desk.

“Pistol cartridges, rifle rounds, or compressed-steam ammunition?”

“For a revolver,” Klein answered.

“Very well. I'll collect them. Do you own a shoulder holster? A gentleman should not walk through the streets with something bulging conspicuously below his waist.”

It was a joke no man could misunderstand.

“Not yet.” Klein smiled obligingly. “Does the Captain need to add it to the authorization?”

Old Neil rose. “No. I'll simply record it. A holster is an accessory. Repeat after me: accessory.”

“Were you once a teacher?” Klein joked.

“I spent some time teaching at the Church's Sunday schools and free schools.”

Old Neil waved the authorization, removed a key from his drawer, and unlocked the iron door to the inner room.

Beyonders don't seem very different from ordinary people...

As the old man disappeared, Klein's gaze returned to the three diary pages.

Emperor Roselle truly was involved in the mystical world.

His diary is invaluable. To everyone else, these pages are meaningless scraps that may not be deciphered for centuries. To me, they're treasure.

Where are the remaining pages?

I must find a way to obtain more.

His thoughts remained in turmoil until Old Neil returned and locked the inner door.

“Ten demon-hunting rounds, thirty ordinary revolver cartridges, a cowhide shoulder holster, and a badge for the Awwa County Police Special Operations Department's Seventh Unit. Count them and try the holster. Then sign the register.”

The ordinary cartridges sat neatly in a three-layered paper box. Like ammunition from Earth, they gleamed yellow, though they were somewhat narrower.

The demon-hunting rounds occupied a small iron box. Their shape was identical, but their surfaces were silver. Closer inspection revealed intricate patterns, with tiny Dark Sacred Emblems—stars clustered around half a crimson moon—engraved into the base.

The cowhide holster felt firm and included its own straps and buckle. Beside it lay a metal badge half the size of Klein's palm. Silver lettering formed two nearly complete circles around the police emblem of two crossed swords beneath a crown:

AWWA COUNTY POLICE DEPARTMENT

SEVENTH UNIT, SPECIAL OPERATIONS DEPARTMENT

“A pity it isn't a Nighthawk badge,” Klein said, half wistful and half probing.

Old Neil only smiled and urged him to test the holster.

Klein removed his coat and, after considerable effort, fastened the straps so the holster rested beneath his left arm.

“Not bad.” He put the coat on again.

Old Neil examined him and nodded with satisfaction. “An excellent fit. My judgment remains as reliable as ever.”

Klein distributed the ammunition and badge among his pockets, signed the register, and exchanged a few casual words with Old Neil before leaving.

Halfway down the passage, he slapped his forehead.

“I forgot to ask about the Church's potion pathways. Roselle's diary distracted me completely.”

He still did not know the beginning of the Church of Evernight's complete Sequence pathway, only that it started at Sequence 9.

Rozanne had mentioned something. Sleepless?

As Klein walked slowly toward the staircase, someone descended from above.
