The man wore close-fitting trousers suited to easy movement and an untucked white shirt. His black hair, green eyes, and distinctly romantic air marked him as the young inspector who had searched Klein's apartment. They had glimpsed each other upstairs earlier but had not spoken.

“Good afternoon.” The poet-like Nighthawk smiled.

“Good afternoon. I assume I need no introduction?” Klein replied lightly.

“None. You left a strong impression.” The young man extended his hand. “Leonard Mitchell. Sequence 8, Midnight Poet.”

Sequence 8. So he truly is a poet.

Klein shook his hand with a smile. “A strong impression?”

Leonard's green eyes seemed unusually deep. A faint smile touched his lips.

“You possess a distinctive quality.”

That sounds remarkably suggestive...

The corners of Klein's mouth shifted. He barely maintained his smile.

“I can't say I've noticed it.”

“You survived an incident like that without our immediate protection. That alone makes you unusual.” Leonard gestured down the passage. “I need to relieve the Captain. I'll see you tomorrow.”

“Tomorrow.” Klein stepped aside.

When he reached the stairwell, Leonard Mitchell turned and stared at the yellow-lit stone floor. Speaking softly to empty air, he murmured:

“Did you notice anything?”

After a pause, he added, “Indeed. There is nothing remarkable about him...”

Chapter 22: Starting Sequences

When Klein returned upstairs to the reception room, he was about to bid Rozanne farewell when the brown-haired woman said brightly, “The Captain says you needn't begin until Monday. He wants you to settle your household affairs first.”

“All right.”

Klein had not expected the Nighthawks' management to be so considerate. He felt genuinely grateful.

He had planned to rise early the following morning and include Tingen University in his prescribed “wandering.” He needed to inform the interview committee that he would withdraw from consideration. After all, he had received the opportunity through his mentor's recommendation. Formally declining was a matter of basic courtesy and respect for the professor's efforts.

In a world without telephones, where telegrams were charged by the character and a letter would arrive too late, taking public transportation to the university was the most economical and appropriate solution.

With the Captain's special permission, however, Klein no longer needed to exhaust himself. He could sleep late and still arrive in good time.

He had just raised his hat in farewell when another thought occurred to him. Looking around, he lowered his voice.

“Rozanne, do you know the starting Sequence of the Church's complete pathway?”

He had forgotten to ask Old Neil.

Rozanne's eyes widened. “You want to become a Beyonder?”

Was I truly that obvious?

Embarrassment entered Klein's posture. “After learning that supernatural and mysterious powers actually exist, how could I not feel some longing for them?”

“Oh, Goddess. Do you understand how dangerous that is? Didn't the Captain tell you? A Beyonder's enemies aren't limited to cultists and dark sorcerers. Their greatest enemy is often themselves. Someone loses control nearly every year. Some even sacrifice their lives.

“Have you considered what that would do to your family?”

Rozanne's animated gestures magnified every word.

“Klein, remaining a civilian employee would be far wiser. The danger is minimal, and our salaries rise every year. After a few years, you'll have saved enough to rent a house in the North Borough or suburbs. Then you can marry a wealthy, beautiful lady, build a wonderful home, and have adorable, mischievous little angels...”

“Rozanne, stop. Please.” Klein interrupted helplessly when he realized how far she had wandered from the question. “I only want a basic understanding.”

“All right...”

Rozanne lowered her eyes and fell silent for several seconds.

“Because of what happened to my father, I become... agitated whenever this subject comes up. You understand. But honestly, I deeply respect every man or woman who willingly becomes a Nighthawk.”

“Of course. I understand,” Klein assured her.

Rozanne blinked her light-brown eyes.

“My father once told me never to believe that becoming stronger or advancing in Sequence would eliminate hidden dangers. The opposite is true. Greater power brings encounters with more terrifying things. Before certain unknown beings, the only outcomes are death and madness.

“He sacrificed himself two weeks after saying that...”

Then she hurriedly added, “Don't look at me with pity, Klein. My life is good now—truly. Fear is the proper response to these matters.”

“I only want to understand the basics,” Klein repeated, unsure whether to laugh or sigh.

The Captain explained the danger far more clearly. And even if I refuse to become a Beyonder, I've already encountered the supernatural.

“Very well.” Rozanne thought for a moment. “I've overheard the Captain and Old Neil discussing this. Extraordinary creatures are disappearing, so high-Sequence Beyonders are extremely rare in the present age. Becoming any kind of Beyonder is already a remarkable achievement.

“Hundreds of thousands of people live in Tingen and its suburbs—perhaps more—yet there are only thirty-some Beyonders. That's merely my estimate, of course, and doesn't include cultists or dark sorcerers hiding in the shadows.”

Without waiting for Klein to respond, she regained her enthusiasm and raised a fist before her chest.

“And most of those thirty-some are only Sequence 9. Ah, I've wandered off topic again.”

“Not at all. I wanted to know that too.”

Klein preferred the talkative Rozanne. Her digressions revealed far more information.

“In any case, becoming a Beyonder is already very, very impressive,” she repeated. “The starting point of our Church's complete pathway is Sleepless. Sequence 9: Sleepless.”

As expected.

Klein nodded while Rozanne, unable to restrain herself, continued with a detailed explanation.

“The name tells you most of it. A Sleepless doesn't need to sleep at night. Three or four hours during the day is enough. I'm so envious—no, I shouldn't be. Sleep is a gift from the Goddess, the purest form of happiness.

“Where was I? Right. A Sleepless can see through darkness without a lamp. The deeper the night, the stronger they become—not only physically, but in intuition and mental ability. They can sense unknown dangers hidden in the darkness. But against creatures that ordinary weapons cannot harm, they still rely on demon-hunting ammunition and similar items.

“My father was a Sleepless.”

Without giving Klein time to ask another question, she added, “Sequence 8 is Midnight Poet, and Sequence 7 is Nightmare.”

Nightmare?

Klein immediately remembered Dunn entering and guiding his dream.

“The Captain?” he asked.

“You already know?” Rozanne's mouth nearly formed a perfect O.

“The Captain once entered my dream.” Klein glanced around and lowered his voice even further.

“I see...” Rozanne whispered in sudden understanding.

She lifted the coffee cup beside her and took a sip.

“The Church has only two Sequence 7 Beyonders in all of Tingen. The Captain is probably one of them. Even in a major diocese like Backlund, he would still be considered formidable. Some deacons may not be stronger than he is.”

“So the Captain truly is impressive.” Klein smiled.

Dunn's appearance during the previous night had already left him convinced that the man was a powerful Beyonder.

“Of course!” Rozanne straightened proudly.

Moments later, her expression turned frustrated.

“I don't know what comes after Sequence 7. Among the Tingen Nighthawks, perhaps only the Captain does.”

“What about the Church's incomplete pathways?” Klein asked, satisfied enough to change direction.

Sleepless matched his expectations of a Beyonder, but it was not what he wanted. His ideal Sequence 9 would allow him to study and master more mystical knowledge, helping him discover the reason for his transmigration and establish a foundation for returning home.

Rozanne sighed thoughtfully.

“I've never been very interested in those. I only know the Church possesses more incomplete pathways than the others. The Goddess is the Mother of Concealment, after all.

“There should be two or three. Some teammates are cold and distant, and they frighten me. They also have a peculiar smell. Others...”

She hesitated.

“You should ask Old Neil. He knows far more, including many interesting magical rituals. Let me think. He once mentioned the Sequence 9 name—which is also the potion's name. Ah, yes: Mystery Pryer.”

Interesting magical rituals? Mystery Pryer sounds very close to what I need.

Klein felt a surge of excitement.

“I also know the name of a Sequence 7 in one incomplete pathway,” Rozanne added boastfully, having just remembered it.

“What is it?” Klein asked with intense curiosity.

In an age when high-Sequence Beyonders were vanishingly rare, Sequence 7 represented considerable power even within a Church.

Rozanne smiled sweetly, pleased with herself.

“Spirit Medium.”

“Madam Daly?” Klein asked automatically.

After the initial surprise, the answer seemed obvious. Only a Sequence 7 Beyonder could have displayed mediumship of that caliber.

Rozanne's eyes widened again. “H-how do you know that too?”

“I've met Madam Daly.” Klein saw no reason to conceal it.

“I see.” Envy colored Rozanne's voice. “If I could become a Spirit Medium like Madam Daly, I might be willing to become a Beyonder. No—I would still think about it carefully for ten minutes.”

“Madam Daly also perfectly matches my idea of a Beyonder,” Klein agreed with deliberate exaggeration.

Having achieved his purpose, he chatted idly with Rozanne for several more minutes. Once it became clear that she had no further information to offer, he removed his hat, bowed, and left.

After descending a few steps, Klein stopped and patted his inner pocket.

He took out the twelve pounds, gripped the notes tightly in his left hand, then returned that hand to his pocket without releasing them. An unconscious smile spread across his face.

According to the traditions of China's Foodie Empire, earning one's first salary required treating the family to dinner.

Tonight, Melissa was getting a feast.

Chapter 23: Sidearm

As Klein walked along Zouteland Street through the warm, humid breeze, he suddenly realized he had a problem.

He possessed only three pence in change. A public carriage to Iron Cross Street cost four. Handing the conductor a one-pound note would be like using a hundred-yuan bill to buy the cheapest bottle of water. There was nothing improper about it, but the awkwardness would be considerable.

Should I spend three pence to travel three kilometers and walk the rest?

With one hand in his pocket, Klein slowed while considering the option.

No. That won't work.

The remaining walk would take too long, and carrying twelve pounds—a small fortune—made traveling on foot unsafe.

Worse, he had deliberately left the revolver at home for fear the Nighthawks might confiscate it. If he encountered the force responsible for Welch's death, he would have no means of defending himself.

Could I exchange a note at a nearby bank? Absolutely not. They charge half a percent.

The very thought of the fee made Klein's heart ache.

After rejecting one solution after another, he noticed a clothing shop ahead, and his eyes lit up.

Of course. The normal way to obtain change is to purchase something appropriately priced.

He needed a formal suit, shirts, waistcoats, trousers, boots, and a cane. All were within his new budget and would have to be purchased sooner or later.

Trying on clothes is troublesome, though. Benson understands quality better than I do and is far better at bargaining. I should wait until he returns.

Then perhaps a cane?

Yes. A cane is a gentleman's finest defensive weapon—almost as useful as a crowbar. A pistol in one hand and a cane in the other: the proper fighting style of a civilized man.

His decision made, Klein entered Wilker Clothing and Hats.

The shop resembled clothing stores on Earth. Rows of formal suits covered the left wall. Shirts, trousers, waistcoats, and bow ties occupied displays through the center, while leather shoes and boots stood in glass cases on the right.

“May I help you, sir?” A salesman in a white shirt and red waistcoat approached politely.

In Loen, wealthy and powerful gentlemen favored black suits with white shirts, black waistcoats, and matching trousers. Because their own clothing was so restrained, they required male servants, clerks, and attendants to dress in brighter colors, clearly distinguishing employee from master.

Ladies and their mistresses, by contrast, wore elaborate gowns in every color. Female servants therefore dressed in black and white.

Klein considered the question. “I need a cane—something heavier and harder than usual.”

Something capable of cracking a skull.

The red-waistcoated salesman furtively examined him before leading him deeper into the shop and indicating a row of canes.

“This gold-inlaid model is made from ironheart wood. It is extremely hard and satisfyingly heavy. Eleven soli and seven pence. Would you care to try it?”

Eleven soli and seven pence? Why not rob a bank? Who cares about the gold inlay?

Although shocked by the price, Klein maintained an unruffled expression and nodded. “Please.”

The salesman removed the ironheart cane and passed it over with exaggerated care, as though afraid Klein might drop and damage it.

It was indeed heavy. Klein attempted several movements and found that he could not swing it smoothly.

“Too heavy.” He shook his head with genuine relief.

That was not an excuse.

The salesman retrieved it and pointed toward three others.

“This walnut cane was made by Mr. Hayes, Tingen's most celebrated craftsman. Ten soli and three pence. This one is ebony with silver inlay, hard as iron, for seven soli and six pence. The third is carved from the heartwood of a white boli tree and also inlaid with silver. Seven soli and ten pence.”

Klein tested each for weight and tapped them with his fingers to judge their hardness. In the end, he selected the least expensive.

“The ebony one.” He pointed toward the silver-inlaid cane in the salesman's hand.

“An excellent choice, sir. Please follow me to the counter. If it becomes scratched or stained, bring it back and we will restore it without charge.”

As the salesman led him forward, Klein finally loosened his grip on the four pound notes and selected one of the smaller denominations.

“Seven soli and six pence, sir,” the cashier said with a smile.

Klein intended to preserve his gentlemanly composure. Yet as he extended the one-pound note, he could not prevent himself from asking, “Is there any discount?”

“Everything we sell is handmade, sir. Our costs are considerable,” the salesman replied. “And with the proprietor absent, we are not authorized to reduce the price.”

“My apologies, sir,” the cashier added.

“Very well.” Klein surrendered the note and accepted the black, silver-inlaid cane.

While waiting for his change, he stepped away from the counter and tested several horizontal swings.

Whoosh! Whoosh! Whoosh!

The cane cut through the air with a satisfyingly heavy sound. Klein nodded in approval.

When he looked back, expecting his change, he found the red-waistcoated salesman several paces farther away. The cashier had retreated into the corner and stood close to a double-barreled shotgun mounted on the wall.

Loen maintained partial restrictions on firearms. Ownership required either a general weapons permit or a hunting license. Neither allowed possession of military weapons such as repeating rifles, compressed-steam guns, or six-barreled machine guns.

A general permit covered almost any civilian firearm but was extraordinarily difficult to obtain; even respectable merchants were often rejected. Hunting licenses were easier—farmers in the suburbs could receive them—but covered only a limited number of hunting weapons. People with substantial property frequently obtained such licenses for emergency protection.

Like the proprietor of this shop, apparently.

Klein looked between the wary employees, the corners of his mouth twitching.

He gave a dry laugh. “Excellent. The cane swings beautifully. I'm very satisfied.”

Realizing he did not intend to assault them, the cashier relaxed and presented the change with both hands.

Klein received two five-soli notes, two one-soli notes, a five-pence coin, and a penny.

After a two-second pause, he ignored their expressions and held all four notes toward the light to verify the anti-counterfeiting watermarks.

Only then did he put away the money. Cane in hand, he tipped his hat and left Wilker Clothing and Hats.

He extravagantly spent six pence on two short rides by untracked carriage and reached home safely.

Once the door was closed, Klein counted his eleven pounds and twelve soli three separate times before placing the notes in the desk drawer. Then he retrieved the brass revolver with its wooden grip.

Clink! Clang!

Five ordinary brass rounds fell onto the desk. Klein replaced them with silver demon-hunting bullets engraved with intricate patterns and the Dark Sacred Emblem.

As before, he loaded only five chambers, leaving one empty to prevent an accidental discharge. He placed the remaining special rounds and the five ordinary cartridges together in the small iron box.

Snap!

The cylinder locked into place, bringing an immediate sense of security.

Excited, Klein slipped the revolver into the holster beneath his arm and secured the strap. He practiced releasing the buckle and drawing the weapon again and again. Whenever his arm grew sore, he rested before continuing.

He trained until sunset, when tenants' footsteps began sounding in the corridor.

Klein released a long breath and returned the revolver to its holster.

Only then did he remove his formal jacket and waistcoat. He put on his usual yellow-brown coat and swung his arms to loosen them.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

Footsteps approached, followed by a key entering the lock.

Melissa stepped inside, her soft black hair framing her face. Her nose twitched as she looked toward the cold stove, and some of the brightness left her eyes.

“Klein, I'll warm up last night's leftovers. Benson will probably return tomorrow.”

Klein leaned against the edge of the desk with both hands in his pockets.

“No. Let's eat out.”

“Eat out?” Melissa repeated in surprise.

“What about the Silver Crown Restaurant on Daffodil Street? I've heard the food is excellent.”

“B-but...” Melissa remained bewildered.

Klein grinned. “We're celebrating my new job.”

“You found a job?” Her voice rose involuntarily. “But isn't your interview with Tingen University tomorrow?”

“A different position.” Klein smiled faintly and removed the stack of notes from the drawer. “They even advanced me four weeks' salary.”

Melissa stared at the pounds and soli, her eyes widening.

“Goddess... You—they—what sort of job did you find?”

Klein's expression stiffened as he searched for a safe explanation.

“A security company that searches for, collects, and protects ancient artifacts. They needed a professional consultant. It's a five-year contract and pays three pounds a week.”

After a moment's silence, Melissa asked, “Is this what troubled you last night?”

Klein nodded. “Yes. An academic position at Tingen University would be respectable, but I prefer this work.”

“It doesn't sound bad.” Melissa offered an encouraging smile. Then suspicion and curiosity entered her voice. “But why would they advance four entire weeks of salary?”

“Because we need to move.” Klein grinned and shrugged. “We need more rooms—and a bathroom of our own.”

He felt his smile was flawless, lacking only the word Surprise!

Melissa stared at him before hurriedly protesting.

“Klein, we live perfectly well here. I complain about the shared bathroom only from habit. Do you remember Jenny, who lived next door? After her father was injured and lost his job, they had to move to Lower Street. All five of them share one room. Three sleep in a bunk bed and two on the floor, and they still hope to rent the last empty sleeping place to someone else.

“Compared with them, we're extremely fortunate. Don't waste your salary on a larger apartment. Besides, I love Mrs. Smyrin's bakery.”

Little sister, why is your reaction completely different from the scene I imagined?

Klein stared at Melissa, speechless.

Chapter 24: Penny-Pincher

Golden light gradually filled the sky outside. Klein looked into Melissa's eyes and found himself momentarily speechless. Not one of the arguments he had prepared seemed usable.

He cleared his throat twice while frantically searching for another approach.

"Melissa, spending some of my salary on a better home wouldn't be wasteful. My colleagues—and Benson's—may visit us in the future. Are we supposed to entertain them here? And when Benson and I marry, are we still going to sleep in bunk beds with our wives?"

"Neither of you even has a fiancée yet," Melissa replied with impeccable logic. "We can wait and save more money in the meantime."

"No, Melissa. It's a matter of social convention." Cornered, Klein could only appeal to grand principles. "Now that I earn three pounds a week, I need to live like someone who earns three pounds a week."

In truth, Zhou Mingrui had shared apartments before and was no stranger to cramped living conditions. He could tolerate them easily. But that same experience had taught him how inconvenient such an arrangement could be for a young woman.

More importantly, he intended to become a Beyonder, study mysticism, and find a way home. He would inevitably need to perform rituals in private. In a crowded apartment building, the chance of an accident—or of being discovered—was far too high.

Seeing that Melissa was about to argue again, Klein hurriedly added, "Don't worry. I'm not suggesting a detached house. A terraced home would be enough, as long as it has a private bathroom. And I like Mrs. Smyrin's bread, Tingen biscuits, and lemon cakes too. We'll look near Iron Cross Street or Daffodil Street first."

Melissa pursed her lips. After a moment of silence, she slowly nodded.

"Besides, there's no rush," Klein said with a chuckle. "We have to wait for Benson to come home. We can't let him open the door and discover that everything is gone, can we? Just imagine him standing there: 'Where are my belongings? Where are my brother and sister? Where is my home? Is this even the right building? Goddess, wake me if this is a dream! How did my home vanish after I was gone for only a few days?'"

His imitation of Benson made Melissa smile despite herself. Her eyes narrowed, revealing the faint dimples in her cheeks.

"No. Mr. Franky would be waiting downstairs to collect the apartment key. Benson wouldn't even make it up here," she said, taking a jab at their miserly landlord.

In the Moretti household, blaming Mr. Franky for every inconvenience, large or small, had become something of a tradition—one Benson himself had started.

"True. He certainly wouldn't pay to replace the lock for the next tenants." Smiling, Klein pointed toward the door. "In that case, Miss Melissa, shall we celebrate at the Silver Crown Restaurant?"

Melissa let out a soft sigh. "Klein, do you know my classmate Selena? She's one of my closest friends."

Selena? The image of a cheerful girl with wine-red hair and dark-brown eyes surfaced in Klein's mind. Her parents were believers of the Evernight Goddess and had named her after Saint Selena in hopes of receiving a blessing. Not yet sixteen, she was half a year younger than Melissa and possessed an open, lively personality.

"Of course." Klein nodded.

"Her older brother, Chris, is a lawyer. He earns almost three pounds a week too, and his fiancée works part-time as a typist," Melissa explained. "They've been engaged for more than four years. They're still saving so they can have a decent, stable life after the wedding, and they plan to wait at least another year. Selena says there are many people like them. Most don't marry until they're twenty-eight or older. You have to plan ahead and save. You can't squander your money."

It's only one dinner at a restaurant. Do I really deserve an entire lecture for that? Klein didn't know whether to laugh or cry.

After thinking for a few seconds, he said, "Melissa, I already make three pounds a week, and my salary will increase every year. You don't need to worry so much."

"We still need savings in case something unexpected happens. What if that security company suddenly closes? One of my classmates had to leave school after her father's company went bankrupt. He could only find temporary work at the docks, and their circumstances became terrible almost overnight." Melissa's expression was utterly serious.

Klein covered his face with one hand.

"Th-the security company has ties to the government. Yes, very close ties. It isn't going to close down easily."

"Even the government isn't stable. Whenever an election puts a different party in power, lots of people lose their positions. Everything becomes a mess," Melissa countered without yielding an inch.

Sis, you really do know a lot... Exasperated and amused in equal measure, Klein shook his head.

"Fine... I'll make soup with yesterday's leftovers. You can buy some fried fish, a piece of black-pepper beef, a small bottle of butter, and a cup of malt beer for me. We should celebrate somehow."

All of those were common foods sold by the vendors on Iron Cross Street. A piece of fried fish cost six to eight pence, a modest serving of black-pepper beef cost five pence, and a cup of malt beer cost one penny. A quarter-pound bottle of butter was four pence, though a full pound cost only one soli and three pence.

The original Klein had often bought ingredients during the holidays, so the prices came readily to mind. He estimated that Melissa would need about one soli and six pence, then took out two one-soli notes.

"All right." Melissa accepted the compromise without further protest. She set down her schoolbag and took the money.

She found a tiny bottle for the butter and containers for the other food, then headed briskly for the door. After a moment's thought, Klein called after her.

"Melissa, use the change to buy some fruit."

Many vendors on Iron Cross Street bought bruised or nearly overripe fruit cheaply from other districts. The residents didn't object. Once the spoiled portions were cut away, they could still enjoy flavors normally beyond their means.

Klein crossed the room in a few quick steps, emptied the remaining copper pennies from his pocket, and pressed them into his sister's palm.

"Huh?" Melissa looked at him in confusion.

Klein stepped back with a smile. "And stop by Mrs. Smyrin's. Buy yourself a small lemon cake."

Melissa's mouth fell slightly open. She blinked several times before finally answering, "Okay."

She turned at once, opened the door, and hurried down the stairs.

...

The Khoy River cut across the countryside, its banks lined with cedars and maples. The air was so fresh that breathing it felt intoxicating.

Klein had come to formally withdraw from the interview. With his revolver concealed beneath his clothes and his cane in hand, he paid six pence for the public carriage, followed a paved path, and approached a three-story stone building shaded by greenery—the administrative building of Tingen University.

"It certainly deserves its reputation as one of the Loen Kingdom's two great universities..." Klein murmured as he walked through the grounds for the first time.

Compared with Tingen University, Khoy University on the opposite bank could only be described as shabby.

"Heave-ho!"

"Heave-ho!"

The rhythmic cries drew closer as two racing shells traveled upstream along the Khoy River, their oars rising and falling in perfect unison.

Rowing was popular at universities throughout the Loen Kingdom. Since Klein had relied on a scholarship to pay for his studies, he, Welch, and several others had joined Khoy University's rowing club. They had been rather good at it.

"Youth..." Klein stopped to watch the distant boats, sighing with wistful emotion.

In another week, the university would close for the summer, and scenes like this would disappear for a time.

He continued along the tree-shaded road until he reached another three-story stone building. After registering at the entrance, he easily found the office of the man who had received him on his previous visit.

Knock. Knock. Knock.

He rapped gently on the half-open door.

"Come in," a man's voice called from within.

A middle-aged instructor in a white shirt and black tailcoat frowned when Klein entered.

"The interview doesn't begin for another hour."

"Mr. Stone, do you remember me? I'm Klein Moretti, a student of Senior Associate Professor Cohen. You read my recommendation letter before." Klein smiled and removed his hat.

Harvin Stone stroked his black beard in puzzlement. "Is something wrong? I'm not responsible for conducting the interviews."

"I've already found a position, so I won't be attending today's interview," Klein explained.

"I see..." Once he understood, Harvin Stone rose and extended his right hand. "Congratulations. It was courteous of you to come in person. I'll inform the professor and senior associate professors."

Klein shook his hand. He was about to exchange a few pleasantries and take his leave when a familiar voice came from behind him.

"Moretti, you found another position?"

Turning, Klein saw an elderly man whose silver hair gave him a striking silhouette. His deep-blue eyes were set far back in a face marked by few wrinkles, and his black tailcoat lent him a sharp, dignified appearance.

"Good afternoon, Mentor. Mr. Azik." Klein greeted them at once. "What brings the two of you here?"

The elderly man was Quentin Cohen, a senior associate professor in Khoy University's history department and Klein's mentor. Beside him stood a bronze-skinned, clean-shaven man of average build with a newspaper in one hand. He had black hair, brown eyes, and gentle features. Yet his gaze held an indescribable weariness, as though he had witnessed the rise and fall of countless ages. A small black mole lay beneath his right ear, easy to miss without looking closely.

Klein recognized him as Mr. Azik, a lecturer in Khoy University's history department who had often helped the original Klein. Azik delighted in debating Senior Associate Professor Cohen. Although their views frequently clashed, the two were close friends; otherwise, they would hardly have sought each other out so often for conversation.

Cohen nodded, his tone relaxed. "Azik and I are here for an academic conference. What position did you find?"

"It's with a security company that searches for, collects, and protects ancient artifacts. They needed a professional consultant and offered me three pounds a week," Klein replied, repeating the explanation he had given Melissa. Then he added, "As you know, I prefer investigating history to merely summarizing it."

Cohen gave a slight nod. "Everyone must choose his own path. I'm glad you took the trouble to inform the university instead of simply failing to appear."

At that moment, Azik interjected, "Klein, do you know what happened to Welch and Naya? The newspaper says they were murdered by burglars."

So the official explanation is armed robbery? And it's already in the papers? Startled, Klein carefully considered his response.

"I don't know the details either. Welch had acquired a diary connected to the Antigonus family of the Solomon Empire from the Fourth Epoch and asked me to help interpret it. I worked with them for the first few days, but then I became occupied with finding employment. The police questioned me two days ago."

He deliberately mentioned the Solomon Empire and the Antigonus family, hoping the two historians might know something useful.

"The Fourth Epoch..." Cohen repeated with a frown.

Azik's weary eyes suddenly lost focus. He drew a sharp breath, then raised the hand holding the newspaper and rubbed his temple.

"Antigonus... It sounds familiar... But why can't I remember?"

Chapter 25: Cathedral

As Azik muttered to himself, he glanced instinctively at Quentin Cohen, as if hoping his friend might offer some clue to jog his memory.

Cohen shook his head without hesitation, his deep-set blue eyes thoughtful. "It means nothing to me."

"Then perhaps I've only heard a word with the same root." Azik lowered his hand and gave a self-deprecating laugh.

Disappointed, Klein couldn't help adding, "Mentor, Mr. Azik, you both know how interested I am in uncovering and reconstructing the history of the Fourth Epoch. If you remember anything—or come across any relevant information—would you please write to me?"

"Of course." The silver-haired senior associate professor seemed especially pleased with Klein after his courteous visit today.

Azik nodded as well. "Is your address still the same?"

"For now. I'll be moving soon, but I'll write to let you know when I do," Klein replied respectfully.

Cohen tapped his black cane against the floor. "It is indeed time you found a place in a better neighborhood."

Klein's gaze fell on the newspaper in Azik's hand. After choosing his words, he asked, "Mentor, Mr. Azik, what did the paper say about Welch and Naya? The officers investigating the case told me very little."

Azik was about to answer when Cohen drew out the pocket watch attached to his black tailcoat by a gold chain.

Click!

He opened the cover, checked the time, and tapped his cane again.

"The meeting is about to begin. We can't delay any longer, Azik. Give Moretti the newspaper."

"All right." Azik handed it to Klein. "We're heading upstairs. Remember to write. Our address hasn't changed—it's still the Khoy University History Department office." He ended with a small laugh, then left the room with Cohen.

Klein removed his hat and bowed. After watching the two gentlemen depart, he said goodbye to Harvin Stone and made his way along the corridor, eventually stepping out of the gray, three-story building.

With his back to the sunlight, he raised his cane under one arm and unfolded the newspaper. Its masthead read: Tingen Morning Post.

Tingen certainly has no shortage of newspapers and magazines... The Morning Post, the Evening Post, the Honest Paper, the Backlund Daily Tribune, the Tussock Times, family magazines, literary reviews...

Several more names surfaced in Klein's memory. Of course, not all were published locally. Steam locomotives carried editions from distant cities.

Advances in papermaking and printing had reduced the price of a newspaper to a single penny, allowing it to reach an ever-growing audience.

Rather than examine every page, Klein quickly turned to the news section and found an article titled "Murder During Armed Burglary."

"According to the police department, the scene at Mr. Welch's residence was appalling. Gold, jewelry, cash, and every portable item of value had been taken. Not a single penny remained. The authorities have reason to believe the crime was committed by a ruthless gang willing to murder innocent witnesses, including Mr. Welch and Miss Naya, to prevent identification.

"This crime shows utter contempt for the laws of our kingdom and constitutes a direct challenge to public order. No citizen should have to fear such an ordeal. Fortunately, the police have identified those responsible and apprehended the principal culprit. We will continue to report on further developments.

"John Browning, correspondent."

So the matter has been resolved—and covered up... Walking beneath the trees, Klein gave an almost imperceptible nod.

He continued down the path, idly reading other reports and serialized stories as he went.

Without warning, the hairs on the back of his neck rose. It felt as though countless needles were pressing against his skin.

Someone is watching me. Observing me... following me?

Thoughts tumbled through his mind as the realization took shape.

Back on Earth, he had occasionally sensed someone's gaze and later discovered who was staring. But the sensation had never been as distinct as it was now.

Nor had anything similar appeared in the original Klein's fragmented memories.

Did transmigrating strengthen my intuition? Or was it that mysterious luck-enhancement ritual?

Klein resisted the urge to search openly for the observer. Drawing on everything he had learned from novels and films, he slowed his pace, lowered the newspaper, and looked toward the Khoy River.

Pretending to admire the scenery, he gradually turned his head from side to side, sweeping his surroundings with his peripheral vision.

There were only trees, lawns, and a few students walking in the distance. No suspicious figure stood out.

Yet Klein was certain someone was watching him.

His heart hammered, sending blood surging through his body.

He raised the newspaper to cover the lower half of his face, afraid his expression might betray him. At the same time, he tightened his grip on his cane and prepared to draw his revolver.

One step. Two steps. Three steps.

Klein continued forward at a measured pace.

The oppressive gaze never left him, but no attack came.

Moving somewhat stiffly beneath the trees, he reached the public carriage stop just as a carriage pulled up.

"Iron—Zout— No. Champagne Street," Klein said, changing his destination twice in quick succession.

He had initially planned to return home, but he couldn't risk leading an unknown pursuer to his apartment. His next impulse was to go straight to Zouteland Street and seek help from the Nighthawks. He rejected that idea as well. If his pursuer's true objective was to locate them, he would be exposing the Nighthawks himself.

So he chose another destination on the spur of the moment.

"Six pence," the conductor said mechanically.

Klein hadn't brought any gold pounds. He had left them hidden in their usual place and carried only two soli. The trip to the university had already cost six pence, leaving him exactly one soli and six pence. He counted out the fare and handed it over.

After boarding, he found a seat. The moment the carriage door closed, the disturbing sense of being watched vanished.

He exhaled slowly. His arms and legs tingled with the aftermath of fear.

What should I do?

What comes next?

Klein stared through the window at the passing streets, desperately trying to devise a plan.

Until he knew the observer's intentions, he had to assume they were hostile.

Possibilities rose one after another, only to be discarded. He had never dealt with anything like this before and needed several minutes to impose order on his thoughts.

I have to alert the Nighthawks. They're the only ones who can truly eliminate the threat.

But I can't go directly to Zouteland Street. That might expose them—and perhaps that's precisely what the observer wants.

Once he followed that line of reasoning, Klein began sketching out several rough possibilities. Gradually, his thoughts grew clearer.

He released a long breath and regained a measure of composure, then watched the scenery rush past with a grave expression.

The journey to Champagne Street passed without incident. But the moment Klein stepped down from the carriage, newspaper and cane in hand, the unsettling gaze returned.

He gave no outward sign that he had noticed. Instead, he walked slowly in the direction of Zouteland Street.

He never entered it. Before reaching the intersection, he turned onto another road leading to Red Moonlight Street. There, beyond a beautiful white plaza, stood a vast cathedral crowned by a pointed roof.

Saint Selena Cathedral—the Tingen headquarters of the Church of Evernight.

There was nothing unusual about a believer attending Mass or offering prayers on his day off.

The cathedral resembled the Gothic churches of Earth. A tall, imposing clock tower of black stone rose above windows patterned with blue and red glass.

Klein entered and followed an aisle toward the prayer hall. Colored light filtered through stained-glass designs along the way. The blue panes were nearly black, while the red ones matched the crimson moon, lending the interior an atmosphere of solemn mystery.

The feeling of being watched disappeared.

Maintaining his composure, Klein continued toward the open prayer hall.

There were no lofty windows here. Darkness dominated the chamber. Yet behind the arched altar, on the wall directly opposite the entrance, some twenty round openings—each the size of a fist—admitted points of brilliant sunlight.

It was like raising one's eyes on a moonless night and suddenly beholding a field of stars, distant and shimmering in all their nobility, purity, and holiness.

Although Klein believed even gods could ultimately be analyzed and understood, he couldn't help lowering his head in that place.

A bishop preached in a gentle voice. Klein moved silently down the central aisle between the two banks of pews, found an empty place near the aisle, and slowly sat.

He leaned his cane against the pew before him, removed his hat, and placed it on his lap atop the folded newspaper. Then he clasped his hands and bowed his head.

Every motion was unhurried and practiced, as though he truly had come only to pray.

Klein closed his eyes and listened to the bishop's voice reverberate through the darkness.

"Without clothing or food, they have no protection from the cold.

"The rain drenches them, and for want of shelter, they huddle against the rocks.

"Orphans are torn from the breast, their hope taken from them. Poverty drives the blameless from the proper path.

"Yet Evernight did not abandon them. She bestowed upon them Her love." [1]

The words echoed through the hall. Darkness filled Klein's vision, and he felt as though both mind and spirit were being washed clean.

He remained there in quiet contemplation until the bishop finished his sermon and brought the Mass to an end.

The bishop then opened the door to a confessional beside the hall. Men and women began forming a line.

Klein opened his eyes and put on his hat. Taking up his cane and newspaper, he rose and joined the queue.

More than twenty minutes passed before his turn came.

He stepped into the confessional and shut the door. Darkness enclosed him once more.

"My child, what do you wish to confess?" the bishop asked from behind the wooden lattice.

Klein took the badge marked "Seventh Unit, Special Operations Department" from his pocket and passed it through the opening.

"Someone is following me. I need to see Dunn Smith." As though the surrounding stillness had seeped into him, his own voice emerged soft and subdued.

The bishop accepted the badge. After several seconds of silence, he said, "When you leave the confessional, turn right and walk to the end. You will find a concealed door in the wall beside you. Enter, and someone will guide you from there."

As he spoke, he pulled a cord inside the booth. Somewhere beyond the wall, a bell chimed to summon a priest.

Klein retrieved his badge, removed his hat, and pressed it to his chest. He bowed slightly, then turned and left.

After confirming that the sensation of being watched had not returned, he settled his half top hat on his head. Keeping his expression calm, he carried his cane to the right and continued until he reached an arched side altar.

There, in the adjacent wall, he found the concealed door. He opened it without a sound and slipped quickly through.

The door closed silently behind him. Beneath the glow of gas lamps stood a middle-aged priest in black robes.

"What is it?" the priest asked tersely.

Klein displayed his badge and repeated what he had told the bishop.

The priest asked no further questions. He turned and walked down the passage in silence.

Klein nodded, removed his hat, and followed with his black cane in hand.

Rozanne had once told him that, at the underground crossroads, the left-hand passage beyond Chanis Gate led all the way to Saint Selena Cathedral.

[1] Adapted from Job 24:8 in the Old Testament.

Chapter 26: Practice

Tap. Tap. Tap.

Footsteps echoed through the otherwise silent, narrow corridor.

Klein kept his back straight and matched the priest's pace. He neither asked questions nor attempted idle conversation, remaining as still and quiet as a windless lake.

After they passed through the heavily guarded passage, the priest unlocked a concealed door and pointed to a stone stairway descending beyond it.

"Turn left at the intersection to reach Chanis Gate."

"May the Goddess bless you." Klein traced the sign of the crimson moon across his chest.

Where ordinary people exchanged polite farewells, members of the Church offered blessings.

"Praise the Lady." The priest returned the gesture.

Without another word, Klein descended the dark stone stairs, guided by the finely crafted gas lamps set into the walls.

Halfway down, he glanced back. The priest still stood at the entrance, motionless in the shadows like a wax figure.

Klein turned away and continued until his feet reached a floor of cold stone slabs. The passage before him led to the underground intersection.

He didn't take the path toward Chanis Gate. Dunn Smith had only recently finished his shift and certainly wouldn't be there.

Instead, he turned right along the familiar passage and climbed another flight of stairs, emerging inside the Blackthorn Security Company.

Several office doors stood either closed or half-open. Rather than interrupt anyone, Klein went to the reception desk, where a brown-haired young woman was engrossed in a magazine, a delighted smile on her face.

"Hello, Rozanne." Klein approached and deliberately rapped his knuckles against the desk.

Knock!

Rozanne sprang upright so violently that she knocked over her chair.

"Hello! Lovely weather today. Y-you—Klein? Why are you here?"

She patted her chest and took several deep breaths, looking like a schoolgirl whose father had caught her neglecting her work.

"I need to see the Captain," Klein answered simply.

"You scared me half to death. I thought the Captain had come out." Rozanne glared at him. "Haven't you learned to knock? Hmph. You should be grateful I'm such a forgiving and kind woman—although I prefer the word 'lady.' Why do you need him? He's in the office across from Mrs. Orianna's."

Despite his unease, Klein couldn't help smiling at her reaction. After a moment's consideration, he replied, "It's a secret."

Rozanne's eyes widened in disbelief. Before she could respond, Klein gave her a slight bow and took his leave.

He passed through the partition beside reception and knocked on the first office door to the right.

"Come in," Dunn Smith called in his deep, gentle voice.

Klein opened the door, stepped inside, and closed it behind him. Removing his hat, he bowed.

"Good morning, Captain."

"Good morning. What can I do for you?"

Dunn's black trench coat and hat hung on a rack nearby. Dressed in a white shirt and black vest, he looked much more refreshed despite his high hairline. His gray eyes remained as deep and unreadable as ever.

"Someone is following me," Klein said without embellishment.

Dunn leaned back and clasped his hands. His dark-gray eyes studied Klein in silence. Rather than asking about the pursuit, he said, "You came through the cathedral?"

"Yes."

Dunn nodded slightly, offering neither praise nor criticism. Then he returned to the matter at hand.

"Welch's father may not believe the cause of death we reported. He could have hired a private investigator from Constant City to look into it."

Constant, one of the three largest cities in the Loen Kingdom, was also known as the City of Wind. Its coal and steel industries were highly developed.

Before Klein could offer an opinion, Dunn continued, "It may also have something to do with the notebook. As it happens, we're investigating how Welch acquired the Antigonus family's notebook. And, of course, we can't rule out the possibility that another person or organization is searching for it."

"What should I do?" Klein asked gravely.

Naturally, he hoped the first explanation was correct.

Dunn didn't answer at once. He lifted his coffee and took a sip, not the slightest ripple visible in his eyes.

"Go back the way you came. Then do whatever you wish."

"Whatever I wish?" Klein repeated.

"Whatever you wish," Dunn confirmed with a nod. "Just don't alert them or break the law."

"Understood." Klein drew a deep breath, said goodbye, and returned underground.

At the intersection, he turned left. Gas lamps shone from both walls as he made his way through the empty, dark, frigid passage.

Every footstep echoed around him, magnifying his isolation and unease.

Before long, he reached the stairway. A shadowy figure waited above—the middle-aged priest.

Neither man spoke. The priest silently turned and led the way.

They soon returned to the prayer hall. The round openings behind the arched altar remained pure and bright, while darkness and silence still filled the vast interior. Men and women continued to line up outside the confessional, though there were fewer of them now.

After waiting a moment, Klein left the prayer hall at an unhurried pace, carrying his cane and newspaper as though nothing unusual had occurred. He walked out of Saint Selena Cathedral without incident.

The moment he stepped into the blazing sunlight, the familiar sensation returned. Once again, he felt like prey beneath a hawk's watchful gaze.

Suddenly, a question occurred to him.

Why didn't the observer follow me into the cathedral? The darkness and the priest helped conceal my temporary disappearance, but couldn't they have continued watching by pretending to pray? If they had nothing to hide, entering openly should have posed no problem. Unless they have a criminal past and fear the Church—or know the bishop might be a Beyonder...

In that case, they're unlikely to be a private investigator.

Klein slowly exhaled. He no longer appeared as tense as before. Adopting a leisurely manner, he wandered around the block and approached the rear of Zouteland Street.

He stopped before an old-fashioned building with mottled walls. A brass number 3 was fixed beside the entrance, beneath a sign reading Zouteland Shooting Club.

Part of the police department's underground firing range had been opened to the public to bring in additional revenue.

The moment Klein entered, the sense of being watched disappeared. Taking advantage of the respite, he showed the attendant his Special Operations Department badge.

After a brief verification, the attendant led him underground to a small, enclosed firing lane.

"Ten-meter target," Klein requested. Then he drew the revolver from his shoulder holster and took the box of brass cartridges from his pocket.

Being targeted without warning had finally made his desire for self-preservation stronger than his tendency to procrastinate. He couldn't wait any longer to practice shooting.

Click!

Once the attendant left, Klein swung out the cylinder and removed the silver demon-hunting rounds. He replaced them with ordinary brass cartridges.

This time he didn't leave an empty chamber as a safety precaution, nor did he remove his formal clothes or half top hat. He intended to train in his everyday attire. After all, an enemy was unlikely to wait politely while he changed into something more comfortable.

Klein snapped the cylinder shut and spun it with his thumb.

He suddenly raised the revolver in both hands, straightened his arms, and aimed at the target more than ten meters away.

He didn't fire immediately. Instead, he recalled his school military training,[1] reviewing how to align the sights and brace himself against the weapon's recoil.

His clothing rustled as he repeatedly raised and lowered the revolver, correcting his stance and aim each time. His concentration rivaled that of a student taking an important examination.

After several repetitions, he retreated to the wall and sat on a long padded bench. He placed the revolver beside him, massaged his arms, and rested for quite some time.

For the next few minutes, he mentally rehearsed what he had practiced. Then he picked up the wooden-gripped revolver with its brass cylinder, assumed a standard firing stance, and pulled the trigger.

Bang!

His arm jerked, and the recoil drove his body backward. The bullet missed the target entirely.

Bang! Bang! Bang!

Adjusting after each shot, he fired until all six chambers were empty.

At least I'm starting to hit the target...

Klein exhaled, stepped back, and sat down once more.

Click!

He swung out the cylinder and let the six spent casings fall to the floor. Without changing expression, he loaded another set of brass cartridges.

After allowing his arm to recover, he rose and returned to the firing line.

Bang! Bang! Bang!

Gunshots rang through the enclosed space, and the paper target trembled with each impact. Klein alternated between practicing and resting until he had expended all thirty new cartridges as well as the five left from before. Gradually, his shots began landing consistently, and he started trying for the bull's-eye.

He rolled his aching shoulders and ejected the final five casings. Lowering his head, he reloaded the revolver with the intricately patterned demon-hunting rounds, leaving one chamber empty to guard against an accidental discharge.

After returning the weapon to his shoulder holster, Klein brushed the dust from his clothes and left the firing range.

The moment he returned to the street, the unseen gaze settled on him again. Calmer now, he walked unhurriedly to Champagne Street, paid four pence for a tracked carriage to Iron Cross Street, and went home.

The sensation of being watched vanished without a trace.

Klein took out his key and opened the apartment door. A short-haired man in a linen shirt sat at the desk, apparently in his late twenties.

Klein's heart tightened, then immediately relaxed.

"Good morning—no, good afternoon, Benson."

The man was Benson Moretti, the elder brother of Klein and Melissa. He was only twenty-five, but his receding hairline and worn appearance made him look nearly thirty.

With black hair and brown eyes, he bore a clear resemblance to Klein, though he lacked his younger brother's faintly scholarly air.

"Good afternoon, Klein. How did the interview go?" Benson rose with a grin.

His black coat and half top hat hung from a peg on the bunk bed.

"Terribly," Klein replied, deadpan.

When Benson stared at him in surprise, Klein laughed and added, "In fact, I didn't attend it at all. I found another position beforehand, and it pays three pounds a week..."

He repeated the explanation he had already given Melissa.

Benson's expression softened. Shaking his head with a laugh, he said, "I feel as though I'm watching a child grow up... Well, it sounds like an excellent position." He sighed. "It's wonderful to come home from a business trip and immediately hear such good news. We should celebrate tonight. Shall we buy some beef?"

"Certainly, but I suspect Melissa will object to the expense." Klein smiled. "We can buy the ingredients this afternoon. We should take at least three soli. Honestly, one pound equals twenty soli, one soli equals twelve pence, and then there are halfpence and quarter-pence besides. The whole system defies reason. It's so needlessly complicated that it must be one of the most foolish currencies in the world."

Benson's expression abruptly turned stern.

An uneasy thought crossed Klein's mind. Had he said something wrong?

Could the gaps in the original Klein's memories be hiding the fact that Benson was a fervent nationalist who tolerated no criticism of the kingdom?

Benson took two steps closer and corrected him gravely.

"Not one of the most foolish. The most foolish."

Not one of them!

Klein froze for a moment before understanding. He met his brother's eyes and burst out laughing.

Indeed, Benson possessed a remarkable talent for deadpan humor.

The corners of Benson's mouth lifted, but he maintained his solemn tone. "You must understand: devising a simple, rational currency requires both arithmetic and a grasp of the decimal system. Unfortunately, those qualities are exceedingly rare among our important personages."

[1] Zhou Mingrui was not a soldier. Students in China commonly undergo short periods of compulsory military training as part of their education.

Chapter 27: Siblings' Dinner

That's remarkably sharp... Klein burst out laughing. Drawing on the wealth of experience from his previous life, he added another barb.

"In fact, there's no evidence that those important personages possess brains at all."

"Excellent! Very good!" Benson roared with laughter and gave him a thumbs-up. "Klein, you've become much funnier than you used to be."

After catching his breath, he continued, "I still have to go to the docks this afternoon. Tomorrow is my only day off. After that, I'll have time to visit the Tingen City Housing Improvement Company with both of you. We'll see whether they have any decent, inexpensive terraced houses for rent. I also need to pay Mr. Franky a visit."

"Our landlord?" Klein asked in confusion.

Does Mr. Franky own terraced houses in better neighborhoods too?

Benson gave him an amused look. "Have you forgotten that we signed a one-year lease? Only six months have passed."

Klein sucked in a breath.

He truly had forgotten!

Although they paid their rent weekly, the contract lasted an entire year. Moving now would constitute a breach. If Mr. Franky took them to court, they might owe substantial compensation.

"You still lack experience with the ways of society." Benson touched his receding black hairline and sighed. "I fought hard to get that clause. Mr. Franky originally wanted to offer us leases of only three months at a time. Landlords are happy to sign one-, two-, or even three-year contracts with wealthy tenants because it guarantees steady income. But with people like us—or rather, like we used to be—and our neighbors, they're always afraid some misfortune will make the rent disappear. So they insist on short-term agreements."

"Which also lets them raise the rent whenever circumstances allow," Klein concluded, combining the original Klein's memories with his own experience as a tenant.

Benson sighed. "That is the cruel reality of modern society. But don't worry. The contract will be easy enough to resolve. Frankly, if we fell even one week behind on rent, Mr. Franky would throw us out immediately and seize whatever valuables he could find. His intelligence ranks below that of a curly-haired baboon. He could never understand anything too complicated."

The remark reminded Klein of a certain Sir Humphrey joke. He shook his head and said with a solemn expression, "No, Benson. You're mistaken."

"How so?" Benson asked, puzzled.

"Mr. Franky's intelligence is slightly higher than a baboon's," Klein replied gravely. Just as Benson began to smile, he added, "On a good day."

"Ha!"

Benson dissolved into helpless laughter. He pointed at Klein, unable to speak for several moments. Only after he had recovered did he return to the matter at hand.

"Naturally, as gentlemen, we cannot resort to such shameless tactics. I'll speak to Mr. Franky tomorrow. Trust me—he's very easy to persuade. Very easy."

Klein had no reason to doubt him. The gas pipes in their apartment were proof enough of Benson's persuasive abilities.

After the brothers chatted for a while, they made soup from the previous night's leftover fried fish and a few vegetables. Steam from the pot softened the rye bread.

They spread a little butter over the bread and ate a simple but deeply satisfying meal. The butter's rich fragrance and faint sweetness lingered long after every bite.

Once Benson left for the docks, Klein took three soli and a handful of loose pennies to the Lettuce and Meat Market. He spent six pence on a pound of beef and seven on a fresh, succulent fish with few bones. He also bought potatoes, peas, radishes, rhubarb, lettuce, turnips, cooking oil, and seasonings such as rosemary, basil, and cumin.

Throughout the trip, he continued to feel the unseen gaze upon him, but his observer made no attempt to approach.

After stopping briefly at Smyrin Bakery, Klein returned home. He improvised weights from books and other heavy objects and began training his arms.

He had intended to practice the military boxing taught during compulsory student training. Unfortunately, he could no longer remember even the calisthenics routines from school, much less a set of boxing movements he had learned only briefly. Resigned, he settled for simpler exercises.

Klein was careful not to overexert himself. Exhaustion would only make him more vulnerable. After a reasonable rest, he began reviewing the original Klein's notes and course materials, searching again for anything related to the Fourth Epoch.

That evening, Benson and Melissa sat side by side at the desk, the dishes arranged neatly before them like well-behaved schoolchildren.

Their aromas blended into a rich and inviting harmony: the mouthwatering fragrance of stewed beef, the mellow warmth of tender potatoes, the sweetness of thick pea soup, the mild tang of stewed rhubarb, and the comforting scent of rye bread spread with butter.

Benson swallowed as he watched Klein transfer a crisp, golden fish onto a plate. The aroma of hot oil seemed to travel through his nose, down his throat, and straight into his empty stomach.

Growl!

His stomach voiced its complaint loud and clear.

With his sleeves rolled up, Klein carried the fried fish to the freshly cleaned desk and set it in the center. Then he returned to the cupboard, brought out two large cups of ginger beer, and placed them before Benson's seat and his own.

Smiling at Melissa, he produced a lemon pudding with the flourish of a stage magician.

"We'll drink beer, and you can have this."

"Thank you." Melissa accepted the pudding.

At the sight, Benson raised his cup and smiled. "To Klein finding a respectable position."

Klein lifted his own cup and clinked it first against Benson's, then against Melissa's pudding dish.

"Praise the Lady!"

He tipped his head back and drank. The spicy liquid warmed his throat and left a pleasant aftertaste.

Despite its name, ginger beer contained no alcohol. The combination of sharp ginger and tart lemon merely produced a flavor reminiscent of beer. Women and children could drink it freely, though Melissa disliked the taste.

"Praise the Lady!" Benson took a long swallow as Melissa nibbled at her lemon pudding. She chewed slowly before reluctantly forcing herself to swallow.

"Try everything." Klein put down his cup, picked up his fork and spoon, and gestured toward the spread.

He had the least confidence in the thick pea soup. Zhou Mingrui had never eaten anything quite like it on Earth and could only reconstruct the recipe from the original Klein's fragmented memories.

As the eldest brother, Benson felt no need to wait on ceremony. He scooped up a spoonful of mashed potatoes and put it straight into his mouth.

The potatoes had been cooked until perfectly soft, then mixed with a trace of lard and just enough salt. The flavor immediately awakened his appetite.

"Not... bad. Not bad at all," Benson praised around the mouthful. "Much better than what they served us at work. They used nothing but butter."

It is one of my specialties, after all... Klein accepted the compliment with satisfaction.

"I learned it from the cook at Welch's house."

Melissa studied the beef stew. Green basil leaves, lettuce, and radishes floated in the clear broth, partly concealing the tender pieces of meat. Its aroma was difficult to resist.

She speared a piece of beef with her fork and began to chew. Though stewed until tender, it retained a pleasant firmness. Salt, the sweetness of radish, and the sharp fragrance of basil enhanced the meat's savory flavor.

Melissa offered no verbal opinion. She simply kept chewing, which amounted to approval in itself.

Klein tried a piece. It was delicious, but he still found it lacking compared with what he could once have made. Several seasonings were unavailable, forcing him to rely on imperfect substitutes. Naturally, the final flavor wasn't quite the same.

Of course, even at his best, his home cooking had never been more than reasonably good.

He suddenly felt a pang of sympathy for Benson and Melissa, whose limited experience had left their culinary standards tragically underdeveloped.

After swallowing the beef, Klein took a piece of fried Tussock fish seasoned with cumin and rosemary. The outside was crisp and golden-brown, while the flesh within remained tender. Salt and the rich aroma of oil blended beautifully.

Nodding in satisfaction, he followed it with a bite of stewed rhubarb. Its tartness was pleasant and cut through the richness of the meat.

At last, he gathered his courage and served himself a bowl of thick pea soup.

Too sweet—and too sour...

Klein couldn't help frowning.

Yet Benson and Melissa both appeared to enjoy it. For a moment, he began to question his own palate. He quickly drank some ginger beer to clear the taste from his tongue.

By the end of the meal, all three siblings were thoroughly stuffed. They slumped in their chairs for quite some time, too content to move.

"Let us praise the Lady once more!" Benson raised his cup, which held only a final mouthful of ginger beer.

"Praise the Lady!" Klein drained the rest of his drink.

"Praise the Lady." Melissa finally placed the last bite of lemon pudding in her mouth and savored it as long as she could.

Watching her, Klein took advantage of his slight giddiness and smiled.

"Melissa, you're doing it wrong. You should eat your favorite thing first, while you're still hungry enough to appreciate it fully. Saving it until you're already stuffed does the food an injustice."

"No. It's just as delicious now," Melissa replied with characteristic firmness.

The siblings chatted happily while their meal settled. Then they cleared the dishes and silverware and carefully stored the oil used to fry the fish.

Once the chores were finished, it was time to study. Benson refreshed his knowledge of accounting while Klein continued reviewing notes and course materials. Not a minute went to waste.

At eleven o'clock, the siblings washed, extinguished the gas lamp, and went to bed.

Klein drifted in a haze, staring into the darkness before him. Without warning, a figure in a black trench coat and half top hat appeared in his vision.

It was Dunn Smith.

"Captain!" Klein jolted alert, instantly realizing that he was dreaming.

Dunn's gray eyes remained calm, as though he were mentioning a matter of no importance.

"Someone has entered your room. Take your revolver and force him into the corridor. Leave the rest to us."

Someone entered my room? Has the observer finally made his move?

Fear surged through Klein, but he didn't dare waste time asking questions. He merely nodded.

"Understood!"

The scene before him transformed at once, colors spreading and bursting like soap bubbles.

Klein opened his eyes and carefully turned his head toward the window.

A thin, unfamiliar man stood with his back to the bed, silently searching through the desk.

Chapter 28: Secret Order

Thump! Thump! Thump!

Klein's heart pounded violently, each contraction sending a tremor through his body.

For an instant, fear drove every thought from his mind. Then the shadowy intruder suddenly stopped and cocked his head, listening for any change in the room.

Klein's mind began working again. He reached beneath his pillow and closed his fingers around the revolver's wooden grip.

Its firm, smooth texture steadied him at once. Moving slowly and without a sound, he drew the weapon and aimed at the back of the intruder's head.

In truth, Klein had little confidence that he could hit the man. He had only just learned to strike a stationary target with some consistency, and a living opponent was an entirely different matter. He wasn't arrogant enough to confuse the two.

However, he vaguely remembered an observation from his previous life: a nuclear weapon was most powerful before it was launched.

The same principle applied now. His revolver was at its most intimidating before he fired it.

As long as Klein didn't pull the trigger and miss, the intruder couldn't know that he was a complete novice. Fear and uncertainty would force the man to hesitate and restrain himself.

The thought made Klein decisive. He wasn't someone who naturally became calmer in the face of danger, but he had already imagined this confrontation many times and resolved to rely on intimidation rather than attack.

As the old saying from the Foodaholic Empire went: preparedness averts peril.

The moment the revolver settled on him, the thin man went rigid, as though he had sensed the threat behind him.

Then he heard a voice touched by quiet amusement.

"Good evening, sir."

The intruder clasped his hands together, his entire body tensing. Sitting upright on the lower bunk, Klein kept the revolver trained on the man's head and made his voice as casual as possible.

"Please raise both hands and turn around—slowly. To be honest, I'm rather timid and easily startled. If you move too quickly, I might panic, and then I can't promise the gun won't go off. Yes, that's right. Nice and slow."

The thin man lifted his hands beside his head and began turning a fraction at a time. Klein first saw a close-fitting black suit fastened with orderly rows of buttons, then a pair of thick, sharply angled brown eyebrows.

There was no fear in the intruder's deep-blue eyes. He stared at Klein with the intensity of a cornered predator, as though a single lapse in concentration would let him spring forward and tear Klein apart.

Klein tightened his grip and did his best to appear calm and indifferent.

Only when the man fully faced him did Klein tilt his chin toward the door.

"Let's continue this conversation outside, sir. There's no need to disturb anyone's pleasant dreams. Keep your movements slow, if you please—and tread lightly. It's a matter of basic courtesy for a gentleman."

The intruder's cold gaze swept over him. Keeping both hands raised, he walked slowly toward the door.

With the revolver still pointed at him, he turned the handle and eased it open.

The moment the door was halfway ajar, he abruptly dropped low and rolled into the corridor. A gust caught the door and slammed it shut behind him.

"Mm..." Benson stirred on the upper bunk, hovering at the edge of wakefulness.

At that moment, a slow and tranquil melody drifted in from outside. A rich, soothing voice began to sing.

"Oh, terror threatens, crimson hope still cries;

"One truth remains: this fleeting lifetime flies.

"One thing is certain—all the rest are lies;

"The flower blooms but once, then fades and dies." [1]

The verse seemed imbued with the power to soothe and relax. Benson sank back into sleep, as did Melissa in the next room.

Peace and stillness settled over Klein's mind as well. He nearly yawned.

The thin man had moved with such agility that Klein hadn't even had time to react.

Staring at the closed door, he smiled and muttered, "You may not believe this, but pulling the trigger wouldn't have fired a bullet."

The hammer had been resting over the empty chamber he left as protection against an accidental discharge.

With the midnight poem continuing outside, Klein waited patiently for the battle to end.

Within a minute, the serene melody—like moonlight reflected on a still lake—came to an end. Profound silence reclaimed the night.

Klein quietly rotated the cylinder, moving a live round beneath the hammer, and waited for news.

Ten uneasy minutes passed. Just as he began wondering whether he should investigate, Dunn Smith's steady, reassuring voice came through the door.

"It's over."

Klein exhaled in relief. Still holding the revolver, he took his key and approached the door barefoot. He opened it cautiously and found Dunn standing outside in his black trench coat and half top hat, his deep-gray eyes calm and composed.

Klein closed the door behind him and followed Dunn to the end of the corridor, where they stood in the dim crimson moonlight.

"It took me some time to enter his dream," Dunn said, gazing out at the red moon.

"Did you learn who he is?" Klein finally felt able to relax.

Dunn nodded. "He belongs to an ancient organization known as the Secret Order. It was founded during the Fourth Epoch and has ties to the Solomon Empire and several noble houses that fell during that era. The Antigonus family's notebook came from them. One of their members was careless, allowing it to enter the antiques market, where Welch acquired it. They had no choice but to send people after it."

He paused briefly before continuing, anticipating Klein's next question.

"We'll use the clues he provided to pursue the other members in the area, though I doubt the operation will end perfectly. Those people hide as well as rats in a sewer. At the very least, they'll assume that we possess the Antigonus notebook—or that we've uncovered an important lead. Unless the matter is absolutely vital, they'll abandon their efforts. That's how the Secret Order survives."

"And if the notebook is absolutely vital?" Klein asked uneasily.

Dunn smiled but didn't answer. Instead, he said, "We know very little about the Secret Order. Tonight's success was possible because you kept your wits about you. The credit belongs to you. Considering the remaining danger—and the fact that enhanced perception might help us locate the notebook—you've earned a choice."

"A choice?" Klein had a vague idea of what Dunn meant. His breathing grew heavier despite himself.

Dunn's smile vanished. He spoke with complete seriousness.

"Do you wish to become a Beyonder? If so, you may choose only from the starting Sequences of incomplete pathways.

"Of course, you can decline and retain the merit you've earned. Once an opening becomes available, you could then use it to become a Sleepless—the first Sequence of the complete pathway bestowed upon the Nighthawks by the Goddess."

Just as I thought...

Delight surged through Klein. Without hesitation, he asked, "Which Sequence 9 potions can I choose from?"

I need all the details before deciding whether to accept—and which one to take.

Dunn turned, the crimson moonlight draping him like a veil. Looking directly into Klein's eyes, he answered slowly.

"Apart from Sleepless, the Church possesses three Sequence 9 potion formulas. The first is Mystery Pryer, the Sequence Old Neil follows. Rozanne has probably mentioned that already. She can never keep anything to herself."

Klein gave an awkward smile, unsure how to respond. Fortunately, Dunn didn't appear to care.

"We obtained the Mystery Pryer formula—and the later Sequences we possess, which do not form an unbroken chain—from the Moses Ascetic Order. At the time, the order supposedly had not yet fallen into corruption. Its members adhered strictly to their principles and precepts, devoted themselves to the pursuit of knowledge, and guarded their secrets with extraordinary care.

"Anyone who entered the order and became a Mystery Pryer was forbidden to speak for five years. By cultivating silence, they learned to strengthen their focus. The Moses Ascetic Order's maxim—'Do as you wish, but do no harm'—originated in that period.

"Mystery Pryers possess broad but rudimentary knowledge of magic, witchcraft, astrology, and other mystical disciplines. They also learn a fair number of rituals. However, they can easily perceive certain beings concealed within the material world. Because of that, they must exercise caution and remain in awe of Beyonder powers.

"Large portions of the pathway are missing from our records, so the chain is incomplete. We don't have Sequence 8, for example, though the Holy Cathedral may possess it."

That satisfies nearly everything I'm looking for...

Klein nodded slightly, already tempted to choose it. Fortunately, he remembered that two options remained.

"What are the others?"

"The second is called Corpse Collector. Many cultists who worship Death on the Southern Continent choose it. After taking the potion, unintelligent spirits of the dead mistake the Beyonder for one of their own and will not attack. Corpse Collectors gain resistance to cold, decay, and the corrosive effects of cadaveric auras. They can directly perceive certain evil spirits, identify the traits and weaknesses of undead creatures, and receive some physical enhancement.

"We also possess the Sequence 8 and Sequence 7 formulas that follow it. You can probably guess the latter—Spirit Medium. That was Daly's choice."

Spirit Medium certainly sounds mysterious and impressive, but what I want most is knowledge of mysticism...

Klein didn't interrupt. He simply listened.

Dunn looked toward the crimson moon once more.

"We possess only Sequence 9 of the third pathway. I don't know whether the Holy Cathedral has the later formulas. It is called Seer."

Seer?

Klein's pupils contracted as he remembered the regret Emperor Roselle had recorded in his diary.

He had regretted not choosing Apprentice, Marauder, or Seer!

[1] Adapted from Edward FitzGerald's English translation of the Rubaiyat of Omar Khayyam.

Chapter 29: "Jobs" and Rentals Are Serious Business

Doing his best to appear as he normally did, Klein asked with genuine curiosity, "What abilities do Seers possess?"

"That isn't quite the right question," Dunn Smith replied with a chuckle. "You should ask what abilities the Seer potion grants."

He turned away from the moon, and shadows concealed both his face and his gray eyes.

"The field encompasses many disciplines: astromancy, cartomancy, spiritual pendulums, scrying, and more. Of course, drinking the potion won't instantly teach you all of them. It merely gives you the aptitude and capacity to learn.

"Seers have few direct ways to fight an enemy. As you can imagine, a magical ritual requires too much preparation to be useful in ordinary combat. In exchange, a Seer possesses broader and more specialized mystical knowledge than a Mystery Pryer."

That meets my needs too... But having no direct means of fighting is a serious drawback. And the Church of Evernight probably doesn't possess the later Sequences. By "Holy Cathedral," he likely means the Church's headquarters, the Cathedral of Serenity...

Then again, at low Sequences, can a Beyonder's offensive abilities even compare with a firearm?

Klein sank into thought, weighing Mystery Pryer against Seer again and again. He had already dismissed Corpse Collector from consideration.

Dunn smiled when he saw Klein's hesitation.

"You don't need to decide tonight. Give me your answer Monday morning. Whether you select a Sequence or decline the opportunity, none of the Nighthawks will think any less of you.

"Calm yourself and listen to your heart."

With that, Dunn removed his hat and bowed slightly. He passed Klein at an unhurried pace and headed toward the stairs.

Klein offered no immediate response. He merely bowed and watched Dunn leave.

He had longed to become a Beyonder, yet now that the opportunity stood before him, he found himself torn. The missing later Sequences, the possibility of losing control, the uncertain reliability of Emperor Roselle's diaries, and the phantom murmurs capable of driving a person mad—all combined into a vast chasm blocking his path.

Klein drew a deep breath and slowly released it.
