"No matter how difficult this is, it can't be worse than asking an eighteen-year-old student to choose his entire career..." he muttered with a self-deprecating laugh.

Gathering his scattered thoughts, he quietly opened the door, returned to bed, and lay down.

His eyes remained open as he stared at the underside of the upper bunk, faintly stained by crimson moonlight.

Outside, a drunk staggered down the road, and a carriage rattled through the empty streets. Rather than disturbing the stillness, the distant sounds made the night seem deeper and more remote.

As his emotions settled, Klein remembered his life on Earth: the exercise he had enjoyed; his loud-voiced father; his mother, who always kept herself busy despite her chronic illness; the friends with whom he had grown from playing soccer and basketball to video games and mahjong; and the person to whom he had once confessed his feelings, only to be rejected.

The memories flowed like a silent river. They held no great drama or overwhelming sentiment, yet little by little, they submerged his heart.

Perhaps people only learned to cherish what they had after losing it.

By the time the crimson moonlight faded and the rising sun painted the sky gold, Klein had made his decision.

He climbed out of bed and went to the public washroom, splashing his face with cold water to wake himself fully. Then he took a one-soli note to Mrs. Wendy's and spent nine pence on eight pounds of rye bread, replacing the staple food consumed during the previous night's feast.

"Bread prices are finally beginning to stabilize," he remarked after breakfast while Benson changed clothes.

It was Sunday, which meant that both Klein and Melissa could finally rest.

Already dressed to go out, Klein sat in a chair leafing through the old newspaper he had brought home the day before.

"Here's a rental listing," he said in surprise. "Number 3 Wendel Street in the North Borough. A two-story detached house with six bedrooms, three bathrooms, and two large balconies upstairs. Downstairs, there's a dining room, a parlor, a kitchen, two more bathrooms, and two guest rooms, plus a cellar belowground. The house has two acres of private grounds in front and a small garden behind. One-, two-, and three-year leases are available for one pound and six soli per week. Interested parties should contact Mr. Gusev on Champagne Street."

"That can be our goal for the future." Benson put on his black half top hat and smiled. "Properties advertised in the newspaper tend to be overpriced. The Tingen City Housing Improvement Company offers homes nearly as good for considerably less."

"Why don't we try the Tingen Housing Improvement Association for the Working Class?" Melissa emerged from her room carrying an old hat with a veil. She had changed into a grayish-white dress that had been mended several times.

Her quiet, reserved manner couldn't hide her youth.

Benson laughed. "Where did you hear about the Tingen Housing Improvement Association for the Working Class? From Jenny? Mrs. Rochelle? Or your good friend Selena?"

Melissa looked away and answered softly, "Mrs. Rochelle. I happened to meet her while washing up last night. She asked about Klein's interview, so I told her more or less what had happened. She suggested the association."

Noticing Klein's puzzled expression, Benson shook his head in amusement.

"It serves the poor—or, to be precise, the lowest levels of society. The association builds and renovates housing with shared bathrooms and offers only three basic arrangements: one room, two rooms, or three. Do you really want to continue living under those conditions?

"The Tingen City Housing Improvement Company works in the same field, but it also provides homes for the lower middle class. Strictly speaking, our income now places us somewhat above that group, but we're still far from living like a true middle-class family. The problem isn't our wages. We simply haven't had time to accumulate any savings."

Understanding dawned on Klein. He folded the newspaper, picked up his top hat, and stood.

"Then let's go."

"If I remember correctly, the Tingen City Housing Improvement Company is on Daffodil Street." Benson opened the door. "Like the Working Class Housing Improvement Association, it belongs to a category known as Five Percent Charities. Do you know why?"

"No." Klein raised his cane and moved beside Melissa.

The black-haired girl shook her head as well.

Leading the way out, Benson explained, "Housing improvement associations and companies like these originated in Backlund. Their financing comes from three sources. First, they solicit donations from charitable foundations. Second, they submit funding proposals and receive money from a government commission at a preferential four-percent rate. Third, they accept private investment and use part of the rent to provide investors with a five-percent return. That's why they're called Five Percent Charities."

The siblings descended the stairs and began walking toward Daffodil Street. They intended to secure a new home before speaking with their present landlord, Mr. Franky. None of them wanted to be forced out before they had somewhere else to live.

"Selena told me that some housing improvement organizations operate entirely as charities," Melissa said thoughtfully.

Benson chuckled. "They do. The Deweyville Trust, established with a donation from Sir Deweyville, is one example. It builds apartments for working-class families and employs dedicated property managers while charging very little rent. The requirements, however, are extremely strict."

"You don't sound very enthusiastic about it," Klein observed with a smile.

"No, I have great respect for Sir Deweyville. But I'm certain he has no idea what true poverty looks like. Living in one of his apartments is like accepting hope from a priest—comforting, but not especially practical. Tenants must receive all standard vaccinations and take turns cleaning the bathrooms. They may neither sublet rooms nor conduct business from their homes. They can't discard rubbish wherever they please, and children aren't permitted to play in the corridors. Goddess, does he expect to turn every tenant into a lady or gentleman?" Benson asked in his usual wry tone.

Klein frowned in confusion. "None of that sounds unreasonable. Those are perfectly sensible requirements."

"I agree," Melissa said with a nod.

Benson turned his head to study them, then gave a quiet laugh.

"Perhaps I've protected the two of you too well. You haven't seen genuine poverty. Do you imagine those families can afford the standard vaccinations? The waiting list for free treatment through charitable organizations is three months long.

"Do you think they all have permanent employment rather than temporary work? If they can't sublet part of the apartment for extra income, what happens when they lose their jobs? Must they immediately move out? Many women survive by mending clothes or making matchboxes at home, but those count as commercial activities. Should they all be evicted?

"Most poor families spend every ounce of strength simply trying to survive. Do you think they have time to supervise their children and keep them from running through the corridors? Their only alternative may be to lock them indoors until they're old enough to work, then send them to a place that hires children at seven or eight."

Benson used no dramatic language, yet his plain description sent a faint shiver through Klein.

Is that really how the poorest people live?

Beside him, Melissa fell silent. A long while passed before she said in a distant voice, "Jenny stopped wanting me to visit after her family moved to Lower Street."

"Let's hope her father recovers from his injury and finds steady work," Benson replied with a somber laugh. "But I've seen too many men turn to drink so they don't have to feel what their lives have become..."

Klein had no answer. Neither, it seemed, did Melissa.

The three siblings continued silently along Daffodil Street until they found the offices of the Tingen City Housing Improvement Company.

An amiable middle-aged man came forward to assist them. Rather than formal clothes and a hat, he wore a white shirt and black vest.

"You may call me Scarter. What sort of home are you looking for?" His smile broadened when he noticed Klein's silver-inlaid cane.

Klein glanced at Benson, who was far better at negotiation, and gestured for him to answer.

"A terraced house," Benson said directly.

Scarter leafed through the files in his hands before smiling again.

"At present, five are available. To be honest, most of our work serves laborers and their families—people facing severe overcrowding, with six, eight, or even ten or twelve individuals squeezed into a single dwelling. We maintain relatively few terraced houses. There is one at Number 2 Daffodil Street, another in the North Borough, one in the East Borough... Weekly rents range from twelve to sixteen soli. You'll find the details here."

He handed the document to Benson, Klein, and Melissa.

After reading it, the siblings exchanged a look. All three pointed to the same listing at once.

"Let's see Number 2 Daffodil Street first," Benson said.

Klein and Melissa nodded. It was in a neighborhood they already knew well.

Chapter 30: A Brand-New Beginning

Numbers 2, 4, and 6 Daffodil Street formed a row of terraced houses with many-sided hipped roofs. Their exteriors were painted a muted blue-gray, and three chimneys rose above them.

There were no lawns, gardens, or porches. Each front door opened directly onto the street.

Scarter of the Tingen City Housing Improvement Company selected a key from a large ring and unlocked Number 2.

"Our terraced houses don't have entrance halls, so the front door opens directly into the living room. The oriel window faces Daffodil Street and provides excellent light..."

Klein, Benson, and Melissa entered to find a fabric sofa bathed in golden sunlight and a room noticeably larger than the entire living area of their old two-bedroom apartment.

"This room can also serve as a parlor for guests. The dining area is to the right, and the fireplace on the left will keep the house warm during winter." Scarter pointed out each feature with practiced ease.

Klein surveyed the room and recognized a simple open-plan layout. No wall or partition separated the living and dining areas, though the latter stood far enough from the oriel window to be rather dim.

A rectangular table of reddish wood occupied the dining space, surrounded by six hardwood chairs with padded seats. The fireplace set into the left wall looked exactly like those Klein remembered from foreign films and television dramas.

"The kitchen is behind the dining area, but no kitchen equipment is included. Across from the living room, you'll find a small guest room and a bathroom..." Scarter continued through the ground floor, explaining the layout as they went.

The bathroom was divided into two sections. The outer portion held the washbasin, while a folding door separated it from the toilet within. Scarter described the guest room as small, but it was every bit as large as Melissa's present bedroom. She stared at it in astonishment.

Once they had inspected the ground floor, Scarter led the three siblings to the staircase beside the bathroom.

"The cellar is below us. It can become stuffy, so always air it out before going down."

Benson nodded absently and followed him upstairs.

"There's a bathroom to my left, followed by two bedrooms. The right side has the same arrangement, except that its bathroom stands beside the balcony."

Scarter opened the bathroom door and stepped aside so Klein, Benson, and Melissa could see inside.

This bathroom included a bathtub. As downstairs, a folding door separated the toilet. The room was dusty but neither filthy, foul-smelling, nor cramped.

Melissa stood transfixed until Scarter moved on to the adjoining bedroom. Only then did she slowly follow the others.

After a few steps, she looked back once more.

Even Klein, with all the experiences of his former life, felt delighted. Their landlord had regularly supervised the cleaning of the communal bathroom, yet it was never truly clean and often smelled nauseating. Worse still, a line always seemed to form whenever someone urgently needed it.

The second bathroom was much the same. Of the four bedrooms, one was slightly larger and furnished with a bookcase. The other three were roughly equal in size, and each contained a bed, desk, and wardrobe.

"The balcony is quite small, so you won't be able to dry many clothes there at once." At the end of the corridor, Scarter pointed toward a locked door. "The property has full underground drainage, gas pipes, a meter, and all other essential facilities. It should suit two gentlemen and a young lady very well. The weekly rent is thirteen soli, plus five pence for use of the furniture. A deposit equal to four weeks' rent is also required."

Before Benson could respond, Klein looked around curiously and asked, "Approximately how much would a house like this cost to buy?"

As someone from the Foodaholic Empire, he retained an almost instinctive desire to own property.

Benson and Melissa both turned toward him in shock, as though he had suddenly transformed into a monster.

Scarter answered with calm certainty. "Buy it? We don't sell our properties. We rent them."

"I only wanted a general idea of the market price," Klein explained awkwardly.

Scarter hesitated before replying. "Last month, the owner of Number 11 Daffodil Street sold a fifteen-year leasehold on a similar house for three hundred pounds. That's considerably cheaper than renting it by the week, but few people can produce such a large sum at once. The asking price for the freehold was eight hundred and fifty pounds."

Eight hundred and fifty pounds?

Klein quickly calculated in his head.

I earn three pounds a week, and Benson earns one pound ten soli. Rent will be thirteen soli. If we eat well every day, food alone will cost nearly two pounds a week. Then there are clothes, transportation, social obligations, and countless other expenses. We'd save less than twenty soli each week—perhaps thirty-five pounds in an entire year.

At that rate, eight hundred and fifty pounds would take more than twenty years. Even the fifteen-year leasehold would require eight or nine years of saving. And that ignores marriage, separate households, children, travel...

In a world without personal mortgages, it's no wonder most people rent.

Having reached that conclusion, Klein stepped back and glanced at Benson, signaling that it was time for him to negotiate.

As for Melissa, the light in her eyes made her opinion perfectly clear.

Klein suddenly looked forward to seeing Benson unleashed.

Tapping his plain cane against the floor, Benson surveyed the house and said, "We should inspect the other properties first. The dining area receives very little light, and the balcony is far too small. Only one bedroom has a fireplace, and the furniture is badly dated. If we moved in, at least half of it would need replacing..."

He delivered a rapid inventory of flaws. Ten minutes later, Scarter had agreed to reduce the rent to twelve soli per week, lower the furniture fee to three pence, and round the deposit down to two pounds.

Without further delay, the siblings returned with Scarter to the offices of the Tingen City Housing Improvement Company and signed two copies of the lease. They then visited the Tingen City Notary Office to have the agreement notarized.

After paying the deposit and the first week's rent, Klein and Benson had a combined total of nine pounds, two soli, and eight pence remaining.

Now they stood before the door of Number 2 Daffodil Street, each holding a set of copper keys. For several moments, none of them could look away. Emotion churned beneath the silence.

"It feels like a dream..." Melissa finally raised her head and looked at the future Moretti residence, her voice low and unsteady.

Benson released a long breath and smiled.

"Then don't wake up."

Klein was less overwhelmed than his siblings. He nodded toward the entrance.

"We should replace the locks on the front and balcony doors as soon as possible."

"There's no hurry. The Tingen City Housing Improvement Company has an excellent reputation. The money we have left is for your formal suit. But first, we need to visit Mr. Franky." Benson pointed back toward Iron Cross Street.

The siblings ate a makeshift lunch of rye bread at their old apartment, then walked to the terraced building where their landlord lived. When they knocked, the short Mr. Franky remained perched imperiously on his sofa and declared, "You know my rules. No one is permitted to fall behind on the rent!"

Benson leaned forward with a smile.

"Mr. Franky, we've come to terminate our lease."

He's going straight to the point? Can that possibly work?

Standing beside his brother, Klein was stunned. On the way there, Benson had said he was willing to pay no more than twelve soli in compensation.

"Terminate it? No!" Franky glared and waved his arms. "We have a contract, and six months remain!"

Benson regarded him seriously. After a deliberate pause, he said in a calm voice, "Mr. Franky, you must understand that you could be earning considerably more."

"More?" Franky touched his narrow face with sudden interest.

Benson sat straighter and explained with a smile, "You've rented the two-room apartment to the three of us for five soli and six pence. But suppose you let it to a family of five or six, with two or three wage earners among them. They would gladly pay more to live here rather than on crime-ridden Lower Street. Five soli and ten pence would be reasonable—perhaps even six soli."

Franky's eyes brightened, and his throat bobbed as Benson continued.

"Besides, you surely know that rents have risen in recent years. The longer we remain under the old agreement, the more money you lose."

"But... I'll need time to find another tenant." Franky, who had inherited the apartment building, was plainly captivated by the idea.

"With your ability and connections, I'm sure you'll find someone almost immediately. Two days, perhaps three. We'll compensate you for that brief loss. You may keep our three-soli deposit. That's more than fair."

Benson had already made the decision on Franky's behalf.

The landlord nodded in satisfaction. "Benson, you are a remarkably honest and conscientious young man. Very well. We'll sign an agreement terminating the lease."

Klein watched in amazement. Now he fully understood what Benson had meant when he said Mr. Franky was easy to "convince."

That was absurdly easy...

With the old lease resolved, the siblings first helped Klein purchase his formal attire, then began moving.

They owned nothing especially large or heavy; the bulky furniture belonged to their landlord. Benson and Melissa therefore rejected Klein's suggestion that they hire a carriage. Instead, the three of them carried everything themselves, making repeated trips between Iron Cross Street and Daffodil Street.

By the time the blazing sun sank toward the west, golden rays streamed through the oriel window and spread across Klein's desk.

He arranged his books and notebooks neatly on the shelves, then placed an ink bottle and fountain pen on the table he had wiped clean earlier.

At last, it's finished...

He breathed a sigh of relief as his stomach growled. Unrolling his sleeves, he walked toward the door.

He had a room of his own now, and a bed of his own. The sheets and blanket were white—old, but clean.

Klein turned the knob and stepped into the hall. Before he could speak, the two doors opposite his opened at the same moment, revealing Benson and Melissa.

Klein and Benson took one look at the dust and grime covering each other's faces and burst out laughing with unrestrained delight.

Melissa bit her lip, but their laughter proved contagious. A moment later, she began to laugh softly too.

...

The following morning, Klein stood before a flawless full-length mirror and carefully smoothed the collar and cuffs of his new shirt.

His outfit consisted of a white shirt, black tailcoat, silk top hat, black vest, matching trousers, boots, and a bow tie. The full ensemble had cost eight pounds—a price that still made him wince.

The result, however, was excellent. The man in the mirror looked more scholarly than before and distinctly more handsome.

Click!

Klein closed his pocket watch and slipped it into an inner pocket. Then he took up his cane, concealed his revolver, and rode a tracked public carriage to Zouteland Street.

Just before entering the Blackthorn Security Company, he realized that old habits had made him forget to give Melissa extra transportation money. He had allowed her to walk to school as usual.

Shaking his head, he made a mental note to correct that oversight and stepped inside.

At reception, the brown-haired Rozanne was making coffee, filling the office with its rich aroma.

"Good morning, Klein. Lovely weather today," she greeted him with a smile. "I've always wondered—don't you men feel unbearably hot in formal clothes when the weather is like this? I know Tingen's summers aren't as warm as those in the south, but summer is still summer."

"It's the price one pays for style," Klein replied lightly. "Good morning, Miss Rozanne. Where is the Captain?"

"The usual place." Rozanne pointed toward the offices beyond the partition.

Klein nodded, walked through, and knocked on Dunn Smith's door.

"Come in." Dunn's voice was as deep and gentle as ever.

When he saw Klein in his handsome new formal clothes, Dunn nodded. A hint of a smile appeared in his gray eyes.

"Have you decided?"

Klein drew a deep breath and answered with complete seriousness, "Yes. I've made my choice."

Dunn slowly straightened in his chair. His expression became solemn, though the depths of his gray eyes remained unchanged.

"Then tell me."

Without hesitation, Klein replied, "Seer."

Chapter 31: Potion

Dunn Smith stared at Klein for a full minute without speaking.

Klein didn't shrink beneath the weight of his silence or avert his gaze. He continued looking directly into Dunn's gray eyes.

"You must understand that once you drink the potion, there will be no room for regret," Dunn finally said, his deep voice devoid of emotion.

Klein smiled. "I understand. But I choose to respect the voice within me."

Sleepless didn't meet his needs. From the description he had heard during the Tarot Club gathering, neither did Spectator. He had no idea when he might encounter another Beyonder pathway, and a distant solution couldn't resolve an immediate crisis. Waiting made no sense.

For the same reason, he had eliminated Corpse Collector, leaving only Mystery Pryer and Seer.

Assuming that potions of the same Sequence carried roughly equal danger, both remaining choices satisfied his basic requirements. With no additional information to distinguish between them, Emperor Roselle's words were enough to tip the balance. Whether Roselle had spoken casually or truly regretted not choosing Apprentice, Marauder, or Seer, his remark still carried weight.

Moreover, the diary suggested that understanding the true principles of digestion and acting could substantially reduce a potion's harmful effects. As for the whispers and phantom temptations capable of corrupting a person or driving them insane, Klein had already encountered those without being a Beyonder.

"Very well." Dunn stood, picked up his half top hat, and put it on. "Come downstairs with me."

Klein nodded and offered a gentleman's bow of gratitude.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

They descended together, their footsteps echoing through the vast, silent stairwell and underground passage.

Anxiety gradually took hold of Klein, prompting him to find a subject for conversation.

"Captain, you said the potion won't directly grant me the corresponding mystical knowledge. It will only give me the aptitude to learn. Where did the basic body of mystical knowledge come from, then? Did our predecessors risk their lives to discover it, or was it obtained by other means?"

Whenever Klein entered the underground complex, he was struck by how fresh the air remained. The ventilation was clearly excellent, though the occasional cold draft still made him shiver.

Dunn glanced at him. In the dim passage, his gray eyes seemed extraordinarily deep.

"Some knowledge was acquired exactly as you said—through experimentation, consolidation, and gradual improvement. Some was bestowed by the gods. And some..." Dunn gave a quiet laugh. "The dangerous whispers ordinary people cannot hear aren't always meaningless roars and ravings. At times, they reveal mystical knowledge.

"However, to my knowledge, everyone who has listened to those voices for an extended period has eventually gone mad—or fallen into corruption and become a monster. We must still thank them, of course. The notebooks they left behind are priceless treasures to students of mysticism."

Human test subjects...

The cold dampness of the passage suddenly seemed to penetrate Klein's clothing.

Could my luck-enhancement ritual—the one that somehow turned into "social-network magic"—eventually produce the same result because of those horrible, deranged whispers?

At the intersection, Dunn neither continued toward Chanis Gate nor turned toward the armory, storerooms, and archives. Instead, he led Klein down the left passage toward Saint Selena Cathedral.

Partway along, Dunn stopped. Klein couldn't see what he touched, but a concealed door silently opened in the wall.

"This is our Nighthawk team's alchemy room. I'll have Old Neil retrieve the Seer formula and the necessary ingredients from behind Chanis Gate." Dunn gestured inside. "You're fortunate. The Goddess has shown you favor. We should still have enough materials for two Seer potions. Otherwise, you might have waited a very long time.

"Stay here and watch Old Neil prepare it. Potion compounding is one of the foundations of mystical study. And don't touch anything without permission. Everything in this room is dangerous, expensive, or both."

Dunn started to leave, then added in his familiar manner, "Ah, yes. I nearly forgot something.

"You're being allowed to become a Beyonder primarily because of the dangers you face and our need to recover the notebook. Your contribution last night is only part of the reason. Therefore, you won't become a formal member of the team yet. You'll remain a civilian employee at your current salary and continue the duties I assigned you before. In addition, you'll study mysticism under Old Neil. Arrange the schedule with him yourself."

"Understood."

Apart from a little disappointment at receiving no raise, Klein found the arrangement entirely reasonable.

As Dunn had explained, consuming the potion was only the beginning. Klein would still need to study and learn to control his new abilities. If he became a formal Nighthawk immediately and joined supernatural operations without training, he would almost certainly die.

Dunn turned and walked two steps toward the intersection, then abruptly looked back.

"One more thing."

I knew it...

Klein was already accustomed to his captain's particular style.

"We learned something useful from the Secret Order's operation," Dunn said with his usual composure. "They're unlikely to trouble you again in the near future, though you mustn't become careless. For the moment, they can't determine how important the Antigonus family's notebook truly is to them. Our investigation also confirmed that they preserve certain ancient customs and have ties to both the Solomon Empire and the fallen nobles of that era."

"I understand. Thank you, Captain." Klein breathed a little easier.

The lingering danger was another reason he had chosen not to wait and had seized this opportunity to become a Beyonder.

He watched Dunn depart. Only after confirming that the captain wasn't going to turn back with yet another forgotten remark did Klein enter the alchemy room.

Long worktables filled the chamber. Test tubes, pipettes, scales, and crucibles made it resemble a chemistry laboratory from his previous life, though a more primitive and antiquated one.

A huge cauldron, a dark wooden ladle, and a translucent crystal ball stood among the equipment. Dark Sacred Emblems and other unfamiliar symbols appeared throughout the room, lending it an air of mystery.

Klein looked around with keen interest but was sensible enough to keep his hands to himself.

Before long, footsteps approached. Old Neil entered carrying a small silver chest covered in intricate patterns. He still wore his distinctive, old-fashioned black robe and a matching round-brimmed felt hat.

"Lad, I never expected you to choose Seer." Old Neil set down the chest and studied Klein with his slightly clouded red eyes. "Your temperament reminds me of myself when I was young. You don't like following the crowd. Good. Light those gas lamps and shut the door."

"All right."

Klein did his best to keep his hands from trembling as he lit each lamp in the alchemy room. Once the door was closed, muted light reigned over the chamber.

Scrape. Scrape. Scrape.

When Klein turned, he found the white-haired, deeply wrinkled Old Neil scrubbing the black cauldron with a bundle of bound twigs.

"Compounding a Sequence potion is remarkably simple, at least for Sequence 7 and below," Old Neil explained with a broad smile. "You don't need a special flame, an accompanying ritual, or an incantation. Spiritual participation isn't required either. Follow the formula, add the specified quantities in the proper order, and mix them. That's all."

"Really?" Klein asked in surprise.

That sounds as simple as my luck-enhancement ritual...

When I put it that way, it's actually rather frightening.

"Perhaps it's a gift from the gods. Praise the Lady." Old Neil traced a rough circle over his chest.

He opened the silver chest and removed an ancient-looking sheet of goatskin parchment.

As the yellow-brown parchment unfurled, writing appeared across its surface. Even from a distance, Klein recognized Hermes, a language he knew very well.

The words were written in blood-colored ink that somehow still seemed wet and fluid. Apart from that, nothing about the formula appeared unusual.

"Seer: one hundred milliliters of pure water, thirteen drops of night vanilla juice, seven gold mint leaves..." Klein silently read.

Old Neil's wrist blocked the remainder, preventing him from seeing the complete formula.

"Pure water means water distilled repeatedly. Fortunately, I prepared some earlier, so we won't have to waste time." As he spoke, Old Neil took a large sealed glass bottle from a worktable.

He removed the stopper and, with little apparent concern for precision, poured approximately one hundred milliliters into the cauldron.

Klein didn't dare question him. Distracting the person preparing the potion seemed unwise when he was the one expected to drink it.

"Thirteen drops of night vanilla juice. It can be extracted in advance and stored as an essential oil."

Old Neil removed a tiny brown bottle from the silver chest. Using a pipette, he calmly added thirteen drops to the cauldron.

A faint, soothing fragrance spread through the room, filling Klein with an unnatural sense of peace.

"Seven gold mint leaves..."

Old Neil opened an intricately patterned silver container and took out several leaves with his bare fingers. As he scattered them into the cauldron, a fresh, stimulating scent joined the first.

"Four, five, six, seven. Perfect." Chuckling, he consulted the parchment again. "Three drops of poison hemlock juice. This isn't something you should ever consume by itself. It can numb the entire body and ultimately cause death. In ancient times, it was regarded as an excellent means of suicide."

It's not as if I'm foolish enough to try it...

Old Neil changed pipettes and added the poison hemlock. A strange, bracing odor rose from the mixture.

"Nine grams of dragon blood grass powder."

Taking his time, Old Neil reached into the silver chest and withdrew a transparent test tube filled with deep-black powder. He used a beaker and scale to measure exactly nine grams, emptied it into the cauldron, and stirred twice with the dark wooden ladle.

His relaxed approach made Klein increasingly uneasy.

"Everything we've added so far is supplementary," Old Neil said jokingly. "Small variations in the quantities won't significantly affect the result. Perhaps I should add a little extra?"

Before Klein could react, the old man continued, "The final two ingredients are the crucial ones. Using slightly less than the formula calls for is acceptable, but too little may cause your advancement to fail. Never use more—not even by the smallest amount. At best, you'll require treatment for severe mental illness. At worst, you'll die immediately."

Klein tensed as Old Neil removed a black glass bottle from the chest.

"Ten milliliters of Lavos Squid blood. The Lavos Squid is an extraordinary creature, clearly mutated and shrouded in mystery. Its blood rapidly decomposes in sunlight and loses its special properties, so it must be stored in an opaque container."

Old Neil's tone had lost all trace of levity. Moving quickly but carefully, he measured ten milliliters into a test tube.

The blood was as blue as the sky. Phantom bubbles appeared within it from time to time, as though it were somehow connected to the spirit world.

"Any drops left clinging to the test tube are deliberately excluded. It's a safety precaution," Old Neil murmured.

The moment the blue blood entered the cauldron and touched the mixture, the liquid began to bubble. Pale-blue light washed across the room, making Klein feel both strangely distant and inexplicably at home.

The sensation reminded him of floating within a mother's womb, his very soul rising beyond his body.

"The final ingredient: fifty grams of Star Crystal."

Old Neil's voice pulled Klein back to awareness. He looked toward the table.

The elderly man held a piece of extraordinarily pure crystal. Yet rather than hard and rigid, it appeared soft and gelatinous, like translucent jelly.

Under the blue light, countless points glittered within it, as though the crystal contained a brilliant expanse of stars.

"An excellent material for making divination crystals... We'll measure very slightly under fifty grams to allow for error."

Old Neil placed the crystal on the scale and used a small, patterned silver knife to trim it.

Pure water, night vanilla, gold mint leaves, poison hemlock juice, dragon blood grass, Lavos Squid blood, and Star Crystal. Together, they make a Seer...

Klein silently reviewed the formula.

Once the weighing was complete, Old Neil dropped several pieces of Star Crystal into the cauldron.

Hiss!

Phantom mist erupted at once, blurring the entire alchemy room.

Klein seemed to glimpse an endless field of stars within the haze. At the same time, he felt an invisible being turn its gaze upon him.

Several seconds later, the fog dispersed.

Old Neil dipped the dark wooden ladle into the cauldron and lifted out a thick, dark-blue liquid. It possessed a peculiar cohesion, clinging together as a single mass. Not one drop remained behind in the cauldron.

He poured the dark-blue liquid into an opaque cup, then pointed toward it.

"There it is—your Seer potion."

Chapter 32: Spirit Vision

Klein stared at the dark-blue, gelatinous substance. He couldn't decide whether to describe it as a solid lump or a cup of liquid. Swallowing hard, he asked, "Do I simply drink it?

"Isn't there any other preparation? A ritual, perhaps, or an incantation or prayer?"

Old Neil gave an acknowledging grunt. "Preparation? Certainly. Pour yourself a glass of Aurmir grape wine from Intis, smoke a Desi cigar, whistle a soothing tune, and perform a lively court dance. Tap dancing is also acceptable, if you prefer. Finish with a round of Gwent..."

When Klein's expression went blank, Old Neil laughed and summarized his advice.

"If it helps you relax."

...You're quite the comedian, aren't you?

The corner of Klein's mouth twitched as he suppressed the urge to draw his revolver.

He set down his cane and extended his right hand. As though lifting something immensely heavy, he raised the opaque cup. The potion gave off a faint, almost ethereal scent.

"Don't hesitate, lad," Old Neil said with his back to Klein, as casually as if discussing the weather. "The longer you wait, the more frightened and anxious you'll become. That will only interfere with the absorption."

At some point, he had moved to the nearby sink and begun washing his hands.

Klein nodded silently and drew a deep breath. Just as he had when taking bitter medicine as a child, he pinched his nose, brought the cup to his lips, tipped back his head, and swallowed everything at once.

A cool, smooth sensation filled his mouth, flowed down his throat, and entered his stomach.

The sticky dark-blue liquid seemed to sprout countless slender tendrils, carrying cold stimulation into every cell of his body.

Klein convulsed. His vision blurred, and every color became unnaturally saturated—reds deepened, blues intensified, and blacks grew darker. The vivid hues ran together like an Impressionist painting.

He had seen something like this once before, when the Spirit Medium Daly questioned him.

His sight remained indistinct, but his mind felt both weightless and extraordinarily lucid, as though he were a castaway drifting alone upon the sea.

Gradually, his surroundings became recognizable again. The colors returned to normal, only for a hazy gray mist to spread across his vision.

Indescribable forms surrounded him: translucent things that seemed not to exist at all, and distant clusters of multicolored radiance that appeared alive—or perhaps laden with immeasurable knowledge.

This resembles what I saw during the luck-enhancement ritual...

Klein instinctively looked down and discovered "himself" still standing in the same place, his physical body trembling.

Realization struck. His consciousness plunged downward and fused with that body once more.

Boom!

The fog dispersed instantly. The colors normalized, and both the luminous clusters and nonexistent objects vanished.

The alchemy room looked ordinary again, but Klein felt as if his head were swelling and being torn apart. Everything left countless overlapping afterimages, while phantom murmurs flooded his ears.

"Hornacis... Flegrea... Hornacis... Flegrea... Hornacis... Flegrea..."

A stabbing pain pierced his forehead. A violent urge to destroy something surged through him, promising relief from the unbearable discomfort.

He frowned and shook his head sharply.

"Has your vision changed? Are you hearing things that weren't there before?" Old Neil asked from beside him, a smile hidden in his voice.

"Yes, Mr. Neil. What should I do?" Klein forced the words out through the mounting frenzy.

Old Neil chuckled. "The potion's power is overflowing because you don't yet know how to control it. Follow my instructions. Picture an ordinary object in your mind—something simple and familiar."

Klein concentrated at once. He envisioned his black silk half top hat, recalling its precise shape and the texture beneath his fingers.

"Put all your attention on it. Trace its outline again and again. Does that feel better?" Old Neil's voice entered his mind like a tranquil melody.

Little by little, Klein shifted his focus entirely to the imagined hat. The murmurs receded into whispers, then disappeared. The afterimages drew together until his vision was clear again.

"Much better." Klein exhaled and brought his chaotic emotions under control.

He looked down and saw no physical changes. Moving his arms and legs experimentally, he asked with equal parts hope and uncertainty, "Did I succeed? Am I a Seer now?"

Old Neil produced a polished mercury plate and held it before him like a mirror.

"Look at your eyes."

Klein studied his reflection. He still wore his black top hat. His features and the contours of his face appeared normal, apart from the sweat covering his skin.

Following Old Neil's instruction, he examined his eyes more closely. Only then did he realize that his brown irises had darkened dramatically. They resembled a moonless night, so deep they seemed capable of drawing in a person's soul.

Dark-brown eyes were commonly mistaken for black. Without careful inspection, even Klein himself might not have noticed the difference.

"That's the potion's power manifesting physically," Old Neil explained with a smile. "Once you learn Cogitation and can draw that power inward, your eyes will return to normal."

He extended his right hand.

"Congratulations—our newest Beyonder, and our new Seer."

"Thank you." Klein shook his hand. "Mr. Neil, when can I learn Cogitation?"

"Right now. The first steps are fairly simple, especially for a Beyonder." Old Neil's smile broadened. "What you just did—creating an image in your mind to redirect your attention and contain the overflowing power—was already the first step. Try it again."

Klein closed his eyes and reconstructed the half top hat in his mind.

Concentration came far more easily now. Stray thoughts surfaced only to vanish at once, leaving behind the clean outline of the hat.

"Now let your mind become partly empty. Replace the object with something that doesn't exist in this world—something created entirely from your imagination.

"You must obey that rule. Only then can you truly enter Cogitation and transcend the concept of the self. The limitless self will become one with the universe, allowing you to perceive and comprehend truth. You'll receive knowledge that only you can interpret. In mysticism, we call that a Mystic Experience."

Old Neil's soothing voice continued, "For now, simply listen to the descriptions I provide. Entering Cogitation is what matters most."

Something that doesn't exist in this world, created entirely from imagination... Would an object from Earth qualify?

Klein tried picturing an olive-green intercontinental missile he had once seen on television. The long, massive weapon replaced the half top hat in his mind.

Yet no matter how carefully he reconstructed it, the image only helped him concentrate. Nothing else happened.

Apparently that doesn't count...

Left with no alternative, Klein let his imagination roam freely. He formed a sphere of light, then another, and another, gathering countless glowing orbs together.

As the spheres overlapped, they became an object that could exist only in fantasy. Klein's thoughts gradually grew light and ethereal.

His body and mind fell utterly still. The things that appeared not to exist returned, along with the gray mist, the brilliant clusters of light, and the intricate layers of color. They floated through the air, seemingly within arm's reach.

His spirituality extended outward inch by inch as he silently observed, sensed, and absorbed everything around him.

"Very good. Just what I'd expect from a Seer—you entered Cogitation remarkably smoothly. Only a little worse than I did at your age. A very little," Old Neil said with a chuckle. "Now I'll teach you the most common, practical, and easily mastered ability in mysticism: Spirit Vision."

He extinguished the gas lamps one after another, then opened the door to the alchemy room. Darkness enveloped Klein, though enough light remained to distinguish the silhouettes of nearby objects.

"While maintaining your present state, raise your hands before your eyes. Point your index fingers toward one another without letting them touch.

"Open your eyes and leave them open until they adjust to the darkness."

Klein followed each instruction. Gradually, the outlines of his fingers and the objects around him emerged.

"Ordinarily, you would lie down so your body could relax completely. But your Cogitation is stable enough for us to continue." Old Neil laughed softly. "Focus on a point beyond your hands—it must be beyond them. Then move your index fingers slowly back and forth, keeping them apart and within your field of vision."

Klein calmly fixed his gaze on the empty space beyond his palms. He began moving his index fingers past each other without allowing them to meet.

Once. Twice. Three times...

Suddenly, a fiery-red glow appeared between them.

"Ah..." Klein breathed.

"You see a color? Good. That's the first stage of Spirit Vision. What you're seeing is your own aura." Old Neil chuckled. "Don't hurry. Repeat the exercise until the image stabilizes, then look elsewhere. Meanwhile, I'll explain what the different colors signify."

"All right."

Klein continued moving his fingers while keeping his attention on the fiery-red glow.

After briefly organizing his thoughts, Old Neil began. "Mainstream mysticism divides the nonphysical aspects of a human being into four levels. At the center is the Soul Body, the source of a person's fundamental spirituality. One school maintains that all living creatures possess spirituality and therefore have Soul Bodies.

"I can't speak for every pathway, but for Mystery Pryers, both Cogitation and the methods we use to strengthen ourselves are directed toward the Soul Body.

"Surrounding the Soul Body is the Astral Projection. It allows the Soul Body to communicate with the spirit world and the cosmos and can be regarded as its outward manifestation. It is also closely connected to your personal ambitions and dominant emotions.

"The scenes you witnessed after taking the potion were perceived by your Astral Projection as it wandered through the spirit world. That realm doesn't obey the laws of the physical world. It transcends the ordinary self, encompassing the limitless self and the universal self. Past, present, and future may overlap there. That is the basis of divination.

"What you see in the spirit world consists only of images and symbols. You must interpret them to discover their true meaning.

"Divination and many magical spells are performed through the Astral Projection. Don't confuse it with the Soul Body or misunderstand the relationship between them."

One is the essential body, while the other is its outward form...

Still watching the aura between his fingertips, Klein reduced the explanation to a simple distinction.

"Beyond that lies the Body of Heart and Mind. From this level outward, the nonphysical begins to overlap with the physical body. The Body of Heart and Mind relates to the brain and represents reasoning, analysis, observation, and recognition as a whole. Certain potions primarily enhance it, and many spells target it.

"The outermost layer is the Ether Body, the manifestation of life energy and physical condition," Old Neil continued in considerable detail.

"The aura you see is an external expression of the Ether Body. With Spirit Vision, you can directly see spiritual beings, ghosts, and specters—as well as certain existences you would be better off not seeing. You can also observe other people's Ether Bodies and auras. Their thickness, brightness, and color reveal physical health and emotional state.

"As your Spirit Vision improves and your mystical knowledge grows, you'll perceive finer details. Eventually, you may even estimate a person's remaining lifespan.

"The emotional information I mentioned is expressed through the Astral Projection. At higher Sequences, Spirit Vision becomes much more powerful, and you'll be able to perceive another person's Astral Projection directly. That reveals far more. Only Seers and Mystery Pryers can reach such a level.

"Some people even claim that Spirit Vision at its highest stage can see anything, anywhere—including the past and future. I remain skeptical."

That sounds extraordinarily useful...

Klein could hardly contain his excitement.

Old Neil cleared his throat. "Returning to the Ether Body and aura colors: the limbs and other structures responsible for movement appear red. The head and the surface of the brain appear purple. Organs that eliminate waste appear orange, while the digestive system appears yellow. The heart and other regulatory systems appear green. The throat and other parts of the nervous system appear blue. When the entire body is balanced, a white glow surrounds it. That is the sign of good health.

"If an aura darkens, thins, or changes color, the corresponding part of the body has developed a problem. It may be exhausted or diseased.

"The inner layer of the Astral Projection reflects dominant emotions. Red signifies passion and excitement. Orange indicates warmth and satisfaction. Yellow represents happiness and an outgoing nature. Green means calm and peace. Blue suggests coldness, stillness, or deep thought. White signifies brightness and a desire for improvement. Dark shades reveal worry, sorrow, and silence. Purple means spirituality has taken precedence, accompanied by detachment and estrangement."

Klein silently memorized every detail while stabilizing his newly awakened Spirit Vision.

"Good. You can look at something else now." Old Neil stopped explaining and nodded.

Klein slowly turned toward him. Sure enough, different parts of the old man's body displayed different colors, and the aura varied in thickness from one area to another. The purple around his head shone brightest, while the red surrounding his limbs was relatively dim. The white glow around his entire body had faded somewhat as well.

He really is getting old...

Only now, confronted with what his transformed eyes could see, did Klein truly feel that he had become a Beyonder.

I'm a Beyonder now!

He shifted his gaze and examined Old Neil more carefully.

Without warning, a pair of translucent eyes appeared in the emptiness behind the old man. They had no eyebrows and radiated cold, merciless intent.

The nearly illusory eyes stared fixedly at Old Neil—and at Klein.

A violent shudder ran through him. He gasped, "There are eyes behind you!"

Old Neil froze. Then he forced a smile.

"Ignore them."

Chapter 33: Switch

The moment Old Neil finished speaking, the phantom eyes lurking in the darkness behind him disappeared. Even with Spirit Vision active, Klein could no longer detect the slightest trace of them.

"That's a feature of ritualistic magic," Old Neil explained with a chuckle.

Fascinating... Is Spirit Vision an enhanced version of yin-yang eyes?

Like a child with a new toy, Klein excitedly turned away and began examining every corner of the room. He wanted to compare the alchemy room as it appeared through Spirit Vision with its ordinary appearance.

The shadowy outlines of tables, test tubes, scales, cups, and cabinets looked no different. They emitted neither light nor color.

Do lifeless objects possess no spirituality?

Klein's gaze moved to the silver chest.

Brilliant colors suddenly filled his vision—blue as the open sky, radiant as starlight, and crimson as living flame.

"Materials taken from extraordinary creatures still retain some vitality—or perhaps activity—even after the creature itself has died?" Klein asked, carefully searching for the right words.

"More precisely, they retain residual spirituality," Old Neil replied frankly. "That is essential to the successful preparation of a potion. It is also one of the reasons Beyonders lose control. Dunn should have explained that to you."

Remembering something, he abruptly laughed.

"The Corpse Collector formula requires a dried adult black-spotted frog. Drinking that potion takes considerable courage."

Klein imagined the experience and immediately felt nauseated. Declining to comment, he turned toward a dark corner, but saw none of the spiritual beings or ghosts he had half expected to find.

"Isn't the spirit world supposed to be everywhere?" he asked curiously.

Old Neil gave a short laugh. "Punk, repeat after me:

"This is the headquarters of a Nighthawk team. We are beneath a cathedral of the Church of Evernight. Numerous Beyonders work here.

"Do you imagine we would allow wandering spirits and ghosts to roam these halls? Besides, the spirit world and spirits are entirely different concepts."

Feeling slightly embarrassed, Klein looked away and pretended to study the faint gaslight spilling in through the doorway.

"I understand."

As he spoke, the space between his eyebrows began to throb.

What's happening?

Klein was about to turn and ask when he noticed a figure standing motionless beside the door at the edge of the light. It appeared human, but its aura blended so perfectly with the darkness that he couldn't distinguish its features.

Hiss!

Pain pulsed through his glabella.[1] His vision dissolved into chaos. When he focused again, the "formless" figure had vanished.

Strange...

He turned to Old Neil. "Mr. Neil, the area between my eyebrows is throbbing, and it hurts a little."

"That's perfectly normal." Old Neil sounded almost delighted. "You're a new Beyonder, and Spirit Vision places a considerable burden on your Soul Body while continuously consuming spirituality. Physical symptoms include spasms of the glabella, headaches, heightened sensitivity, and minor hallucinations.

"The unfamiliar environment revealed by Spirit Vision can also make you uncomfortable, and other people's emotions may influence you too easily. You must remain mindful of these effects. Practice will help you adapt and gradually overcome them. More importantly, use Spirit Vision sparingly and end it before the strain becomes excessive."

Why do you sound so pleased about this?

Klein hurriedly asked, "Then how do I turn Spirit Vision off?"

He had intended to mention the indistinct figure by the door, but Old Neil's reference to minor hallucinations changed his mind. Given the throbbing in his glabella and the headache beginning behind his eyes, the explanation seemed obvious.

"As before, picture an object to redirect your attention and leave Cogitation. Close your eyes, rein in your spirituality, and repeatedly command it to stop. When you open your eyes, Spirit Vision will have ended."

Old Neil explained this at an easy pace, then added, "Of course, that's the clumsiest and most elementary method. With repeated practice, you can plant a suggestion within your spirituality while in Cogitation, creating a simple switch.

"For example, tapping your glabella twice could activate Spirit Vision, and tapping it twice more could deactivate it. The exact gesture depends on your habits and preferences."

"I see."

After considering the matter, Klein decided to imitate Old Neil and use two taps on the glabella as his switch.

A single tap could easily happen by accident. Three would waste precious time in a dangerous situation. Gestures such as snapping his fingers would attract too much attention.

He relaxed his focus, reconstructed the overlapping spheres of light, and entered Cogitation once more.

Under Old Neil's guidance, Klein repeated the suggestion and practiced it until he had finally established his "switch."

He loosely clenched one hand and tapped his glabella twice with the knuckle of his index finger.

Glowing auras of varying colors and thicknesses immediately appeared before his eyes.

Two more taps returned everything to normal.

"I've finally mastered it..." Klein said with satisfaction.

Only then did he notice how exhausted he had become. He felt capable of falling asleep where he stood, and his head ached as if he had remained awake for three consecutive nights.

Old Neil chuckled. "We aren't Sleepless. Whenever you practice too intensely or overuse Spirit Vision, you'll need to recover with actual sleep. Go home and rest. This afternoon, walk around Iron Cross Street near Welch's residence and do your best to find clues concerning the Antigonus family's notebook.

"Tomorrow, I'll continue teaching you mysticism. And don't neglect your historical documents."

"All right."

Klein found no fault with the arrangement.

He retrieved his cane and left the alchemy room. As Old Neil returned toward the armory and the concealed door closed behind him, Klein massaged his glabella and temples. Leaning slightly on his cane, he trudged up the stairs.

At that moment, Dunn Smith approached from behind, the corners of his mouth raised in a faint smile.

"Old Neil tells me you're remarkably well suited to this. You managed to use Spirit Vision before you even learned Cogitation."

"Perhaps that's an inherent quality of a Seer," Klein replied modestly.

He guessed that Dunn had been covering the armory while Old Neil prepared the potion.

Dunn slowed until he was only a step ahead. After several seconds of silence, he turned around.

"Remember this: curiosity killed the cat, and it can kill a Beyonder just as easily. Never investigate whispers you shouldn't hear or seek out existences you shouldn't see."

"I understand."

Klein recognized the warning as yet another reminder of how easily a Beyonder could lose control.

After entering the Blackthorn Security Company, he greeted Rozanne, who plainly had no idea he had become a Beyonder. Then he stepped outside and took a trackless carriage to Daffodil Street, nearly falling asleep on the way.

It was still morning, and the temperature had already reached roughly twenty-six degrees Celsius. Klein removed a copper key from his waistband and unlocked the door to his new home.

Much of the house remained unfurnished. The living and dining rooms were especially bare. Benson had left early for work, and Melissa was already at school.

Klein had no energy to concern himself with any of it. He shut the front door, hurried upstairs, and entered the bedroom with the bookcase—his bedroom.

After removing his tailcoat and hanging it on the rack, he threw himself eagerly onto the bed. The moment his head touched the pillow, he fell asleep.

...

Bright sunlight woke him.

Klein turned his head and slowly opened his eyes to find the blazing sun outside.

"What time is it? Did I sleep through the Tarot Club gathering?"

He struggled out of bed and crossed to the clothes rack, fumbling for the pocket watch in the inner pocket of his tailcoat.

Not only had he forgotten the gathering, he had also left his bedroom door open and neglected to draw the curtains over the oriel window.

Click!

He opened the watch and immediately relaxed.

It was only a little past noon. Nearly three hours remained before the scheduled gathering.

Today was Monday—the day he would meet with The Hanged Man and Justice.

After a moment's thought, Klein tapped his glabella twice. His vision changed, revealing that his body's aura had recovered its bright luster.

He tapped twice more to end Spirit Vision. Reassured, he went downstairs and boiled water for tea. He used a few low-quality leaves and ate rye bread spread with a little butter.

Afterward, Klein reviewed the historical texts and the original Klein's diary, revising and consolidating his knowledge.

At 2:57 p.m., he closed his book, capped his fountain pen, and drew the curtains.

Then he locked the bedroom door, plunging the room into unusual darkness.

Klein tapped his glabella twice and used Spirit Vision to inspect his surroundings.

Once he had confirmed that no invisible spiritual beings were present, he deactivated Spirit Vision and checked his pocket watch again.

Tick. Tock. Tick. Tock.

One minute before three, he cleared a space and walked four steps counterclockwise to form a square, softly reciting the incantation in Chinese as before.

This time, he had prepared no staple foods.

Klein closed his eyes. The backs of his hands began to itch, as though the four black dots arranged in a square were protruding from his skin and projecting something outward.

Hysterical cries and seductive murmurs rang through his mind, but the resulting headache was less severe than during his first attempt.

It wasn't that the voices no longer affected him. He was simply doing his utmost not to listen.

As a Beyonder, he needed greater self-control in such an environment.

Soon his body became weightless and floated upward. Gray-white mist spread around him, and dark-red "stars" appeared in the distance. Two shared a faint connection with him, carrying a peculiar sense of familiarity.

Klein examined his indistinct form and murmured, "Is this the Astral Projection Old Neil described?"

After remaining still for several seconds, he once again willed the magnificent palace into existence: the lofty bronze table beneath its domed ceiling and the twenty-two high-backed chairs corresponding to different constellations.

Klein walked calmly to the seat of honor and shrouded his body and face in denser gray fog. Then he extended his right hand and touched the two familiar crimson stars, establishing the miraculous connection once more.

[1] The glabella is the area of skin between the eyebrows and above the nose.

Chapter 34: Advance Payment

In a windowless underground chamber, the broad-shouldered Alger Wilson sat at a long table covered with assorted instruments and sheets of goatskin parchment.

A half-burned candle stood before him. Its dim yellow flame flickered, making the shadows of the table and its contents sway like mirages.

Alger's disheveled hair resembled dark-blue seaweed, nearly black in the poor light. His robe was embroidered with patterns of lightning. He clasped his hands with his thumbs touching and focused intently on a bottle of black liquid to the left of the candle.

Whoosh! Whoosh! Whoosh!

Crash! Crash! Crash!

The roar of gale winds and breaking waves came from within the sealed bottle. Wherever the black liquid failed to settle, faint mist swirled and gathered, seeming to form eyes and a mouth.

Alger glanced at the clock on the wall and watched its hand reach three.

He pressed his fingers to his temple, and his eyes darkened. Vivid colors emerged from the various objects spread across the table.

At that moment, crimson light appeared from nowhere, surging over him like a tidal wave.

...

In the Hall family's luxurious mansion in Backlund's Empress Borough, Audrey dismissed her dance instructor, locked the door, and sat straight-backed before her dressing table.

Brilliant sunlight streamed in from outside. An exquisite notebook bound in light-brown goatskin lay open before her, displaying a blank page. Beside it rested a gold-nibbed fountain pen set with rubies.

Audrey tested the arrangement to make certain she could seize the pen and record the formula the instant she returned from the Gathering.

"I can hardly wait..."

She inhaled to restrain her excitement, then pursed her lips at the mirror.

Her reflection didn't appear. Instead, illusory dark-red light burst from her body.

...

Above the gray fog stood the majestic palace that seemed built for giants.

Crimson light bloomed on both sides of the bronze table. It surged upward like fountains before cascading down, sculpting two indistinct figures into the same seats they had occupied before.

Audrey, with her soft blond hair and tall, slender figure, instinctively looked toward the seat of honor. The figure shrouded in dense gray fog reclined there, one hand resting flat beside him on the table while the other stroked his chin.

"Good afternoon, Mr. Fool!" Audrey greeted him brightly.

Then she turned to the person across from her and continued in the same cheerful tone, "Good afternoon, Mr. Hanged Man!"

This young lady is remarkably naive. Is she so certain I'm a good person? Why isn't she afraid at all? Perhaps she's a sheltered noblewoman...

Klein smiled while maintaining his inscrutable bearing.

"Good afternoon, Miss Justice."

As he spoke, he lowered his head slightly and tapped his glabella twice with his left hand.

His vision changed at once. Colorful auras appeared around Justice and The Hanged Man.

The surrounding gray fog and dark-red stars, however, remained unchanged. He saw none of the apparently nonexistent objects or living clusters of radiance he had witnessed elsewhere.

Klein shifted his attention to Justice. Her aura matched Old Neil's descriptions precisely. Every region displayed its proper shade of red, purple, blue, or white, and each color possessed a healthy luster and thickness. She was plainly a vigorous young woman.

Her emotional colors are red and yellow—joy, enthusiasm, and excitement...

After making that assessment, Klein looked toward The Hanged Man.

Like Justice, his ordinary aura colors showed nothing unusual. His emotions appeared blue mixed with touches of orange.

Calm, thoughtful, cautious, and somewhat pleased?

This was Klein's first attempt to interpret another person's aura, so he had little confidence in the conclusion.

Just as he began looking away, he noticed something strange.

The Hanged Man's aura was nearly uniform at its innermost layer.

Klein concentrated and examined it again. Deep within The Hanged Man's Ether Body, he faintly perceived a blue as dark as the sea, carrying the impression of a violent storm tide.

Is that his Astral Projection—or perhaps only its surface? It proves he's a Beyonder, and probably one stronger than Old Neil.

Questions flooded Klein's mind.

Not necessarily. This unusual environment may be responsible. Because this place is effectively my domain, perhaps I can see things Old Neil's Spirit Vision doesn't reveal.

He turned back toward Justice and confirmed that she possessed no similar feature. It was apparently unique to Beyonders.

By then, Alger had completed his greetings.

Audrey drew a small breath and asked eagerly, "Mr. Hanged Man, did you receive the box containing the Ghost Shark blood?"

Alger glanced at Klein, who seemed to be tapping his glabella while lost in thought.

"Thank you. It met my requirements perfectly. I didn't expect you to send it so quickly. Ghost Shark blood isn't an ordinary extraordinary material," he replied frankly.

Audrey smiled with modest satisfaction. "I'm very glad it worked out."

Her fascination with mysticism had begun in childhood. Over the years, she had befriended aristocrats with similar interests, exchanging information, books, and rare curiosities with them. Yet none had ever gained genuine supernatural power or become a Beyonder. Several princes had merely hinted that they could give her what she wanted if she agreed to become a princess consort.

Audrey had taken the Ghost Shark blood directly from her family's treasury. After all, the inventory listed only "one large bottle" without specifying its volume or how full it should be. She doubted anyone would notice a small amount missing. Even if the matter somehow came to light, her parents were unlikely to punish her seriously.

Alger studied The Fool, still veiled in fog, before looking back at Audrey with a smile.

"As agreed, I will now give you the formula for the Spectator potion."

"Let me prepare myself... All right, begin." Audrey inhaled and concentrated completely.

"Low-Sequence potions are simple to compound. Add the ingredients in the order I provide. Remember: using slightly less than the required amount is acceptable, but using more can cause grave problems. You've heard of Beyonders losing control, so I assume I needn't explain further?" Alger began with the necessary warnings.

Audrey nodded. "I understand completely."

As she answered, she glanced toward Mr. Fool, hoping the mysterious expert might have something to add. Unfortunately, The Fool sat motionless and silent, like a statue.

After some thought, Alger continued, "Slightly less doesn't mean you can deviate too far from the prescribed quantity. If you have no assistant, I recommend spending time learning basic laboratory methods first."

"I have a private tutor for that," Audrey replied without concern.

After explaining the allowable margin of error, Alger recited the formula fluently.

"Spectator, Sequence 9. Eighty milliliters of pure water. Five drops of autumn crocus essence. Thirteen grams of cow teeth paeonol powder. Seven elf flower petals. The eyeball of a mature Manhal Fish. Finally, thirty-five milliliters of goat-horned black fish blood.

"The last two are the main ingredients. Both come from extraordinary marine creatures, so handle them carefully."

"All right." Audrey began repeating the formula from memory. "Eighty milliliters of pure water, five drops of autumn crocus essence, thirteen grams of cow teeth..."

"Paeonol powder," Alger prompted.

With his help, Audrey gradually memorized both the exact ingredients and their proper order. Even so, she looked worried and continued murmuring the formula to herself.

"Do you know how to perform Cogitation?" Alger asked. When Justice nodded, he continued, "I don't know how thoroughly you were taught, so I'll explain it once more. After drinking the potion, enter Cogitation immediately to control your spirituality and the potion's power.

"Practice every day so you can truly master its abilities. Explore the meaning represented by the potion and uncover its deeper mysteries. That will reduce the risk of losing control as much as possible. The essential meaning of a potion lies in its name—for example, Spectator."

Klein listened without interrupting. He silently memorized and analyzed everything, but Alger's final statement abruptly stirred a thought.

Audrey had been listening intently and was about to ask for more detail when she heard fingers tapping against the table.

She and Alger both turned toward The Fool. The mysterious, powerful figure was gently drumming his fingers on the bronze surface.

In a deep voice, he said, "It isn't mastery, but digestion.

"It isn't exploration, but acting.

"A potion's name is not merely symbolic. It is an image—and the key to digestion."

Audrey stared at him in confusion. She couldn't grasp what Mr. Fool was trying to convey.

From the corner of her eye, she looked toward The Hanged Man for his reaction. To her surprise, he had gone rigid, as though an ordinary person had just heard thunder explode beside him.

"Digestion... acting... digestion... acting... The name is the key..." Alger repeated under his breath, as though he had seized upon a vital revelation—or fallen under a peculiar curse.

After a long moment, he raised his head. His voice emerged hoarse.

"Thank you, Mr. Fool. That guidance is as valuable as my life. It has enlightened me profoundly, though I don't believe I yet comprehend its full meaning."

Maintaining his mysterious composure, Klein smiled.

"Consider it an advance payment."

In truth, he didn't fully understand the principle himself. He was only certain that Emperor Roselle had been far more powerful than an ordinary Beyonder—and stronger than The Hanged Man.

Advance payment...

Judging by The Hanged Man's reaction, Audrey understood that the hint was extraordinarily valuable. After considering it, she asked, "Mr. Fool, what would you like us to do?"

Across from her, Alger nodded. "What task do you wish to entrust to us?"

Klein leaned back and looked from one to the other before answering in a low, pleasant voice.

"Collect the secret diary of Roselle Gustav for me—even a single page will do."

Chapter 35: Exchange of Information

Roselle Gustav's secret diary?

Emperor Roselle?

At first, Audrey was startled. Then she decided there was nothing surprising about it. Naturally, only a matter of such importance would interest a powerful figure like Mr. Fool.

Rumor claimed that Emperor Roselle had once seen the Blasphemy Slate. The secret cards he created were said to conceal the twenty-two pathways of the divine—knowledge that any high-Sequence Beyonder would covet.

"A diary? You're saying it truly is a diary?" Alger frowned slightly, immediately seizing on that detail.

Mr. Fool had described the writings Roselle Gustav left behind as a diary.

How did he know?

What allowed him to reach that conclusion?

Could he decipher Roselle's mysterious script?

Having produced the reaction he wanted, Klein leaned back and interlaced his fingers.

"For the moment, let us regard it as a diary," he replied casually.

He neither confirmed nor denied anything.

Audrey had heard the children of other nobles mention Roselle's writings, but she knew little about them. Her curiosity aroused, she asked, "I've heard that Emperor Roselle's—well, his diary—was written in a secret language or system of symbols he invented."

"Correct," Alger answered simply. "Some believe the writing consists of unique mystical symbols. Others consider it a form of hieroglyphics. To this day, no one has discovered the correct method of deciphering it—at least, not to my knowledge."

As he finished, he turned toward Klein, seeking either confirmation or some reaction to his suspicions.

The copies have passed through countless hands and no longer resemble the originals. Even if your theories were right, how could anyone decipher such distorted versions?

Klein maintained his calm exterior while privately laughing at the absurdity.

The claim that the characters were mystical symbols conjured an especially ridiculous image.

An evil sorcerer in a pointed black hat and long robe pulled up his sleeve to reveal a tattoo supposedly endowed with mysterious power by Emperor Roselle. Written in bold blue simplified Chinese were two words:

"Stupid Clown!"

The corners of Klein's mouth rose slightly. His mood improved at once.

After listening to The Hanged Man, Audrey asked in confusion, "If we can't understand the writing, how are we supposed to give the information to you, Mr. Fool? Should we mail the pages somewhere?"

That is an important question. I have no secure way to receive physical objects...

Klein didn't answer immediately. He repeatedly separated his interlaced thumbs and brought them together again.

An idea soon occurred to him.

If I can create this palace and table here through sheer will, could I also project something from another person's memory?

It's worth trying.

Audrey and Alger watched Mr. Fool slowly straighten amid the dense gray fog.

"Miss Justice, let us conduct an experiment. Picture a passage of writing and summon the urgent desire to record it. Then pick up the fountain pen beside you and write on the paper."

Before Klein finished speaking, a sheet of yellow-brown goatskin parchment and a dark-red fountain pen appeared before Audrey.

Curious but doubtful, she picked up the pen. Following his instructions, she recalled a line of poetry Emperor Roselle had once written:

"If Winter comes, can Spring be far behind?" [1]

After visualizing the words clearly, she touched the pen to the parchment and concentrated on the desire to project them.

Klein sensed her intention and guided it through the fountain pen.

As soon as Audrey began to write, a sentence appeared on the parchment:

"If Winter comes, can Spring be far behind?"

"Goddess, that's incredible!" Audrey exclaimed in astonishment.

Then a trace of fear entered her expression as she looked at Klein.

"Mr. Fool, can you read my thoughts?"

"No. I merely guided you, simplifying the act of writing until it became a direct imprint. If you had not wished to reveal the words, nothing would have appeared," Klein assured her in a low voice.

"I see... So we need only memorize the symbols—or the appearance of the mysterious writing—and then willingly reproduce it here?" Audrey asked, visibly relieved.

"Correct."

"A useful method," Alger observed from the opposite side. "Miss Justice, don't doubt your memory. Once you become a Spectator, it will improve considerably."

Watching the experiment had only deepened his conviction that The Fool was more mysterious and powerful than he had imagined.

As for his own memory, Alger believed his next advancement would enhance it sufficiently.

Audrey nodded in delight. "That's very encouraging, Mr. Hanged Man. Do you have any further advice about being a Spectator?"

Then she looked toward the seat of honor.

"Mr. Fool, I'll work diligently to complete your request. I'll do everything I can to gather more pages from Emperor Roselle's secret diary."

"As I mentioned before, I favor fair and equal exchanges. The advance payment I provided is worth two pages from each of you. If you obtain more, I will offer additional compensation," Klein replied calmly, like an adult determined not to take advantage of children.

As for where that additional compensation would come from, he naturally intended to use the information contained in the diary pages themselves. It would form a self-sustaining cycle.

"You are truly a generous gentleman." After several seconds of silence, Alger placed a hand over his chest and bowed slightly.

When he straightened, he turned to Justice.

"Let me emphasize this once more: a Spectator must always remain a spectator.

"Many people watching a performance imagine themselves as the protagonist or another character. They invest so much emotion that they laugh, weep, rage, and grieve along with the drama. A Spectator must never do that.

"Society is filled with dramas and with people consciously or unconsciously playing particular roles. You must observe them as an absolute outsider. Only then can you remain calm and objective enough to notice their habits, the small gestures that accompany a lie, or the scent of nervousness. Those details will reveal their true thoughts.

"Believe me, every emotion causes a person to secrete different substances and produce different scents. Only a true Spectator can detect them.

"If you invest too much of your own emotion, your observations will be distorted, and your sensitivity to the emotions of others will become unreliable."

Audrey listened attentively, her eyes growing brighter by the moment.

"That sounds incredibly interesting!"

Klein felt something stir in his mind.

The requirements of the Spectator potion could be summarized as remaining an absolutely objective and neutral observer.

In a sense, that was equivalent to acting...

Acting?

Was this the "acting" Emperor Roselle had referred to?

Then I need to act as a Seer and gradually digest the potion through that role?

While Klein pondered, Alger finished explaining everything he knew about Spectators. Then he sighed.

"It seems we've concluded our business.

"Perhaps we can converse more casually now and exchange recent events from our respective surroundings. Something ordinary to one person may provide an important clue to another."

"Agreed." Klein returned his attention to the gathering and nodded slightly.

He had already decided to try acting as a Seer. At least on the surface, the experiment appeared harmless.

"Then shall we begin with you, Mr. Hanged Man?" Audrey asked eagerly.

Alger considered before saying, "The notorious pirate who calls himself Admiral Ludwell has once again begun a voyage to explore the eastern reaches of the Sonia Sea."

"The master of the Black Tulip?" Audrey asked after a moment's recollection.

"Yes." Alger nodded.

I have no idea who that is...

Klein listened in silence while deciding what information he could contribute. It needed to invite useful feedback without revealing his identity.

He soon made his choice. Maintaining The Fool's unfathomable demeanor, he ran his fingers along the edge of the bronze table.

"According to information I possess, the Secret Order has lost a notebook belonging to the Antigonus family."

The matter wasn't known only to Tingen's Nighthawks. The Secret Order and Beyonders closely associated with it would naturally know as well.

"An Antigonus family notebook?" Alger repeated. He smiled and shook his head. "I wonder how the Church of Evernight will react when it learns of this."

Why did he specifically mention the Church of Evernight?
