Klein immediately sensed that the remark concealed something important, but asking directly would be inappropriate. It would damage the image of the mysterious, all-knowing Fool.

Fortunately, Audrey asked the question for him.

"Why are you curious? Would the Goddess's Church react in some special way?"

Alger smiled. "The Antigonus family was destroyed by the Church of Evernight.

"I'm not certain whether it happened near the end of the Fourth Epoch or in the early years of the current one."

Klein's pupils contracted as a chill swept through him.

It seems the Nighthawks value this notebook far more than I realized.

My contribution and the danger to me were probably secondary reasons for allowing me to become a Beyonder. Their real purpose was to strengthen my spiritual perception so I could help locate it.

Captain didn't exactly conceal that. He mentioned it himself. I simply failed to appreciate its significance...

After hearing The Hanged Man's explanation, Audrey said with great interest, "I never imagined anything like that had happened...

"All right, it's my turn. Let me decide what I should share."

She tilted her head and rested it against one hand, then laughed.

"Yesterday, my etiquette instructor taught me how to faint—how to do it elegantly, without committing a social blunder. It's apparently a useful skill for escaping awkward situations or unpleasant gentlemen at formal events..."

Audrey giggled. "I was only organizing my thoughts. What I truly wanted to say is this: ever since the defeat on Balam's eastern coast, the king, the prime minister, and the other gentlemen in government have been under tremendous pressure. They are desperate for change."

[1] From Percy Bysshe Shelley's "Ode to the West Wind."

Chapter 36: A Simple Question

Recalling a conversation between her father and eldest brother, Audrey summarized the situation in her own words.

"They believe the government's structure is too chaotic. Whenever an election places a different party in power, personnel change from the highest offices to the lowest. It creates disorder and severely reduces efficiency. That contributed to the military defeat and also causes tremendous inconvenience for ordinary citizens."

Klein understood the problem well. With no foreign example to imitate, the Loen Kingdom had not yet developed a civil service examination system. Its political institutions remained immature. Consequently, a victorious party treated countless government positions as rewards for its members and supporters.

Hmm. It seems out of character for Emperor Roselle not to establish such a system in Intis. Did something else consume his attention later in life?

The Hanged Man, Alger, gave a quiet chuckle.

"They've only just realized this? Their reactions are somewhat slow. Perhaps they don't feel an itch until a full year after a black mosquito bites them."

Black mosquitoes were native to southern Loen. Their venom produced an itching so unbearable that victims sometimes wanted to tear off their own skin.

Audrey covered her mouth with one hand. Ignoring The Hanged Man's mockery, she continued to the essential point.

"Unfortunately, they haven't found a suitable system to replace the present one."

Klein listened carefully and realized the discussion had entered familiar territory. He smiled faintly.

"It's a simple problem."

The Foodaholic Empire—and the developed countries its institutions had studied—possessed a wealth of successful experience in this area.

"Simple?" Audrey repeated in confusion.

Politics had never been part of her formal education, but she frequently overheard her father, brother, and their acquaintances discussing such matters. She understood enough to know that the problem was considered extremely difficult.

Klein felt as though he had returned to the online discussion boards of his previous life.

"Use examinations, much like university entrance examinations," he said calmly. "Hold a test open to the public, divided into two or three stages, and employ the most objective standards possible to select capable candidates."

"But..." Audrey already anticipated several objections.

Before she could organize them, Klein continued, "Those who pass would fill positions in the Cabinet, county and city governments, and the various towns—specifically, the permanent offices that handle actual administration, such as cabinet secretaries.

"Different posts should have different requirements. Later stages of the examination can be administered locally and tailored to the relevant field. Professional responsibilities should be entrusted to qualified professionals.

"Political offices—ministers, governors, mayors, and the like—can remain under the control of the party that wins the election. That share of authority is the victor's rightful reward."

Alger had shown little interest at first, but now he unconsciously turned and listened closely. Audrey frowned in concentration.

"There's no need to replace everyone at once. Doing so would paralyze the Cabinet and every branch of the civil service. Hold examinations annually or once every three years, then introduce successful candidates gradually. Vacancies created by expansion, retirement, and resignation can be filled according to a systematic quota."

Klein fully displayed the talents of a veteran armchair politician. Turning one palm outward, he concluded, "Such a system would bring the kingdom's most capable and insightful people into government. Regardless of which party holds power or who serves as minister, the permanent civil service would keep the kingdom operating at a basic level of competence and efficiency."

Of course, one side effect would be the birth of that immortal demon known as bureaucracy.

Still considering the proposal, Audrey asked uncertainly, "Do you mean that even if the ministers were replaced with curly-haired baboons, it wouldn't matter very much?"

"No," Alger interjected. "I believe baboons would be an improvement over the present ministers."

He paused, then added, "Baboons only eat, sleep, and mate. They don't devise idiotic policies and insist on carrying out senseless projects."

Mr. Hanged Man, it sounds as though you have an exceptionally terrible superior...

Seated at the head of the table, Klein shook his head in silent amusement.

Audrey contemplated Mr. Fool's proposal for a little longer, then said with dawning surprise, "It truly sounds as though it could work...

"It's such a simple and effective solution!"

She looked at Klein with sincere admiration. "Mr. Fool, you must be an elderly gentleman of extraordinary wisdom and vast experience!"

The corner of Klein's mouth twitched.

He looked silently from Justice to The Hanged Man for several seconds before saying, "Let us conclude today's gathering."

If Miss Justice can influence her family and bring this proposal to the government's attention, I might prepare Benson in advance and give him a chance to enter the civil service.

On reflection, Benson was remarkably well suited to such a career.

Justice was unlikely to raise the proposal openly, however. The Hanged Man and Klein could easily discover which noble had introduced the idea and use that information to deduce her identity.

Of course, she might find a discreet, indirect method.

"As you wish." Audrey and Alger rose together.

Klein leaned back slightly and severed the connections. The indistinct, illusory figures of Justice and The Hanged Man shattered and dissolved at once.

Within the magnificent palace above the gray fog—a place seemingly built for the residence of gods—Klein alone remained seated at the head of the bronze table.

Unlike after the previous gathering, he didn't immediately descend into the fog and leave. Becoming a Beyonder had strengthened his mind enough that he still felt energetic.

He had ended the Tarot Club early because he now understood the Nighthawks' true attitude toward the Antigonus notebook. He needed to search for it seriously rather than sleep through the entire afternoon. Otherwise, Dunn Smith might begin wondering what he did alone at home.

Besides, he had gained a great deal from today's meeting.

Klein remained in the high-backed chair at the head of the table. Resting his arms on its sides, he interlaced his fingers and carefully observed the boundless gray fog. The entire realm possessed the stillness of a place untouched for ten million years.

When he had established the connections and summoned The Hanged Man and Justice, he had noticed something new.

Now that he was a Beyonder, he could reach another dark-red star.

"Does that mean I can summon one more person?" Klein murmured, recalling the sensation to confirm his impression.

He felt no urge to experiment. He had no idea who the newcomer might be or how they would react. Not everyone possessed Justice's or The Hanged Man's distinctive temperament. Those two had adapted quickly, each obtaining what they needed while willingly keeping the gathering secret.

If he accidentally brought in someone like Dunn Smith, his newly founded mysterious organization would immediately attract the Church's attention.

As the leader of an "evil" organization, his future prospects would become distinctly bleak.

Klein knew that the gray fog was extraordinary and doubted a Beyonder of Dunn Smith's Sequence could see through it. But now that he possessed supernatural powers himself, he had to account for the possible existence of gods.

He had chosen, cautiously, to believe that the seven orthodox gods truly existed. Admittedly, he was more inclined to regard them as beings far more powerful than high-Sequence Beyonders and subject to strict limitations. At the very least, aside from delivering a handful of oracles, none had visibly appeared since the beginning of the Fifth Epoch.

"Besides, dragging people here against their will isn't exactly courteous. No one wants to be pulled into a mysterious place without explanation. I'll wait and see what the future brings..."

With a sigh, Klein rose.

He released his spirituality, sensed the distant presence of his physical body, and imagined himself plunging rapidly downward.

The scene transformed. Gray fog and crimson stars rushed away as Klein seemed to tear through layer after layer of invisible membrane. At last, the real world appeared: his bedroom, dark behind its drawn curtains.

This time, he remained fully conscious and carefully observed the entire transition.

"Strange... The gray fog is somehow different from the spirit world..."

Klein moved his limbs, reacquainting himself with the solidity of his body.

After seriously reviewing the experience, he shook his head, walked to the oriel window, and opened the curtains.

Whoosh!

Sunlight poured into the room, illuminating everything within.

Klein watched pedestrians moving along the street below. Drawing a deep breath, he silently told himself, Time to go to work.

How am I supposed to act as a Seer?

I can't rush this. Spirit Vision is the only relevant ability I know how to use...

...

Backlund, Empress Borough.

Audrey Hall stared at her reflection. Excitement had flushed her cheeks, and her eyes shone so brightly that another person might have found it difficult to meet them.

Paying no attention to her appearance, she hurriedly seized the ruby-studded fountain pen and wrote the Spectator potion formula on the fine goatskin paper.

"Eighty milliliters of pure water. Five drops of autumn crocus essence. Thirteen grams of cow teeth paeonol powder. Seven elf flower petals. The eyeball of a mature Manhal Fish. Thirty-five milliliters of goat-horned black fish blood."

Audrey exhaled in relief. She read the formula several times and confirmed that she had made no mistakes.

She felt an urge to dance, but sternly reminded herself to show restraint.

After some consideration, she filled the space around the formula with the names of various chemicals, disguising the page as a set of complicated, disorganized chemistry notes.

Yes. As long as no one examines it carefully, a person casually flipping through the notebook will never notice what I've hidden. Excellent!

Having praised her own cleverness, Audrey turned to the problem of acquiring the ingredients.

I'll search all our storerooms first. Then I'll try exchanging with the others for anything missing.

If I still can't assemble the complete formula, I'll have to ask The Hanged Man or The Fool for help. But what could I offer in payment?

Audrey considered the question, closed the notebook, and placed it on a small bookshelf. Then she crossed briskly to the door and opened it.

A golden retriever sat obediently outside.

Audrey's lips curved into a radiant smile.

"Susie, you completed your mission perfectly!

"The detectives in newspaper serials always have capable assistants. I think a true Spectator ought to have a large dog helping her too."

...

In an underground chamber illuminated only by a flickering candle, Alger Wilson raised one hand and examined it carefully.

After a long while, he sighed.

"Still just as miraculous. I couldn't perceive a single concrete detail..."

Despite all his preparations, he remained unable to understand how The Fool had summoned him.

Alger lowered his gaze to the goatskin parchment on the table.

At the top of the yellow-brown sheet, a line had been written in Hermes with dark-blue ink:

"7. Seafarer."

Chapter 37: The Club

Klein left home beneath the scorching afternoon sun.

Since he planned to walk all the way from Daffodil Street to Welch's residence near Iron Cross Street, he had set aside his formal suit, top hat, and polished boots. Instead, he wore a linen shirt, a brown coat, a round felt hat, and an old pair of leather shoes. There was no sense soaking an expensive new suit with sweat.

He followed Daffodil Street toward Iron Cross Street. As he passed the square around the corner, he glanced over instinctively.

The tents were gone. Having completed its performances, the circus had moved on.

Klein had once imagined that the animal trainer who told his fortune might be a hidden expert. Perhaps she had recognized something unusual about him, appeared deliberately to offer guidance, and would return later with hints about his future.

Nothing of the sort had happened. She had simply departed with the rest of the circus for its next destination.

Life can't possibly contain that many tired storybook twists...

Klein shook his head with a rueful smile and turned onto Iron Cross Street.

Despite its name, Iron Cross Street wasn't a single road. It consisted of two streets intersecting in the shape of a cross.

Centered on the intersection, the district was divided into Left Street, Right Street, Upper Street, and Lower Street. Klein, Benson, and Melissa had lived on Lower Street.

However, the residents of their old building and those nearby refused to call the neighborhood Lower Street. They had invented the name Middle Street instead, drawing a clear distinction between themselves and the truly destitute families living only two hundred meters farther down the road.

There, five or six people commonly shared a single room, and sometimes as many as ten.

Klein wandered along the edge of Left Street, allowing his thoughts to drift. He remembered the missing Antigonus notebook, the importance the Nighthawks placed on it, and the deaths it had already caused.

His mood slowly darkened, and the color drained from his face.

At that moment, a familiar voice called to him.

"Lad."

Klein looked around in surprise and discovered that he had reached the entrance of Smyrin Bakery. Gray-haired Mrs. Wendy waved to him with a warm smile.

"You don't look very happy," she observed kindly.

Klein rubbed his face. "I'm not, particularly."

"Whatever troubles you today, tomorrow will still arrive." Mrs. Wendy smiled. "Here, try my new sweet iced tea. I'm not certain it will suit the local palate."

"The local palate? Aren't you a local, Mrs. Smyrin?" Klein asked with an amused shake of his head.

When someone asks you to sample a new recipe, that means it's free, doesn't it?

Wendy Smyrin raised her chin slightly. "You guessed correctly. I'm originally from the south. I came to Tingen with my husband, but that was more than forty years ago. Benson hadn't even been born then. Your parents hadn't met.

"I've never entirely adapted to northern tastes, and I still miss the food from home—pork sausages, potato bread, roasted pancakes, vegetables fried in lard, and roasted meat with our special sauces.

"And sweet iced tea, of course..."

A smile spread across Klein's face as he listened.

"Mrs. Smyrin, that is a remarkably effective way to make someone hungry. But I do feel better. Thank you."

"Good food can always cure sorrow." Wendy handed him a cup filled with reddish-brown liquid. "I made this from memory. Taste it and tell me what you think."

After thanking her, Klein took a sip. It resembled the iced black tea he remembered from Earth, though it was less sharp, with a stronger tea flavor and a more refreshing finish. The heat of the blazing sun seemed to vanish at once.

"It's excellent!" he declared.

"Then I can stop worrying." Wendy's eyes narrowed with pleasure as she watched him finish the cup.

After telling Mrs. Smyrin about the move and chatting briefly, Klein returned to the street he knew best.

Far fewer vendors worked during the afternoon. They would gather again after half past five. Those who remained looked sleepy and listless beneath the heat.

The moment Klein entered the neighborhood, an oppressive darkness settled over his heart. For no obvious reason, he felt heavy, dispirited, and deeply gloomy.

What's happening?

His heightened senses immediately warned him that something was wrong. He stopped and surveyed his surroundings but saw nothing unusual.

After a moment's thought, Klein raised a hand as though pondering something and tapped his glabella twice.

His vision transformed. Auras appeared around the vendors and passing pedestrians.

Before he could examine the colors representing their physical health, his attention was drawn to the shades of emotional desolation.

He couldn't determine each person's exact thoughts, but their apathy, despair, and gloom carved themselves deeply into his heart.

As he looked around, he realized that even the brilliant sun couldn't disperse those dark colors.

It was a melancholy born from years of relentless oppression.

Klein immediately understood.

As Old Neil had warned him, Spirit Vision exposed its user to an unfamiliar world and easily caused discomfort. It also left him unusually susceptible to other people's emotions.

His enhanced perception worked on a similar principle. The ability had awakened naturally after he became a Seer and remained active without conscious effort, allowing him to sense the presence of abnormalities directly.

Perception inevitably created a connection between observer and observed. To a Beyonder such as a Spirit Medium, a highly perceptive person might shine like a flame in the night. By the same token, a person with heightened perception would be strongly affected by any intense supernatural atmosphere. Only repeated practice could teach him to control, endure, and adapt to those influences.

"These oppressive colors must have accumulated over many years..."

Klein sighed and shook his head, unable to keep himself entirely detached.

He tapped his glabella twice more, deactivating Spirit Vision, and did his best to draw his spirituality inward.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

As Klein approached his former apartment, he extended his perception in search of abnormal existences, faint connections, or any sign of the Antigonus notebook that "he" had hidden.

The street appeared much as it always had, with filthy water and refuse covering the ground. Only the space immediately outside the apartment entrance had been swept reasonably clean.

Klein pushed open the half-closed door and circled the ground floor through shadows untouched by the afternoon sun.

The wooden staircase creaked continuously as he climbed.

The second floor was as dim as ever. Klein released his perception and peered into the darkness.

He found no trace of the notebook—and not a single invisible spiritual being.

"If such things were that easy to encounter, ordinary people would have recognized the supernatural long ago," Klein murmured thoughtfully.

He had already learned that most "spirits" didn't persist as complete spiritual bodies. They remained only as spirituality, with which only a Spirit Medium could communicate effectively.

After making a circuit of the third floor, Klein left the apartment and retraced the route preserved in the original Klein's memories toward Welch's residence.

He walked for a full hour without discovering anything.

Standing outside the garden of the detached house, he looked through the locked iron gate and muttered, "There's no point searching Welch's home itself, is there? Captain and Madam Daly must already have examined every inch of it.

"Besides, I don't have a key. Surely they don't expect me to climb the wall...

"I'll try another route tomorrow.

"All this walking, and there isn't even a step-count leaderboard..."

Still complaining inwardly, Klein returned to the nearby commercial district. He planned to take a public carriage to the Blackthorn Security Company and collect his daily allotment of thirty cartridges. He needed to make full use of his time and continue practicing.

A Seer's lack of swift and effective offensive abilities had to be compensated for with a revolver and cane.

The neighborhood around Welch's house was comparatively clean. Shops with bright, spotless windows lined both sides of the road.

At the next corner, Klein was searching for the carriage stop when several signs on the second story caught his eye.

"Harrods Department Store."

"Military Veterans' Mess."

"Divination Club."

Divination Club...

Klein silently repeated the name and remembered that he needed to "act" as a Seer.

I should investigate. Perhaps it will give me some ideas.

With conflicting thoughts running through his mind, Klein crossed the street, climbed to the second floor, and entered the foyer. An attractive female attendant greeted him.

The woman, whose yellow-brown hair was arranged in coils, looked him over and smiled.

"Sir, would you like a divination, or are you interested in joining the club?"

"What are the membership requirements?" Klein asked casually.

The woman delivered the explanation with practiced ease. "You fill out a registration form and pay the annual dues. Membership costs five pounds for the first year and one pound each year afterward. Don't worry—we aren't like political or business clubs, where an existing member must recommend you.

"Members may freely use our meeting room, divination rooms, and various tools. We provide complimentary coffee and tea, as well as newspapers and magazines. Lunch, dinner, alcoholic beverages, instructional texts, and divination supplies can be purchased at cost.

"We also invite at least one well-known diviner to lecture and answer questions every month.

"Most importantly, you'll meet a group of friends who share your interests and can exchange ideas with you."

That sounds rather appealing, except... I don't have the money.

Klein gave a self-deprecating smile.

"And if I only want a divination?"

Chapter 38: Novice Hobbyist

At Klein's question, the attractive attendant with elegantly coiled yellow-brown hair appeared to lose some of her enthusiasm. Nevertheless, she maintained her professional smile.

"Members may offer divinations to other people at the club. They set their own prices, and we collect a very small service fee. If you'd like your fortune told, you can consult this album. It lists the members currently accepting clients, along with their specialties and fees.

"However, it's Monday afternoon, so most of our members are at work. Only five are here today..."

As she spoke, she invited Klein to sit on a sofa beside the reception hall window. Then she opened the album and pointed out the available diviners.

"Hanass Vincent. A well-known Tingen diviner and the club's resident instructor. He is proficient in many forms of divination and charges four soli per session."

That's outrageously expensive. Four soli would buy Benson, Melissa, and me two excellent dinners...

Klein clicked his tongue inwardly but said nothing.

Noticing his reaction, the attendant turned the page and introduced one member after another.

"And finally, Glacis. He joined the club this year and specializes in tarot divination. His fee is two pence per session.

"Whom would you like to choose, sir?"

Klein answered without ceremony. "Mr. Glacis."

The attendant fell silent for two seconds.

"Sir, I should warn you that Mr. Glacis is still considered a novice."

"I understand, and I accept responsibility for my choice." Klein nodded with a smile.

"Then please follow me."

The woman rose and led him through a door beside the reception hall.

A short corridor stretched beyond it, ending in an open meeting room filled with sunlight. Tables and chairs occupied the space, along with newspapers, magazines, and decks of cards. The faint aroma of coffee drifted into the hall.

When they were two doors away from the meeting room, the attendant gestured for Klein to wait. She walked ahead and called softly through the doorway, "Mr. Glacis, someone would like a divination."

"From me?" a surprised, doubtful voice answered. A chair scraped against the floor.

"Yes. Which divination room would you prefer?" the attendant asked without emotion.

"The Topaz Room. I like topaz."

Glacis appeared at the meeting room entrance and looked curiously toward Klein.

He was in his thirties, with slightly dark skin and deep-green eyes. Soft, light-yellow hair framed his face. Dressed in a white shirt and black vest, with a monocle hanging from a cord against his chest, he gave the impression of a respectable gentleman.

The attendant said nothing further. She unlocked the Topaz Room beside the meeting room and opened its door.

The curtains within had been tightly drawn, leaving the room dim. Apparently, diviners believed darkness made it easier to receive revelations from gods and spirits—and thus obtain more accurate results.

"Good afternoon. I'm Glacis. I confess, I didn't expect anyone to choose me." He offered a gentleman's bow, entered briskly, and sat behind a long table. "To be perfectly honest, I've only recently begun giving readings to other people. I have little experience and can't yet call myself an accomplished diviner. You still have time to reconsider."

Klein returned the bow, stepped inside, and closed the door behind him.

By the faint light filtering through the curtains, he smiled. "You're an admirably honest man, but I rarely reconsider once I've made a choice."

"Please sit." Glacis indicated the chair across from him. After thinking for several seconds, he said, "Divination is only a hobby for me. In life, people often receive guidance from the divine, but ordinary individuals cannot properly understand it. Divination exists to interpret that guidance, and that's why I joined the club.

"Since I still lack confidence, let us treat today's reading as an exchange between fellow enthusiasts, free of charge. I'll pay the club's fee myself. It's only a quarter-penny. What do you say?"

Klein neither accepted nor refused. Instead, he smiled.

"You must have a respectable position with a comfortable salary."

As he spoke, Klein leaned slightly forward, rested his forehead against his right fist, and tapped his glabella twice.

"That doesn't improve the accuracy of my readings," Glacis replied humorously. "Do you have a headache? Are you seeking a divination about your health?"

"Only a slight one. I want to determine the location of an object." Klein had already prepared the excuse and slowly leaned back.

Glacis's aura now appeared clearly before him. The orange glow around his lungs was dark and thin, and the weakness had begun to diminish the brightness of neighboring regions.

Those aren't signs of simple exhaustion...

Klein gave an almost imperceptible nod.

"Are you searching for something you've lost?" After a little thought, Glacis said, "Then we'll begin with a simple determination."

He pushed the neatly stacked tarot deck across the black tabletop.

"Calm yourself. Picture the object and repeatedly ask, 'Can it still be found?' While maintaining that thought, shuffle and cut the deck."

"All right."

Klein didn't actually remember what the ancient notebook looked like. He could only repeat the question in his mind:

Can the Antigonus family's notebook still be found?

As he concentrated, he shuffled and cut the deck with practiced hands.

Glacis drew the top card and placed it facedown and horizontal before Klein.

"Rotate it clockwise until it's vertical, then turn it over. If the card is reversed—meaning the illustration faces away from you—the object cannot be found. If it is upright, we can continue divining its actual location."

Following the instructions, Klein rotated the horizontal card clockwise.

He gripped its lower edge and flipped it over.

The card was reversed.

"What a pity," Glacis said with a sigh.

Klein didn't answer. All his attention was fixed on the image before him.

The reversed card depicted a figure in magnificent clothing and an elaborate headdress—The Fool.

The Fool again? That can't possibly be mere coincidence...

According to The Hanged Man and Old Neil, divination resulted from communication among one's spirituality, the spirit world, and a higher-dimensional self. Tarot cards were merely convenient tools for interpreting the truth's symbolic expression. In principle, the particular divination instrument shouldn't matter or alter the result.

Klein frowned and thought for a moment.

"Can we determine whether someone else has already taken the item?"

"Certainly. Repeat the same procedure." Glacis nodded with renewed interest.

Klein shuffled and cut the cards while concentrating on the new question.

He drew a card, placed it horizontally, and rotated it clockwise. His expression remained solemn throughout the preparation.

Drawing a deep breath, he reached out and turned it over.

Please don't let it be The Fool again...

He immediately relaxed. The reversed card was The Star.

"It appears that no one else has taken the object," Glacis interpreted with a smile.

Klein nodded, raised his right hand, and tapped his glabella as though lost in thought. Then he removed two dark copper pennies from his pocket and pushed them across the table.

"Didn't I say the reading was free?" Glacis frowned.

Klein stood with a laugh.

"This is the respect divination deserves."

"Very well. Thank you for your generosity." Glacis rose and extended his hand.

After shaking it, Klein stepped back twice, turned, and opened the door.

Just before leaving, he looked over his shoulder and cleared his throat.

"Mr. Glacis, I suggest that you consult a doctor as soon as possible. Ask them to pay particular attention to your lungs."

"Why?" Glacis asked in surprise.

Is he cursing me because he disliked the result?

Klein considered how to explain, then said, "It's based on your complexion. Your, ah... your glabella appears dark."

"My glabella appears dark?" Glacis had never heard such a diagnosis before.

Klein offered no further explanation. Smiling, he left and closed the wooden door behind him.

"Is he an unlicensed physician, or perhaps an herbalist?" Glacis shook his head in amusement and picked up his divination monocle.

Upon closer inspection, his glabella did indeed look dark.

But that was plainly due to the room. With the curtains shut, not only his glabella but his entire face was wrapped in shadow.

"Not a particularly amusing joke," Glacis muttered.

Even so, unease prompted him to perform a divination concerning his own health, just to make certain nothing was wrong.

After leaving the Divination Club, Klein added another item to his plans.

He would save as much as possible until he could afford the annual membership fee. Once he became a member, he could begin acting as a Seer in earnest.

He had no resources or contacts with which to practice independently, and his pride wouldn't allow him to set up a stall on the street like a common hawker.

A public carriage arrived several minutes later. Klein paid two pence and rode the short distance to Zouteland Street.

When he entered the Blackthorn Security Company, the familiar brown-haired receptionist was nowhere to be seen. Instead, the black-haired, green-eyed Leonard Mitchell sat behind the counter, his manner as poetic as ever.

"Good afternoon. Where is Rozanne?" Klein asked, removing his hat and bowing.

Leonard smiled and pointed beyond the partition. "She has tonight's shift in the armory."

Before Klein could ask anything else, Leonard rose and spoke with an air of casual reflection.

"Klein, there's a question that has been troubling me."

"What is it?" Klein looked blankly at him.

Leonard paced several steps, then turned and looked directly into Klein's eyes.

"Why did Welch and Naya kill themselves at the scene, while you returned home before doing the same?"

"The official theory is that the unknown existence first compelled me to take and hide the Antigonus family's notebook," Klein replied.

Leonard continued pacing, then stopped once more before Klein.

"If the purpose of your suicide was to silence you and erase every clue, why weren't you made to destroy the notebook immediately?"

[1] In Chinese folk belief, a darkened glabella is traditionally regarded as an ominous sign.

Chapter 39: An Interesting Trick

In truth, I don't know whether the notebook was hidden or destroyed. But reasoning backward, if the goal was destruction, it could have been done at Welch's house. There was no need for me to carry it elsewhere first...

Leonard's question instantly transformed Klein into an armchair detective.

With a sigh, he said, "Perhaps the unknown existence Welch, Naya, and I encountered delighted in sacrifices—or wanted the same tragedy to repeat. Our suicides at Welch's residence would have been discovered almost immediately. By compelling me to take and hide the notebook, it could prepare a second round of entertainment. Then something unexpected interfered, and my suicide attempt failed."

Klein had derived the theory from all the novels, films, and television dramas involving cults and human sacrifice he had consumed in his previous life.

As for the unexpected interference, he knew exactly what it had been: the arrival of a transmigrator.

"A reasonable explanation, but I can imagine other possibilities," Leonard Mitchell replied. "Welch and Naya's sacrificial deaths may have allowed the unknown existence to descend into our world. Perhaps the notebook was a vessel—or an incubator for something evil. It made you carry the notebook away and conceal it because it feared we would discover and destroy the newborn evil before it became powerful."

He stared into Klein's eyes and smiled faintly.

"Or perhaps the notebook truly was destroyed. Its contents needed to disappear in order to conceal the vessel—or the thing being incubated. That would also explain why your suicide failed."

What is he implying? Does he suspect me? Does he think the original Klein's body became a vessel or incubator for some evil presence?

No. What it actually became was a vessel for a transmigrator... Although "incubator" certainly isn't the right word.

Startled, Klein privately criticized the theory while carefully composing his response.

"I won't attempt to defend what I can't remember. Both the Captain and Madam Daly have already confirmed that nothing is wrong with me. Your joke isn't amusing."

"I'm merely considering possibilities. Perhaps the unknown existence suffered some setback while descending, which caused your suicide to fail. In any event, we trust that the Goddess will protect us."

Leonard laughed and changed the subject. "Did you find anything this afternoon?"

After this exchange and their previous encounters, Klein was deeply wary of him.

"No," he answered evenly. "I intend to try another route tomorrow afternoon."

He pointed beyond the partition. "For now, I need to collect my ammunition."

The Shooting Club remained open until nine in the evening. Most of its members could only visit after finishing work, so its busiest hours came late in the day.

"May the Goddess bless you." Leonard smiled and traced the sign of the crimson moon across his chest.

He watched Klein pass through the partition and listened as his footsteps descended the stairs. Gradually, Leonard's smile disappeared, replaced by doubt in his green eyes.

He muttered something in a dissatisfied tone.

...

Klein descended the stairs and followed the gaslit passage toward the armory and archives.

The iron door stood open. Brown-haired Rozanne was beside the table, speaking with a middle-aged man in a top hat and a thick black beard.

"Good afternoon—no, good evening. It's always night down here." Rozanne turned and immediately bombarded Klein with questions. "Old Neil told me you've become a Beyonder. Is it called Seer?"

She made no attempt to hide either her curiosity or concern.

Klein smiled and nodded. "Good afternoon, Miss Rozanne. It certainly feels like perpetual night here, though I find the atmosphere peaceful. And your description isn't quite accurate. You should say that the Sequence potion I consumed was called Seer."

"So you chose to become a Beyonder after all..." Rozanne sighed and fell silent in thought.

Klein looked politely at the man beside her. "And you are?"

Another Nighthawk? Or one of the two civilian employees I haven't met?

Rozanne pursed her lips. "Bredt, one of our colleagues. He wants to exchange shifts so he can have the evening off the day after tomorrow. He and his wife are going to a theater in the North Borough to see The Prideful One for their fifteenth wedding anniversary. He's a true romantic gentleman."

Bredt smiled and extended a hand. "With Miss Rozanne present, no introduction requires repeating. Hello, Klein. I didn't expect you to become a Beyonder so soon. As for me... I doubt I'll ever find the courage."

"Perhaps ignorance truly is courage," Klein replied self-deprecatingly as they shook hands.

"Lacking courage isn't necessarily a weakness." Bredt shook his head. "A Beyonder once told me shortly before his death never to investigate strange and dangerous matters. The less you know, the longer you live."

Rozanne interjected, "Don't let that worry you, Klein. Old Neil says a Seer serves primarily in a support role. You should be relatively safe as long as you don't try communicating with unknown existences. But why are you dressed like that? It's hardly becoming of a gentleman. And what brought you here?"

"I came for my daily thirty cartridges."

Klein deliberately ignored her first question, confident she would soon forget it.

"All right." Rozanne pointed toward the table. "Bredt, he's all yours. You know where the keys and ammunition are. Oh, Old Neil is so miserly. He didn't leave any of his hand-ground coffee, even though he promised I could drink as much as I wanted today..."

She continued chattering while Klein received his cartridges.

The two left together and parted ways on Zouteland Street. Rozanne took a public carriage home, while Klein walked into the Shooting Club.

Bang! Bang! Bang!

Again and again, Klein practiced the complete sequence: gripping the revolver, raising it, firing, opening the cylinder, ejecting the spent casings, and loading fresh cartridges. He repeated each movement until it began settling into muscle memory.

He rested periodically, using the pauses to review his technique and correct mistakes.

After completing his shooting practice, Klein remained at the club to exercise. He performed push-ups and other simple movements, working hard to improve his physical condition.

By the time he finished and boarded a trackless carriage home, it was nearly seven and the sky had gone dark.

Klein was considering a visit to the market or the street vendors for dinner ingredients when the front door opened. Melissa entered carrying her schoolbag, heavy with stationery, and a considerable quantity of groceries.

"I expected you and Benson to return late." Seeing her brother's questioning expression, she explained in her usual serious manner, "I took one soli from your hiding place this morning."

"If you took the money, why didn't you ride the public carriage to school?" Klein asked, remembering his concern from that morning.

Melissa frowned. "Why should I? It costs four pence each way—eight pence for a round trip. If you, Benson, and I all used public transportation, we'd spend twenty-four pence every day. That's two whole soli! Six days a week comes to twelve soli, almost as much as our rent."

Stop. Please stop showing off your arithmetic...

Amused, Klein lowered one hand in a calming gesture.

Melissa paused, then added, "Walking is good exercise. Our teacher says everyone should exercise regularly. Besides, I can collect damaged mechanical parts along the way."

Klein chuckled. "Then let's calculate it differently. Twelve soli for transportation, plus twelve soli and three pence for rent, comes to one pound, four soli, and three pence. Benson's weekly salary covers all of that with money left over—and he received last week's wages already.

"I still earn another pound and ten soli each week. Even if we eat meat every day and include gas, coal, firewood, seasonings, and other expenses, we'll have money left as long as we're sensible about lunch. We could even subscribe to the morning newspaper for a penny a day.

"In two months, once the advance on my salary has been balanced out, I'll begin saving for you and Benson. Both of you can have new clothes."

"But we have to prepare for accidents," Melissa insisted.

Klein smiled at her. "Then we'll eat meat a little less often. Don't you think spending fifty—no, nearly a hundred minutes walking every day is wasteful? You could use that time to read, solve problems, and improve your marks.

"Then you'll graduate with excellent results and find a well-paid position. Once that happens, what will we have to worry about?"

Melissa fell silent.

Drawing on all his experience arguing with strangers online, Klein finally persuaded her to use the public carriage for school.

Phew. I finally tricked her into it. No, "tricked" is unfair. I convinced her.

He took the groceries from Melissa and sighed.

"Remember to buy beef—or other meat, such as lamb or chicken. Eat well and enjoy your meals. A healthy body and sharp mind are essential if you're going to meet the demands of your studies."

Just talking about it is making me hungry...

Melissa pursed her lips. After several seconds, she answered, "All right."

...

The following morning, Klein made certain Melissa boarded a public carriage. Then he and Benson separated for their respective workplaces.

The moment Klein entered the Blackthorn Security Company, he saw Old Neil conversing with Rozanne beside the reception desk. Old Neil still wore his antiquated black robe without the slightest concern for other people's stares. Rozanne had changed into a casual cream-colored dress.

"Good morning, Mr. Neil. Miss Rozanne." Klein removed his hat in greeting.

Old Neil gave him a mischievous look. "Good morning. You didn't hear anything you shouldn't have last night, did you?"

"No. I slept extremely well." Klein was rather puzzled by that himself.

Perhaps his perception simply wasn't strong enough yet.

"Good. Don't dwell on it. Those things aren't so easily heard." Old Neil pointed beyond the partition. "Go to the armory. We'll continue your lessons in mysticism this morning."

Klein nodded and followed him downstairs, arriving at the armory in time to relieve Bredt after his night shift.

"What are we studying today?" Klein asked curiously.

Old Neil drew out his answer. "Complicated foundational knowledge. But first, I'll teach you an interesting little trick."

He pointed to the silver chain around his wrist. A pure moonstone hung from its end.

Chapter 40: Mysticism Curriculum

"An interesting trick?" Klein asked, his curiosity immediately aroused.

Old Neil chuckled. "I'll make my inspection of the armory, storeroom, and archives. While I'm gone, use the cups on the table to prepare two servings of coffee. Put something unpleasant into one of them—whatever you like. Use your imagination. I ask only that you don't waste too much coffee. Those beans were grown on the Feynapotter Plateau and ground by hand—my hand!"

"All right."

Although Klein had no idea what Old Neil intended, he agreed readily.

He watched the old man unlock the armory's iron gate with a copper key and listened as his footsteps echoed within. Then Klein arranged the two cups and confirmed that the kettle still held hot water.

He opened a silver tin and used a small, gleaming spoon to place one measure of rich, aromatic coffee grounds in each cup. He poured in hot water and stirred.

As a transmigrator from an age of material abundance, Klein was no stranger to coffee—though his experience was largely limited to the instant variety.

Once the coffee was ready, he considered what to add. He sat, crossed his right leg, scraped a little dried mud from the sole of his leather boot, and dropped it into the cup on the left.

Then he stirred carefully until the two cups were virtually indistinguishable in color and scent.

Several minutes later, Old Neil emerged from the armory swinging his keys. He shut the iron gate behind him with a clang.

"Finished?" His slightly clouded, dark-red eyes settled on Klein across the table.

"Yes." Klein nodded.

Old Neil chuckled, removed the silver chain from his wrist, and sat.

His expression quickly became serene. Holding the chain in his left hand, he suspended its moonstone above the cup on his right, almost touching the surface.

Within the calm stillness, the moonstone suddenly trembled. The chain began rotating counterclockwise.

"This is the cup containing the unpleasant substance," Old Neil declared with certainty.

Without waiting for Klein to confirm, he put away the chain, picked up the other cup, and took a sip.

"Do you prefer your coffee black? I normally add a spoonful of sugar and a spoonful of milk."

Rather than answer, Klein asked eagerly, "That divination was remarkably accurate. Was it because of the moonstone? It is moonstone, isn't it?"

"The method is called pendulum dowsing, or spirit dowsing. By using a natural material such as crystal, a gemstone, or a special metal, your Astral Projection draws upon its connection with the spirit world and the cosmos to communicate with spiritual intelligence. This allows you to determine whether something is favorable or harmful.

"Take these two cups of coffee. Counterclockwise motion indicates something negative, while clockwise means positive. No movement indicates neutrality. You may also write the proposition on a piece of paper—but remember, it must be a statement, not a question," Old Neil explained in detail.

Klein thought for a moment. "So interrogative sentences don't work?"

"Correct. Don't write, 'Is so-and-so willing to become my fiancée?' Write, 'So-and-so is willing to become my fiancée.' Place the paper on a table, then hold the pendulum chain in your dominant hand. Your dominant hand, mind you."

Old Neil smiled before continuing. "Keep your arm steady and adjust the chain until the moonstone hangs immediately above the writing, nearly touching the page. Close your eyes and repeat the statement silently seven times. Then open them and observe the pendulum. If it hasn't moved, close your eyes and repeat the process until it does."

Klein nodded. "Counterclockwise means no, and clockwise means yes?"

"It may also signify failure and success," Old Neil corrected.

He proceeded to teach Klein several other applications and practical details of spirit pendulum divination.

After considering them, Klein realized that the technique was extremely useful. In an unfamiliar place, for example, he could quickly determine whether food offered to him was poisonous without possessing any knowledge of field biology.

Of course, the method was excessively simple. It could provide only two or three possible answers and allowed no deeper investigation or interpretation. Something harmful in its present state might become beneficial after processing. Certain foods were technically unhealthy but caused no serious harm, and eating them would obviously be preferable to starvation. Spirit dowsing couldn't distinguish such nuances.

"I'll need to save money for a crystal or a piece of pure silver so I can make a spirit pendulum," Klein said with a sigh.

Old Neil looked at him in puzzlement. "Why would you buy one? Apply for it. A spirit pendulum is standard equipment for Beyonders, especially those of us in support roles. The armory still has one made from topaz and pure silver."

"But I'm not yet a formal member of the team..."

Klein's heart quickened with excitement, though he remained hesitant.

Old Neil chuckled. "Formal member or not, every Beyonder must receive certain conveniences. After all, they haven't increased your salary."

"Perhaps 'benefit' is the proper word. I'll apply to the Captain later."

Klein privately clenched his fists and made up his mind.

He wouldn't know whether Dunn would approve unless he asked.

"Very good." Old Neil smiled. "Now we can formally begin your curriculum in mysticism. We start with symbolism. Do you know what that means?"

Klein recalled fragments of what he had heard and what he had witnessed in the spirit world and above the gray fog. After considering his answer, he said, "The spirit world, the illusory cosmos, and other unknown realms lie beyond the world accessible to our ordinary senses. Information obtained through our eyes, ears, and noses cannot accurately describe them.

"Instead, we receive indescribable experiences and direct revelations. These manifest as abstract characters or pictorial symbols, each representing particular things and meanings."

"Precisely. Just what I would expect from a Seer." Old Neil nodded solemnly. "Only after learning to interpret symbols can you truly take your next step into mysticism. The images on tarot cards—and every element within those images—are symbols. They are human creations designed to help us understand and interpret primordial revelation."

He pulled a sheet of paper toward himself, picked up a fountain pen, and drew a short curved line.

Then he added several vertical strokes beneath it.

Looking up at Klein, he asked, "Do you know what this represents?"

Klein studied it for a long time before answering uncertainly, "Eyelashes?"

Old Neil exhaled slowly.

"This is the symbol of the Bumper Harvest constellation. This one represents the Thunderous constellation, and this is Frost..."

He rapidly drew several more symbols.

As Klein memorized them, he couldn't help commenting, "Those constellation names are remarkably... unsophisticated. Yes, that's the word. Unsophisticated."

They're painfully crude and primitive.

Old Neil smiled. "Emperor Roselle felt the same way. He repeatedly proposed changing the constellations' names to things such as Virgo, Cancer, and Scorpio. Unfortunately, even he lacked the power to overcome tradition. At least the old names and their corresponding dates remain useful for planting and harvesting."

"Emperor Roselle certainly had ideas," Klein replied, at a loss for anything else to say.

Yes. Roselle was probably a reasonably decent man while he was alive...

Old Neil failed to recognize the private joke. He continued teaching the foundational symbols of mysticism: the constellations, the sun, the crimson moon, the Brown Planet, the Scarlet Planet, the Blue Planet, and many others.

Along the way, he explained how to draw an astrological chart for divination, what precautions to observe, how crystal balls were made, which materials were suitable, and how to choose incantations. The torrent of information left Klein overwhelmed.

Had the Seer potion not modestly improved his memory, he would have asked Old Neil to stop much earlier so he could absorb what he had already heard.

"That will be enough for today's lesson." Old Neil took out a gold pocket watch, opened it with a snap, and checked the time. "Review everything yourself, and ask me about anything you don't understand. Don't forget the historical documents I prepared for you. Frankly, the sight of them frightens even me."

"All right."

Klein collected the sheets covered with Old Neil's symbols and rapidly reviewed the day's material to prevent it from slipping away.

Old Neil took a sip from a freshly prepared cup of coffee.

"Memory alone isn't sufficient. You must use the knowledge repeatedly until it becomes instinctive. The same applies to Cogitation—you need to practice every day. Only continued practice and use will allow you to master the potion's powers, uncover its concealed mysteries, and eliminate its undesirable effects."

The remark reminded Klein of acting and the Divination Club.

Carefully probing, he said, "My potion's abilities are closely connected to divination. Practicing alone won't be enough. I need contact with many people so I can perform readings for them and master the abilities more quickly. Once I have some money to spare, I intend to join the Divination Club on Howes Street in the North Borough and become a genuine Seer."

He couldn't conceal such activity from the Nighthawks forever. It was better to prepare them now.

"Your idea is very similar to Daly's. She was always insisting that she needed to become a true Spirit Medium." Old Neil shook his head and laughed. "But why wait until you have money to spare? Submit an application to Dunn and have the expense reimbursed."

Klein stared at him.

Old Neil continued with a perfectly serious expression. "Organizations such as divination clubs may shelter cultists or members of evil organizations. As a Nighthawk civilian employee and registered Beyonder, your membership would allow us to monitor them conveniently. It's part of your work.

"We already inspect such places periodically, but we lack the manpower for continuous observation. Now we can assign the responsibility to you."

I can do that?

Klein was dumbfounded by Old Neil's solemn reasoning.

That's using an official expense account for a private purpose!

I have no experience with anything like this.

In the end, I'm only an armchair expert...

"Would you prefer to pay with your own money?" Old Neil asked with a smile when he saw Klein's expression.

Klein immediately shook his head. "I intend to report the matter to the Captain."

Old Neil nodded in satisfaction, then glanced at the untouched cup containing the unpleasant substance.

"What did you put in that?"

Klein smiled awkwardly. "Only a little dirt from the bottom of my boot. It was nearly the same color as your coffee grounds."

Old Neil froze. Then he clapped one hand over his mouth and roared, "Why haven't you thrown it away yet?"

Chapter 41: Audrey and Her Susie

After disposing of the contaminated coffee, Klein returned to the armory and collected the thick stack of historical documents and explanatory notes Old Neil had prepared. Then he followed the line of wall lamps up the stairs to the Blackthorn Security Company.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

His footsteps echoed through the enclosed, silent basement.

Emerging from the spiral staircase, Klein pushed open the door, oriented himself, and headed toward the second office across the hall.

After two days, he had developed a general understanding of Blackthorn's layout.

Visitors entered through a spacious reception room furnished with sofas and tables. Beyond the partition lay an inner corridor. Three rooms stood on the left: nearest was Mrs. Orianna's accounting office, followed by a break room containing several sofa beds, and finally the staircase leading underground.

Three more rooms occupied the right side. Dunn Smith's office came first, then the civilian employees' office with its typewriter, and at the far end, the recreation room reserved for full members of the Nighthawk team.

Klein had once seen Leonard Mitchell playing cards there with two teammates. He suspected the game was Fighting the Landlord, though Emperor Roselle had renamed it Fighting Evil. The rules remained exactly as Klein remembered them.

Bredt was entitled to sleep after working the night shift. Rozanne occupied the reception desk, while Cesare Francis—the carriage driver responsible for purchasing necessities and collecting supplies—was away as usual. When Klein opened the civilian office, all three desks were vacant. Only the typewriter waited in silence.

"An Akerson Company Model 1346..."

Klein had seen similar machines in his mentor's office and at Welch's home. The intricate mechanism possessed a distinctly industrial beauty.

He sat before it, prepared himself, and practiced several keystrokes without paper.

At first, he instinctively tried to convert the local language into Chinese pinyin. Once he adjusted, the corresponding fragments of the original Klein's memory settled into place, and the mistakes ceased.

Clack! Clack! Clack!

The typewriter's rhythmic impacts formed a melody born from the heavy union of metal and industry. To that accompaniment, Klein quickly composed an expense application.

He didn't immediately take it to Dunn. Instead, he settled down and carefully studied Old Neil's materials, some reviewing what he already knew and some introducing entirely new information.

Near noon, Klein stretched his neck and put away the documents. He then reviewed and consolidated everything he had learned during the morning's mysticism lesson.

Only after finishing did he carry the application next door and knock gently.

Dunn was waiting for lunch to arrive. When Klein handed him the paper, the corners of his mouth rose.

"Did Old Neil teach you to do this?"

"Yes." Klein betrayed Old Neil without the slightest hesitation.

Dunn picked up his dark-red fountain pen and signed the application.

"As it happens, I'm preparing our funding request to the Church and the police department for July, August, and September. I'll include this. Once it's approved, collect the money from Mrs. Orianna. You may withdraw the spirit pendulum from the armory this afternoon."

"Thank you, Captain."

Klein's concise response failed to conceal the delight in his voice and eyes.

Before leaving, he casually asked, "Shouldn't the budget for July through September have been submitted in June?"

Why are you applying for July's funding after July has already begun?

Dunn remained silent for several seconds, then took a sip of coffee.

"We handled three cases in June. I was so busy that I forgot."

Just what I'd expect from the Captain and his terrible memory...

Realizing he had asked a question better left unspoken, Klein gave a small laugh and departed immediately.

After that, he settled into a simple but regular routine. He spent half an hour practicing Cogitation early each morning, followed by two hours of mysticism instruction and ninety minutes studying historical documents. After lunch, he took a brief nap in the break room to restore his energy.

In the afternoon, he collected his cartridges and went to the Shooting Club. Once practice ended, he walked to Welch's neighborhood, searched along a different route, and returned through Iron Cross Street. This allowed him to save the carriage fare. Whenever time permitted, he practiced Spirit Vision and spirit dowsing, buying groceries on the way home.

...

In a private laboratory equipped for chemistry, the tall, blond Audrey stared at the cup in her hand. Countless bubbles rose through its contents, and an unnatural stillness filled the room.

At last, the liquid settled into a thick silver substance.

"I really am talented at mysticism. I succeeded on my first attempt! And here I was so afraid of failure that I prepared two complete sets of ingredients," she murmured happily.

Audrey put away the materials she had taken from her family's storerooms or obtained through exchanges. Then she drew a deep breath, preparing to close her eyes and drink the Spectator potion.

A dog barked outside the laboratory.

Audrey frowned at once.

She hid the cup of silver liquid in a dark corner, crossed to the door, and turned the handle.

"Susie, who is it?" she asked the golden retriever sitting outside.

Susie wagged her tail ingratiatingly. Audrey's personal maid, Annie, had appeared farther along the corridor.

Audrey stepped out and shut the laboratory door.

"Didn't I tell you not to disturb me during chemistry experiments?"

Annie looked distressed. "But an invitation has arrived from the Duchess—Duchess Della."

"Duke Negan's wife?" Audrey walked several steps toward her.

"Yes. She has secured the services of Madam Vivi, one of the palace pastry chefs, and wishes to invite you and Madam to afternoon tea," Annie explained.

Audrey discreetly tapped her cheek. "Tell Mother I have a headache. Perhaps the heat has left me slightly dehydrated. Ask her to convey my apologies to Duchess Della."

As she spoke, Audrey allowed herself to look increasingly frail.

"Miss, it isn't merely afternoon tea. She's also holding a literary salon," Annie added.

"A literary salon won't cure my dizziness. I need to rest," Audrey refused firmly.

Privately, she thought, If they insist, I'll faint in front of everyone. My etiquette instructor says I can do it perfectly... Wait. Did I hear something?

"Very well." Annie sighed. "Would you like me to help you back to your room?"

"No. I need to clean the laboratory first."

Audrey yearned to return and drink the potion immediately, but forced herself to remain patient. Only after Annie disappeared did she go back to the laboratory entrance.

The golden retriever that had been waiting outside was gone. Worse, the laboratory door stood half-open.

I forgot Susie knows how to open handled doors... What was that noise? Oh no!

Hearing a brittle sound from within, Audrey suddenly understood and rushed into the laboratory.

The cup lay shattered on the floor. Susie was licking up the final drop of silver liquid.

Audrey froze in the doorway like a statue.

Susie immediately sat and looked at her owner with perfect innocence, tail wagging.

Beyond Pritz Harbor lay an island perpetually shrouded in storms. An ancient sailing ship rested in its harbor.

A blond man in a robe embroidered with lightning stood across from Alger Wilson.

"Alger, you could have returned to the kingdom and become captain of a Mandated Punisher team—or a respected bishop. Why did you choose to sail the open sea as captain of the Blue Avenger?"

Alger's rough features remained stoic.

"The sea belongs to the Storm. It is the Lord's kingdom. I choose to obey His will and watch over this part of His domain."

"Very well." The blond man clenched one fist and struck it against his chest. "May the Storm be with you."

"May the Storm be with you." Alger returned the formal salute.

Standing on deck beside several sailors, he watched his companions disembark and disappear into the distance.

Sainz, you don't understand because you don't know enough...

...

Meanwhile, a panic-stricken Audrey finished compounding her second potion.

When the silver liquid appeared identical to the first, she nearly wept with relief.

Gulp!

She drank the Spectator potion at once.

...

On Friday, a storm descended upon Tingen. Heavy rain hammered the windows of every home and business.

Inside the Blackthorn Security Company, Klein, Rozanne, and Bredt sat on the reception room sofas eating lunch.

The office had only a kettle for boiling water, so leftovers couldn't be reheated. Klein couldn't eat rye bread every day, nor could he afford a carriage home for lunch. Doing so would force him either to walk from Iron Cross Street to Welch's neighborhood in the afternoon or pay for yet another ride back. Both options were wasteful. Consequently, he had begun joining Rozanne and the others for the so-called office rations.

At half past ten each morning, a waiter from nearby Old Will's Restaurant arrived to take their orders. Once he knew the quantity, he returned at half past twelve with the food packed in individual containers. At three, he came back to collect the dishes and take orders for dinner.

Each meal contained meat, vegetables, and bread. The portions were small but just sufficient to satisfy an ordinary person. Depending on quality, prices ranged from seven to ten pence.

Klein always swallowed his pride and ordered the seven-pence meal. It usually contained half a pound of wheat bread, a small piece of meat prepared in varying ways, one ladle of thick vegetable soup, and a little butter or margarine.

"Only one Nighthawk is in the office today," Rozanne remarked as she lifted a spoonful of thick soup to her mouth.

"I heard there's a case involving cultists in Golden Indus. The police requested two Nighthawks," Bredt said, setting down his bread.

Klein used the last of his wheat bread to soak up the remaining meat juices and put it into his mouth without speaking.

Beneath his left sleeve, he wore a silver chain with a topaz pendant.

At that moment, someone knocked on the half-closed front door.

"Please come in." Startled, Rozanne set down her spoon, wiped her mouth quickly with a handkerchief, and stood.

The door opened. A man in a half top hat entered, the left shoulder of his black formal coat soaked through.

His hair was graying at the temples. Closing his umbrella, he looked at Klein and the others.

"Is this the former mercenary company?"

"You could say that," Rozanne answered automatically.

The thin man coughed.

"I have a commission for you."

Chapter 42: Butler Klee

A commission? You've probably come to the wrong place. The words "Security Company" on our sign are little more than a disguise...

Klein immediately suppressed the urge to make a sarcastic remark. How he missed having an online forum and a screen on which to share his complaints.

Then he remembered asking Dunn a similar question. The Captain had explained that Blackthorn could accept ordinary commissions whenever the team was free. The proceeds went into the team's discretionary fund, with bonuses awarded to those who participated.

Rozanne's eyes shifted as she considered the situation.

"All our security personnel are currently away on assignments. The earliest any of them will return is in about an hour. If your matter isn't urgent, you're welcome to wait and consider our services."

Of the six full Nighthawks, Captain Dunn Smith had been summoned to the cathedral for a discussion with the bishop. Leonard Mitchell was guarding Chanis Gate in his place.

Corpse Collector Frye and Sleepless Royale Reideen had gone to the Golden Indus Borough to assist the police with a robbery that showed signs of cult involvement. Sleepless Kenley White was on leave, while Midnight Poet Seeka Tron was conducting the routine patrol of Raphael Cemetery in the northern suburbs.

That left only two Beyonders. Old Neil was elderly and physically frail and had stopped accepting field assignments long ago. Klein was an inexperienced novice, still inadequate in almost every respect.

"Everyone is away..." Holding his umbrella in one hand, the thin man's expression darkened. He removed his hat and bowed. "Forgive the intrusion. Goodbye."

He turned, descended the stairs, and left Number 36 Zouteland Street amid the splattering rain and howling wind.

"What a terrible pity." Rozanne watched him go and sighed regretfully.

She wouldn't have received a share of the commission, but a successful job would almost certainly have meant an excellent meal for everyone.

"There's nothing we can do. Chanis Gate must be guarded at all times." Satisfied, Klein set down his utensils. Although he disliked the turnip-and-vegetable soup, he had finished every drop. "Would you have sent Bredt on the assignment? Or gone yourself?"

Rozanne rolled her eyes and giggled. "Bredt couldn't manage it, but you might. Our very own Mr. Seer—"

She abruptly realized what she had said and clapped a hand over her mouth. The front door wasn't fully closed. If someone passed outside and overheard her mention Beyonders, it would constitute a breach of secrecy.

"Thank goodness the Captain isn't here..." Rozanne peered toward the door and furtively stuck out her tongue. "Otherwise, I'd have to confess again."

Bredt and Klein exchanged a look and laughed together before clearing away their dishes.

Once they finished, Klein decided to remain at Blackthorn because he hadn't brought an umbrella and the rain showed no sign of stopping.

He selected several newspapers, settled onto a soft but springy sofa, and leisurely began his afternoon break.

"Airship route between Backlund and Desi Bay officially opens..."

"The Complete Adventures of Great Detective Manseng to be published..."

"An advertisement for Lagolas Weapons. A standard six-shot revolver costs three pounds ten soli, while a double-barreled shotgun costs two pounds..."

As Klein leafed through the Tingen City Honest Paper, one report caught his attention.

"The suspect responsible for the deaths of Mr. Welch and Miss Naya has been apprehended. We hope this news will bring much-needed relief from the fear that has gripped the North, Golden Indus, and East Boroughs... Mr. McGovern, banker and father of the late Mr. Welch, has escorted his youngest son's remains to Constant City, where a grand funeral will be held..."

Klein read the passage several times before sighing.

It appears Welch's father accepted the police explanation and didn't hire a private investigator after all.

His grief over losing his youngest son can't be greater than what my parents felt when they lost their only child...

A somber mood descended over Klein. He sat motionless for a long time.

He neither found it strange that he hadn't been invited to Welch's or Naya's funeral nor felt offended by the omission.

Once everything settles down, I'll find an opportunity to place flowers on their graves.

Klein was preparing to nap in the break room when someone knocked on the reception hall door again.

"Please come in." Rozanne, who had been nodding off, jerked awake.

The half-closed door opened, and the same thin man returned.

"May I wait here? Your mercenaries—no, your security personnel—should return soon, shouldn't they?" He spoke earnestly, though he tried to conceal his anxiety.

"Certainly. Please sit." Rozanne gestured toward a nearby sofa.

Curious, Klein asked, "How did you hear about our company? Did someone recommend us?"

The man had made two journeys through a violent afternoon storm and was still willing to wait.

Perhaps the Nighthawks have solved enough difficult ordinary cases to earn a considerable reputation.

Leaving his umbrella outside, the man approached the sofa with a rueful smile.

"I've visited every mercenary company, security firm, and private investigator on the neighboring streets. You're my last hope. None of the others has enough available personnel to take another assignment. To be honest, if I hadn't seen the restaurant waiter delivering your lunch, I would never have guessed another security company operated here."

That's entirely different from what I imagined...

Klein stared at him.

Rozanne asked, "They're all that busy? Are there really so many commissions?"

The man sat and sighed. "You're a mercenary group—pardon me, a security company. Surely you've heard about the armed robbery and murders on Howes Street?"

Howes Street. Armed robbery and murder. Unfortunately, I was one of the people involved...

Klein nodded with a heavy heart. "Yes."

"A vicious and merciless criminal was apparently operating in the city. Wealthy families in the surrounding streets—and throughout Tingen—became terrified. Many expanded their household guards and hired additional security personnel or private detectives. Demand in your profession now far exceeds the supply," the thin man explained clearly.

A textbook chain reaction...

Klein and Rozanne exchanged self-deprecating smiles.

The security business was enjoying a golden age, yet Blackthorn had received no benefit whatsoever. That said a great deal about how poorly the company was managed.

Of course, in another sense, it demonstrated how successfully the Nighthawks concealed their true purpose.

More than twenty minutes passed. The rain was finally weakening, and Klein was preparing to leave for the Shooting Club when Leonard Mitchell emerged from behind the partition. The black-haired, green-eyed man looked curiously toward the sofa.

"Who is this?"

"A client. Is the Captain back?" Rozanne asked happily.

"Back?" The thin man looked startled.

He had spent the entire time watching the front door. How could anyone have returned without his noticing?

Rozanne's expression stiffened. Then she laughed.

"A security company doesn't rely on a single entrance."

"Naturally." The man nodded in understanding.

Nor did he find the title "Captain" unusual. Security firms had evolved from mercenary bands and small mercenary guilds, so the term was common.

Leonard's white shirt was untucked, and his black vest hung loosely over it. After glancing at the client, he snapped his fingers.

"I'm one of Blackthorn's security personnel. How may I address you, and what service do you require?"

Perhaps accustomed to tales of mercenaries' unconventional manners, the man showed no offense. Instead, he released a breath of relief as Leonard sat opposite him.

After organizing his thoughts, he began.

"My name is Klee. I am the butler of Mr. Vickroy, a tobacco merchant. His only son, young Elliott, was kidnapped this morning. We informed the police immediately, and they have given the case high priority. Even so, Mr. Vickroy remains deeply worried.

"He hopes your knowledge of Tingen and the special channels available to mercenaries—security professionals—might allow you to investigate from another angle and help ensure young Elliott's safe recovery.

"If you locate the kidnappers' hideout, Mr. Vickroy will pay one hundred pounds. If you successfully rescue Young Master Elliott yourselves, he will double the amount to two hundred pounds."

Leonard smiled lazily.

"It seems Mr. Vickroy only wants us to locate them. Otherwise, he wouldn't value his only son's rescue at a mere one hundred pounds. A tobacco merchant with close ties to southern plantations could offer considerably more than two hundred."

"No. Mr. Vickroy is only an ordinary merchant, not a truly wealthy man. Besides, he believes the police are better equipped to conduct the actual rescue," Klee answered frankly.

"Very well. We'll accept." Leonard snapped his fingers again.

His green eyes turned toward Rozanne.

"My beautiful lady, please prepare a contract."

"Stop pretending to be a poet. All you do is recite other people's work," Rozanne shot back, momentarily forgetting the client as she fell into her habitual banter with Leonard.

Blackthorn didn't truly depend on ordinary clients. Their business was welcome but hardly necessary.

Rozanne left the reception desk and entered the civilian office. The clatter of the typewriter soon emerged.

The corners of Klein's mouth twitched. This is painfully unprofessional.

They don't even have a standard contract template.

How tragic...

And more tragic still, I work for this company.

Almost as soon as those thoughts arose, Rozanne returned with a simple contract containing only a handful of clauses. Klee and Leonard signed it.

After Klee applied his seal, Rozanne carried the document into the accounting office and asked Mrs. Orianna to stamp it with Blackthorn's company seal—a largely ceremonial object Dunn normally entrusted to Orianna, with Rozanne and the others receiving it on Sundays.

"I look forward to good news." Klee accepted his copy of the contract, rose, removed his hat, and bowed.

Leonard didn't respond. He appeared lost in thought.

Then he suddenly turned toward Klein and smiled.

"I'll need your help."

"What?" Klein stared at him.

"You and I will handle the commission together." Leonard's smile deepened. "I'm competent at fighting, shooting, climbing, sensing, chanting, and certain supporting tasks. Finding people isn't one of my strengths. And you don't expect Old Neil to go out in this weather, do you?"

When he said "sensing," his voice dropped so low that Klein could barely hear it.

"All right."

Klein wanted an opportunity to test his new abilities, though he remained wary of Leonard Mitchell.

Let's hope this goes smoothly. I wonder how useful a Seer's abilities will prove in practice...

Anticipation stirred within him.

Chapter 43: Search

Leonard looked at Klein, smiled, and nodded.

"Will you need anything belonging to the child?"

He had worked with Old Neil and the others often enough to know that divination required a medium, especially when its subject wasn't present.

Klein considered before saying to Klee, "I need clothes Elliott wore recently that haven't been washed or starched. An accessory he regularly carries would be even better."

He deliberately requested ordinary items that wouldn't invite too many questions.

Even so, Klee looked puzzled. "Why?"

Then he added, "I have a photograph of Young Master Elliott with me."

Why? Because I'm going to divine his location...

For a moment, Klein couldn't think of a suitable explanation.

Answering truthfully would violate the confidentiality rules. More importantly, Klee would probably storm out, tear up the contract, and curse them as frauds. If divination could really find the child, why wouldn't he hire the most famous Spirit Medium in Awwa County?

Leonard chuckled beside him.

"Mr. Klee, my partner—my colleague—keeps a rather unusual animal. Its sense of smell is keener than a hound's. We need clothes worn by young Elliott and an object he regularly carried to help the creature find his trail. As you know, most clues can guide us only to a general area.

"We'll need the photograph as well. Both of us should know what Elliott looks like."

Klee slowly nodded, accepting the explanation.

"Will you wait here, or come with me to Mr. Vickroy's residence?"

"We'll go with you. It will save time," Klein answered promptly.

He was eager not only to test his Beyonder abilities but also to save the kidnapped child.

"Very well. The carriage is waiting downstairs."

As he spoke, Klee took out a black-and-white photograph and handed it to Leonard.

It showed Elliott Vickroy alone. He was about ten, with hair long enough to nearly cover his eyes. Prominent freckles dotted an otherwise unremarkable face.

Leonard examined the picture and passed it to Klein.

After studying it carefully, Klein slipped it into his pocket, picked up his cane, and put on his hat. He followed the other two out of Blackthorn and into the carriage below.

The interior was spacious, with thick carpeting and a small table for passengers' belongings.

With Klee present, Klein and Leonard remained silent, watching through the windows as the carriage crossed rain-flooded streets.

After some time, Leonard broke the silence with a smile. "Your driver is quite skilled."

"Indeed," Klein agreed perfunctorily.

Klee forced a smile. "He'll be honored by your praise. We're nearly there."
