To avoid alerting the kidnappers, the carriage didn't stop at the Vickroy residence. Instead, it pulled over on a nearby side street.

Klee raised his umbrella and hurried back alone. Once they had waited a while, Leonard spoke to Klein again.

"My earlier speculation wasn't pointless. I wanted you to understand that the notebook will inevitably reappear—perhaps very soon."

"That isn't a comforting prediction." Klein tilted his chin toward the driver outside, warning Leonard not to discuss sensitive matters within an ordinary person's hearing.

Leonard whistled and turned toward the window. Raindrops streamed down the glass in wavering tracks, reducing the outside world to a shapeless blur.

Klee returned after some time carrying a bag. His hurried walk had splashed mud across the hems of his trousers and dampened the front of his shirt.

"These are the clothes Young Master Elliott wore yesterday. This is the Storm Amulet he normally carries."

Klein accepted the bag and inspected its contents. The clothes formed a miniature gentleman's suit: a small shirt, vest, bow tie, and matching pieces.

The bronze Storm Amulet was engraved with symbols representing gales and ocean waves, but it produced no reaction from Klein's spiritual perception.

"I'll describe everything that happened before the kidnapping in detail. Perhaps it will help you find him," Klee said as he sat, desperate for the specialists he had worked so hard to hire to prove useful.

He recounted the morning's nightmare.

Klein and Leonard had little interest in most of the details. They cared about three things: the number of kidnappers, whether anything unusual had occurred, and what weapons the criminals possessed.

Three kidnappers. Nothing supernatural. Two hunting rifles and one revolver.

Once they had the information they needed, they said goodbye to Klee and hired a light two-wheeled carriage.

Unlike public transportation, private carriages had either two or four wheels and charged by distance or time. Within the city, distance fares were four pence per kilometer; outside it, eight. Time fares began at two soli for the first hour or any part of it, followed by six pence for each additional fifteen minutes. Bad weather and requests for greater speed could raise the price further.

Azik had once told Klein that carriage drivers in Backlund were notorious for outrageous quotations.

Under ordinary circumstances, Klein would have considered a private carriage an extravagance. Today, he didn't need to worry. Leonard tossed the driver two one-soli notes.

"Charge us by time." He closed the carriage door.

The delighted driver looked at the notes in his hand. "Where would you like to go?"

"Wait a moment." Leonard turned toward Klein.

Klein nodded and removed Elliott's clothes from the bag. He spread them across the carriage floor, then wound the Storm Amulet's cord around the handle of his cane.

Holding the silver-inlaid black cane upright, he suspended its tip above the clothes.

Klein formed the sphere of light in his mind. His thoughts rapidly stilled, and his brown eyes darkened as he entered a state of partial Cogitation.

His spirit seemed to become weightless. He faintly perceived the spirit world that existed everywhere around him.

Silently, he repeated, Elliott's location.

After reciting the statement seven times, Klein released the black cane.

It didn't fall. Despite the carriage's motion, it remained standing upright before him.

Faint, invisible disturbances stirred around Klein. He felt as though pairs of unseen eyes had turned toward him.

During the past several days, he had occasionally experienced the same sensation while using Cogitation or Spirit Vision.

Suppressing his fear, Klein fixed his darkened eyes upon the cane and silently repeated the statement.

Elliott's location.

Elliott's location.

When he completed the final repetition, the cane toppled forward, pointing directly along the street.

"Straight ahead," Klein said in a deep voice as he retrieved it.

His words carried an ethereal quality, as though capable of penetrating an unknown realm.

This was one of the divination techniques he had learned: Dowsing Rod Seeking. The required instrument could be wooden, metallic, or made from a combination of both.

Ordinarily, the practitioner used two proper dowsing rods, each shaped like an angled wire. Holding the short ends, the diviner observed how the longer portions turned to determine a direction. As a Seer, however, Klein had discovered through practice that he could use the method to search directly for people and substitute his cane for the paired rods. The direction in which the cane fell indicated the direction of the target.

He couldn't use the method on the Antigonus family's notebook because he didn't remember the object at all. Without even the faintest impression of it, there was nothing for his spirituality to seek.

"Continue straight," Leonard called to the driver. "We'll tell you when to turn."

The driver didn't understand the peculiar arrangement, but the two notes in his pocket persuaded him not to question his passengers. He simply followed their instructions.

The carriage traveled slowly from one street to another.

Along the way, Klein repeated Dowsing Rod Seeking several times to correct their course.

After the carriage circled one building, he finally determined that Elliott was inside. Only thirty minutes had passed since they left Klee.

After dismissing the carriage, Klein no longer used Elliott's clothes. Instead, he placed the cane—with the Storm Amulet still wrapped around its handle—upright against the ground.

His eyes darkened again, and the raindrops around him seemed to whirl in place.

The cane fell at an angle toward the building.

Klein pointed to the stairs. "There."

"Sometimes I envy Old Neil. At this moment, I envy you too," Leonard said with a smiling sigh.

Klein glanced at him and replied evenly, "It isn't especially difficult. With training, you could certainly master it. Your spiritual perception is quite strong, isn't it?"

Leonard nodded and chuckled. "That isn't necessarily a good thing."

He quickened his pace and entered the building as the rain faded.

Afraid of soaking his formal suit, Klein jogged after him.

The building had only three stories and resembled an apartment block from Earth. Each floor opened directly from the stairwell and contained two units. Klein used Dowsing Rod Seeking on the first and second floors, but the cane remained upright, indicating that they should continue higher.

They silenced their footsteps and reached the third floor. Klein once more set his black cane against the floor.

Whoosh!

A breeze swept through the stairwell as his pupils darkened into depths that seemed capable of swallowing souls.

Whoosh! Whoosh! Whoosh!

The wind almost resembled distant sobbing.

Klein relaxed his hand. The cane, with the Storm Amulet wound around its handle, remained standing impossibly upright.

He silently repeated, Elliott's location.

The black cane fell without a sound and pointed toward the apartment on the right.

"They're inside." Klein picked up the cane and tapped his glabella twice.

Colors flooded his vision as he looked toward the right-hand apartment. Multiple auras glowed within.

"One, two, three, four... Three kidnappers and one hostage. The number matches. One aura is much smaller—that should be Elliott. Mr. Klee said they have two hunting rifles and a revolver," Klein whispered.

Leonard chuckled. "Then I'll recite a poem for them.

"Why become kidnappers when they could have lived happily as civilized men?"

He set down the bag containing Elliott's clothes and stepped forward. His expression became serene and melancholy.

His deep, magnetic voice gradually filled the corridor.

"Oh, terror threatens, crimson hope still cries;

"One truth remains: this fleeting lifetime flies.

"One thing is certain—all the rest are lies;

"The flower blooms but once, then fades and dies..."

Chapter 44: Fate

Leonard's song drifted through the door and along the winding wooden staircase like a lullaby.

Klein's thoughts immediately grew sluggish. He seemed to see silent moonlight shining upon a tranquil, rippling lake.

His eyelids became unbearably heavy, and he nearly fell asleep standing.

Amid the hazy sensations, he also felt a strange, invisible, and indifferent attention settle on his back. It resembled the feeling of wandering through the spirit world himself.

An inexplicable sense of déjà vu rose within him.

That jolt of familiarity allowed Klein to recover his train of thought. With his strong spiritual perception and extensive practice in Cogitation, he barely escaped the Midnight Poem's influence.

Even so, his mind remained profoundly calm, almost incapable of emotion.

Leonard soon stopped singing and looked over his shoulder with a smile.

"I'm considering asking the Captain for permission to requisition a Feynapotter lute. What kind of singer performs without accompaniment?

"I'm joking. I can hear them sleeping."

The black-haired, green-eyed Nighthawk strode toward the door separating them from the kidnappers and their hostage.

He abruptly twisted his shoulder and drove a fist into the lock.

Crack!

The surrounding wood broke apart with a muffled snap.

"That requires precise control." Leonard turned and smiled. Then he reached through the opening and unlocked the door from within.

Klein, now fully alert, possessed far less confidence. He reached beneath his arm, drew his revolver, and rotated the cylinder so a live cartridge rested beneath the hammer.

As the door swung open, he saw one man asleep across a table, his firearm near his feet. Another was rubbing his eyes and trying dazedly to stand.

Thud!

Leonard glided forward and knocked the awakening kidnapper unconscious with a single blow.

Klein was about to enter when his perception suddenly warned him. He spun toward the staircase.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

Footsteps approached from below. Their source soon appeared: a hatless man in a brown coat climbing toward the third floor with a paper bag of bread cradled in his arms.

He stopped abruptly when he saw the metallic barrel of a revolver aimed at him.

Reflected in his pupils was a young man wearing a half top hat, black formal suit, and matching bow tie. A cane rested against the railing beside him, while a much more dangerous revolver remained steady in his hands.

"Don't move. Raise your hands. Three, two, one..." Klein's voice was deep but relaxed.

Holding the revolver with both hands, he tried to imagine the stranger as an ordinary target at the Shooting Club.

Under the crushing tension, the man in the brown coat dropped the bread and slowly raised his hands.

"Sir, is this some kind of joke? Has there been a misunderstanding?" He forced a smile, unable to take his eyes off Klein's finger on the trigger.

Klein couldn't yet determine whether the man was an accomplice or merely a neighbor, but he revealed no uncertainty.

"Don't resist. Someone will determine whether this is a misunderstanding shortly."

At that moment, Leonard emerged after subduing the kidnappers. He looked down the stairwell and said lazily, "So they had another accomplice responsible for buying and delivering food."

The man's pupils contracted. He abruptly kicked the paper bag upward, trying to block Klein's sight with the flying bread.

Klein remained unaffected. Just as he had practiced, he calmly pulled the trigger.

Bang!

Blood erupted from the man's left shoulder.

He tumbled down and tried to escape toward the second floor, but Leonard had already grasped the handrail and vaulted over it.

With a heavy thud, Leonard landed on top of him.

The accomplice lost consciousness. Leonard brushed away several drops of blood that had splashed onto him, looked up at Klein, and chuckled.

"Fine shooting."

I was aiming for his leg...

The corner of Klein's mouth twitched almost imperceptibly as the metallic scent of blood reached him.

The Seer potion hadn't visibly improved his eyesight, hearing, or touch. Nevertheless, he could somehow "see" concealed objects and "hear" faint approaching footsteps, allowing him to anticipate events before they occurred.

Is that another aspect of spiritual perception?

Klein nodded thoughtfully as Leonard found a sharp dagger on the accomplice and dragged him into the apartment.

Carrying his cane in one hand and revolver in the other, Klein followed.

The gunshot had startled Elliott Vickroy awake. The child slowly straightened from the curled position in which he had been sleeping and sat upright.

Leonard had bound the first three kidnappers with the rope they had used on Elliott and piled them in a corner. Where rope ran short, he tore strips from their clothing.

He had bandaged the unconscious man shot in the shoulder but, unwilling to dirty his hands, had made no attempt to remove the bullet.

"W-who are you?" Elliott stammered, hope and delight entering his face as he took in the scene.

"Yes, you've guessed correctly. Remarkably perceptive," Leonard answered casually from his crouch.

I didn't expect this bastard to have a sense of humor...

Klein lowered his revolver. "We're mercenaries hired by your father—or security professionals, if you prefer."

"Really? I'm safe?" Elliott asked joyfully, though he still didn't dare move.

The few hours he had spent as a hostage had plainly been harsh. None of the ordinary recklessness of a ten-year-old remained.

Leonard stood and said to Klein, "Go downstairs and find a patrol officer. Have the police inform the tobacco merchant. I'd rather not walk through the streets with a child and four idiots. People might mistake me for another kidnapper."

Klein, who had been wondering how to handle the aftermath, nodded. He holstered his revolver, picked up his cane, and headed for the stairs.

As he descended, he had the nagging sensation that he had forgotten something. Behind him, Leonard spoke to Elliott.

"Don't be nervous. You'll see your father, mother, and old Klee soon. Why don't we play a round of Quint while we wait?"

Suppressing a laugh, Klein stepped outside. With directions from several pedestrians, he located two patrolling officers.

He didn't show his Special Operations Department badge. Instead, he identified himself as an employee of a licensed security company and gave a factual account of what had happened.

Nor was he concerned about carrying a revolver. Two days earlier, the appropriate internal channels had expedited a comprehensive firearms permit for him.

The officers exchanged a look. One left to summon reinforcements and notify the Vickroy family, while the other followed Klein back to the kidnappers' apartment.

More than forty minutes later, while the officer's attention was elsewhere, Leonard signaled for Klein to slip out with him.

"Trust me, going to the police station will consume an extraordinary amount of time. We should leave now," the poetic Nighthawk explained with casual confidence.

Since Leonard had made it clear that he would accept responsibility for any consequences, Klein didn't argue. He followed him away.

Nearly five minutes later, several carriages raced to the building. Old Butler Klee climbed from one beside his portly employer, Mr. Vickroy.

Klee still seemed dazed. He couldn't believe news had arrived so quickly. The entire affair felt like a dream.

A crisp snap made him turn.

A two-wheeled carriage passed with its window open. Inside, the black-haired, green-eyed Leonard had just snapped his fingers again.

Once they passed Vickroy's carriage, Leonard closed the window and turned toward Klein. He extended his right hand with a smile.

"A pleasure working with you."

I don't believe we're that friendly...

Klein politely shook his hand.

He hadn't expected the kidnapping case to be resolved so quickly. All he could do was marvel at what Beyonders were capable of. Even as a barely trained Sequence 9, he had already accomplished things that ordinary people would consider impossible.

"Among aristocrats, this gesture traditionally signifies peace after a clash of swords," Leonard explained with a smile.

"I know." Klein had studied alongside many aristocratic students.

Looking out the window, he frowned. "Shouldn't we speak with Mr. Klee? If he concludes that the police rescued Elliott, our commission will be cut in half."

One hundred pounds was at stake!

Admittedly, their earlier meeting proved beyond doubt that Blackthorn had provided the kidnappers' location.

"Don't concern yourself with it. Money isn't especially important to us." Leonard shrugged.

It is extremely important to me!

Klein forced a polite smile. "Many poets die young because of poverty."

Leonard chuckled. "Elliott won't lie about what happened. I can tell he still possesses a child's innocence. But you shouldn't expect a large share of the two hundred pounds."

"How much will I receive?" Klein asked immediately.

"By longstanding custom, half the commission goes to Mrs. Orianna for the team's additional operating funds. The participants divide the remainder. Unfortunately, you aren't a formal member, so you'll receive only about ten percent of that half."

Ten pounds? That's still quite good.

Pretending to remain disappointed, Klein asked, "Aren't you concerned that the kidnappers will realize they were affected by a Beyonder after they awaken?"

"They won't suspect anything. They'll believe the pleasant weather made them drowsy and that the song existed only in a dream. We've verified the effect before," Leonard replied confidently. "Your demon-hunting bullets are more likely to raise questions. But everyone can simply assume you're an eccentric obsessed with mysticism."

"I see."

Relieved, Klein nevertheless retained the feeling that he had forgotten or overlooked something.

After returning to Zouteland Street, he didn't wait for Klee. He walked to Welch's neighborhood, searched along another route, and then headed home. On the way, he bought beef and olives for dinner.

The evening meal was as pleasant as ever, with the three siblings chatting idly. They did receive one visitor: a worker who came to collect the penny required by the gas meter.

Night deepened. The siblings wished one another good night and retired to their rooms.

Klein was sleeping soundly when something familiar beyond his door suddenly awakened him. Confused, he stepped into the corridor and approached the unoccupied bedroom.

He pushed open its mottled door and saw a gray desk.

A notebook lay upon it, bound in a cover of hard black paper.

An inexplicable sense of déjà vu filled him as he approached and opened it.

The page displayed an illustration: a figure in magnificent clothes and an ornate headdress—The Fool.

Beneath it, a line had been written in Hermes:

"Everyone will die, including me."

Horror seized Klein as he suddenly realized that the corners of The Fool's mouth were curling upward.

He jerked upright with a gasp.

Crimson moonlight seeped through the curtains, outlining the bookcase, desk, and familiar shapes of his own bedroom. It had been a nightmare.

As a Seer, Klein understood that dreams often carried omens or revelations. He immediately searched his memories with great care.

Then he froze. He finally knew what he had overlooked that day.

While caught in Leonard's song, he had felt an invisible, indifferent attention focused on his back.

The sensation differed from the feeling he experienced during Cogitation or Spirit Vision. It had carried a distinct sense of déjà vu.

According to Captain Dunn, whenever that feeling arose, it probably meant...

Klein abruptly sat straighter and confirmed the connection.

Yes. It was the notebook—the Antigonus family's notebook!

Chapter 45: Returning

The Antigonus notebook is in the apartment across from the kidnappers!

The coincidence seemed extraordinary, but Klein trusted his intuition.

He sprang from bed and hurriedly removed the old clothes he slept in. Snatching up a white shirt, he pulled it on and rapidly began fastening the buttons.

One, two, three...

He abruptly realized that one side had somehow run out of buttons. The two halves of the shirt didn't align.

Looking more carefully, Klein saw that he had started with the wrong button, twisting the entire garment.

He shook his head helplessly, drew a deep breath, and slowly exhaled. Employing techniques from Cogitation, he forced himself to recover his calm.

Once properly dressed in the white shirt and black trousers, he managed to secure his shoulder holster with reasonably steady hands. He retrieved the revolver hidden beneath his soft pillow and slipped it inside.

There was no time for a bow tie. Klein threw on his formal coat, took his hat in one hand and cane in the other, and went to the door. After settling the half top hat on his head, he quietly turned the handle and entered the corridor.

He carefully closed his bedroom door and crept downstairs like a burglar. In the living room, he used a fountain pen and sheet of paper to leave a message for his siblings, claiming he had forgotten to mention an unusually early workday.

The moment Klein stepped outside, cool air swept over him and calmed his mind further.

The street was dark, silent, and deserted. Only the gas lamps broke the gloom.

He took out his pocket watch and opened it with a snap. It was barely six. The crimson moonlight hadn't completely faded, though the horizon already carried the first tint of dawn.

Klein was about to search for an expensive private carriage when he saw a four-wheeled, two-horse public carriage approaching without tracks.

"Public carriages run this early?"

Puzzled, he stepped forward and waved it down.

"Good morning, sir." The driver skillfully halted the horses.

Beside him, the conductor covered his mouth and yawned.

"Zouteland Street." Klein took two pennies and four halfpennies from his pocket.

"Four pence," the conductor said without hesitation.

After paying, Klein boarded and found the carriage empty. In the lingering darkness, the vacant interior felt distinctly lonely.

"You're our first passenger," the driver said with a smile.

The two brown horses lengthened their strides and moved briskly down the road.

"Honestly, I never expected to find a public carriage this early." Klein sat near the driver and made conversation to distract himself from his tension.

The driver laughed bitterly. "We operate from six in the morning until nine at night, and I earn only one pound a week."

"You don't receive breaks?" Klein asked in surprise.

"We rotate shifts and receive one rest day each week." The driver's voice grew heavy.

The conductor added, "Our shift runs from six until eleven in the morning. Then we eat lunch and rest through the afternoon. Around six in the evening, we replace the other crew. Even if we didn't require sleep, the horses certainly would."

"It wasn't always arranged this way," the driver scoffed. "An exhausted driver once lost control and overturned his carriage. People were hurt in an accident that should never have happened. Only then did they introduce shifts. Those bloodsuckers would never have become considerate without spilled blood."

As dawn brightened, the carriage continued toward Zouteland Street, collecting seven or eight passengers along the way.

Once his tension had subsided, Klein stopped talking. He closed his eyes and reviewed the previous day's events, searching for anything else he might have overlooked.

The sun had fully risen by the time the carriage reached Zouteland Street.

Holding down his hat with his left hand, Klein jumped briskly to the pavement.

He hurried into Number 36, climbed the stairs, and reached the Blackthorn Security Company's entrance.

The door was still locked.

Klein removed the key ring from his waist, selected the appropriate brass key, inserted it, and turned.

As the door slowly opened, he found Leonard Mitchell inside. The black-haired, green-eyed man was sniffing a cigarette of a recently fashionable brand.

"To be honest, I prefer cigars... You seem to be in a hurry," the poetic Nighthawk observed lazily.

Rather than answer, Klein asked, "Where is the Captain?"

Leonard pointed behind the partition. "In his office. As an advanced Sleepless, he needs only two hours of sleep each day. Factory owners and bankers would probably consider that the most desirable potion in existence."

Klein nodded and quickly passed through the partition. Dunn Smith had already opened his office door and stood at the threshold.

Dressed in his black trench coat and holding a gold-inlaid cane, he appeared solemn and severe.

"What happened?"

"I experienced déjà vu. It must have been the notebook—the Antigonus family's notebook." Klein struggled to keep his explanation clear and orderly.

"Where?" Dunn's expression didn't visibly change.

Klein's intuition nevertheless sensed a distinct, invisible disturbance within him—perhaps a flash of spirituality or a sudden shift in emotion.

"At the building where Leonard and I rescued the hostage yesterday, in the apartment across from the kidnappers. I didn't recognize the feeling at the time. A dream revealed it to me afterward."

Klein concealed nothing.

"It appears I missed an opportunity for a considerable contribution." Leonard had followed him to the partition and spoke with a chuckle.

Dunn nodded slightly, his expression grave.

"Have Kenley relieve Old Neil at the armory. Old Neil and Frye will accompany us."

Leonard immediately abandoned his frivolous manner and went to notify Kenley and Frye in the Nighthawks' recreation room. One was a Sleepless, the other a Corpse Collector.

Five minutes later, the Nighthawks' two-wheeled carriage rolled through the sparsely populated morning streets.

Leonard, wearing a feathered hat, shirt, and vest, served as driver. From time to time, he cracked the whip sharply above the horses.

Inside, Klein and Old Neil sat on one side, facing Dunn Smith and Frye.

The Corpse Collector's skin was unnaturally pale, as though he had avoided sunlight for years or suffered from a severe deficiency of blood. He appeared to be in his thirties, with black hair, blue eyes, a high nose, and very thin lips. A cold, shadowed air surrounded him, along with the faint odor of someone who worked frequently with corpses.

"Describe everything again, in detail," Dunn instructed, adjusting the collar of his black trench coat.

Klein stroked the topaz pendant hidden beneath his sleeve and recounted the events from the original commission through the dream.

Old Neil chuckled beside him. "Your fate seems thoroughly entangled with the Antigonus family's notebook. I never expected you to encounter it in such a manner."

That's precisely the problem. Isn't this far too coincidental?

Fortunately, Leonard said the preliminary investigation found no sign that the Elliott kidnapping involved a hidden supernatural faction. It was an ordinary crime motivated entirely by money. Otherwise, I would suspect that someone deliberately arranged every step...

The coincidence remained deeply unsettling.

Dunn expressed no opinion, remaining lost in thought. Frye was equally silent within his black trench coat.

The quiet persisted until the carriage stopped outside the building Klein had identified.

"We'll go up. Klein, you and Old Neil remain at the rear. Be careful—extremely careful."

Dunn climbed down and produced a peculiar revolver with an unusually long, thick barrel. He placed it in the right pocket of his coat.

"Understood."

Klein had no desire whatsoever to lead the way.

After Leonard found someone to watch the carriage, the five Beyonders entered the building in formation. Their footsteps nearly silent, they climbed to the third floor.

"This apartment?" Leonard pointed to the door opposite the kidnappers' former room.

Klein tapped his glabella twice and activated Spirit Vision.

His spiritual perception sharpened further. The door felt familiar, as though he had passed through it once before.

"Yes." He nodded with certainty.

Old Neil also heightened his spiritual perception. After studying the apartment, he said, "No one is inside, and I detect no spiritual radiance from magic."

Corpse Collector Frye added in his hoarse voice, "No evil spirits."

Even without consciously activating Spirit Vision, he could perceive many spiritual bodies, including evil spirits and lingering wraiths.

Leonard stepped forward and, as he had the day before, punched the lock.

This time, the surrounding wood shattered and the lock itself tore free, clattering loudly across the floor.

Klein felt an invisible seal break.

An overpowering stench struck him immediately.

"A corpse," Frye said coldly. "Badly decomposed."

He showed no hint of nausea.

Dunn extended his black-gloved right hand and slowly pushed open the door. The first thing they saw was a fireplace. For early July, the room radiated abnormal heat.

A rocking chair stood before the fireplace. An old woman dressed in black and white sat upon it with her head bowed.

Her body had swollen grotesquely. The skin was black-green and distended, looking ready to burst at the slightest touch and release the putrid matter within. Maggots and other parasites writhed among decaying flesh, blood, bodily fluids, folds of skin, and fabric. Through Spirit Vision, they resembled tiny points of light clinging to a darkness that had long since gone out.

Plop. Plop.

The old woman's eyes dropped from their sockets and rolled across the floor, leaving yellow-brown trails behind them.

Revulsion overwhelmed Klein. Unable to endure the stench any longer, he bent over and vomited.

Chapter 46: Portrait

"Ugh! Ugh!"

Crouched outside the apartment, Klein vomited uncontrollably. Since he hadn't eaten breakfast, his stomach was soon empty.

A square, tin-colored flask shaped like a cigar case appeared before him.

Its open mouth released a pungent blend of tobacco, disinfectant, and mint. The sharp aroma cleared Klein's nose and revived his senses.

The stench of decomposition still filled the air, but it no longer made him violently nauseated. His retching gradually stopped.

Klein followed the flask upward to a pale hand that scarcely seemed to belong to the living. Beyond the cuff of a black trench coat stood Corpse Collector Frye, cold and shadowed as ever.

"Thank you."

Having recovered, Klein braced his hands against his knees and stood.

Frye nodded without expression. "You'll be fine once you're accustomed to it."

He replaced the stopper, returned the flask to his pocket, and turned toward the badly decomposed corpse. Without gloves, he began examining the old woman.

Dunn Smith and Leonard Mitchell slowly moved around the room, occasionally touching the tabletop, newspapers, or other objects.

Old Neil pinched his nose and remained outside the door.

"Disgusting," he complained in a muffled voice. "I'm requesting extra pay this month."

Dunn turned his head and ran his gloved right hand over the wall beside the fireplace.

"Does this room feel familiar?" he asked Klein.

Holding his breath, Klein constructed the image of his silver pocket watch to calm himself.

Because Spirit Vision was already active, he immediately sensed something different. A scene flashed from the deepest recesses of his memory.

A fireplace. A rocking chair. A table and newspapers. Rusted nails in the door. Tin containers decorated with silver...

The images were dark and faded, like footage from an old documentary on Earth, but far blurrier and more illusory.

They rapidly aligned with the room before him. Déjà vu surged with unmistakable force, accompanied by a distant, ethereal cry that seemed to pass through invisible walls.

"Hornacis... Flegrea... Hornacis... Flegrea... Hornacis... Flegrea..."

"It feels somewhat familiar," Klein answered honestly as stabbing pain pierced his mind.

He quickly tapped his glabella twice and deactivated Spirit Vision.

Hornacis... The Hornacis mountain range mentioned in the original Klein's diary?

Those words were deciphered from the Antigonus family's notebook.

The whisper resembles one I've heard before, and it also included "Hornacis." Is it some form of temptation?

Alarm gripped Klein. He refused to pursue the thought, afraid of placing himself on the path toward losing control.

Dunn nodded slightly and approached a cupboard. Without warning, he pulled open its wooden door.

The bread inside had grown moldy. Beside it lay seven stiff, gray rats.

"Leonard, find a patrol officer downstairs and explain the situation," Dunn ordered.

"All right."

Leonard turned and left the apartment.

Dunn opened the doors to the two bedrooms and searched each carefully.

He found neither useful clues nor any sign of the Antigonus family's notebook. By then, Frye had also risen. He wiped his bare hands with a white handkerchief he had brought and said, "She died more than five days ago. There are no external wounds and no obvious signs of Beyonder involvement. Determining the precise cause will require a postmortem examination."

"Did either of you find anything?" Dunn asked Old Neil and Klein.

Neither was using Spirit Vision now. They shook their heads together.

"Apart from the corpse, everything seems ordinary," Old Neil said. Then he reconsidered. "Actually, no. An invisible force originally sealed the apartment. Similar barriers often form during ritualistic magic, as you know."

Dunn was about to respond when he looked toward the door.

Several seconds later, Klein and Old Neil sensed the same thing and turned toward the stairwell.

Faint footsteps gradually grew louder. Leonard appeared with a police officer.

The moment the officer smelled the room, his expression changed. Even so, he cooperated with his "colleagues" from the Special Operations Department and began knocking on the second-floor residents' doors to gather information about the occupants above.

Before long, the corporal—with two silver chevrons on his uniform—stood looking at the body in the rocking chair.

"Katy Stefania Bieber, approximately fifty-five to sixty years old, widowed. She and her son, Ray Bieber, have rented this apartment for more than ten years.

"Her late husband was a gemstone craftsman. Ray is around thirty, unmarried, and inherited his father's trade. He earns approximately one pound fifteen soli per week. According to the neighbors, neither of them has been seen for more than a week."

Before the officer could continue, Klein understood the crucial fact.

Ray Bieber was missing—or, more precisely, no one knew where he had gone.

He might very well have the ancient notebook with him.

"Do you have a photograph of Ray Bieber?" Dunn asked, adopting the role of a senior inspector.

It required little acting. Dunn genuinely held the corresponding rank on the police department's register, receiving its salary and allowances in addition to his income from the Church.

The officer nervously shook his head. "I'm not certain. I'll need to search the station records. We don't normally keep photographs of every citizen."

"Understood. Continue questioning the residents on the first floor. Be thorough," Dunn instructed.

After watching the officer leave, he closed the door and turned toward Old Neil.

"I'll leave the next step to you. Otherwise, we'll have to put every resident to sleep and extract Ray Bieber's appearance from their dreams. I don't trust portraits drawn from verbal descriptions."

Old Neil nodded. He removed several thumb-sized bottles from a pocket of his antiquated black robe and sprinkled their contents in a particular sequence.

Then he took out a handful of powder and scattered it in a circle around himself.

A sharp odor spread through the room, strangely unaffected by the overwhelming stench of decay. Klein suddenly sensed an invisible field enclosing Old Neil, separating him from both his surroundings and everyone present—much like the barrier that had sealed the apartment.

Old Neil half closed his eyes and murmured an indistinct incantation. Though he hadn't prepared to listen, Klein faintly caught phrases such as "Goddess, grant me strength" and "We seek the protection of Night."

Hum!

A sudden wind rushed through the windows and lifted the powder.

Klein's heart trembled, and gooseflesh rose across his body. A terrifying spiritual "scent" rapidly filled the room, something he instinctively feared to confront directly.

Confusion and tension seized him. His mind felt as strained as it did while attempting an advanced mathematical problem far beyond his level.

Old Neil's eyes suddenly opened. They had become entirely black.

He took a fountain pen from his pocket and began drawing on a scrap of paper atop the table. His movements were so rapid that his entire body trembled.

Klein focused on the page. A face quickly emerged, with deep-set eyes and a high nose.

After adding short, naturally curly hair, Old Neil wrote a line beneath the portrait:

"Black hair. Deep-blue eyes. A fully ceramic replacement tooth on the left side of his mouth."

Clatter!

The fountain pen fell onto the paper as Old Neil convulsed several times.

"This is Ray Bieber's appearance, reconstructed from the traces he left in the room," he whispered as his eyes rapidly returned to their ordinary color.

Then he turned toward his original position and slowly circled it. The invisible barrier dissolved into a light breeze.

"Praise the Lady."

Old Neil touched four points on his chest, tracing the shape of the crimson moon.

Klein's taut nerves relaxed. Examining the portrait closely, he found Ray Bieber's features otherwise unremarkable. His expression appeared mild, though his philtrum sagged conspicuously.

"I'll attempt Dowsing Rod Seeking."

He took the portrait, found men's clothing in one bedroom, and spread both on the floor.

Dunn, Leonard, and Old Neil watched without interfering as Klein positioned his black cane above the clothing and portrait. Frye remained as silent as ever.

Klein's eyes darkened from brown to black. After completing the required repetitions, he released his grip.

The cane remained standing, as though embedded in the floor.

Ray Bieber's location, Klein repeated silently.

Wind hummed. The cane began to fall, only to change direction repeatedly. Finally, it traced small circles through the air.

Without anyone touching it, the black cane returned to a stable upright position.

Klein repeated the divination several times and received the same result. He could only shake his head at Dunn and Old Neil.

Some strange power was interfering with the search.

Dunn removed one black glove.

"Leonard, Klein—take the portrait and ask the neighbors to confirm the likeness. Afterward, we'll issue a warrant for Ray Bieber on suspicion of murdering his mother."

"Understood."

Holding his cane, Klein bent to retrieve the portrait.

Once the neighbors confirmed that it did indeed depict Ray Bieber, Dunn ordered Leonard and the officer to complete the necessary procedures at the station. He and Frye went to several Tingen bars to pursue information through other channels.

Klein and Old Neil returned to Blackthorn by public carriage. It wasn't yet eight when they arrived, and Rozanne hadn't reported for work.

After shutting the door, Klein turned toward Old Neil. Hoping both to learn and to resolve his doubts, he asked, "Why would I have delivered the Antigonus family's notebook to Ray Bieber's home?"

The apartment lay in an entirely different direction from both Iron Cross Street and Welch's residence.

Old Neil walked to the sofa and chuckled.

"Isn't it obvious? No one knows what power within the notebook you invoked. Perhaps curiosity led you to perform one of its rituals, attracting the attention of an existence you should never have disturbed. That entity wanted the notebook delivered to Ray Bieber and every trail severed so no one could discover it.

"Thus, aside from you—the person chosen to carry it—Welch and Naya committed suicide. As for you... Frankly, I still don't understand how you survived."

"Neither do I," Klein replied with a deliberately rueful smile as he sat. "I've considered essentially the same sequence of events. But one thing remains unclear: why did the notebook need to reach Ray Bieber?"

Old Neil shrugged. "Perhaps his Life Path Number[1] met some requirement. Or perhaps he is one of the Antigonus family's last surviving descendants. There are far too many possibilities. The notebook's appearance in Tingen may have been governed by similar causes."

"I think the descendant theory is more likely." Understanding dawned on Klein, followed by a sigh. "Unfortunately, I didn't recognize the clue immediately. Now both Ray Bieber and the notebook have vanished."

Old Neil laughed. "That's Dunn's problem. For you, this may actually be fortunate."

Klein frowned. "Why?"

[1] A Life Path Number, a concept associated with Pythagorean numerology, is calculated from a person's date of birth and is said to describe the nature of their journey through life.

Chapter 47: Old Neil's Lack of Money

Old Neil rubbed his temples.

"We now have a general understanding of why the three of you attempted suicide, and the notebook is most likely in Ray Bieber's possession. More importantly, the matter has already been exposed. Whether you're alive or dead will have little effect on what happens next.

"I believe the hidden existence—or mysterious force—responsible for everything will no longer pay you any special attention. You don't notice every ant crawling across the ground, do you? Just make certain you don't do anything that reminds Him of you.

"Our warrant for Ray Bieber will also reach the Secret Order quickly. They'll realize that it concerns the Antigonus family's notebook. Believe me, an organization that has survived in secrecy for more than a thousand years possesses many sources of information.

"Their attention will shift entirely to finding Ray Bieber and recovering the notebook before we do. They will neither want nor have the capacity to continue harassing, following, or dealing with you.

"Congratulations, lad. You've finally stepped out of the shadow cast by your past. A new journey filled with sunlight lies ahead."

Klein nodded, relief and happiness entering his voice.

"I hope you're right."

Ever since transmigrating, uncertainty had surrounded him like fog. Now it finally seemed to be dispersing.

Even so, Klein couldn't entirely relax. The notebook appeared connected to him in some mysterious fashion. During an otherwise ordinary hostage-rescue commission, he had encountered its lingering traces by sheer coincidence.

He feared that one day a deliveryman would hand him a parcel, only for him to open it and discover the Antigonus family's notebook inside.

May everything proceed as Old Neil described...

Old Neil scoffed at his response.

"You don't sound like a devout believer of the Goddess. Shouldn't you be tracing the crimson moon over your chest and saying, 'May the Goddess bless us'?"

"You don't sound especially devout either, Mr. Neil. A true believer wouldn't describe the future as 'a journey filled with sunlight.'"

After studying mysticism under Old Neil for several days, Klein had developed a comfortable friendship with him and felt no need to restrain his sarcasm.

They met each other's eyes and chuckled in perfect understanding. At the same time, both tapped four points on their chests.

"Praise the Lady!"

The grinding of machinery came from the entrance as the Blackthorn Security Company's front door opened.

Elegant Mrs. Orianna entered the reception hall in a light-green dress, her hair arranged in fashionable coils.

"Good morning, Mr. Neil. Good morning, Klein." Carrying a small leather handbag, she smiled. "Another wonderful day. A very fine day indeed."

"Good morning, Orianna. You remain as beautiful as you were ten years ago," Old Neil replied with a chuckle.

Orianna narrowed her eyes and raised her chin.

"Mr. Neil, your compliments remain as infuriating as they were ten years ago."

She placed unmistakable emphasis on the words "ten years."

"Is that so?" Old Neil looked at Klein in genuine confusion.

Never say anything that reminds a lady of her age.

As an armchair expert who knew a little about almost everything, Klein immediately understood Orianna's irritation.

He smiled. "Good morning, Mrs. Orianna. You look as beautiful as ever."

"Thank you, our distinguished Khoy University graduate." Orianna nodded with a smile. "The old butler has already paid the commission. By the Captain's rules, half becomes additional team funding, while you and Leonard divide the rest. Since you aren't a full member, however, you receive only ten percent of that half. Come to the accounting office later and sign for it."

"How much did he pay?" Klein asked eagerly, though the deductions still pained him.

"Two hundred pounds. He said—and I quote—'Lord of Storms above! I never imagined or believed the matter could be resolved so easily. It's more unbelievable than a dream! Why has no one heard of your security company? Your obscurity is an insult to the entire profession!'"

Mrs. Orianna imitated the old butler's southern accent.

Klein thought seriously for several seconds, then said, "That seems terribly unfair to the kidnappers."

Two Beyonders had resolved the case quickly through methods that felt almost effortless. It was like an adult wearing full combat equipment bullying a group of children.

"They were simply unlucky. The divine must have withdrawn its protection from them." Orianna laughed softly. "I told the butler we had merely been fortunate. One of our informants happened to see the kidnappers bringing the child into their hideout. Therefore, he shouldn't expect too much from us. We truly are a very ordinary security company."

Generally, the more often someone insists that something is ordinary, the less ordinary it is.

Klein watched with a smile as Mrs. Orianna passed through the partition and entered the accounting office.

Old Neil pursed his lips enviously.

"You really are a fortunate lad. You haven't worked here long, yet you've already encountered a two-hundred-pound commission."

"Is that unusual?" Klein asked.

Until now, he had spent his time studying history and mysticism or wandering the city in hopes that spiritual perception would lead him to a clue.

"According to Orianna's records, some weeks pass without a single ordinary commission. Most of the ones we do receive are worth less than twenty pounds." Old Neil rubbed the moonstone at his wrist and sighed.

Then he looked at Klein expectantly.

"If you encounter another valuable assignment, remember to include me."

Something about his tone struck Klein as strange.

"Mr. Neil, are you short of money? How much do you earn each week? If you'd rather not say, please ignore the question."

Old Neil leaned back against the sofa and chuckled.

"There's no reason to hide it. I've served here for many years. Between the Church and the police department, I currently receive twelve pounds per week."

"Twelve pounds a week?" Klein blurted in astonishment.

At fifty-two weeks a year, that amounted to more than six hundred pounds annually.

The Tingen Morning Post and Honest Paper had reported that prominent attorneys earned eight hundred to a thousand pounds per year—and those were the finest lawyers in the profession.

Managers at Benson's trading company earned only six pounds a week, already considered an excellent salary.

"Yes. The pay is quite generous, and we owe no taxes on it," Old Neil added with a smile.

Benson had explained that anyone earning more than one pound per week paid Category E income tax. Government and company employees paid three percent on weekly earnings between one and two pounds, five percent between two and five, ten percent between five and ten, fifteen percent between ten and twenty, and a maximum of twenty percent above that.

Klein had also read about four other categories in the newspapers. Category A covered income from land, houses, and other tangible property, including rent. Category B applied to farmers. Category C taxed profits from bonds, funds, and shares. Category D covered commercial, financial, and professional income.

"An enviable arrangement," Klein agreed.

"However..." Old Neil shook his head. "Even that income isn't sufficient for Beyonders like us, who constantly study hidden mysteries and practice or experiment with rituals."

"Can't you apply for the required materials?" Klein asked in confusion.

Old Neil scoffed. "There are limits. Sometimes we must provide an adequate official justification. If you want to study further or experiment independently, you have to purchase the materials yourself, either through internal channels or at underground markets."

Klein jolted. "Beyonder materials are openly sold at underground markets? I thought the Churches prohibited that."

He desperately lacked independent sources for such things. As the leader of a mysterious organization in its infancy, he couldn't rely on the Nighthawks to provide everything forever.

"Such commerce can't be completely controlled. In mysticism, all things possess spirituality, and all spirituality originates from the same source. Our materials aren't limited to extraordinary creatures. They include ordinary animals, plants, and minerals.

"Consider the poison hemlock, gold mint leaves, and night vanilla in your Seer potion. Those are common substances encountered in daily life. They may lack obvious extraordinary properties, but each possesses special characteristics. Proper compounding and blending can produce particular effects. The Church cannot prohibit trade in all of them," Old Neil explained.

Without waiting for Klein to respond, he continued, "Nor is only the core of an extraordinary creature useful. Take the Lavos Squid: besides its blood, its eyes, skin, and tentacles are all valuable materials. Unless the Church captures every creature using only its own personnel, preventing any material from entering circulation would impose an enormous financial burden. The lower the grade, the more impractical complete control becomes. We can only do our utmost to contain the rarest and most dangerous substances."

Old Neil abruptly laughed.

"There's another important consideration. Knowing where an underground market operates is preferable to having one we know nothing about. As long as secret organizations continue to exist, tolerating a monitored market is sensible. It also gives us access to materials we otherwise lack.

"Naturally, contraband appears. Unless an item is absurdly dangerous, we generally turn a blind eye. At most, we confiscate it to enrich our own storerooms."

"Is that partly because the major Churches restrain one another, preventing any single Church from acting too aggressively?" Klein guessed.

Old Neil grunted in acknowledgment but offered no details.

"As a Seer, I'll eventually need many materials for practice. Could you take me to an underground market, Mr. Neil?" Klein asked with perfectly valid justification.

Old Neil looked troubled.

"Most of the people active in those places aren't actually Beyonders. Some are aristocrats fascinated by mysteries, while others are wealthy enthusiasts of mysticism... Unfortunately, I have a thirty-pound debt coming due. Visiting the market would be inconvenient for me at present."

"I see..."

Klein hadn't expected Old Neil's debt to be the obstacle.

After a moment, he carefully asked, "Would you like me to lend you something? I just earned ten pounds from the commission."

"No need. I'll manage." Old Neil patted the sofa and slowly rose. "Age is an enemy no living creature can defeat. Last night's watch has exhausted me. Spend the rest of the morning reviewing my lessons and reading the documents. Tomorrow, I'll teach you the foundations of ritualistic magic."

"All right."

Klein stood, removed his hat, and said goodbye.

When Dunn hadn't returned by noon, Klein pretended to continue searching for the notebook and resumed his wandering through the streets.

Now that he had earned ten pounds, he no longer needed to wait for the next funding disbursement.

He could go directly to the Divination Club.

The occasional whispers and illusions produced during Cogitation and Spirit Vision made him increasingly eager to begin "acting."

Chapter 48: Hanass Vincent

At the Divination Club on the second floor of Number 13 Howes Street in Tingen's North Borough, Klein once again encountered the attractive attendant who received visitors.

Her long yellow-brown hair remained arranged in elegant coils, lending her a mature grace that made her age difficult to guess.

"Good afternoon. Mr. Glacis isn't here today. Would you like to choose another diviner?" she asked with a smile.

Klein had just removed his silk top hat and was about to put it back on. Her words stopped him in surprise.

"You remember me?"

That visit had been five days ago.

The woman pursed her lips in amusement.

"You were the first customer ever to request Mr. Glacis—and, to this day, the only one. It would be difficult to forget you."

So she remembers me as someone willing to sacrifice quality to save a few pennies?

Klein mocked himself inwardly, then asked after some thought, "When did Mr. Glacis last visit the club?"

The attendant glanced at him and searched her memory.

"Honestly, we don't keep track of exactly when members come and go. They have their own lives and obligations. Still, I don't believe Mr. Glacis has returned since giving you that reading."

I wish him well. May the Goddess protect him...

Klein silently offered a prayer but asked no further questions.

Instead, he smiled. "I'm not here for a reading today. I'd like to join the club."

"Truly? We're delighted." The attendant displayed an appropriate degree of pleased surprise. "The first year's membership fee is five pounds, with annual renewals costing one pound thereafter. I assume I don't need to explain all the benefits again?"

Klein took out one of the five-pound notes he had recently received and watched Henry Augustus's portrait leave his possession.

After carefully inspecting the watermark and other protections against counterfeiting, the woman stored the note and handed Klein a form.

"Please provide your details while I prepare the receipt."

A receipt? I should have you invoice the Blackthorn Security Company...

Amused by the thought, Klein picked up the fountain pen on the desk and filled in his name, age, address, and employer using blue-black ink.

He deliberately left his exact date of birth blank. For a Seer, that information could expose profound secrets through the calculation of a Life Path Number.

Once his registration was complete and he had received the receipt, the attendant extended her right hand.

"Welcome to the Tingen Divination Club. I'm Angelica Barrehart, one of the attendants. These are your membership cuff links. The special inscription identifies you as a member."

"A pleasure to meet you, Madam Angelica."

Klein shook her hand and accepted the dark-gold cuff links.

Their special inscription used the Hermes root for "diviner."

Angelica withdrew her hand and thought for several seconds.

"May I ask which forms of divination you know best? Or would you prefer to study a particular method through the club? We take members' interests into account when inviting prominent diviners to lecture. We can also introduce you to others with similar expertise so that you may exchange knowledge."

"I know a little about most forms. There's no need to make any special arrangements for me," Klein replied with some exaggeration. Then he asked, "May I begin offering readings to clients? I'm not a complete novice."

He had come to act as a Seer, not to learn ordinary divination techniques that anyone could study.

Angelica maintained her courteous smile.

"Members may give readings at any time. Until we've confirmed your skill, however, we won't specifically recommend you when clients ask for guidance. What fee would you like to charge?"

"Two pence."

While unknown, Klein intended to compete on price.

"The club retains one-eighth, so the service charge will be a quarter-penny..."

Angelica reviewed the relevant rules, then added Klein's information to the album from which clients selected their diviners.

When everything was finished, she pointed toward the meeting room at the end of the corridor.

"Mr. Hanass Vincent is currently teaching astrolabe divination. You're welcome to sit quietly and listen or ask questions if you have any."

"Thank you."

His interest aroused, Klein headed toward the meeting room. He wanted to compare Hanass Vincent's instruction with Old Neil's.

Angelica quickly caught up and whispered, "Mr. Moretti, would you prefer coffee or tea? We offer Sibe black tea, Southville coffee, and Desi coffee."

From reading the newspapers, Klein knew that all three were considered inexpensive varieties. Even so, they were certainly superior to what he drank at home.

After considering, he said, "Southville coffee, please. Three teaspoons of sugar and no milk."

Southville in the Loen Kingdom was famous for its beer and red wine, both favored by many important figures. Its coffee attracted far less admiration.

"Certainly. I'll bring it shortly." Angelica gestured toward the meeting room.

Klein approached the half-open door and heard a voice with a pronounced Awwa accent.

"Among the divinatory arts, astrolabe divination is relatively complex..."

Only for ordinary people...

Klein silently appended the qualification.

Inside, five tables had been arranged in a ring around a middle-aged man in an old-fashioned black robe: Hanass Vincent.

The gentleman had conspicuous dark circles beneath his eyes. His brown hair was thick and stiff, every strand standing upright like a porcupine's quills.

Apart from that, nothing about him seemed unusual.

When Klein entered, Hanass acknowledged him with a slight nod but didn't interrupt the lesson. He merely slowed his speech.

With one hand in his pocket and the other holding his cane, Klein selected a seat near the side and leaned back comfortably. He surveyed the six members around the ring—four men and two women.

Some took careful notes. Others whispered or returned Klein's glance with rueful smiles.

After setting down his cane, Klein adjusted his half top hat and used the movement to tap his glabella twice.

He focused on Hanass. The man's aura appeared in its various colors, levels of brightness, and thickness.

Dark red. He's somewhat worried... His body otherwise appears healthy, but something is wrong in that area. I wonder what.

Klein listened to the lesson while analyzing silently.

He suddenly covered his mouth with one clasped hand to keep himself from laughing. At that moment, he felt very much like a fraudulent physician.

Even so, he was quite pleased with Spirit Vision. Although it provided only broad impressions rather than precise diagnoses, those impressions still revealed valuable information.

After examining the room, he tapped his glabella twice more under the guise of reflecting on Hanass's explanation.

Astrolabe divination was a branch of astromancy that ordinary people could also attempt. The simplest birth chart, for example, used the positions of the sun, crimson moon, Blue Planet, and Scarlet Planet at the subject's birth. The diviner matched their positions in the sky and their representative symbols against the astrolabe and the relevant constellations to infer the person's fate.

The method required calculating planetary and stellar positions, making it fairly complicated. Published tables existed to help practitioners find the necessary values. Some people simplified the process further by making vague interpretations based only on constellations.

Klein listened without interrupting or asking questions. Occasionally, he rubbed the topaz pendant hidden beneath his sleeve or sipped the Southville coffee Angelica had delivered.

After some time, Hanass rubbed his glabella.

"You should now try drawing your own astrolabes. If questions arise, come and find me in the Moonstone Room."

After Hanass left, a young man wearing a white shirt and black vest rose with a smile and approached Klein.

"A pleasure to meet you. I'm Edward Steve."

"Likewise. Klein Moretti."

Klein stood and returned the bow.

"Astrolabes are far too complicated. Every lesson puts me to sleep," Edward said self-deprecatingly.

Klein grinned. "Mr. Vincent can't resist sharing everything he knows. It's like being served a complete Intis banquet—too much to digest."

"I could finish an Intis banquet. They serve tiny portions on enormous plates." Edward laughed and sat beside him. "Are you new? I've belonged to the club for two years and never seen you."

"I joined today," Klein answered frankly.

"What is your specialty? Mine is divination with tarot and ordinary playing cards," Edward asked casually.

"I know a little of everything—but only a little."

Klein used the same description he often applied to himself. It wasn't false modesty. Vast areas of mystical knowledge and divination remained beyond him.

Just as the others began discussing horoscope divination, Angelica entered the meeting room.

"Mr. Steve, a client has requested you."

"Of course." Edward rose with a smile.

"You must be an excellent diviner," Klein observed.

"No. My price is simply ideal." Edward chuckled. "Ordinary clients almost never choose the most expensive option. Unless a donkey has kicked them in the head, they don't choose the cheapest few either. Those of us in the middle receive the most opportunities."

I am apparently someone who was kicked in the head by a donkey...

Watching Edward leave, Klein shook his head with a wry smile.

It seems my pricing strategy has a flaw.

He stood, retrieved his cane, left the meeting room, and found Angelica.

"I'd like to change my fee. Set it at eight pence."

Angelica gave him a long look. "We'll honor the request, but we'll also inform clients that you only recently joined."

"That's fine."

Klein nodded without concern. A hint of mystery could itself help a Seer attract clients.

After changing his listing, Klein returned to the meeting room.

At that moment, Hanass Vincent emerged from the Moonstone Room carrying a silver-plated mirror.

The well-known diviner addressed the five members still present.

"I've recently learned a new technique—magic mirror divination. Would anyone like me to teach it?"

Magic mirror divination? That can be dangerous...

Klein stopped outside the meeting room and frowned.

Chapter 49: Divination Art

As a Seer who had only recently crossed the threshold of mysticism, Klein would never claim to possess profound knowledge. He was certain, however, that he knew considerably more than an ordinary enthusiast.

Depending on the source of the revelation, divination could be divided into three broad categories.

The first included tarot cards, playing cards, spirit pendulums, dowsing rods, and dreams. In these methods, the subject's spirituality communicated with the spirit world to receive a revelation, which the diviner then interpreted.

Spirit pendulums and dowsing rods placed high demands on spirituality, the Soul Body, and the Astral Projection. Non-Beyonders couldn't obtain precise or distinct results from them. Card divination, by contrast, provided a fixed collection of symbols that allowed even faint revelations received by ordinary people to be expressed. Dreams occupied a position somewhere between the two.

The second category included Spirit Numerology, astromancy, and their many derivative methods. Using either the subject's personal information or changes in the natural world, the diviner reached an answer through calculation, deduction, and interpretation. Here, the initiative belonged not to the subject but to the diviner.

The third category relied on an outside entity independent of both subject and diviner. The Ouija boards Klein remembered from his previous life belonged to this group. Through a ritual, the practitioner directly requested an answer from an unknown or supernatural being.

An ordinary person would usually receive no response. But some succeeded in contacting malicious spirits or other entities that drove them insane. Such divinations frequently ended in tragedy.

The magic mirror divination Hanass Vincent had mentioned belonged to the third category. In mysticism, mirrors were associated with mystery and the unknown and could be regarded as doors to the spirit world.

Klein stopped outside the meeting room to hear how the well-known diviner would explain the method. Depending on what Hanass said, Klein might need to report him to the Captain and participate in a nighttime raid.

Of course, magic mirror divination could be conducted safely by requesting answers from one of the seven orthodox deities. An ordinary person was unlikely to receive a genuine revelation, but neither would they suffer danger or lasting consequences.

The Nighthawks and Mandated Punishers strictly controlled only those versions that appealed to evil gods or mysterious existences. Nor could practitioners simply invent titles and ritual phrases at random. Certain descriptions or qualities might attract the attention of an unknown entity.

In a world where Beyonder powers genuinely existed, such experiments often led to disaster. Klein even suspected that the original Klein, Welch, and Naya had followed instructions in the Antigonus family's notebook and performed precisely that sort of forbidden divination.

By then, Hanass had begun explaining the principles and procedure of magic mirror divination.

"First, choose an appropriate day and hour corresponding to the deity you worship. The Astromancy Manual can help you determine them. For example, we know that Sunday symbolizes the Evernight Goddess because the day represents rest.

"The periods from two to three in the morning, nine to ten in the morning, four to five in the afternoon, and eleven to midnight are all associated with the moon and therefore governed by the Evernight Goddess. A diviner praying to Her may conduct magic mirror divination during those hours on Sunday."

His fundamentals are fairly sound...

Klein nodded slightly, concealing himself behind the half-open door.

With the seven great Churches restraining one another, some mystical knowledge had inevitably spread among the public. The meanings of many symbols, for example, could be found in the Astromancy Manual. Without potions or Beyonder abilities, however, ordinary practitioners seldom obtained meaningful results.

"Second, inspect the mirror carefully. It must be coated with silver. Place it in the position representing the moon..."

Hanass demonstrated using the mirror in his hands.

No. He should perform spirit dowsing first. Select a position, repeat the statement "This place is suitable for magic mirror divination" seven times, and observe the pendulum. Clockwise means true, counterclockwise false.

Of course, when appealing to a malicious supernatural existence, the location matters less than whether the entity finds the questioner interesting...

Silently correcting Hanass, Klein felt like an instructor observing someone else's lesson.

Unable to hear those thoughts, Hanass Vincent continued describing the preparation in an ordinary tone.

When the members finished taking notes, he said, "After bathing, draw every curtain and lock every door. Light one candle and place it before the mirror, then pray sincerely to the deity you worship. Keep the request simple. Ornate language is unnecessary.

"After repeating the prayer seven times, pick up the mirror and let it fall gently to the floor. Gently, mind you. Memorize the pattern of the broken pieces, because that is the revelation granted by the divine. I'll now explain the principal symbols."

So this is an orthodox version of magic mirror divination.

Klein sighed in relief, returned to his former seat, and drained the remainder of his Southville coffee.

"Orthodox" divination of this kind meant that the practitioner might receive a revelation but would usually be unable to interpret it with confidence.

A Beyonder who reached the corresponding stage, however, could look directly into the mirror and receive clear information—assuming something answered.

Because a shattered mirror could form countless symbolic patterns, Hanass's explanation lasted a long time. He was still speaking when Edward Steve returned after finishing his client's reading.

Klein didn't ask what question Edward had answered or which method he had used. Respecting the privacy of another diviner's client was an unwritten rule. While acting as a Seer, Klein needed to obey such customs strictly unless the other party volunteered the information.

"I've noticed that our interpretations are often so vague they can satisfy almost any request," Edward said after drinking some Sibe black tea. "Different people can always find a way to apply the same statement to themselves.

"For example: 'After adversity and disaster, you will eventually see the light of hope.' No one says when that light will appear. Or: 'The journey will be difficult, but you will certainly reach your destination alive.' The dead can't return to dispute the prediction."

Because he hadn't heard the beginning, Edward paid little attention to Hanass's magic mirror lesson.

"Survivorship bias," Klein supplied with a smile.

The term referred to statistical conclusions drawn only from people fortunate enough to survive while ignoring those who had died, creating an obvious distortion.

"Exactly. Emperor Roselle truly was a philosopher," Edward marveled.

Speechless, Klein raised his empty cup and pretended to drink.

The members spent the entire afternoon immersed in horoscope studies and magic mirror divination, occasionally drawing Klein and Edward into their discussions.

Whenever that happened, Klein did his best to fulfill his unofficial Nighthawk duty. He subtly steered the conversation away from Beyonders and genuinely dangerous ideas.

He failed, however, in the task he cared about most. Several clients visited the club, but not one selected him for a reading.

Perhaps I need to approach them more aggressively. Should I say things like, "Misfortune surrounds you," "You've suffered bad luck recently," or "Nothing you attempt will proceed smoothly"?

No. That isn't how a Seer should behave.

Klein shook his head with a self-deprecating smile.

He picked up his cane, rose, said goodbye to the others, and left.

At half past five, Edward Steve put on his coat and prepared to leave the Divination Club when he saw a familiar figure.

"Good afternoon, Glacis. It's been some time." He greeted his friend with a smile.

Glacis wore his usual formal suit and black bow tie, with a monocle hanging against his chest.

Then Edward noticed that he looked unwell. Even his normally soft blond hair appeared dry and lifeless.

"Good afternoon, Edward..." Glacis covered his mouth with one fist and coughed several times.

Edward's expression filled with concern. "Are you ill?"

"I was gravely ill. It developed into pneumonia. If my wife hadn't encountered a remarkable apothecary and obtained a miraculous medicine, you would probably be visiting me in the cemetery." Lingering fear and joy mingled in Glacis's voice.

"Lord above, I can hardly believe it. You were perfectly healthy before, and now you look so frail. I remember performing a divination for you, but nothing suggested that a severe illness was approaching." Edward waved his cane in astonishment.

"My own reading produced the same result. Perhaps neither of us deserves to call himself a diviner. Besides..."

Glacis suddenly remembered what had happened on Monday, and his expression became extraordinarily serious.

At that moment, the attractive Angelica approached and bowed with a pleasant smile.

After exchanging greetings, she expressed concern over Glacis's health and offered several suggestions. Then, as though mentioning something incidental, she said, "Mr. Glacis, Mr. Moretti—the gentleman who requested your services before—has joined our club."

"The man whose fortune I read?" Glacis's eyes lit up. "Lord, where is he?"

"He left only a short while ago."

Neither Angelica nor Edward understood his extraordinary reaction.

Glacis paced two agitated steps. "The next time he visits, please ask when he plans to return if I'm not here."

"Glacis, what happened? Did Mr. Klein Moretti do something to you?" Edward asked in confusion.

Glacis raised one arm and stared into Edward's and Angelica's questioning eyes.

"He is a very, very, very miraculous..."

After emphasizing "very" three times, he lowered his arm and finished excitedly:

"Doctor!"

Chapter 50: Old Neil's Method of Repayment

At half past seven that evening, the Moretti family gathered around the dining table.

"Klein, why would a consultant need to report so early? Are emergency assignments at the security company more dangerous?" Benson asked with concern as he speared a potato from the beef stew.

Klein carefully removed the bones from a piece of fried fish and delivered the explanation he had prepared.

"A collection of historical documents had to be shipped to Backlund immediately. I needed to supervise the transfer and make certain nothing was missing. As you can imagine, those fist-swinging brutes can't read a word of Feysac."

After finishing his mouthful, Benson sighed.

"Knowledge truly is important."

Seizing the opportunity, Klein took out his remaining five-pound note and handed it to Benson.

"I received an additional payment today. It's time you bought some respectable clothes too."

"Five pounds?" Benson and Melissa exclaimed together.

Benson accepted the note and examined it repeatedly, disbelief and suspicion on his face.

"That security company is remarkably generous..."

He earned one pound ten soli per week—exactly six pounds every four weeks. This single bonus was only one pound short of his entire monthly salary.

Yet with that income, Benson had supported his younger siblings, kept a decent roof over their heads, provided meat two or three times a week, and bought them several new garments each year.

"Do you doubt me?" Klein asked deliberately.

Benson laughed. "I doubt you possess either the skill or the courage to rob a bank."

"You aren't good at lying," Melissa added seriously after lowering her knife and fork.

Actually, I've become quite accustomed to lying...

Klein immediately felt ashamed. Circumstances had forced him into secrecy, but his sister's complete faith still left him melancholy.

"Today's work was urgent and important, and I played a crucial role. That's why they paid me five pounds," he explained.

In a sense, every word was true.

He intended to conceal the five pounds Blackthorn would reimburse for his Divination Club membership. Bringing home another large payment would frighten his siblings and make them suspect illegal activity. Besides, he needed to save money for materials, Seer practice, and further mystical study.

Benson took a bite of wheat bread and thought for more than ten seconds.

"My present work doesn't require elegant clothing. To be precise, what I already own is adequate."

Before Klein could argue, he continued, "This additional income means we finally possess genuine savings. I plan to buy several accounting books and study them. Klein, Melissa, I refuse to remain below two pounds a week five years from now. As you know, my boss and manager have manure for brains, and every time they open their mouths, the smell escapes."

"An excellent idea," Klein agreed. He used the opportunity to guide the conversation. "You should also read the grammar texts in my room. If you want a dignified position and handsome salary, proper language will be essential."

Civil service examinations may appear in the Loen Kingdom someday. Preparing early would give Benson an advantage.

Benson's eyes brightened.

"I'd forgotten about that. Come, let us toast to a splendid future."

Instead of beer, he poured clear oyster soup into three cups and clinked his against those of his siblings.

After drinking, he looked at Melissa, who was struggling with the fried fish, and chuckled.

"Besides Benson's books, I believe Melissa needs a new dress."

Melissa immediately looked up and shook her head.

"No. I think it would be better—"

"To save the money," Klein finished for her.

"Yes." Melissa nodded firmly.

"A dress won't cost very much if we avoid the finest fabric and newest fashion. We can save whatever remains," Klein said in a tone that permitted no disagreement.

Benson added, "Do you intend to wear the same old dress to Selena's sixteenth-birthday party?"

Selena Wood was Melissa's classmate and close friend. Her family was comfortably situated: her elder brother practiced as a solicitor, while her father held a senior position at the Tingen branch of Backlund Bank.

The so-called party would be modest—a dinner with friends, followed by conversation and cards.

"All right," Melissa murmured with her head lowered. Then she stabbed a piece of stewed beef with unusual ferocity.

After a brief silence, she remembered something and abruptly looked up.

"Mrs. Shaud next door sent her maid with a calling card. She wants to make a formal visit tomorrow—Sunday—at four in the afternoon, so she can meet her new neighbors."

"Mrs. Shaud?" Klein looked at his siblings in confusion.

Benson tapped the table while thinking.

"Mrs. Shaud of Number 4 Daffodil Street? I've met her husband. He's a senior solicitor."

"Perhaps he knows Selena's brother," Melissa said with a trace of pleasure.

We live at Number 2, and they're at Number 4...

Klein nodded slightly.

"Getting to know our neighbors is important, but I still have to work Sunday. Monday is my day off. Please give Mrs. Shaud my apologies."

The subject reminded him of the neighbors from his childhood and those in the old Iron Cross Street apartment. Amused, he sighed.

"A formal visit? Shouldn't neighbors become acquainted through ordinary daily encounters?"

"You don't understand because, despite reading so many newspapers lately, you haven't examined the magazines written for households and middle-aged women," Benson replied cheerfully.

"They define families earning between one hundred and one thousand pounds a year as the middle class. According to them, this group forms the kingdom's foundation, free from both the arrogance of aristocrats and the wealthy and the coarseness of the lower classes.

"The magazines teach simplified versions of aristocratic social customs, which the middle class eagerly imitates. That creates distinctions among intimate calls, semiformal calls, and formal calls."

Shaking his head with a laugh, Benson continued, "Ladies and gentlemen who consider themselves part of that class pay close attention to such details. They visit friends and neighbors between two and six in the afternoon, an occasion known as a morning call.[1]"

"A morning call?" Klein and Melissa repeated in surprise.

Why would a visit between two and six in the afternoon be called a morning call?

Benson put down his utensils, raised both hands, and smiled.

"I have no idea. I only read the magazines one of my female colleagues brought to work. Perhaps it's because visitors wear morning dress."

Morning dress was formal daytime attire worn to Mass and social gatherings, as distinct from formal evening clothes.

"Very well. Tomorrow afternoon, remember to buy decent coffee and tea, along with muffins and lemon tarts from Mrs. Smyrin. We must treat our neighbors properly."

Chuckling, Klein soaked the last of his bread in the meat juices, gathered a piece of potato, and ate it.

...

The following morning was Sunday.

Klein finished the last mouthful of inferior tea, set aside the newspaper, and put on his half top hat. Taking his silver-inlaid black cane, he left at a leisurely pace and rode a public carriage to Zouteland Street.

He greeted Rozanne, who was preparing to sleep in the break room after her night shift, then descended all the way into the basement.

After rounding a corner, he encountered one of the Nighthawks: Sleepless Royale Reideen.

She was a cold-looking woman, with long, slender brows above large eyes and silky black hair.

"Good morning, Madam Reideen," Klein greeted with a smile.

Royale glanced at him with deep-blue eyes and gave an almost imperceptible nod.

They passed one another. Then Royale stopped, still looking straight ahead.

"Ritualistic magic is extremely dangerous."

Klein froze. By the time he turned, he saw only her retreating back.

"Thank you," he called after her with a frown.

Taking the next left, he soon found Old Neil in the armory, accompanied by Bredt, who wasn't scheduled to be there.

"We'll conduct the lesson at my home. I've already collected the necessary materials, and Bredt has agreed to guard the armory for me," Old Neil said with a chuckle.

Klein was immediately surprised. "We aren't doing it here?"

Old Neil lifted a silver chest and clicked his tongue.

"There isn't enough space here to practice ritualistic magic."

Klein asked no further questions. He followed Old Neil to the street, and they took a public carriage toward the outskirts of the North Borough.

Old Neil lived in a detached house. Roses, gold mint, and other "materials" filled the front garden.

Inside was a carpeted foyer furnished with two high-backed chairs and an umbrella stand.

Beyond it lay a spacious parlor. Pale wallpaper covered the walls, and dark-brown boards formed the floor. A small floral rug occupied the center of the room beneath a heavy round table.

Comfortable benches and armchairs surrounded the table, along with a piano.

"My late wife loved music." Old Neil gestured toward the piano as though mentioning it in passing. "The sofa and coffee table are in the bedrooms. We'll conduct the ritual in the parlor."

"All right," Klein replied cautiously.

Old Neil set down the silver chest, laughed, and said, "I'll demonstrate ritualistic magic. Observe closely and remember every step."

He removed a sheet of imitation goatskin parchment from the chest. It had been specially prepared, and strange patterns drawn in black ink released a tranquil fragrance.

Klein watched intently. Gradually, he realized that Old Neil appeared to be drawing an IOU.

When the old man entered the number "30" and the corresponding pound symbol, Klein could no longer restrain his confusion.

"Mr. Neil, what kind of ritual are you performing?"

Old Neil cleared his throat and answered with complete seriousness.

"Today, I will use magic to settle my thirty-pound debt."

You can do that?

Klein's eyes widened, and his mouth fell open.

[1] The calling-card customs and "morning calls" described here are based on Victorian-era social etiquette.
